# CHAPTER 441

## THE SAILOR

Harrow Cape was closed.

Solved one.

Dawn board had red strip nailed across route line.

**CROSSING HELD / NORTH SWELL / RECHECK TOMORROW**

Bellweather coach still two copper.

Old Merrow packet still four.

Dask stood beside board.

"Bellweather."

"I know."

"Second bell."

"I know."

"Old relay room."

"I know."

He looked at me.

"You don't want Bellweather."

"I want boat."

There.

No reason grander.

I had been on roads.

Wanted sea.

Old Merrow meant island town.

Half day if sea kind.

Four copper.

Twice Bellweather.

Also boarding with crutches.

Also trial leg not boat leg.

Also new place.

Dask folded coach notice.

"I'm taking Bellweather."

"Good."

"You taking packet?"

"Yes."

"Good."

No weird face this time.

Growth.

We ate breakfast.

At second bell he left.

Not dramatic.

He had pack, cracked tourist tide index, two pages of old lecture questions he still had not thrown away, and enough confidence to interrogate beacon relay town alone.

"Send note if you buy broken beacon," I said.

"I won't buy broken beacon."

"That's not what Sori said."

"She said don't let you buy one."

"Technicality."

"Accuracy."

Coach bell rang.

He climbed aboard.

"Greg."

"Yeah?"

"Road Response Kit is not auction trash."

"It currently lies two times in ten."

"Uncharacterized artifact."

"Five-copper liar."

"Retired comparative demonstrator."

"Bye, Dask."

"Bye."

Coach left north.

Now one.

Interesting.

I bought Old Merrow ticket.

Four copper.

Thirty-nine to thirty-five.

Ticket clerk looked at crutches.

"Lower boarding gate. Tell deckhand before gangplank."

"Why?"

"Upper plank steeper. Lower has cargo ramp."

Good.

"Trial limb?" she asked, seeing bundle.

"Shop property. Not for wet deck."

"Keep wrapped."

"Plan."

Packet **Narrow Gull** sat lower bay.

Two masts.

One low black hull.

Broad enough for cargo.

Passenger deck aft under partial awning.

Smelled fish, tar, wet rope, lamp oil.

Homey if home hated furniture.

I told deckhand.

He looked at crutches.

"Cargo ramp. Wait after handcart."

No fuss.

Handcart rolled aboard.

Then me.

Ramp wide.

Ribbed wood.

Wet.

Crutch tips held okay if vertical.

I took it slow.

Deckhand did not grab me.

Also good.

At top he said, "Aft bench. Rail left. Don't use loose coil as handhold."

"Wasn't planning."

"Everyone says that."

Boat people.

I liked them already.

Passengers maybe fourteen.

Two traders.

Family with sleeping toddler.

Three young men in matching blue work coats.

Older woman carrying basket covered cloth.

Thin man with red scarf who looked seasick while tied to dock.

Promising.

Cargo included flour sacks, two crates marked lamp glass, barrels, bundled reeds, one locked iron chest with customs seal.

Not mine.

Important reminder.

Captain gave safety speech.

Short.

"North swell. We run lee route around South Tooth. If I say sit, sit. If crew says move, move. Red line on deck is working lane. Stay out. If sick, rail leeward, not windward unless you want it back."

Useful.

Red-scarf man closed eyes.

We left third bell.

Lerrin pulled away.

Beacon field changed from infrastructure to pattern.

White South Head.

Amber inland.

Green north.

From water, spacing made sense differently.

Lines not town.

Directions.

Old life had sea travel.

Different ships.

Different magic.

Same truth.

Land looked simpler from water than living on it.

Water looked simpler from land than being on it.

Narrow Gull cleared bay.

Then moved.

Actually moved.

Coach motion was road translated through wheels.

Boat motion came from everywhere.

Up.

Down.

Roll.

Yaw.

Small constant corrections.

Crutches terrible.

I stayed seated.

Correct.

Trial leg stayed wrapped.

Very correct.

Red-scarf man lasted maybe twelve minutes.

He went leeward rail.

Captain's advice saved him from learning weather personally.

I ate nothing.

Also correct.

First hour quiet.

Then coast broke into black teeth.

South Tooth apparently one giant basalt spine sticking sea.

Narrow Gull went inside it where water dark but flatter.

Crew changed sail.

One of blue-coat men pointed.

"Glassbacks."

I looked.

Nothing.

Then water bulged.

Long translucent ridge under surface.

Not fish exactly.

Maybe fish.

Fantasy classification dangerous.

Second ridge surfaced thirty paces out.

Clear dorsal plate caught light like smoked glass.

Then vanished.

"Dangerous?" I asked.

Blue coat shrugged.

"If netting. Not packet."

"Why?"

"They follow charge lines. Nets catch fins. Boats too loud."

Good enough.

Magical ecology without lecture.

We rounded South Tooth.

Sea got worse.

Not storm.

Swell.

Long heavy rise.

Narrow Gull climbed.

Dropped.

My stomach filed complaint.

Denied.

Captain shouted, "Sit tight through next three!"

Everyone sat.

Crew didn't.

Third swell hit sideways enough to throw spray over port rail.

Old woman basket screamed.

Not woman.

Basket.

I looked.

Cloth moved.

Tiny horned head appeared.

Goat.

Of course another goat.

It screamed again.

Woman patted basket.

"Don't start."

Goat started.

Excellent.

Fourth swell smaller.

Crew relaxed fraction.

Then one of lamp-glass crates shifted.

Not far.

Maybe hand width.

But rope lashing changed angle.

Deckhand saw same time.

"Hold lane!"

Crewman moved.

Passenger blue coat moved too.

Deckhand shoved him back.

"Crew only."

Correct.

They reset wedge.

No Greg.

Good.

I watched lashing.

One line had gone slack.

Not broken.

Knot? No.

Barrel beside crate had settled against chock, changing pressure.

Crew fixed.

Done.

I had HT-LP-4 in pack.

Could measure peak load maybe.

Not relevant.

No one asked.

Keep pack closed.

Harder than sounded.

Tools made every problem whisper.

Use me.

Competence included saying no.

After South Tooth, route turned west around low island chain.

Old Merrow not visible.

Captain checked sky.

Then water.

Then a small bronze plate mounted rail.

Artifact.

Three grooves.

One bead trembling center.

He tapped plate once.

Bead shifted left.

"What is that?" I asked deckhand when he passed.

"Wake shear plate."

"Measures?"

"Cross-current disagreement under hull."

"Disagreement?"

"Bow wants one way. Stern wants another. Plate tells captain how rude water is being."

Good explanation.

"Accurate?"

"Accurate enough to know when stop being clever."

Excellent instrument.

Not for sale.

I wanted one anyway.

Problem list item maybe.

No.

I already had environmental warning category.

Don't multiply wants by seeing nouns.

Midway, captain changed course farther south than chart board at Lerrin had suggested.

Passengers noticed because island stayed wrong side.

One trader complained.

"Old Merrow is north of that cut."

Captain said, "Yes."

"You're going south."

"Temporarily."

"Why?"

Captain pointed rail plate.

"Shear."

Trader looked at sea.

"Looks fine."

Captain looked at him.

"Good. You can navigate looks from your seat."

Trader shut up.

Not because captain charming.

Because boat still captain's.

We detoured maybe half bell.

Cost time.

No crisis.

Sea eventually flattened.

Old Merrow appeared as green-black island with pale stone harbor cut into east side.

Not town first.

Terraces.

Rows climbing hill.

Something silver between them.

Water channels maybe.

Then roofs.

Then one tall white structure at ridge.

Not beacon.

Looked like giant fork with cloth or skin stretched between arms.

Wind catcher?

As we approached, it flexed.

Whole membrane bowed.

Then snapped flat.

A low note rolled across harbor.

Not loud.

Felt in chest.

Passengers ignored.

I did not.

"What the hell is that?"

Older woman with goat basket answered.

"Rain tongue."

"Rain tongue."

"Calls cistern crews when wet wind crosses east ridge."

"Calls rain?"

She snorted.

"No. If it called rain we'd be rich. Calls workers."

Better.

Old Merrow infrastructure already.

Packet entered harbor.

Then stopped.

Not dock.

Twenty paces out.

Captain swore.

Dock gate displayed black square.

Crewman said, "Why black?"

Captain: "Don't know."

That changed tone.

Black apparently not normal.

Harbor worker waved from pier.

Not signal code I knew.

Captain shouted.

Worker shouted back.

Wind stole half.

"...chain..."

Captain shouted again.

Worker pointed water between us and dock.

Chain gate.

Underwater harbor chain maybe.

Raised when shouldn't be.

Captain backed sail.

We drifted.

Passengers started questions.

Captain ignored.

Crew lowered small weighted line over side.

Depth? Maybe chain location.

Deckhand came aft.

"Stay seated. Harbor chain didn't drop."

Trader from before said, "How long?"

"Don't know."

"I have delivery."

"Then you'll deliver after we don't tear hull open."

Good.

I looked dock.

Three workers around capstan house.

One gesturing angry.

Another crouched near mechanism.

Third ran inland.

Human problem maybe.

Mechanical maybe.

Not ours.

Yet.

We waited.

Ten minutes.

Fifteen.

Rain tongue on ridge sounded again.

Low note.

Wet wind.

Cloud bank east.

Captain looked.

"Wonderful."

Rain coming.

Chain still up.

One worker on dock held two flags now.

White.

Black.

Alternating.

Captain frowned.

"Manual release jam."

Deckhand said, "Can they lower from west house?"

"If west clutch is live."

"Is it?"

"Black-white means they don't know."

There.

System uncertainty.

Packet couldn't dock.

Could wait outside harbor.

Rain coming.

Could return Lerrin? Bad swell behind.

Could anchor outer lee maybe.

Captain making choices.

I remained passenger.

Then old woman with goat basket grabbed my sleeve.

"Young man."

I looked.

She was pale.

Not seasick.

Breathing wrong.

Short.

"You okay?"

"No."

Good answer.

She pointed basket.

Not goat.

Under basket side, small leather pouch.

"Medicine."

"Yours?"

Nod.

I got pouch.

Small glass vial.

Label I could not read fully.

She knew.

"Three drops. Under tongue."

"You sure?"

"I've been taking it twelve years."

Enough authority.

I handed vial.

Her fingers shook.

"Can you?"

There.

I could.

Three drops.

No doctor Greg.

No diagnosis.

Her instruction.

I counted carefully.

One.

Two.

Three.

She closed mouth.

Breathed.

I called deckhand anyway.

"She says regular medicine, breathing trouble. Took three drops by her instruction."

Deckhand came.

Captain heard.

Now dock delay had person cost.

Old woman waved irritation.

"I am not dying."

Deckhand said, "Excellent. Keep doing that."

She almost laughed.

Good.

Five minutes.

Breathing eased some.

Not fixed.

Rain tongue sounded third time.

Captain made decision.

"Outer lee anchorage. We wait chain or weather."

Trader protested.

Captain didn't answer.

Packet moved away from chain.

Old Merrow twenty paces became fifty.

Then hundred.

We anchored under south harbor wall.

Rain arrived.

Hard.

Island vanished behind gray sheet.

So close.

Not arrived.

I sat beside old woman while goat chewed basket rim.

"Name?" I asked.

"Vessa Orm."

Good. Not Nessa.

"Greg."

She looked at missing leg.

Then wrapped trial build.

"Peg-Leg Greg?"

I stared.

"How the fuck?"

She smiled weakly.

"Merehook bread woman is my niece."

Of course.

Fucking nickname had boats now.

"I don't have peg."

"She said that too."

"Future-proof."

"What?"

"Long story."

Rain hammered awning.

Vessa breathed easier.

Dock invisible.

Dask north.

Sori south-ish.

Maro east.

Me on packet outside island harbor with medicine woman, basket goat, broken chain gate, and thirty-five copper.

Adventure, apparently.

No return ticket.

Still good.

# CHAPTER 442

## THE STRANGER

Rain made waiting smaller.

Old Merrow disappeared.

Harbor wall.

Chain house.

Terraces.

All gray.

Narrow Gull sat under south wall with anchor line pulling steady.

Vessa breathed easier.

Not normal maybe.

Better.

Deckhand checked twice.

She complained both times.

Good sign.

"I am breathing," she said.

"We're enthusiastic about that," he said.

Goat sneezed.

Also breathing.

Rain hit awning hard enough conversation needed effort.

Captain stayed forward.

Crew watched harbor through glass.

I watched because nothing else.

Black-white signal changed.

Black.

White.

White.

Then black again.

Deckhand swore.

"What?"

"West clutch moved. Didn't hold."

I had no idea how he read that from flags.

Didn't ask yet.

Passenger trader did.

"So chain down?"

"No."

"Then what does white mean?"

"Depends sequence."

"Helpful."

"It is if you work harbor."

Good.

Another twenty minutes.

Rain tongue sounded through rain.

Lower now.

Long note.

Captain came aft.

"We're taking shore tender."

Passengers looked up.

"Chain won't be ready before high-water turn. Harbor can ferry passengers and hand luggage over inner wall landing. Cargo stays aboard until chain drops."

Trader said, "My goods?"

"Stay aboard."

"I have delivery window."

"Your goods have hull. People don't need to sleep in mine."

Fair.

Captain pointed at me and Vessa.

"You two first tender."

Not special Greg.

Mobility and breathing.

Reasonable.

I said, "Lower transfer?"

"Yes. Tender comes starboard lee. Two crew hands. You keep crutches unless you want help."

"Keep."

"Fine."

Vessa said, "I can walk."

Captain said, "Then walk into tender when told and save argument for land."

She muttered something rude.

Alive.

Tender arrived as squat open boat with canvas hood.

Harbor crew in yellow oilskins.

Transfer looked bad.

Not dangerous bad.

Annoying bad.

Packet rose half pace.

Tender rose different half pace.

Gap changed constantly.

Deckhand showed timing.

"Wait for level. Step when I say."

I couldn't step normally.

Right foot to tender bench.

Both crutches handed separately? Better.

I looked.

"Can I sit transfer?"

Deckhand blinked.

Then nodded.

"Better."

Good.

I sat packet edge when tender came level.

One harbor crew steadied shoulder only after I said yes.

Lowered onto tender bench.

Crutches passed.

Pack passed.

No heroics.

No falling.

Vessa came after, much angrier about assistance than me.

Goat basket last.

Goat screamed at sea.

Sea ignored.

Tender crossed maybe sixty paces.

Chain visible under water once rain thinned.

Thick dark links stretched across harbor mouth maybe two feet below surface.

Would absolutely ruin hull.

Good captain.

Inner wall landing low and broad.

We disembarked via wet stone ramp.

Crutches worked if slow.

Old Merrow officially.

No bell.

No sign.

Just wet island.

Harbor clerk took packet tickets and wrote **PASSENGER LANDED / CARGO HELD**.

Trader behind us argued from second tender before feet dry.

Not my problem.

Vessa leaned on wall.

"You staying where?"

"No idea."

"Excellent planning."

"It's going well."

She pointed uphill.

"Merrow House cheap if you don't mind cistern bell. Two copper bed. One copper loft behind bakery if still open."

"Which do you use?"

"My daughter."

"Cheaper."

"Usually."

Goat sneezed again.

"You need help?"

"No."

Then she looked uphill.

Rain.

Steep terrace.

Basket.

Breathing.

"Maybe with goat."

There.

Actual ask.

I could carry goat basket badly with crutches.

Could strap to pack maybe.

No trial leg in rain.

Not worth.

Harbor porter cart stood nearby.

Two wheels.

One copper lower harbor to third terrace according board.

I pointed.

"Porter cart."

Vessa grimaced.

"One copper for hill I have climbed forty years."

"Today you breathed weird on boat."

"Rude."

"Accurate."

She paid one copper herself.

Good.

Porter loaded basket.

Goat objected.

I walked beside cart because same direction maybe.

Old Merrow terraces were water first.

That became clear immediately.

Every roof fed gutter.

Every gutter fed stone channel.

Every channel crossed lane under little grates into wall cisterns.

Rainwater moved everywhere but almost none loose.

Road edges had carved lips.

Steps had center grooves.

Door awnings drained into ceramic jars.

Children in rain capes cleared leaves from gutter mouths with sticks.

Not emergency.

Routine.

Rain tongue sounded again above.

People changed behavior.

Two shopkeepers rolled open side channels.

Cistern crew in blue belts ran uphill carrying hooks.

Vessa noticed me looking.

"East wet."

"Meaning?"

"Rain tongue says wind carrying enough water to fill high cisterns. Lower crews open spill paths before top tanks overflow."

"Every rain?"

"Not every rain. Wrong wind runs off west cliffs."

Specific.

Good.

"How old?"

"Tongue? Older than me. Re-skinned three times."

"Magic?"

"Some. Mostly tension cloth and charged ridge needles. Ask someone who likes explaining."

She did not.

Perfect.

Third terrace split.

Porter turned left.

Vessa pointed right.

"Bakery loft there."

I looked left.

"Your daughter?"

"Two streets."

"You good?"

She stared.

"I have lived here forty-eight years."

"That's not breathing answer."

Pause.

"Better."

"Good."

"You worry like old aunt."

"I had one."

Maybe.

Different life.

She touched my arm.

"Thank you for drops."

"You told me what to do."

"Most men hear old woman shake and become stupid."

"I am stupid in specialized ways."

She smiled.

"Peg-Leg Greg."

"Stop."

Porter cart left.

Vessa left.

No new party.

Good.

I found bakery loft.

One copper.

Floor mat.

Dry roof.

Shared wash room.

Paid.

Thirty-five to thirty-four.

Pack down.

Trial leg checked dry.

Road Response Kit dry.

Everything else damp edges only.

Good.

I ate bread heel and fish paste for bits.

Then rain eased.

Old Merrow came back.

Town climbed maybe five terraces above harbor before ridge farms.

Not large.

Dense.

Stone retaining walls pale gray.

Roofs green tile.

Cistern mouths marked colored ceramic disks.

Blue full?

Red restricted?

Didn't know.

Stranger.

Important.

I went outside on crutches.

No guide.

No Dask asking system questions.

No Sori reading signs faster.

No Maro noticing ecology first.

Just me.

Weird.

First thing I did was misread sign.

**UPPER CATCH / THIRD RING / PUBLIC DRAW AFTER SIXTH**

I thought ring meant terrace.

Local boy said, "Bell ring."

"Ah."

He kept walking.

Humbling efficient.

I found harbor overlook.

Chain still up.

Narrow Gull anchored outside.

Cargo trapped.

Two other small boats waited inside unable leave.

Not just packet delay.

Island exchange pinched both directions.

Harbor board had chalk notices:

**CHAIN HOUSE WEST CLUTCH / SHEAR PIN FAILED**

**REPLACEMENT PIN FROM UPPER MILL / RUNNER SENT**

**PASSENGER TENDER ACTIVE**

**CARGO HOLD UNTIL MECHANICAL CLEARANCE**

There.

Specific failure.

Not mystery.

Shear pin.

Designed failure maybe.

Better than breaking chain machinery.

One fisherman said to another, "Should've had spare in house."

Other said, "Used last spare spring storm."

"And nobody replaced?"

"Mill order delayed."

Human problem.

Maintenance debt.

Not corruption.

First fisherman spat water.

"Now my ice boat sits inside and mainland bait sits outside."

Second said, "Complain to chain committee."

"I am on chain committee."

Excellent.

I kept walking.

No employment shoulders.

Harbor market smaller than Merehook.

Island goods.

Dried kelp cakes.

Rain cloth.

Ceramic water markers.

Rope treated with something silver.

One stall sold cistern floats.

Not artifacts exactly.

Some charged.

One object caught eye.

Small clay disk with blue center.

Seller said, "Leak singer."

"What?"

"Stick against cistern wall. If water passes crack behind it, center whistles."

"How?"

"Porous salt clay around charged reed bead."

"Reusable?"

"Until salt clogs. Then soak fresh water."

"Price?"

"Three copper."

Useful.

Portable environmental warning.

My list.

Three copper under old cap four.

But problem specific to water leaks.

Do I have cistern?

No.

Could detect seep behind wall maybe.

Still.

I did not buy.

Seller did not care.

Growth.

At fifth bell, rain tongue sounded one short note.

People relaxed.

Cistern crews came downhill wet.

One woman carried broken ceramic float and swore continuously.

Different voice variety.

"Third ring's full," she told stall seller. "Fourth nearly. West catch gave us mud because somebody thinks grate cleaning is a philosophical position."

Seller said, "You want float?"

"I want Dera to clean grate. Float won't fix Dera."

Good.

She noticed me staring at leak singer.

"Don't buy that for travel."

I looked.

"Why?"

"Because seller will tell you it hears all leaks. It hears clean-wall seep in quiet room. Put it on ship hull and it'll sing because world is wet."

Seller said, "I did not say ship."

"You thought ship."

"I thought sale."

Honest.

I laughed.

"Thanks."

Woman shrugged.

"I'm Rell Aster. Upper cistern crew."

"Greg."

She looked at crutches.

Then at me.

No nickname.

Good.

"Visitor?"

"Very."

"Then don't drink from red-mark spouts. Those are wash water."

Useful.

"Blue?"

"Public draw. White private. Yellow restricted during low reserve."

"Thank you."

"Also bakery loft woman cheats blankets."

"I noticed blanket thin."

"See."

Then she left.

No quest.

Perfect.

Evening chain dropped.

I heard before saw.

Harbor bell three slow.

People near market looked toward water.

Narrow Gull entered maybe half bell later.

Cargo unloading began immediately in rain-dark.

Trader sprinted toward dock like delivery had personally insulted him.

Vessa's goat probably already home.

I went down because curious.

Chain house door open.

Workers carried broken shear pin out.

Iron cylinder maybe forearm long, snapped clean at groove.

Designed weak point confirmed.

Harbor mechanic held replacement.

New pin bright.

Fisherman chain-committee man argued with him.

"Why no spare?"

Mechanic said, "Because last spare went in spring storm and requisition sat at upper mill waiting batch forge."

"We approved emergency stock."

"You approved purchase. Treasurer delayed because mill surcharge doubled."

"Because you ordered one instead of three."

"Because budget approved one."

There.

Nobody wholly wrong.

Maintenance was money.

Redundancy cost money before failure.

After failure everyone loved redundancy.

Familiar everywhere.

I stayed outside argument.

Good.

Then saw something else.

Broken pin groove had a dark ceramic ring around center.

Witness material maybe.

Load indicator?

Harbor mechanic saw me looking.

"Shear witness."

"Shows overload?"

"Shows whether pin failed at rated shear or cracked early."

"Can tell now?"

"Tomorrow after dry. Wet darkens everything."

Useful.

"If failed rated, replacement and move on?"

"Mostly. If early, inspect alignment before trusting gate."

There.

Artifact integrated into mundane maintenance.

Not treasure.

Evidence.

My kind of thing.

I almost asked more.

Stopped.

Stranger.

Mechanic had work.

I went uphill.

At loft, I considered trial leg.

Floor dry.

Body okay.

But day had packet transfer, crutches, hills.

No need.

Rest.

I unpacked Lot 67 instead only enough to check moisture.

Dry.

No experiment.

Sticky mark still liar.

I wrote notes.

**Old Merrow chain gate: designed shear pin failed. No spare at house because prior spare consumed + replacement delayed by small-batch surcharge/budget. Cargo both directions held. Passenger tender workaround. Evidence ring on pin distinguishes rated shear vs early crack after drying. Redundancy is cheap only after failure.**

Then stopped.

No report requested.

Just mine.

Solo travel strange.

Nobody asked where tomorrow.

No group table.

No shared plan.

I had thirty-four copper.

Old Merrow had upper ridge farms, west rain catch, small packet routes, and apparently an inland quarry road according wall board.

Also next Narrow Gull departure maybe two days.

No obligation.

No companion to default toward.

I lay under cheating blanket.

Thin indeed.

Rain tongue silent.

Cistern water moved inside walls.

Somewhere below, chain gate worked again.

For tonight.

Stranger meant nobody knew what I was useful for.

That was freeing.

Also inconvenient.

Good.

# CHAPTER 443

## THE WAYFARER

The broken pin had failed correctly.

Good for everyone except broken pin.

Harbor chain house posted result before breakfast.

**WEST CLUTCH SHEAR PIN / WITNESS DRY**

**RATED FAILURE CONFIRMED**

**NO EARLY-CRACK INDICATION**

**ALIGNMENT INSPECTION ROUTINE / NOT EMERGENCY**

That meant yesterday had been maintenance debt and bad timing.

Not hidden structural doom.

Comforting.

Also boring.

Good.

I ate bread under harbor awning while two fishermen argued whether committee should now order four pins or six.

Neither asked me.

Excellent.

Old Merrow looked different dry.

Rain had polished everything.

Stone terraces pale.

Roof channels still dripping.

Cistern markers bright.

Green-black ridge above town steamed where sun hit wet grass.

Rain tongue stretched flat between its white arms.

Silent.

I had no reason to stay harbor.

That was new problem.

No Sori paperwork.

No Dask instrument.

No Maro bird.

Just me.

Route board stood beside bakery lane.

Yesterday I had walked past because wet stairs and stranger brain.

Today I read slower.

**RIDGE CART / FIRST & FOURTH RING / 1C**

**WEST CATCH / UPPER FARMS / QUARRY SPLIT**

**WEST BAY FOOT ROAD / DRY WEATHER ONLY**

**PACKET WEST BAY -> TERNHAVEN / TOMORROW THIRD RING / 3C IF RUNNING**

There.

Road.

Not home road.

Other side of island.

Ternhaven meant nothing to me.

Perfect.

I asked bakery woman, "West Bay how far?"

"Ridge cart to split. Then two bells down if you're walking normal."

She looked at crutches.

"Longer for you."

"Thank you for advanced mathematics."

"You're welcome."

"Inn?"

"One cheap bunk house. One not cheap."

"Packet tomorrow actually reliable?"

She laughed.

Question answered.

"What's Ternhaven?"

"Town."

"Useful."

"You asked what, not why."

Old Merrow people apparently enjoyed accuracy.

I paid bakery loft nothing further because night done.

Packed.

Trial leg still wrapped.

Lot 67 dry.

No leak singer.

No broken chain souvenir.

Good.

Fourth ring cart gave me morning to climb ridge slowly instead of taking it.

Bad idea maybe.

Then I remembered cart cost one copper.

Very good idea.

I bought seat.

Thirty-four to thirty-three.

Cart wasn't passenger coach.

Flat freight wagon with two side benches and rain tarp rolled overhead.

Cargo:

empty produce crates.

three sacks lime.

coiled irrigation hose.

five people.

Me.

No assigned seats.

Driver pointed rear left.

"Less bounce."

"Why?"

"Axle hates right side."

Important information.

I chose left.

Other riders:

woman with two baskets green beans.

young man smelling quarry dust.

old farmer with one eye and no interest in anyone.

mother carrying sleeping child.

boy maybe sixteen with rolled copper gutter strip.

Nobody cared who I was.

Excellent.

Fourth ring.

Cart climbed.

Old Merrow terraces narrowed below.

Water system became clearer from above.

Not one cistern network.

Hundreds.

Roof catch.

Lane catch.

Farm catch.

Overflow channels feeding lower rings.

Every terrace borrowed water from weather before giving remainder downhill.

Useful social structure hidden inside plumbing.

I did not write essay.

Growth.

At second switchback cart stopped because boy with copper strip jumped off.

Driver shouted, "Wrong ring!"

Boy froze.

Looked uphill.

Looked downhill.

"Fuck."

Driver laughed.

"Third, not second. Get back."

Boy climbed.

I felt better.

Stranger mistakes universal.

Upper ridge farms appeared after half bell.

Stone walls.

Low trees bent west.

Terraced beds under stretched mesh.

Not grain fields.

Herbs, beans, dark-leaf greens, something red growing close ground.

Between plots stood tall ceramic poles capped with shallow bowls.

Rain gauges maybe.

One farmer got off.

Cart continued.

At next stop woman with beans left.

Then mother.

Me, quarry man, old farmer.

Quarry split sat near ridge saddle.

Sign:

**WEST CATCH -> LEFT**

**MERROW QUARRY -> RIGHT**

**WEST BAY -> LEFT THEN DOWN**

Driver said, "West Bay?"

"Maybe."

"Then off here. Cart turns quarry."

There.

Decision.

Could go quarry.

Could go west catch.

Could go back.

No companion preference to lean against.

I got off.

Left.

West Bay.

Simple.

Driver said, "Rain channel path slippery after yesterday. Use upper farm road until marker nine, then cut down."

"Thank you."

"Don't use blue stones."

"Why?"

"Those are water crew paths."

Useful.

Cart left.

Now actually alone.

Road dry enough.

Grass wet edges.

Black stone center.

Crutches good.

Wind smelled green instead of salt.

I walked.

Slow.

No hurry.

Marker one.

Two.

Three.

Farm dogs barked behind walls.

Four.

At five, road widened beside empty stone shed.

Flat packed ground.

Dry.

No people.

Body felt good.

This was trial-leg opportunity.

Not test because chapter needed one.

Because road ahead mostly level to nine and both hands would help with pack straps.

I sat shed step.

Shell on.

Medium foot.

Crutches primary.

Started watch by sun position plus rough count.

Not Lot 67.

No false precision.

Walked.

Stable road.

Short steps.

Crutches light, not absent.

After maybe ten minutes, I stopped at wall gap and retightened pack chest strap with both hands standing.

There.

Ordinary.

Then moved.

At marker seven road had shallow cross-drain.

I did not step over unsupported.

Crutches down.

Good.

Marker eight.

Hip fatigue mild.

No socket heat.

Marker nine came around twenty-two minutes maybe.

I stopped.

Not because failure.

Because plan.

Shell off behind low wall.

Upper pink mild.

Lower front clear.

No pain.

At twenty-minute skin check pink gone.

Note:

**Old Merrow ridge farm road. ~22 min. Packed dry stone. Crutches light/primary. Two-handed pack adjustment standing. Cross-drain with crutches. Mild hip fatigue. Upper pink cleared. No lower-front mark.**

Good.

Baseline moving.

Not miracle.

I ate hard bread.

Then did something I had not done in too long.

Magic.

Not because artifact.

Not because crisis.

Because quiet road and dry wall gave clean conditions.

I picked a dead bean leaf from roadside.

Light.

Dry.

Placed it on flat stone one pace away.

Wind minimal behind wall.

I waited.

Old instinct wanted complicated.

No.

External effect.

Small.

Known target.

No timing claims.

No Lot 67.

I set crutches stable.

Breathed.

Reached with sense I had been rebuilding badly and slowly for months.

Not grabbing.

Pressure.

Direction.

Leaf edge lifted.

Could be air.

Stopped.

Waited.

Second attempt would contaminate first if I started wanting result.

I moved leaf to different stone.

Checked wind with finger.

Almost none.

Tried again.

Leaf slid maybe half finger.

Stopped.

There.

Small enough to insult first-life me.

Useful enough current me smiled.

External attempt one hundred thirty-six.

Not breakthrough.

Not rank.

Not proof of range increase.

One light dry leaf.

Sheltered wall.

Maybe half finger.

I wrote exactly that.

**External 136. Dead dry bean leaf, sheltered wall, ~1 pace. Minimal wind by hand check. Visible slide ~half finger after directed pressure. No timing measurement. Do not generalize.**

Then did not repeat.

Important.

Training brain wanted ten.

Road brain wanted west.

Road won.

Good.

I packed leaf nowhere.

Not artifact.

Just leaf.

Path down west side changed fast.

East side green terraces.

West side darker.

Rock exposed.

Water channels cut deep beside road.

At first overlook I saw West Bay.

Tiny harbor.

Maybe thirty roofs.

One pier.

No tall beacon.

Beyond, sea open and gray.

Far horizon had low island shape.

Ternhaven maybe.

Or something else.

Closer, west catch system occupied whole slope.

Not cisterns.

Huge stepped stone basins.

Rainwater collected in broad terraces, settled mud, then spilled cleaner water down through reed beds.

Industrial watershed.

Beautiful in practical way.

And damaged.

One upper basin brown.

Workers clustered around breach in side wall.

Water running where shouldn't.

Yesterday rain consequence.

I stayed road.

Nobody hired me.

Good.

Then woman on road ahead waved.

At me? Maybe.

I looked behind.

Nobody.

Me.

She carried red staff and wore yellow water-crew sash.

"West Bay?"

"Yes."

"Main path closed below basin six. Mud slide."

"Alternative?"

"Farm contour path. Longer."

"How much?"

She looked at crutches.

"For you? Maybe bell and half."

Fuck.

"Any cart?"

"Crew cart after sixth ring if space. Two bits to bay."

There.

Choice.

Walk longer.

Pay bits.

Wait.

"When sixth?"

"About half bell."

Easy.

"I'll wait."

She nodded.

No bravery points for exhausting myself downhill after trial wear.

Good.

I sat on dry wall outside crew lane.

Workers moved mud baskets.

One basin spill gate opened.

Brown water diverted into reed trench.

Nobody explained.

I watched enough to infer some.

Not enough to pretend.

Crew cart came late.

Of course.

Bench mostly tools.

Driver shifted two shovels.

"Two bits."

I paid.

Copper unchanged.

Ride down rough.

Very glad not walking.

West Bay reached near evening.

Smaller than sign implied.

Or sign implied nothing.

One packet office.

One fish shed.

One long bunk house.

Rain barrels everywhere.

Route board:

**TERNHAVEN / THIRD RING / 3C / WEATHER CHECK SECOND**

**SOUTH RETURN OLD MERROW / CART FIRST RING / 1C**

**NORTH SKIFF / KELP ISLES / CARGO ONLY**

Kelp Isles.

Interesting.

Cargo only.

Not today.

Bunk house cost one copper.

I paid.

Thirty-three to thirty-two.

Dinner bits.

Room had six bunks.

Three occupied by water workers.

One old fisherman.

One me.

One empty.

No familiar voices.

Good.

I looked.

"Eventually."

"Crutches look harbor-made."

"Carrow first. Duskport recently."

"Long way."

"Getting longer."

He nodded like that was answer.

Didn't ask why.

I liked him.

Later rain started light on roof.

Not enough for tongue maybe.

West Bay had smaller warning instrument outside bunk house.

Three hanging reed plates.

Wind made one click.

Then two.

Different town.

Same weather.

I counted money.

Thirty-two copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

Road eating capital.

Leg still expensive.

But today I had crossed island by cart, leg, crutches, water crew wagon, and stupidity avoidance.

Also moved a leaf half a finger.

Maybe.

Enough.

Tomorrow Ternhaven if weather allowed.

Three copper.

Another island or mainland? Didn't know.

Then didn't.

Wayfarer.

Not because I knew way.

Because I kept taking one.

# CHAPTER 444

## THE CASTAWAY

Weather permitted.

Mostly.

West Bay board changed at second ring.

**TERNHAVEN / RUNNING / THIRD RING / 3C**

Under it, smaller chalk:

**WEST SWELL MODERATE / LANDING SUBJECT TO EAST COVE**

I asked clerk what that meant.

"Means packet runs. Means Ternhaven main pier might not take us. East Cove usually can."

"How far from town?"

"Depends which town you mean."

Of course.

"Ternhaven."

"Island's Ternhaven. Main harbor village also Ternhaven. East Cove is three miles by shore track."

"Three miles."

"If dry."

I looked outside.

Cloudy.

Not raining.

"Good."

"You say that now."

I bought ticket.

Three copper.

Thirty-two to twenty-nine.

That number bothered me.

Not because poor.

Because direction.

Now twenty-nine.

Still had deposit back Duskport.

Good.

Annoying.

Good.

**Moss Finch**.

One mast.

Broad belly.

Cargo mostly baskets, lamp oil, rolled netting, and six long boxes marked with green fern symbol.

No.

Slow.

Fine.

Excellent.

"Unlimited."

Boat culture consistent.

First two bells easy.

No glassbacks.

Different sea.

Dark green.

Long ribbons tangled together, some broad as table.

Kelp.

"Fish?"

"Bug."

"That's worse."

"They taste good."

"Better."

She looked maybe fifteen.

Not interested in explaining world to old stranger.

Good.

Kelp mats thickened.

Crew watched them.

Not because pretty.

Captain adjusted course twice.

"Prop?" I asked deckhand.

He looked at sail.

Right.

"Rudder. Kelp wraps pintle. Also hides driftwood."

No prop.

Different boat.

I had first-life knowledge.

Also first-life habits from different technology.

Useful correction.

Third bell, wind shifted.

Not stronger.

Wrong angle.

Moss Finch leaned.

Crew shortened sail.

Captain looked west.

Ternhaven appeared low on horizon.

Not cliffs.

Flat.

Long green island barely above sea.

White strips maybe dunes.

No towers.

No ridge instruments.

Then red flag became visible at main harbor mast.

Captain swore once.

"East Cove."

Carpenter complained immediately.

Captain pointed at him.

"You used allowance before choice."

"I saved some."

We turned south of island.

East Cove was not harbor.

That became clear.

Beach between two low stone arms.

One wooden landing stage ending before surf line.

Three flat-bottom shore boats waiting.

No breakwater.

No chain.

No proper pier.

Packet anchored outside surf.

Passengers transferred.

Again.

Travel had opinions about my body.

This transfer worse than Old Merrow tender.

Shore boat rose and fell against packet.

Deckhand said, "Sit transfer?"

"Yes."

Correct answer getting easier.

Pack passed.

Crutches passed.

Me next.

Two crew held forearms after asking.

I sat edge.

Timed drop with boat rise.

Not elegant.

Safe.

Then shore.

Shore boat ran up sand.

Everyone climbed out into ankle water.

Problem.

My one foot could get wet.

Crutch tips could.

Pack shouldn't.

Trial limb definitely shouldn't.

Crewman saw.

"Wait."

He and another dragged narrow plank from boat.

Not for me specifically.

For flour sacks too.

Plank to dry sand.

Good infrastructure because cargo hates water as much as disabled man.

I crossed after sacks.

Ternhaven east cove.

Twenty-nine copper.

No idea where sleep.

Good.

Then packet captain shouted from surf boat.

"Road track washed at Salt Mouth!"

Shore worker shouted back.

"When?"

"Morning tide!"

Worker swore.

Carpenter swore.

I waited.

Apparently three-mile shore track now not simple.

East Cove had shed.

One fish smokehouse.

Six huts.

No inn.

No passenger cart waiting.

Shore worker explained.

Salt Mouth was tidal channel halfway to harbor village.

Normally crossed by timber footbridge.

Morning surge lifted two spans.

People could detour inland through reed flats.

"How far?" carpenter asked.

"Five miles maybe."

Everyone looked at me.

Not because protagonist.

Because crutches.

I appreciated honesty less than thought.

"No road through reeds."

"Boat?"

"Maybe evening. Maybe tomorrow."

There.

Mostly.
