Her cages clicked.

True.

Couple resumed argument about mother.

Fisherman sat on overturned basket and slept again.

I liked him.

Shore worker said east cove shelter could hold passengers overnight if needed.

No charge.

Not free lodging exactly.

Emergency transit shelter.

Wood floor.

Roof.

No beds.

Could stay.

Or walk five-mile reed detour.

My body had already done packet transfer.

Trial leg rested yesterday? Used ridge day before. One rest night.

Five miles still stupid.

Even with limb.

Especially unfamiliar wet reeds.

I chose shelter.

Immediately.

Carpenter looked.

"You're not trying detour?"

"No."

"Fair."

No inspirational grit.

Wonderful.

Flour woman did try.

She hired local boy to carry one sack and left inland with second herself.

Carpenter went too.

Couple argued then went.

Crab girl stayed.

Fisherman stayed asleep.

I had time.

Too much.

East Cove transit shed had wall map.

Ternhaven different from expected.

Island shaped like bent leaf.

Main harbor northeast.

East Cove southeast.

Salt Mouth channel between.

Interior marked **REED COMMON**, **LOW FERN FIELDS**, **SOUTH LIGHT**, **OLD SALT ROAD**, **NORTH FERRY**.

North Ferry arrow pointed off island toward **Kelp Isles**.

There.

Again.

Not cargo-only here maybe.

I asked shore worker.

"North Ferry takes passengers?"

"When running."

"To Kelp Isles?"

"Outer three. Main kelp market at Dorrin."

"Fare?"

"Depends tide. Four copper usually Dorrin."

Four.

Future.

Not today.

"How get north ferry?"

"Harbor village then north road."

So first cross island problem.

Good.

I sat shed doorway.

Crab girl sat opposite.

Her name eventually **Lio Pell** because fisherman woke and called her Lio.

Pell.

I looked.

"You related to Pell Varo?"

"Who?"

Good.

Names could repeat without cosmic family tree.

"Nobody."

She narrowed eyes.

"That's weird answer."

"Yes."

She fed crabs bits of dried fish.

White shells.

Blue joints.

One had tiny green glow under belly.

"Charged?" I asked.

"Eggs."

"Glowing eggs."

"Warm eggs. Light means warm enough."

"Magic crab incubator."

"Crab."

Right.

Ordinary here.

I unpacked Road Response Kit.

Not because transit emergency.

Because dry shed, flat table, nothing urgent.

Time to characterize liar.

I had already seen 2/10 false fourth-mark hangs under immediate manual trigger.

Question: track friction or trigger tongue?

Could isolate mechanically without magic/body.

I set frame on table.

Manual reference trigger only.

Ten runs with track level.

Results:

one below mark one.

two below one.

three below one.

four hung near four.

There.

five below one.

six below one.

seven below one.

eight hung near four.

nine below one.

ten below one.

Same two-in-ten-ish pattern.

Then I changed only orientation.

Turned frame around 180 degrees on same table.

Ten more.

One below one.

Two below one.

Three hung near four.

Four below.

Five below.

Six below.

Seven below.

Eight below.

Nine hung.

Ten below.

Still.

Not table slope.

I watched bead closely.

On bad run, bead reached between three and four then paused.

Tiny.

Then snapped forward.

Track.

Probably.

Could be bead.

I removed bead? No.

Not without knowing disassembly.

Don't turn five-copper tool into parts.

I wrote:

**Lot 67. Manual immediate reference only. Dry level shed. 20 runs, 4 false long hangs near mark 4. Orientation reversal did not remove. Visible bead pause between 3-4 on bad runs. Suspect track/bead friction, not trigger. Do not disassemble yet.**

Better.

Still liar.

More specific liar.

Lio watched.

"What's it do?"

"Currently? Misleads me in repeatable way."

"Why own it?"

"I paid five copper before knowing that."

She nodded.

"My uncle bought boat like that."

Universal.

Afternoon swell stayed ugly.

Shore boats did not run.

Rain didn't come.

Good.

I considered trial limb in shed.

Dry floor.

Could wear.

But why?

No task.

Don't turn every stable floor into training lab.

Left it wrapped.

Instead repaired crutch grip wrap where salt had stiffened edge.

Artifact maintenance.

Body-tool maintenance.

Ordinary.

Near fifth ring, person came from inland track.

Running.

Young man.

Mud to knees.

Breathing hard.

Shore worker met him.

Conversation changed shed.

Salt Mouth bridge damage worse.

One remaining abutment had shifted with falling tide.

Main harbor sent warning: nobody use crossing even if water exposes stones.

Fine.

Then second thing.

A child from reed hamlet was missing.

Not ours.

Immediately ours socially because room heard.

Boy maybe ten.

Last seen near south reed path before noon.

Family thought he followed flour carrier group toward East Cove.

East Cove hadn't seen him.

Reed workers searching inland.

Shore worker looked at people in shed.

"Anyone saw a boy? Red cap, green vest?"

No.

Lio shook head.

Fisherman awake now.

No.

I had arrived third-ish bell.

Could not know before.

Runner said, "He knows flats. He doesn't wander."

That sentence carried fear.

Not helpless child lost in woods.

Kid who knows place and still missing.

Shore worker grabbed rain cloak.

"I'll check cove south."

Lio stood.

"I can check crab path."

Worker said, "You stay."

"I know it."

"And tide turning. Stay."

Conflict.

Lio angry.

"Tenn knows it too."

Tenn.

Not Lerrin boy Tenn? Could be common name. Too close.

No.

Bad.

Let's avoid.

Runner said boy's name **Orric**.

Better.

"Orric knows it too," Lio said.

Worker: "That's why we're searching. You staying gives me one less person to search for."

Lio sat hard.

Correct procedure.

Still hurt.

I asked, "What can someone here do that helps without becoming another missing person?"

Worker pointed map.

"If you can write, mark sightings. People will come through. Time, place, direction. Don't let stories become one story unless same sighting."

Oh.

That I could do.

Not because Greg special.

Because notebook.

I took table.

New page.

**ORRIC / RED CAP / GREEN VEST**

Columns.

Time reported.

Time seen.

Place.

Direction.

Source.

Confidence.

First runner: last confirmed by aunt near reed hamlet, before noon, carrying lunch cloth, said going south path to see crab traps.

Second report ten minutes later from flour woman returning without flour sack? No. She wouldn't return yet.

Local eel worker came cove.

Saw red cap near low fern wall around second ring.

Boy alone? Maybe.

Could be Orric.

Direction east.

Third: shore boatman saw child on south rocks yesterday.

Wrong day.

Logged separate, not current.

Fourth: fisherman remembered green vest at dawn near smokehouse.

Probably another kid.

Uncertain.

Stories multiplied.

I kept them separate.

This was familiar skill.

Harder because stakes person.

By sixth ring, three search groups active.

One reed hamlet.

One Salt Mouth north bank.

One south cove track.

No Orric.

Lio paced.

"He could've come here and left before packet."

"Did you see him?"

"No."

"Then that's possibility, not sighting."

"I know."

She sounded pissed.

Not at me exactly.

"Sorry."

"Don't be. Keep it separate."

She hated that too.

Good.

Sun lowering.

Tide exposing dark flats.

Worker returned south.

Nothing.

Then old fisherman spoke.

"Red cap boy."

Everyone looked.

"What?"

"Saw one from packet before landing."

My stomach tightened.

"Where?"

He pointed map.

Not east cove.

Offshore.

Small reed islet south of Salt Mouth.

"Standing?"

"Sitting. Thought trapper."

"When?"

"Before we turned cove."

"How long before landing?"

He thought.

"Quarter bell. Maybe less."

Could be Orric.

Could be another red cap.

I marked.

**Packet fisherman / ~quarter bell pre-landing / reed islet S of Salt Mouth / child-sized red cap / seated / identity uncertain.**

Runner leaned over.

"There isn't trapper access at high tide."

That changed probability.

Not certainty.

Shore worker looked sea.

Swell still bad outside.

Inside cove calmer.

Reed islet reachable by shallow pole skiff from inner channel if tide low enough.

Tide falling.

Local worker said, "We can get there from south cut in twenty minutes."

Runner: "Go."

Worker: "You come. Lio stays."

Lio swore.

I stayed too.

No need Greg join rescue with crutches in tidal mud.

They left.

Twenty minutes.

Thirty.

Forty.

No one talked much.

At maybe forty-five, we heard whistle.

Two short.

Lio ran doorway.

Shore worker appeared down track.

Runner behind.

Between them, muddy boy in red cap.

Walking.

Alive.

Lio started crying and immediately got angry about it.

"You idiot!"

Orric yelled back, "Bridge broke!"

"I know!"

"I couldn't cross!"

"Then why island?"

"Water came!"

Explanation later.

Family later.

He had gone crab traps before bridge failure fully closed path, got cut off by tide, climbed reed islet because he knew higher patch, then waited.

Correct survival.

Bad communication impossible because ten-year-old without signal tool.

Nobody villain.

Fisherman's uncertain sighting mattered.

Not because perfect memory.

Because kept separate long enough to become testable.

Runner thanked fisherman.

Then me.

"You kept page?"

"Yes."

"Give it."

I tore copy? Only one notebook page.

I copied key sightings onto spare paper.

Handed.

No payment.

Obviously.

No job.

No new companion.

Just useful.

Orric looked at crutches.

"You Greg?"

Oh fuck off.

"Which Greg?"

"Peg-Leg."

Lio stared at him.

I stared at him.

"How?"

"Aunt Vessa said Merehook niece met you."

Island gossip faster than packet.

I put face in hand.

One hand because other on crutch.

Orric grinned.

Kid nearly stranded by tide and still had energy for this.

Good sign.

"I still don't have peg."

He looked at wrapped trial build.

"That's leg."

"Shop's leg."

"Why you carrying someone else's leg?"

Excellent question.

"Long story."

"Okay."

He accepted.

Evening shore boats still not running.

Transit shed overnight.

No charge.

I spent bits on smoked fish from cove worker.

Copper remained twenty-nine.

Road had not moved far.

But route changed anyway.

Tomorrow, maybe shore boat to harbor village.

Maybe Salt Mouth bridge temporary crossing.

Then north ferry.

Kelp Isles.

Dorrin.

Names getting farther from home.

I lay on wood floor with pack under head.

Castaway was too dramatic for someone with roof, food, money, and scheduled transport problem.

Still.

I had bought ticket to Ternhaven.

Ternhaven had deposited me on wrong side of itself.

Close enough.

For tonight.

# CHAPTER 445

## THE DRIFTER

Morning solved the water before it solved the bridge.

Salt Mouth stayed broken.

Swell dropped.

East Cove worker changed board after first ring.

**SHORE BOAT / HARBOR VILLAGE / 1C / SECOND RING**

Under it:

**FOOTBRIDGE CLOSED**

Good.

I paid one copper.

Twenty-nine to twenty-eight.

Road by boat.

Ternhaven apparently preferred not choosing categories.

Lio Pell left before me.

Her crab cages went inland on local reed cart because crab route used south mud track I was not stupid enough to attempt.

Orric came by with his aunt.

Not Vessa.

Different aunt.

He had red cap cleaned badly and green vest cleaned worse.

"You leaving?"

"Trying."

"North ferry goes Kelp Isles."

"I heard."

"Dorrin has huge kelp bell."

"What's kelp bell?"

"Huge bell. For kelp."

Excellent information.

His aunt said, "Orric."

"What?"

"Stop advertising islands to strangers."

"He's already stranger."

Technically.

I waved.

No emotional rescue reunion.

Good.

Shore boat was flatter than yesterday's.

Six passengers.

Two sacks mail.

Three baskets smoked fish.

One wooden crate full of ceramic floats.

Crutches aboard first.

Pack second.

Me seated transfer third.

Getting less embarrassing mostly because I stopped assigning embarrassment to correct technique.

That felt useful.

Boat hugged coast.

Salt Mouth looked worse from water.

Two missing bridge spans.

One remaining support tilted downstream.

Workers had ropes across both banks and a red board nailed to each approach.

Nobody crossing.

Good.

A narrow pole skiff moved through inner channel carrying lumber.

Bridge repair starting.

Not my problem.

Also good.

Ternhaven main harbor appeared after maybe half bell.

Not much bigger than East Cove.

Busier by factor ten.

Low stone quay.

Flat-roof sheds.

Long racks running parallel shore.

Green everywhere.

Kelp.

Not pretty decorative kelp.

Industrial kelp.

Wet bundles hung from racks.

Dry sheets stacked under weights.

Workers carried black ropes dripping green water.

Boilers steamed behind sheds.

A sharp mineral smell sat over everything.

Fish plus salt plus hot leaves.

Ternhaven smelled like ocean being cooked into paperwork.

I liked it.

Shore boat docked at low steps.

Bad for me.

Worker pointed side ramp used for handcarts.

Better.

I used that.

Town board stood directly off quay.

**NORTH FERRY / DORRIN / TOMORROW THIRD RING / 4C / TIDE CHECK SECOND**

**NORTH ROAD CART / FERRY HAMLET / TODAY FIFTH RING / 1C**

**SOUTH LIGHT / WALK ONLY**

**OLD SALT ROAD / CLOSED HEAVY CARTS**

There it was.

Dorrin tomorrow.

Four copper.

And one more copper just to get north ferry hamlet unless I wanted walk.

Twenty-eight.

Five more to keep going.

Twenty-three.

Same as Vale.

That symmetry annoyed me.

I did not buy anything yet.

First find town.

Main lane had no stairs.

Thank every god interested in civil engineering.

Boardwalk over damp ground.

Packed shell roads.

Low buildings because wind.

Channels everywhere because tides.

At intersections, short poles held little white ceramic paddles.

Paddles turned not with wind but with current in shallow channels below.

One side painted green.

Other black.

I asked kelp worker.

"Tide paddles?"

"Drain paddles. Green means channels pulling out. Black means sea pushing in."

"Town floods?"

"Town negotiates."

Good.

Ternhaven identity.

Not stop water.

Negotiate.

Kelp sheds used that same rhythm.

Workers moved fresh kelp onto lower racks while drainage green.

When paddles turned black, they lifted lower bundles higher.

Not magic exactly.

Then I saw magic.

Long drying rack had thin blue stones set every armspan.

Each glowed faintly where kelp touched.

"What do those do?"

Worker looked like question obvious.

"Pull charge out."

"Why?"

"Because charged kelp cooks uneven."

"Kelp is charged?"

"Some."

"From what?"

He shrugged.

"Sea. Storm. Glassbacks. Deep lines. Depends kelp."

Useful uncertainty.

"Blue stones tell how much?"

"No. They bleed it. Red ones tell. Blue cheaper."

There.

Artifact infrastructure as ordinary processing.

I did not ask to buy stone.

Progress.

Maybe.

Breakfast market sold kelp cakes.

I bought one with bits.

Bad.

Not inedible.

Just aggressively ocean.

Woman selling them said, "You need hot sauce."

"Why sell without?"

"Tourists learn."

I added sauce.

Better.

A man at next stall had a tiny brass device clamped to table.

Black bead.

Track.

Not same as Lot 67.

Similar enough my brain turned.

Repair stall.

Sign:

**CLOCKS / TIDE COUNTERS / DRYING TIMERS / SMALL MECHANICALS**

I stared.

Then remembered five-copper liar.

Leg money.

I walked over anyway.

Not every approach purchase.

Old woman behind bench wore thick magnifying lens over one eye.

"What broke?"

"Maybe nothing. Maybe friction."

"Those are different prices."

Good opening.

I unpacked Road Response Kit.

Careful.

Set it down.

"Retired road response demonstrator. Bought surplus. False hangs near mark four around two runs in ten. Manual immediate trigger still does it. Orientation reversal still does it. I can see bead pause between three and four. I haven't opened track."

She looked at me.

Then tool.

"Good."

"Good it's broken?"

"Good you didn't fill it with lamp oil."

Pera vindicated.

She put lens down.

Did not touch yet.

"Inspection one bit. Opening two bits. Repair quoted after."

Reasonable.

I paid one bit.

She examined externally.

Ran bead with a little ivory pick through side slot.

Slow.

Stopped near fourth mark.

Again.

"Track burr."

"Certain?"

"No. Likely."

Excellent woman.

"Could also be bead flat. Need open to tell."

"Two bits?"

"To open and close without losing spring."

I paid.

Bits, not copper.

She opened back plate with proper driver.

Inside looked simpler than expected.

Tiny brass channel.

Black stone bead captured between guide lips.

Small return spring.

At fourth mark, one guide lip showed bright scratch.

She rotated bead with pick.

One side also had faint flat.

"Both."

"Of course."

"Burr catches flat when they meet. That's why intermittent."

"Fix?"

"Dress burr and turn bead. Might hold. Better bead replacement if you want reliable."

"Price?"

"Dress and reorient, one copper. Replacement bead three if I have right size. I don't."

One copper.

I had twenty-eight.

This was owned tool already.

Repair could make five-copper purchase useful.

But one-copper fix remained provisional.

"If you dress it, can we test before I trust it?"

"Obviously."

I liked her.

"Name?"

"Mera Tonn."

"Greg."

"Peg-Leg?"

Fuck this island chain.

I closed eyes.

"Who told you?"

"Vessa came through last night and bought lamp wick from my sister."

"Of course."

"You don't have peg."

"Thank you."

"Bad branding."

"Future-proof."

"Worse branding."

I paid one copper.

Twenty-eight to twenty-seven.

Mera used tiny stone file on guide lip.

Barely touched it.

Then rotated bead so flat no longer aligned same way under return.

No replacement.

No magic.

Maintenance.

She reassembled.

"Now test."

I did manual immediate reference twenty times.

All below mark one.

Not proof fixed forever.

Better.

Then another twenty.

One run rose just over one.

No false fourth-mark hang.

Mera said, "Forty isn't forever."

"I know."

"Write repair. If problem returns, bead."

I wrote:

**Ternhaven, Mera Tonn. Opened with proper tool. Burr at guide lip near mark 4 + faint bead flat. Burr dressed, bead reoriented. Post-service manual immediate: 40 runs, 0 false fourth-mark hangs; one slightly >1 mark. Provisional improvement only. Continue characterization before body/magic use.**

There.

Artifact improved through expertise and money.

Not miracle.

Still not authority.

Worth one copper.

Probably.

Mera pointed trial-leg bundle.

"That broken too?"

"No. Shop property."

"Whose shop?"

"Hark and Venn, Duskport."

She whistled.

"Far."

"Getting farther."

"Then don't fall in sea. Shipping property back annoying."

Practical sympathy.

I packed Lot 67.

Town noon.

Twenty-seven copper.

North road cart fifth ring.

Could stay main harbor for one copper maybe.

Could go north ferry hamlet for one.

Dorrin tomorrow four.

I walked harbor edge to decide.

Not long.

Maybe twenty minutes crutches.

Found kelp grading yard.

This was Ternhaven's real money.

Bundles arrived wet and got divided not just by species but by charge behavior.

Some stayed dull against red tester strip.

Some made strip glow orange.

One flashed white and worker immediately isolated whole basket.

"Bad?"

I asked from outside rail.

Grader said, "Expensive."

Of course.

"Why?"

"High charge kelp goes battery vats. Food kelp can't carry that much."

"Battery vats?"

"Dorrin."

There.

Dorrin stopped being name.

Kelp Isles processed charge-rich kelp into something valuable enough for dedicated vats.

Maybe storage medium.

Maybe industrial charge.

I didn't know.

Wanted to.

Four copper.

Plus cart.

Plus bunk.

Road eating leg.

I sat wall.

Counted.

Twenty-seven.

If north cart one, twenty-six.

North ferry bunk maybe one.

Twenty-five.

Dorrin four tomorrow.

Twenty-one.

Then food bits.

Then whatever after.

Twenty-one copper cash.

Still not destitute.

Still less than half North Reach.

The travel-work limb remained one silver plus eighteen copper with twenty deposit already paid.

No conversion.

Twenty-one copper could matter enormously depending silver reality.

I could turn around.

Not home tomorrow.

But southward.

Merehook eventually.

Duskport.

Carrow.

Nothing wrong with that.

I didn't want to.

That answer getting expensive.

At fifth ring I paid north-road cart.

One copper.

Twenty-seven to twenty-six.

Cart carried dried kelp bales, two workers, me, and a box of ceramic paddles.

No Lio.

No Orric.

No named future friend required.

Road crossed low fern fields.

Actual low fern.

Knee height for standing people.

Chest height for me seated cart.

Silver underside flashed in wind.

Some fields had narrow black posts with hanging glass threads.

Workers explained threads collected dew charge used to start drying stones after long rain.

"Free power?" I asked.

One worker laughed.

"Free if you don't count posts, thread, cleaning, storms, thieves, and waiting."

Economics universal.

North side drier.

Wind stronger.

Ternhaven narrowed until I could sometimes see sea both directions.

That was new.

Island felt temporary under feet.

North Ferry hamlet was twelve roofs and one long pier angled into kelp water.

Board:

**DORRIN / THIRD RING / 4C / TIDE CHECK SECOND**

**OUTER ISLES FREIGHT / ASK MASTER**

**MAINLAND RETURN / FIFTH DAY**

Bunk room one copper.

I paid.

Twenty-six to twenty-five.

There.

Twenty-five.

Tomorrow maybe twenty-one.

I ate cheap stew with bits.

No trial-leg wear today.

Transfers, town crutch mileage, cart were enough.

No magic either.

Tool repair already gave brain enough numbers.

At dusk, ferry workers prepared kelp boats.

These were strange.

Wide shallow hulls with tall lattice frames instead of normal cargo stacks.

Kelp bundles hung vertically so water drained underway.

Along rails sat ceramic jars with blue-lit seams.

Not fuel.

Maybe charge sinks.

I asked ferryman.

"What's jars?"

"Bleed pots."

"For kelp charge?"

"Yes. Keep hot loads from talking to compass stones."

"Talking?"

"Making them stupid."

Better.

Dorrin suddenly had artifact-economy pull.

Not treasure.

Industry.

I looked north.

Dark shapes on horizon.

Kelp Isles.

Maybe three.

Maybe more.

Hard to tell.

No companions.

No assignment.

Twenty-five copper.

Trial leg in pack.

One timing tool slightly less of liar.

Tomorrow ferry cost four.

I had not bought ticket.

Could still decide no.

That mattered.

Drifter sounded passive.

Like current did choosing.

Wasn't true.

I kept paying for direction.

Maybe that was worse.

Maybe better.

Ask tomorrow.

# CHAPTER 446

## THE INVESTOR

Second ring said tide good.

Of course it did.

Would have been easier if weather made decision for me.

North Ferry board changed from chalk question to black tile.

**DORRIN / THIRD RING / 4C / RUNNING**

Twenty-five copper in pocket.

Twenty-three Vale.

Twenty copper already sitting in another city pretending to be future leg.

Four copper to keep going.

I stood under board long enough ferry clerk looked at me twice.

"You buying?"

"Thinking."

"Do it somewhere left."

Fair.

I moved left.

Thought.

Twenty-one after fare.

Then probably one copper bed.

Maybe food stayed bits.

Maybe Dorrin expensive.

Maybe return cost more.

Maybe work existed.

Maybe not.

Could go south.

One copper cart back Ternhaven harbor.

Three copper eventually Old Merrow if packet.

Then Lerrin.

Then Duskport.

Then home.

Not impossible.

Not failure.

Actually responsible.

I hated it.

Good sign? Bad sign?

Probably neither.

I pulled notebook.

Not because accounting needed drama.

Because money lied when feelings rounded numbers.

**Cash 25c.**

**Vale 23c.**

**Limb deposit paid 20c. Remaining quote 1 silver + 18c, mixed denomination unresolved.**

**Dorrin ferry 4c.**

**Likely remaining after fare 21c.**

Then under that:

**Why go?**

Charge-rich kelp.

Battery vats.

Bleed-pot system.

Kelp Isles.

Regional market.

New route.

Curiosity.

Then:

**Why not?**

Money.

Leg.

Return cost.

No job.

No obligation.

No one waiting there.

I stared.

That last one should have counted against going.

Instead it counted for.

Problem.

Or point.

I bought ticket.

Four copper.

Twenty-five to twenty-one.

Ferry clerk bit coin.

"Dorrin only?"

"Yes."

"Passenger deck forward. Pack under bench unless crew says move it. No touching bleed pots."

"Wouldn't."

"Everyone says that."

Boat workers everywhere had same religion.

The ferry was **Low Lantern**.

Wide.

Shallow.

Ugly in useful way.

Two square sails.

Long lattice racks over center deck.

Six ceramic bleed pots set between cargo lanes.

Blue seams glowed faint even daylight.

Cargo came first.

Wet kelp in open baskets.

Dry rope bundles.

Three sealed black crocks marked charge warning.

Crab cages.

One narrow crate with brass corners and no label.

Not mine.

Again.

Passenger deck forward had eight benches.

Maybe twenty people.

Workers mostly.

Two families.

One woman with ledger case.

Three young men carrying hooked kelp knives wrapped in leather.

Old man with a basket of onions.

Nobody looked adventurous.

Excellent.

Dock was dry.

Boarding ramp broad.

I considered trial limb.

Could use it for boarding, maybe hands free for pack.

Wet deck after departure still problem.

No.

Crutches.

Crewman took pack after asking.

I boarded.

Simple.

Ferry pushed off third ring.

Ternhaven north shore flattened behind us.

Kelp water opened ahead.

Not open water exactly.

Fields.

That was first difference.

Long dark bands stretched between low islands.

Some natural.

Some clearly arranged.

Lines of floating white markers.

Wood stakes.

Net corridors.

Boats moving between them like carts in roads.

Kelp farms.

Actual sea agriculture.

Low Lantern followed cleared lane.

Captain didn't steer by visible land alone.

He watched pole markers with colored caps.

Blue.

White.

Yellow.

No red.

I asked passenger beside me.

Woman with ledger case.

"Lane markers?"

She nodded.

"Depth and charge restriction. Blue ordinary. White shallow draft. Yellow high bleed."

"High bleed?"

"Kelp beds carrying too much residual charge for unshielded compass stones."

"So ferry has pots."

"So ferry has pots."

She looked at my crutches.

Then pack.

Then stopped caring.

Good.

"Dorrin worker?" I asked.

"Assessor."

"Kelp?"

"Everything wet people argue is kelp."

Messy enough answer I liked her.

"Greg."

"Nara Kesh."

Distinct name.

Thank god.

Nara opened ledger.

Conversation over.

Also good.

We crossed first yellow lane maybe half bell later.

Crew changed something at bleed pots.

Not opening.

Rotated ceramic collars.

Blue seam brightened.

Compass stone near helm flickered once.

Then settled.

I watched.

Captain noticed.

"Passenger."

Me.

"Yes?"

"You can look. Don't lean over cargo line."

"Understood."

Good boundary.

Bleed pot function clearer in motion.

Kelp baskets themselves had faint prickling light in wet folds.

Not glowing dramatically.

Little flashes when water ran.

Blue pot seams brightened as ferry passed yellow markers.

Then faded after.

Charge transfer?

Suppression?

Grounding into something?

Didn't know.

Could ask later.

Not invent.

Halfway, one of young kelp workers dropped metal hook.

It hit deck near black crock.

Nothing happened.

He swore anyway.

Crewman shouted.

"Wrap your steel!"

Worker: "It slipped."

"And if jar seam jumps?"

"It didn't."

"Great. Put that on tombstone."

Worker wrapped hook.

Human systems same everywhere.

Rules existed because somebody got tired of almost dying.

Sea changed closer Dorrin.

Islands lower.

Some barely more than platforms of root and stone.

Structures stood on piles.

Long drying frames.

Wind towers with hanging ceramic ribbons.

Floating workyards tethered between islands.

One island had no houses, only enormous black vats under roofs.

Battery vats maybe.

Nara looked up when I stared.

"Bleed works."

"Dorrin?"

"Outer yard. Main market ahead."

"Those charge storage?"

"Those prevent charge storage where you don't want it."

Correction.

"Where does useful charge go?"

"Depends grade. Some into ceramic cells. Some bled into sink stone. Some sold wet to people who like problems."

"People buy dangerous kelp wet?"

"People buy everything if they think margin is hiding in it."

True across worlds.

Dorrin appeared not as island town but cluster.

Five low islands connected by bridges, floating lanes, ferry slips, drying platforms.

Central market roof enormous.

Green-black, maybe kelp thatch.

Three tall lattice towers rose over it with blue glass at top.

No city wall.

No single harbor.

Water inside everything.

Low Lantern entered through marked channel.

Then stopped before dock.

Of course.

I sighed.

Nara glanced.

"First time?"

"Apparently my face says delays."

"This isn't delay."

Crew lowered long pole into water.

Second crew checked pot seams.

Captain waited.

Then one tower glass shifted blue to clear.

Ferry moved.

"What was that?"

"Charge gate."

Nara closed ledger.

"Dorrin doesn't let loaded kelp boats bunch inside market basin if bleed balance is high. They meter entries."

"Traffic light for magical seaweed."

She stared.

"What?"

"Nothing."

Need local metaphors.

Dock dry enough.

Disembarking easier.

Twenty-one copper.

Dorrin.

Immediately louder than Ternhaven.

Not people.

Things.

Wood racks knocking.

Ceramic bells.

Water slapping piles.

Winches.

Vent hisses.

Someone shouting lot numbers.

Market smelled salt, iodine, smoke, vinegar, wet rope, hot clay.

Good.

Different.

Very different.

I moved slowly off dock.

Board at entrance:

**DORRIN MARKET BASIN**

**PASSENGER LODGING -> ISLAND THREE**

**NORTH OUTER FERRY -> ISLAND FIVE**

**CHARGE GOODS DECLARATION BEFORE COVERED MARKET**

**UNMARKED WET KELP MAY BE SEIZED / TEST FEE TO OWNER**

Below:

**PUBLIC WORK BOARD / ISLAND TWO**

I noticed that.

Not because desperate.

Because twenty-one copper.

Maybe little desperate.

First lodging.

Island Three connected by low arched bridge.

Bridge wet but textured stone.

Crutches fine.

Bunk house charged **2c**.

Dorrin expensive.

Of course.

I asked.

"Anything one?"

Keeper pointed down lane.

"Net loft if space. One copper. Smells worse."

"How worse?"

"You stop noticing."

Perfect.

Net loft had space.

One copper.

Twenty-one to twenty.

Twenty.

Same number as deposit.

Interesting psychological cliff.

I paid.

Bed was rope platform above stacked dry nets.

Smelled tar and sea rot.

Could have been worse.

Probably would be later.

Pack stored.

Trial limb still wrapped.

Lot 67 safe.

I had afternoon.

Could wander market.

Could work board.

Could rest.

Money voted work board.

Island Two board covered jobs.

Most not mine.

**KELP HAUL / STRONG BACK / WET WORK / 7C DAY**

No.

**VAT SCRUB / WATER BOOTS PROVIDED / 5C HALF**

Tempting pay.

Bad limb/crutch environment.

No.

**LEDGER COPY / CLEAR HAND / 2C PAGE SET**

Maybe.

**LOT RUNNER / FAST FEET / 4C RING**

Funny.

No.

**DRY RACK TAG SORT / SEATED OK / 3C HALF SHIFT**

There.

Posted by **Rusk & Mere Charge House**.

Mere.

Not Merrow.

Fine.

I took chit.

Charge house on Island Four.

Worker at desk looked at crutches.

"You read marks?"

"If you show key."

"Write?"

"Yes."

"Count?"

"Usually."

She didn't laugh.

"Half shift pays three. Mistakes on red tags cost us more than you make, so don't guess."

"Good policy."

"You work here before?"

"No."

"Then slower than you think."

Also good.

Her name **Peth Orin**.

Voice clipped.

Work simple enough.

Dry rack tags came in baskets.

Ceramic slivers with punched edge codes and painted charge grade.

White ordinary food.

Blue utility fiber.

Yellow residual charged.

Red isolation.

Black rejection.

Numbers for weight lots.

My job sort by grade, then sequence rack numbers, flag duplicates or missing pair.

Seated table.

No magic.

No lifting beyond small baskets.

Perfect.

First basket twenty-seven tags.

One duplicate 18-yellow.

Missing 19-yellow.

I flagged.

Peth checked.

"Good."

Second basket had black tag painted over blue.

I stopped.

"Which wins?"

"Black."

"Even if blue under?"

"Especially if blue under. Means downgrade after retest."

Local rule.

Third basket two red tags stuck together by salt.

Separated carefully.

No problem.

Half shift maybe two bells.

Paid three copper.

Twenty to twenty-three.

That felt enormous.

Not actually enormous.

But direction changed.

For first time since North Reach, road didn't only eat.

Small thing.

Important.

Peth said, "Tomorrow?"

I hesitated.

Could earn again.

Could stay.

This is how city catches you.

Not prison.

Work.

Bed.

Interesting market.

Then suddenly week.

"Maybe."

"Maybe doesn't reserve chair."

"Fair."

"Board first ring."

Done.

I wandered covered market after shift.

Not buying.

Looking.

Important distinction I kept repeating.

Dorrin market sold kelp products in categories I didn't know existed.

Food sheets.

Rope fiber.

Charge gel in sealed crocks.

Dry conductive braid.

Storm-scorched fronds sold to artificers.

Kelp glass.

That one stopped me.

Thin green-black translucent plates made from ash and charged binder.

Seller demonstrated flex.

Plate bent slightly.

Returned.

"Window?" I asked.

"Gauge face, lamp shield, low-pressure viewing slit. Not building window unless you hate house."

"Cost?"

"This size six copper."

I put hands behind back.

Not buying.

Problem list did not need six-copper weird glass.

Nearby stall had retired **bleed clips**.

Small brass clamps with ceramic jaw inserts.

Two copper each.

Used to shunt residual charge from narrow wet braid during repair.

Actual portable environmental/tool interaction.

Also tempting.

I asked seller operating limits.

He said, "Depends what you clamp."

"Useful."

"You asked bad question."

Fair.

I did not buy.

Twenty-three copper now.

Do not reward earning three by spending six.

Training brain for money.

At one stall I saw artifact that genuinely made me stop.

Not flashy.

Palm-sized gray disk with four shallow grooves.

Mounted on cork.

Label:

**RESIDUAL FIELD WITNESS / OLD DORRIN DRYING HOUSE / 9C**

Problem list number three maybe.

Warning before environmental change reaches me?

Not exactly.

I asked.

Seller lifted disk by cork.

"Records strongest directional charge crossing surface since reset. Grooves darken in quadrant."

Passive witness.

Closer number two.

HT-LP-4 already passive load witness.

This passive field direction.

Potentially useful.

Nine copper.

Almost half current cash.

"Reset?"

"Warm salt wash."

"How long does record persist?"

"Days if dry. Hours wet."

"Threshold?"

"Depends age."

"Calibrated?"

"No."

"Known reference?"

"Old drying house used them for lane comparison, not absolute measure."

Interesting.

Nine.

No.

I wanted it.

Problem was real.

Price maybe fair.

Still no.

Leg money.

Return money.

Current tool inventory already heavy enough.

Seller saw answer.

"Seven if you come tomorrow."

That was not how negotiation usually worked.

"Why tomorrow?"

"Because today you look like man who wants to be convinced. Tomorrow maybe you're man who decided."

Good seller.

Dangerous.

"Still no."

"Tomorrow then."

"That's not what no means."

"Market interpretation."

I left.

Dorrin evening had no sunset view because structures everywhere.

Blue tower glass deepened as market basin charge rose.

Ferries queued outside gate.

Battery yard lamps came on green.

Workers changed shifts.

No one cared I had crossed half region to get here.

Wonderful.

Net loft smelled worse.

Keeper had been right.

I ate bowl kelp noodles for bits.

Actually good.

Not revelation.

Just dinner.

Then trial limb.

Dry loft floor.

Stable.

Useful task: repack after ferry and market, move gear from floor hooks to sleeping platform with both hands.

I fitted build.

Crutches close.

Twenty-five minutes max? Previous 22 dry ridge. Road protocol originally 30 max, but no reason push.

I chose **24 minutes**.

Flat boards.

Standing.

Short transfers.

Carried wrapped Lot 67 from pack to shelf with both hands.

Five or six steps.

Then notebook.

Then clothing.

Nothing heavy.

At minute fourteen, gait felt easier than first Duskport trials.

Not normal.

Different.

I noticed I had stopped thinking about each turn before making it.

That mattered more than speed.

At eighteen, hip fatigue started.

At twenty-two, I made one sloppy pivot.

Stopped walking.

Stood to twenty-four with hands on table.

Then off.

Skin check.

Upper pink mild.

Lower front faint line.

No pain.

Twenty minutes later both cleared.

Good.

I wrote:

**Dorrin loft. 24 min dry flat board. Mostly standing/repack. ~12 total steps. Turn planning less conscious until fatigue. Mild upper pink + faint lower-front line, both clear by 20. Stop before gait degrades next time.**

Potential.

Not miracle.

More hands.

Still whole point.

Then money.

Twenty-three cash.

I had spent four to get here, one bed, earned three.

Net movement minus two from morning.

That felt different from minus five.

Dangerous thought.

Because earning could rationalize staying.

Staying could rationalize buying.

Buying could rationalize more work.

Suddenly life.

Could build here too.

That was strange.

Carrow still home.

Duskport still had unfinished leg.

Theatre.

Tavi.

Cala.

People.

Obligations.

But road wasn't empty space between home and destination anymore.

Places could catch.

Not with destiny.

With three-copper half shifts and bad-smelling bunks.

More dangerous honestly.

I checked route board copied from market.

Dorrin outward options:

**Outer Kelp Isles / fourth day freight-passenger mixed / ask basin master.**

**North mainland / sixth day / 6c.**

**Ternhaven return / daily if tide / 4c.**

**Storm Shelf work boats / contract crews only.**

North mainland.

Six copper.

Farther.

Not tomorrow.

Maybe not at all.

I put notebook away.

Investor was stupid title.

I hadn't bought business.

Hadn't funded expedition.

Had paid four copper to see whether curiosity was worth money.

Then earned three because being there created work.

Maybe that's what investment was before people made it boring.

Spend something now because you believe future options are worth more.

Could lose.

Could gain.

Could just get noodles and wet seaweed.

Twenty-three copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

One unfinished leg somewhere south.

One provisional timing tool.

One city full of magical kelp economics I barely understood.

Not bad.

Tomorrow, first ring work board.

Then decide whether Dorrin was stop.

Or another door.

# CHAPTER 447

## THE ARBITER

First-ring work board had no chair.

Good.

Not good financially.

Good structurally.

I did not need become professional tag sorter because one chair paid three copper yesterday.

Board had:

**BASIN THREE / BLEED-POT WASH / WET FEET / 4C**

No.

**NORTH RACK / BUNDLE TURN / STRONG BACK / 5C**

No.

**MARKET COUNT / LOT TALLIES / 2C HALF / WRITING REQUIRED**

Maybe.

Then woman nailed new strip over bottom.

**RED QUAY / DISPUTED LOT HOLD / TWO WITNESSES / 3C / REPORT TO ASSESSOR**

Interesting.

"What does disputed lot hold do?" I asked.

Board woman looked at crutches.

"Sit near kelp and stop people moving it."

"That's within my training."

"Can you write?"

"Yes."

"Can you not decide things nobody asked you to decide?"

Harder.

"Probably."

She tore tab.

"Red Quay. Nara Kesh."

Of course.

I knew one assessor in Dorrin.

Dorrin knew six thousand kelp.

Odds weird.

I went Red Quay.

Dry boardwalk most way.

Crutches.

Trial leg rested.

Still left wrapped.

Red Quay smelled vinegar and hot clay.

Three bundles of black-green kelp sat isolated on ceramic slats under red rope.

Not much.

Maybe wagon-load if dry.

Two people arguing.

One I recognized.

Nara Kesh.

Other man broad, wet sleeves, gray beard, face built for saying no before question finished.

"I brought witnesses," Nara said.

"One witness," I said.

She looked behind me.

"Where's second?"

Board runner arrived with young woman carrying tally slate.

"There."

Good.

Gray beard said, "We're paying three copper each for people to stare at kelp?"

Nara said, "You're paying hold cost because your lot is disputed."

"My lot passed dock strip."

"Then failed house cup."

"House cup is wrong."

"Maybe."

"Then release it."

"No."

There.

I sat.

Young tally woman sat other end.

Name **Fenn Rusk**.

No relation question.

I learned.

Nara gave instructions.

"Nothing crosses red rope. Nobody washes, trims, swaps ties, separates fronds, adds brine, removes brine, or performs helpful magic. Record who approaches and why. If lot changes, report."

"Do we test?" Fenn asked.

"No."

"Touch?"

"No."

"Smell?"

Nara stared.

"You may smell from where your face already is."

Fenn nodded seriously.

I liked her.

Gray beard did not leave.

"Name?" I asked him.

"Dorr Venn."

Venn.

I almost laughed.

He noticed.

"What?"

"Nothing."

Could be no relation.

World had surnames.

"Grower?"

"Lease grower. South Hook beds. Twelve years."

"What's disputed?"

Nara answered before him.

"Residual charge grade."

Dorr answered over her.

"Bad cup."

Nara: "Dock strip read yellow. House cup read red."

I knew yellow residual charged.

Red isolation.

Difference mattered.

"Price?"

Dorr said, "Yellow sells. Red sits until buyer or bleed."

Nara said, "And if we sell red as yellow, someone downstream gets charge they didn't buy."

Dorr: "It's not red."

Nara: "That's dispute."

Simple.

Not simple.

Nara left for second test.

Dorr paced.

Fenn tallied approaches.

First was buyer clerk.

Denied access.

Second was yard boy asking if bundle belonged South Hook.

Yes.

Third was woman carrying wash bucket.

Wrong lot.

Fourth was Dorr again even though he never left enough to count.

Fenn counted him anyway.

"I am owner."

"Lease grower," she said.

"That isn't different here."

"You approached rope."

"I've been standing at rope."

"Then you approached continuously."

He looked at me.

"Is she serious?"

"Extremely."

Fenn wrote something.

Probably me interfering.

Nara returned with ceramic cup and older man in blue apron.

Cup looked like shallow white bowl with four dark studs around rim.

No magic spectacle.

She set it on separate test table.

Took clipped sample from sealed evidence pouch, not lot itself.

Blue apron said, "Second sample witnessed at intake."

Dorr: "From outer edge."

Nara: "Yes."

"Outer edge took spray."

"Yes."

"Then it isn't representative."

Nara stopped.

That was real point.

"Dock strip was also outer face," she said.

"Dock strip touches bundle. House sample was cut from one wet frond."

"Correct."

"So one says yellow and one says red from same outer side."

"Correct."

"Then your instruments disagree."

Nara looked annoyed.

Not because wrong.

Because accurate.

Blue apron said, "Cup measures retained charge after compression. Strip measures surface bleed response. They don't measure same thing."

Dorr: "Then don't call one grade."

Ah.

There.

Not broken instrument necessarily.

Different measurement.

Same commercial category.

Nara ran cup.

Sample placed center.

Studs warmed faint blue.

Then one went violet.

Then two.

Red mark appeared in glaze.

"Red," blue apron said.

Dorr swore.

"Outer wet frond."

Nara said, "I agree sample location is weak."

Dorr blinked.

Expected fight.

"Then release."

"No."

There again.

Nara explained.

Lot could be heterogeneous.

Maybe only outer fronds high.

Maybe whole bundle.

Could not know without opening and sampling interior.

Opening lot changed commercial condition.

Dorr objected because buyer contract priced tied wet bundles by intake weight.

If untied and sampled, buyer could reject as disturbed.

If held overnight, kelp lost water weight and Dorr got paid less even if later yellow.

If released yellow now and actually red, buyer inherited dangerous handling cost.

Correct procedure hurt somebody either way.

Good.

Not good for Dorr.

Nara asked contract clerk.

Clerk came.

Read terms.

Disputed grade allowed hold up to one bell without weight adjustment.

After one bell, either:

1. seller authorizes interior sample and preserves intake weight for pricing if lot passes;

2. seller refuses sample and lot remains isolation grade until seller removes/regrades at own cost;

3. buyer accepts lot at red terms.

Dorr said, "Buyer won't accept red."

Clerk: "Correct."

"So my choices are let you cut it or lose sale."

"Under contract, yes."

"That's not choice."

Clerk did not answer.

Because kind of true.

Nara looked at clock marker.

Half bell left.

Dorr wanted something badly.

Not abstract fairness.

Money.

He had crew share due tonight.

He said it eventually.

"South Hook crew gets paid from this load. Nine people. If you cut and buyer rejects disturbed bundle anyway, I still owe them."

Nara said, "If I release red as yellow and charge cooks someone's drying rack, their crew loses too."

"Mine are here."

"So are theirs."

No clean answer.

I was paid to stare.

Kept staring.

Then Nara asked me.

"Greg."

"Yeah?"

"You keep notes. Tell me what we actually know."

Not decide.

Good.

I read page.

"Dock strip yellow. House cup first sample red. Second witnessed sample from outer face also red. Strip and cup measure different properties, so disagreement doesn't prove either broken. Both cup samples outer material. Interior untested. Lot heterogeneity unknown. Contract gives one bell protected hold, then seller can authorize interior sample with intake weight preserved if passes, refuse and take isolation classification, or buyer can accept red. Buyer won't."

Dorr said, "You forgot outer fronds took spray."

"That's your explanation. Plausible. Not established."

"It matters."

"Yes."

"Put it down."

I did.

**Seller states outer fronds exposed to charged spray during south crossing; may explain elevated retained charge. Not independently established.**

He read over shoulder.

"You write like asshole."

"Professional hazard."

Fenn snorted.

Nara didn't.

Maybe almost.

Then buyer arrived.

Not clerk.

Buyer herself.

Small woman in red vest named **Ira Molt**.

She wanted lot.

That changed.

"At yellow price," Dorr said.

"At yellow grade," Ira corrected.

"Same thing."

"No."

She explained her vats had narrow charge window today because one bleed bank was offline for ceramic relining.

A red lot wasn't merely worth less.

She couldn't safely take it into current batch.

Could hold separately, but isolation berth already occupied.

If she accepted red, it sat outside and risked spoilage.

So buyer had real constraint too.

Nara asked whether Ira would accept an interior-sampled disturbed bundle if sample passed yellow.

Ira hesitated.

Contract allowed.

Operationally she hated it because untied wet kelp dried unevenly before vat.

"If re-tied immediately and total sample under one handweight," she said, "I'll accept at yellow less one copper handling deduction."

Dorr exploded.

"Why am I paying because your bleed bank is down?"

Ira: "You're not. You're paying because your lot tested red twice."

"Outer fronds."

"Then prove interior."

"By damaging bundle."

"Yes."

There.

Everybody right enough to be miserable.

Dorr looked at nine workers across quay.

I had not noticed them before.

Waiting.

His crew.

One eating bread.

One sitting barrel.

One staring directly.

Crew share due.

One copper handling deduction maybe spread across nine plus Dorr.

Not ruin.

Still insult.

Also precedent maybe.

Dorr said, "No deduction."

Ira said, "Then no disturbed lot."

Nara said, "You have twenty minutes."

Dorr: "Stop saying time like it makes this fair."

Nara: "It makes contract expire."

Oof.

I was not arbiter.

Title maybe liar.

I was witness.

But everyone kept looking at notes like paper could manufacture fourth option.

Could there be fourth?

Maybe retest different outer samples.

Wouldn't answer interior.

Could use residual field witness from market? No calibration. Wrong context. Also not mine.

HT-LP-4 irrelevant.

WS-F/9 direction current, not retained charge grade.

Line quietener irrelevant.

Lot 67 timing, definitely irrelevant.

Magic? No.

No tool in bag solved values.

Good lesson.

I asked Nara, "Can handling deduction be split?"

Dorr looked hopeful.

Ira annoyed.

Nara said, "Contract doesn't care who pays deduction. Buyer only needs one copper off invoice."

Ah.

Not solution yet.

"Can market hold fund cover?"

"No."

"Assessor?"

"Absolutely not."

"Crew?"

Dorr: "No."

Correct.

"Seller and buyer split bits?"

Ira said, "I don't have to."

Dorr: "Neither do I."

Exactly.

Could compromise.

Nobody obligated.

Ira wanted lot enough to come herself.

Dorr needed sale enough to stand here.

One copper was gap.

They could each eat half if currency bits supported. Bits unspecified value; don't invent conversion.

Could split one-copper invoice deduction conceptually by other consideration? Avoid conversion.

Maybe Dorr accepts 1c deduction, Ira pays immediate crew transfer fee? Too contrived.

Keep painful choice.

Dorr eventually chose.

"Cut it. One handweight max. Re-tie here. If interior yellow, I eat copper."

Ira nodded.

No triumph.

Nara prepared sample.

Two witnesses.

Me and Fenn.

Red rope opened.

Bundle untied.

Interior darker, cooler.

Nara cut small central frond section.

Cup.

Studs blue.

One violet.

Wait.

Glaze mark yellow.

Dorr exhaled.

Not cheer.

Second interior sample from opposite side.

Yellow.

Lot heterogeneous.

Outer retained more charge.

Interior within yellow.

Nara classified **yellow with handling note / outer-surface red readings / interior two-sample yellow**.

Ira accepted under one-copper deduction.

Crew re-tied immediately.

Sale moved.

Dorr still angry.

"I lost copper because crossing sprayed outside."

Nara said, "Yes."

"And if I refused?"

"You'd lose sale."

"Good system."

"It's a system."

That line hurt more.

Dorr's crew got paid.

Ira got usable lot.

Nobody got perfect fairness.

I got three copper.

That felt awkward.

Nara signed witness sheet.

Fenn signed.

I signed.

Dorr looked at my name.

"Peg-Leg Greg?"

I closed eyes.

"No."

"Crew said that's you."

"Which crew?"

He pointed one worker.

Worker shrugged.

"Cousin on Ternhaven."

Fuck islands.

"I don't have peg."

Dorr looked at wrapped trial build.

"You have enough leg to make nickname inconvenient."

"Thank you."

He held hand.

I shook.

Still angry.

Not at me exactly.

"Your notes were fair," he said.

"Sorry about copper."

"Don't be. You didn't take it."

Then he left.

Good.

Nara paid me three.

Twenty-three to twenty-six.

Better.

"You could do assessor clerk work," she said.

Dangerous sentence.

"How much?"

"Depends."

"Bad start."

"Four to six copper day if attached to yard. More if licensed."

That was real money.

Not huge.

Real.

"License?"

"Training. Exams. Sponsorship. Time."

"How much time?"

"Months."

There.

Not adventure side quest.

Career.

I laughed.

Nara looked offended.

"It's respectable."

"That's why funny."

She did not understand.

Fine.

Market afternoon.

Residual Field Witness stall still there.

Disk still seven copper.

I had twenty-six now.

Could buy and remain nineteen.

No.

Actually yes mathematically.

Bad financially.

Seller saw me.

"Seven."

"Still no."

"You came back."

"Market is between work and bed."

"Six."

Oh fuck.

Better.

Six copper.

Tool fit passive directional witness category.

Could pair conceptually with WS-F/9 current direction and HT-LP-4 load peak.

Could record strongest directional charge while not watching.

Could matter for magic training eventually.

Could matter environmental field changes.

Could be useful.

Also I had a trial leg I didn't own.

Cash twenty-six.

North mainland six.

Return four.

Bunks.

Food mostly bits until not.

Six was too much.

I asked seller, "Can you demonstrate reset and persistence?"

"Reset yes. Persistence takes time."

"Known defect?"

"One groove darkens slower when wet."

There.

"How much slower?"

"Don't know."

"Calibration?"

"None."

"Provenance?"

"Old Nine Rack house. Retired when they switched lane strips."

"Why retired?"

"Lane strips cheaper to train workers on."

Not necessarily bad.

Still six.

"Five," I said.

Seller smiled.

Shit.

I had bid.

"Six."

"Five."

"Six."

I could walk.

Pera Menn in head.

Decide what problem buying solution for before bell starts.

Problem: passive directional charge witness where cannot watch continuously.

Did I currently have that problem?

Not really.

Future maybe.

Future was expensive liar.

"No."

I walked.

Seller called, "Five and a half!"

No universal half copper conversion. Ignore.

I kept walking.

Good.

My brain screamed idiot.

Also good.

I bought bunk one copper.

Twenty-six to twenty-five.

Net loft again.

At dinner board, north route changed.

**NORTH MAINLAND / SIXTH DAY / 6C / BERTHS 11**

Tomorrow was fourth day.

Outer Isles mixed ferry tomorrow.

North mainland two days after.

I could work tomorrow.

Maybe earn.

Could also get stuck.

Could also spend.

Dorrin offered something dangerous now.

Not artifact.

A wage.

A possible profession.

A place where my weird habit of separating claims had market value.

Carrow had that too, in different forms.

Duskport had leg waiting.

Road had started proving I could reproduce usefulness elsewhere.

That was good.

Maybe too good.

I wrote home note.

**Dorrin, Kelp Isles. Working small jobs. 25c cash, 23c Vale. Trial build fine. No return date yet. Dask went Bellweather. Maro salt farms. Sori last Merehook. Fine. - Greg**

Then another line.

**Did not buy six-copper artifact. Please praise restraint.**

Cala would not.

Tavi might draw medal.

Lyssa would ask what artifact.

Home existed.

Farther now.

Not gone.

I lay down.

Arbiter sounded like person who decided.

I hadn't.

Dorr decided to lose copper.

Ira decided lot worth trouble.

Nara decided grade from evidence and contract.

Crew decided nothing and still lived with result.

I had kept facts from pretending they were choices.

Maybe that was enough.

Maybe title wrong.

Too late.

# CHAPTER 448

## THE OUTRIDER

Outer Isles ferry cost three copper.

Cheap enough to be dangerous.

Board said:

**OUTER THREE / FOURTH DAY / MIXED FREIGHT-PASSENGER / 3C / RETURN NOT GUARANTEED SAME DAY**

There.

Important line last.

I asked basin clerk.

"Which three?"

"Lark, Weir, Black Reed."

"Stops all?"

"If cargo."

"Passenger can get off any?"

"Yes."

"Return?"

"Depends load."

"Tomorrow?"

"Probably."

"Probably is not schedule."

"Correct."

Good.

North mainland still sixth day.

Six copper.

Two days away.

I could stay Dorrin, maybe work.

Or pay three and go farther sideways.

Not north exactly.

Outward.

Twenty-five cash.

Three out meant twenty-two.

Bunk maybe one wherever.

Return maybe three if needed.

Potentially eighteen after a round trip and sleep.

Leg money screaming.

Adventure brain louder.

I stared at board long enough clerk said, "You buying or praying?"

"Budgeting."

"Looks similar."

I bought.

Three copper.

Twenty-five to twenty-two.

Outrider.

Maybe.

Boat called **Silt Mare**.

Ugly name.

Ugly boat.

Perfect.

Long shallow hull with reinforced bow runners and side lattice racks.

Freight:

wet kelp bundles.

two clay battery crocks.

fish traps.

salt sacks.

one crate of green-glass rods.

Passengers five.

Me.

Woman carrying ledger box.

Two brothers with rolled nets.

Old man with four live black eels in lidded tub.

Young person in orange coat sleeping against mast.

No one asked life story.

Trial leg wrapped.

Wet boat.

No.

Crew recognized crutches and gave me aft corner where deck had cross slats.

Good enough.

Silt Mare left after second ring.

Dorrin behind looked like machinery scattered across water.

Outer Isles ahead looked like mistakes.

Low dark lumps.

Some barely land.

Work platforms larger than villages.

Kelp lanes denser.

Markers changed.

Not Dorrin blue-white-yellow.

Here posts carried woven flags:

black stripe.

green stripe.

split white.

I asked crew.

"Ownership lanes?"

"Lease boundaries."

"Charge?"

"No. Charge bells."

Then I noticed ceramic bells hanging low over water on tiny floats.

Some quiet.

Some ticking faintly.

"What do they do?"

Crewman said, "When kelp field starts bleeding above lane agreement, bell ticks."

"Agreement?"

"Neighbor doesn't want your charge drift."

Ah.

Property law underwater.

Excellent.

First island Lark.

Mostly racks and two dozen houses.

Ledger woman got off.

Cargo unloaded three wet bundles.

Crew took on six dry braid coils.

Ten minutes.

No tour.

Weir next.

Longer island with reed roofs and one big kiln chimney.

One net brother got off.

Other didn't.

They argued across dock while boat untied.

"Mother said both!"

"Mother said one!"

"She said bring Jaro!"

"I'm Jaro!"

Boat crew laughed.

Brother on boat yelled, "Then who am I?"

Dock brother yelled back, "Idiot!"

Useful family record.

Then Black Reed.

Name accurate.

Island dark with reed beds nearly black-purple around edges.

Main platform sat on piles above mudflat.

Not much town.

Maybe fifteen buildings.

Boatmaster announced:

"Return likely tomorrow afternoon if north loads ready. Maybe fifth day morning if not. Don't complain at me about island."

I asked lodging.

"Reed House. One copper floor bunk if space."

"Work?"

"Ask lot board."

"Return fare?"

"Three."

So getting off created minimum four copper more if overnight and return.

Could stay boat through circuit? Silt Mare ended Black Reed today.

No.

This was terminus.

I got off.

Twenty-two.

Black Reed smelled different.

Less iodine.

More mud, hot mineral, smoked reed.

Boardwalk narrow.

Crutch tips okay on dry planks.

Mud below looked like death.

No trial leg outside.

Lot board had maybe seven notices.

**NORTH CUT REED PULL / 4C / KNEE MUD**

No.

**DRY HOUSE SCALE CHECK / 2C / THIRD RING**

Maybe.

**LEASE FLAG REHANG / 3C / BOAT REQUIRED**

No.

**KILN WATCH / EVENING / 2C / SIT OKAY**

Interesting.

Then separate red paper:

**FIELD TWELVE BELL DISAGREEMENT / OWNER + NEIGHBOR PRESENT / BASIN READER REQUESTED**

No pay listed.

Not my problem.

Good.

I went Reed House.

Floor bunk one copper.

Twenty-two to twenty-one.

Left pack.

Kept tools I usually carried.

Not all.

Then dry house scale check.

Two copper.

Different work.

Scale house used hanging wet-weight balances for kelp bundles before drying.

Not fancy.

Three beam scales.

Ceramic reference weights.

My job not calibrate.

Watch operator cycle known weights and write deviations.

Seated stool.

Good.

Operator **Mett Sarl**.

Voice too fast.

"You write number I say, not number you think I should say, because last boy improved my scales on paper and then everybody improved his face behind kiln."

"Comforting workplace."

"He's fine."

"Also comforting."

We ran scales.

Scale one clean.

Scale two drifted slightly with third reference weight.

Scale three stuck on return.

Not my expertise.

Mett cursed scale three.

"Salt in pivot?"

I asked.

"Maybe. Or reed dust. Or ghost."

"Ghost least likely?"

"Most annoying."

We logged.

No Greg fix.

Then Field Twelve bell disagreement arrived at door in form of shouting.

Two women.

One man.

And one ceramic lane bell in a basket.

Of course.

Mett looked at me.

"Don't volunteer."

"Wasn't."

"Your face volunteered."

Damn face.

Woman one, **Sena Holt**, owned Field Twelve.

Woman two, **Bria Noll**, leased neighboring Eleven.

Man was local basin reader **Olan Tress**.

Bell from boundary float had ticked high-charge warning twice overnight.

Bria claimed Twelve was bleeding charge into Eleven and damaging young kelp.

Sena claimed bell itself was fouled and false-triggering because Eleven's floats hadn't been cleaned after storm.

Olan had inspected field water that morning.

Normal then.

Which proved almost nothing about overnight.

Mett said, "Take it outside."

They did not.

Sena saw my notebook.

"You reader?"

"No."

Bria: "He writes."

"Unfortunately."

Olan looked tired.

"We need second observer for reset test."

Mett pointed at me.

Traitor.

"He can count."

"Pay?" I asked.

Sena and Bria both looked offended.

Olan said, "One copper from basin fund for witness."

Fine.

New layer, not same dispute exactly.

We went boundary field.

Boat.

Problem.

Flat punt stable enough.

Crutches laid lengthwise.

I sat.

No trial limb.

Fields looked like submerged gardens.

Rows of kelp under dark water.

Boundary bell mounted float between Eleven and Twelve.

Small ceramic shell with interior reed tongue and blue thread wrapped neck.

Olan explained minimally.

Bell doesn't measure exact charge.

It accumulates directional excess over threshold.

Ticks when enough crosses from one side.

Directional by float orientation and reed tongue.

Can be fouled by salt film, kelp slime, cracked thread, bad orientation, or actual excess.

Reset requires fresh-water rinse, dry wipe, known reference strip passed each side.

We did.

Fresh rinse.

Dry.

Reference strip from Eleven side.

One tick.

Expected.

From Twelve side.

One tick.

Expected.

Repeat.

Clean.

Bell not obviously broken now.

Bria said, "Then Twelve did it."

Sena: "No. It means bell works now."

Correct.

Olan agreed.

No historical proof.

Then he checked float anchor.

Anchor line had twisted maybe quarter-turn around weed clump.

Orientation arrow no longer aligned lease boundary.

That mattered.

"How long?" Bria asked.

Olan: "Don't know."

"Storm?"

"Maybe."

"Could it have been pointed into Eleven current?"

"Yes."

"Could it have been pointed into Twelve?"

"Yes."

Sena crossed arms.

Bria swore.

Historical event unrecoverable.

But damage real?

Bria showed young kelp at edge with browned tips.

Could be charge exposure.

Could be temperature, abrasion, disease maybe.

Olan said charge injury plausible.

Not certain.

Lease rule required owner causing verified cross-boundary bleed to pay mitigation.

But cause not verified.

If unverified, each field bears own loss.

Bria furious.

"So because your float twisted, I eat damage?"

Olan: "Float is basin property."

"Even better. Basin eats it."

"Rule doesn't allow compensation without verified fault."

"Then rule protects basin from its own bad float."

There.

Human problem again.

Maybe too soon after Dorrin?

But different enough.

This one institutional liability, not buyer-seller grade.

Sena said, "And if basin pays every brown tip after storm, everyone claims everything."

Also true.

Bria: "Mine are actually brown."

Sena: "Yes. Cause unknown."

Nobody happy.

Olan documented twisted anchor and reset bell.

He offered extra temporary second bell for three days, basin-owned, no charge.

Not compensation.

Monitoring.

Bria wanted money now.

Didn't get it.

Sena wanted exoneration.

Didn't get that either.

Report would say **cause unresolved; boundary instrument orientation compromised; cross-charge exposure possible but unverified; temporary redundant monitoring installed**.

Useful.

Painful.

I witnessed reset and orientation.

One copper.

Twenty-one to twenty-two.

Small recovery.

Back at scale house, Mett paid two.

Twenty-two to twenty-four.

So Outer Isles day cost three ferry + one bunk, earned three.

Net minus one.

Road could almost sustain itself if I kept finding chairs.

Dangerous realization.

Evening I took kiln-watch shift too? That would be another repetitive work perhaps but different and could overdo. Better not stack every job.

No.

Body needed not infinite work.

I declined.

Good.

At Reed House, dry porch wide.

Trial leg rested two days.

There was real task: carry water basin and wash clothes while standing two-handed.

Stable boards.

Dry.

I wore trial build.

Twenty-six minutes.

Longer than Dorrin by two.

Not because goal chase.

Task lasted.

Crutches within reach.

I carried half-full wash basin six steps from pump shelf to bench.

Both hands.

That still felt stupidly luxurious.

Turn at bench cleaner than last time.

Second carry with wet clothes maybe seven steps.

Hip fatigue at twenty.

Socket heat mild around twenty-three.

Stopped at twenty-six.

No sloppy pivot.

Skin check: mild upper pink, faint lower-front line, both mostly cleared by twenty. Upper trace at twenty? Let's keep conservative.

At twenty-minute check upper pink nearly clear, lower-front clear; at thirty upper clear.

No pain.

Logged.

**Black Reed Reed House. 26 min dry board. Wash task. Two-handed half-basin carry 6 steps; wet-clothes carry 7. Crutches within reach. Hip fatigue ~20. Mild heat ~23. Stopped 26 before gait change. Lower-front clear by 20; upper nearly clear 20, clear 30.**

Good.

More hands.

Still body.

After shower? No showers. Wash.

I sat porch.

Clean shirt-ish.

Sun low.

Outer fields bells ticking faintly.

Could hear three separate rhythms.

Not clocks.

Warnings.

I thought about Lot 67.

Provisional repair.

Could maybe test against known timing here?

No need.

Not every tick is invitation.

Magic maybe.

Dry porch.

No wind? Island always some wind.

Could use enclosed room.

But after 26-min leg wear, not necessary.

Rest.

Then boatmaster from Silt Mare came Reed House.

"Return tomorrow delayed."

Of course.

"How delayed?"

"North load not ready. Maybe fifth-day second ring."

"Maybe."

"Correct."

I laughed.

"North mainland ferry?"

"From Dorrin sixth day."

"If I miss fifth-day return?"

"You miss it."

There.

To make north mainland I needed Silt Mare back by fifth day and then Dorrin night.

Probably possible.

Not guaranteed.

Alternative from Black Reed?

Boatmaster said small mail punt leaves dawn for **Storm Shelf south station**, then supply cutter to north mainland sometimes.

Contract/work only.

Not passenger.

No cheat.

Could stay and return.

Could give up north mainland cycle and wait next.

Could explore Lark/Weir depending freight.

No easy itinerary.

Good.

I asked local board about Black Reed work tomorrow.

Kiln watch.
