**BRINEWARD SURVEY TRANSIT / GREY POST / CLERK SEAT / FIRST RING**

Duties:

manifest copies.

sealed weather packet custody.

no field authority.

Five copper on delivery.

I liked `no field authority` enough to frame.

At inn I added note.

**Brineward. Mainland. Took one-day survey clerk seat to Grey Post tomorrow. Paid 2c employee coach. 5c on delivery if we make it. Cash 15c, Vale 23c. Ten floor intact. Trial build 28 min dry use, fine. - Greg**

Postage bits.

Maybe too many notes home.

But route farther.

Home needed crumbs.

I sat with notebook.

Passenger.

That was title.

Not traveler.

Not explorer.

Passenger.

Today I had paid to let other systems move me.

Boat.

Port gate.

Work ledger.

Inn.

Tomorrow survey coach.

Being passenger did not mean passive.

It meant knowing which part wasn't yours to steer.

Useful distinction.

Also cheaper than owning boat.

Tomorrow Grey Post.

# CHAPTER 451

## THE COURIER

Survey coach had six wheels.

That seemed excessive until I saw Shelf Road.

Then it seemed optimistic.

I arrived Brineward Survey Transit before first ring with pack, crutches, wrapped trial leg, fifteen copper, twenty-three Vale, and a temporary employment relationship with three sealed weather packets.

Woman at dispatch desk held them up.

"Dry."

"Yes."

"Sealed."

"Yes."

"You don't open them."

"That was implied by sealed."

She stared.

"I say it because people open them."

"Then useful."

Her name was **Mira Solt**.

No smile.

Good.

She handed me leather packet case with waxed flap and shoulder strap.

"Case is Survey property. Grey Post signs receipt. You copy road manifest at each staffed gate if changed. Driver owns movement. Gate officer owns road status. You own packets and paper."

"Excellent division."

"Don't improve it."

Brineward loved that instruction.

Coach driver was **Jeren Pike**.

Pike.

Not Jorren.

Names could rhyme without conspiracy.

He was narrow man with gray scarf and one missing front tooth.

"Lower seat," he said, pointing rear bench.

"Thanks."

"Don't thank me. Front axle throws mud."

"Still thanks."

Two other passengers rode under Survey permits.

A weather-glass technician named **Elsa Marr** with padded case.

Marr.

Again.

World had surnames.

And a road mason named **Kerr Holt** carrying tool roll and enough lunch for siege.

Nobody knew me.

Beautiful.

We left first ring.

Brineward flattened behind us.

Shelf Road climbed almost immediately.

Not mountain yet.

Long rising shelf above coastal plain.

Stone road, broad enough two freight carts, drainage trenches both sides.

Every half mile stood low posts with three ceramic fins.

White.

Amber.

Black.

I watched third one.

"Wind?" I asked.

Elsa looked.

"Shear posts."

"Measure?"

"Indicate."

Good distinction.

"White face means lower road wind within ordinary cart tolerance. Amber means high-side gusts. Black face turns when cross-shear exceeds freight rule."

"Charged?"

"Top fin lightly. Bottom two mechanical. Charge helps hold peak direction long enough to read after gust passes."

Useful.

Not exact instrument.

Road climbed.

At mile four, first staffed gate.

**LOW SHELF HOUSE**.

Jeren stopped.

Gate officer checked coach seal, road board, passenger count.

No change.

I copied manifest line anyway because job said each staffed gate if changed.

Actually no change meant no copy.

I almost copied.

Stopped.

Mira: if changed.

Good.

We continued.

At mile seven, sea behind us looked flat and stupidly far.

Brineward already small.

Carrow invisible by several systems.

Road bent north.

Wind increased.

Coach suspension earned all six wheels.

Front pair tracked shallow ruts.

Middle carried weight.

Rear pair kept long body from pitching as hard over stone joints.

Probably.

I wasn't wagonwright.

Ask.

"Why six?" I asked Jeren at water stop.

"Because four breaks more axles up here."

Simpler.

"Long wheelbase?"

"Middle axle takes belly load. Rear keeps packet cases from learning flight."

Good.

At mile ten, road changed.

Less grass.

More exposed gray stone.

Wind came sideways hard enough to push coat.

I was seated.

Fine.

Crutches tied under bench by my own straps.

Trial leg wrapped in pack.

Fine.

A shear post showed amber.

Next amber.

Third black.

Jeren slowed before gate appeared.

**THREE PIN GATE**.

Staffed.

Stone shelter.

Two coaches already waiting.

Gate officer raised hand.

Stop.

Jeren stopped.

I waited.

Nobody asked me to assess wind.

Beautiful.

Gate officer climbed aboard.

"Road status changed. High-side gust closure from Pin Twelve to Pin Sixteen. Minimum half bell."

There.

My job.

I opened ledger.

Original:

**BRINEWARD -> GREY POST / OPEN / HIGH WIND ABOVE M10 / SURVEY TRANSIT**

New:

**THREE PIN HOLD / HIGH-SIDE GUST CLOSURE P12-P16 / START SECOND RING LESS QUARTER / REVIEW HALF BELL**

I showed officer.

He corrected `high-side` to `cross-shear`.

I corrected.

Good.

No ego.

Packets dry.

We waited.

Half bell.

Wind did not improve.

Black fin stayed showing.

Officer had proper instruments inside shelter.

I did not touch.

Elsa did not touch either, despite weather technician.

Different authority.

Good world.

Kerr ate first lunch.

"That's first?" I asked.

"Road lunch."

"How many?"

"Depends road."

Fair.

Another quarter bell.

A freight driver started arguing.

Not dramatic.

He had salt medicine under priority mark.

Needed Marsh Shelf clinic before afternoon.

Gate officer said closure applied all high-sided freight.

Driver said his wagon was low-sided.

Officer said load stack made it high profile.

Driver said tarps could come off.

Officer said loose medicine crates in crosswind worse.

Correct procedure.

Human cost.

Clinic waiting.

No easy answer.

Then another wagon arrived from north.

Empty.

Low flat bed.

Gate officer looked.

Talked to drivers.

Could medicine crates transfer to low bed and send north driver back with them?

Medicine driver objected to custody transfer.

Priority seal required named carrier.

Gate officer checked rule book.

Could re-seal under emergency carrier substitution with two witnesses and clinic receipt.

That took time.

But moved medicine safely.

Not Greg solution.

I watched paperwork happen.

My manifest changed because road lane reopened only for low-profile authorized traffic under escort.

I copied:

**LIMITED RELEASE / LOW PROFILE ONLY / ESCORT PIN12-P16 / GENERAL COACH HOLD**

Jeren stayed.

Our coach high enough.

Medicine went.

Driver stayed behind with original wagon, furious and relieved at same time.

Good emotion.

Third ring approached.

We had been held more than one bell? Started second less quarter. Now near third. Roughly one and quarter rings maybe. Bell/ring system unclear. Don't calculate exact.

Survey contract delay pay only after first day at staffed gate.

Not relevant.

Jeren said, "If we don't clear by third-and-half, Grey Post before dark gets ugly."

"Return?"

He laughed.

"You were told same-day not guaranteed?"

"Yes."

"Believe them."

I did.

Ten copper floor.

Fifteen cash.

Pay five if delivered.

No pay until Grey Post.

Gate shelter had floor bunks for stranded Survey staff if overnight.

Wouldn't cost maybe? Infrastructure, but don't assume unless happens.

Wind shifted.

Black fin turned amber.

Then black again.

Officer checked inside.

Waited.

Another ten minutes? Don't use minutes if local time system. A short while.

Then amber held.

Officer came out.

"Test coach through Pin Twelve to Fourteen. Survey six-wheel only. No passenger transfer. Driver discretion."

Jeren said, "Mine?"

"Yours."

I disliked phrase test coach.

"Are we testing road?" I asked.

Jeren: "We're testing whether I hate my job today."

"Results?"

"Preliminary."

He checked wheel straps, axle pins, roof ties.

Gate officer asked passengers individually if we wanted remain at gate for next general release.

Elsa stayed aboard. Instrument delivery.

Kerr stayed. Mason job.

Me?

Packets.

Could remain and packets delayed.

Could ride authorized test transit.

Driver accepted.

Gate officer authorized.

This was not reckless improvisation.

I stayed.

"Crutches tied?" Jeren asked.

"Yes."

"Case strap around you, not bench. If coach rolls, bench goes somewhere too."

Comforting.

I changed strap.

We moved.

Pin Twelve.

Wind.

Hard.

Coach leaned.

Not tipping.

Enough body notice.

Jeren slowed to crawl.

Six wheels suddenly made sense viscerally.

Middle axle kept body from rocking as freely.

At Pin Thirteen gust hit.

Coach shifted half lane.

Kerr grabbed overhead rail.

Elsa hugged case.

I braced right foot and stump? Trial leg not on. Left residual limb not weight. Seat bracing with intact right + hip/back. Packet case across chest.

Jeren swore once.

Steered into gust.

Old-life combat brain woke.

Locked-in reserve maybe not needed.

But perception narrowed.

Wheel sound.

Body roll.

Next gust visible in grass? Sparse scrub flattened before hit.

I saw it.

"Left coming," I said.

Jeren already saw.

"I know."

Good.

I shut up.

Driver drives.

Gust hit.

Coach held.

Pin Fourteen shelter appeared.

We stopped behind stone wall.

Jeren exhaled.

"I continue if Fourteen officer clears north side."

Officer checked road.

North of Fourteen dipped behind ridge.

Less exposed.

Cleared to Pin Sixteen at slow transit.

We went.

No monster.

No broken axle.

No Greg heroics.

Still adrenaline.

At Pin Sixteen general road reopened behind us shortly after.

We had gained maybe little.

Mostly proved route.

Grey Post appeared late afternoon.

Not town first.

Towers.

Three thin weather towers on shelf edge, each with rotating glass vanes.

Then stone yard.

Coach sheds.

Low houses behind wall.

Road split:

**BRINEWARD SOUTH**

**HIGH SHELF NORTH**

**QUARRY ROAD WEST**

There.

Bigger map again.

Survey office had level rear entry as promised.

I got off by crutches.

Packets dry.

Seals intact.

Mira's case intact.

Desk clerk checked three seals one by one.

Signed receipt.

"Transit clerk?"

"Temporary."

"Best kind."

"Agreed."

He counted five copper.

Fifteen to twenty.

Good.

Real.

Not miracle.

I handed Survey case back.

Job done.

No field authority acquired by osmosis.

Desk clerk name **Oren Tull**.

He stamped my chit closed.

"Return coach?"

I asked.

"Tomorrow maybe. Today's returns held by shear closure."

"Maybe?"

"Depends road."

Of course.

"Cheap sleep?"

"Road House floor, two copper tonight. Closure crowd. Survey delay bunk only for active employees held under contract. Your delivery closed contract."

Excellent distinction.

No free protagonist roof.

Two copper.

Twenty to eighteen.

Still eight above floor.

Could afford.

I paid Road House.

Twenty to eighteen.

Wait, already said. Yes.

Road House floor was packed.

Freight crews.

Two quarry workers.

Woman with cage of pale birds that all faced windward even indoors.

Magic ecology.

I put pack under wall.

Trial leg still wrapped.

Rested two days since Brineward wear? Ch450 wore, Ch451 travel next day, so one day rest after wear by evening. Could leave.

Stable floor but crowded.

No need.

Crutches fine.

I went outside before dark.

Grey Post shelf edge view enormous.

South: road descending toward invisible sea.

North: land rose again in long gray steps.

High Shelf.

Wind towers clicked softly.

At public board:

**HIGH SHELF COACH / WEATHER PERMIT / 5C / NEXT DEPARTURE EIGHTH DAY**

Not seven from Brineward because Grey Post closer.

**QUARRY ROAD / WEST CUT / WORK WAGONS / PERMIT OR CONTRACT**

**WEATHER YARD DAY HAND / 3C HALF / DRY COPY + FLAG RESET / APPLY FIRST RING**

Flag reset sounded physical maybe poles. Dry copy possible.

Could ask.

Another notice:

**INSTRUMENT COMPARISON HOUR / PUBLIC BENCH / FIRST BELL AFTER DAWN / SMALL TIMERS, PRESSURE TILES, ROAD WITNESSES / 1 BIT BENCH FEE / NO CERTIFICATION**

Oh.

There.

Lot 67.

Legitimate delayed-interval reference opportunity.

Public comparison bench.

Not certification.

Small fee bits.

I read twice.

Tomorrow.

Return coach maybe.

High Shelf next day? Eighth day. What day now? Seventh? Brineward north mainland sixth day Ch450 arrival likely sixth. Ch451 next morning maybe seventh day. High Shelf departure eighth day tomorrow? Board says next eighth. If current seventh, yes next day. Need avoid calendar confusion. State can phrase next scheduled High Shelf coach on eighth day, exact relation likely following day.

Five copper.

Cash eighteen.

Floor ten.

If buy five leaves thirteen.

Then lodging maybe.

Thin.

Again.

Could work half for three.

Could compare Lot 67.

Could return south.

Could go north.

Grey Post had compressed choices into one board.

I liked it.

Bad sign.

I walked to weather-yard fence.

Not inside.

Public side had demonstration clock.

Mechanical pendulum? No.

A drip-wheel interval drum behind glass.

Marks around wheel.

Sign:

**PUBLIC REFERENCE DRUM. CHECKED EACH FIRST RING. COMPARATIVE USE ONLY. NOT LEGAL TIME STANDARD.**

Perfect.

Not tonight.

Could use tomorrow bench under staff procedure.

Lot 67 might finally move from immediate trigger to delayed intervals.

Still not body/magic.

Good.

Wind tower above clicked.

I thought about external attempt 136.

Could use Lot eventually to measure response delay maybe.

Not yet.

Characterize tool first.

Old me would have loved this place.

Current me also did.

Annoying continuity.

At Road House I ate stew with bits.

Kerr Holt appeared.

He had found mason bunk elsewhere then came for stew.

"You staying?" he asked.

"Tonight."

"High Shelf?"

"Maybe."

"Cold."

"Useful."

"That's not opposite of cold."

"I know."

He ate.

No invitation.

No party.

Good.

Elsa had gone weather office.

Jeren coach yard.

Temporary road people dispersed into functions.

I wrote home note.

**Grey Post. Shelf Road. Survey packets delivered, earned 5c. Cash 18c, Vale 23c. Wind closure delayed road but we got through under gate clearance. Nobody hurt. Ten floor intact. High Shelf exists north. Not committed. - Greg**

Then:

**There is a public instrument comparison bench tomorrow. This is unfortunately exciting.**

Postage bits.

Communication delay now ridiculous.

Cala might receive Dorrin note while I was already here.

Tavi might think I was on island when I was mainland shelf.

Home knew versions of me delayed by roads.

That felt stranger than distance.

At floor bunk, I checked money.

Eighteen.

Ten floor.

Eight discretionary.

High Shelf five.

Comparison bench bits.

Potential half-shift three.

Return unknown.

Limb money far away conceptually and literally.

Still mine.

Still waiting.

"Sorry," I whispered.

Man beside me said, "What?"

"Talking to leg."

He moved blanket slightly farther away.

Reasonable.

Courier.

That title actually fit more than recent ones.

Nearly got blown sideways in process.

Damn her.

# CHAPTER 452

## THE MEASURER

Instrument Comparison Hour had rules.

Good.

Rules meant someone had already made expensive mistakes before me.

Grey Post weather yard opened the public bench after dawn.

One bit.

I paid.

Bench clerk stamped my hand card.

"No certification."

"Understood."

"No legal claims from bench result."

"Understood."

"No opening instruments on the table."

"Mine already opens."

"Then don't."

"Understood."

Her name was **Vera Halm**.

Gray hair.

Ink on three fingers.

Voice like every extra noun cost money.

I liked her immediately.

Public bench held one drip-wheel interval drum behind glass, two mechanical reference flags, one pressure-tile cradle, one reset basin, and a chalk board with comparison procedure.

Not laboratory.

Not certification office.

Enough.

Other users included a road guard with two wind witnesses, a young artificer testing a pocket timer, and a farmer with a pressure tile that apparently blamed his wagon for something it could not explain.

Good crowd.

I set Lot 67 down.

Vera inspected exterior only.

"What are you testing?"

"Delayed interval repeatability."

"Against?"

I pointed to drip drum.

"Good. What are you not testing?"

"Seconds. Absolute accuracy. Magic response. Body response. Anything legal."

She nodded.

Passed first test.

Drip-wheel drum worked simple enough.

Reservoir released fixed drops into a weighted cup.

Cup tipped wheel one notch after accumulation.

Each marked interval repeated by mechanism.

Bench sign said it was checked each first ring against yard reference.

Comparative only.

Perfect.

Lot 67 had flexible tongue trigger and bead track.

Manual immediate trigger already sixty clean after repair.

Now I needed repeated delay.

Vera showed bench attachment.

Small clamp arm could release Lot tongue when drip wheel reached chosen notch.

That removed my hand from delayed trigger.

Better.

"Don't infer duration from bead mark," she said.

"I won't."

"Everyone says that."

"I've been told that about loose rope coils too."

She ignored me.

Procedure:

reset Lot.

Set drum to reference interval A.

Clamp tongue under release arm.

Start.

Drum reaches notch.

Arm releases tongue.

Read bead.

Ten repeats.

Then interval B.

Then maybe C if queue allowed.

I started A.

Run one: bead near mark two.

Two: mark two.

Three: just below two.

Four: two.

Five: two.

Six: slightly above two.

Seven: two.

Eight: below two.

Nine: two.

Ten: two.

No fourth-mark hang.

Good.

Not exact.

Consistent cluster.

I logged range.

Vera checked notebook.

"Don't average marks like linear scale."

"Wasn't going to."

"Good."

I absolutely had been about to think about average.

Saved.

Interval B.

Longer drum setting.

Run one: near three.

Two: three.

Three: slightly below three.

Four: three.

Five: three.

Six: three.

Seven: just above three.

Eight: three.

Nine: three.

Ten: below three.

Again cluster.

No sticky four.

Interesting.

Not certification.

Still interesting.

Queue behind me grew.

I asked Vera if third interval okay.

She looked.

"Five runs."

Fair.

Interval C longer again.

One: between three and four.

Two: similar.

Three: nearer four but below.

My stomach tightened.

Four: similar to one.

Five: similar.

No false snap.

No sticky pause visible.

Good.

I wrote:

**Grey Post public comparison bench. No certification. External drip-wheel release, not hand-triggered delay. Interval A x10 clusters around mark 2. Interval B x10 clusters around mark 3. Interval C x5 clusters between 3 and 4. No mark-four hang in 25 delayed trials. Combined post-service immediate 60 clean + delayed 25 clean. Scale non-linear/uncalibrated. Do not infer seconds.**

Vera read.

"Good."

Praise from Vera worth maybe half copper.

Not real half copper.

Don't invent conversion.

"Can I say it distinguishes longer repeated interval from shorter under this setup?"

She thought.

"Under this setup, today, yes."

"Not absolute."

"Correct."

"Not certified."

"Correct."

"Not body or magic."

She looked at me.

"Why would you use this on body?"

"Long story."

"Don't."

"That's current plan."

Good.

Lot 67 had upgraded from five-copper liar to five-copper thing that might actually do comparative delay.

Excellent.

Artifact win.

No fireworks.

Bench fee bits only.

Cash still eighteen.

I packed tool.

Grey Post morning board.

High Shelf coach still five copper.

Departure eighth day, second ring.

Weather check first.

Berths six.

Cash eighteen.

Ticket five leaves thirteen.

Ten floor.

Three discretionary after purchase.

Need lodging at High Shelf unknown.

Too thin.

Could break floor only with work plan.

Need work.

Weather Yard board still had half-shift three copper.

**DRY COPY + FLAG RESET**.

I asked clerk.

"Flag reset means climbing?"

"Some."

"Then no."

"Dry copy alone pays two."

There.

"What copy?"

"Yesterday closure sheets into route ledger."

Very similar to ledger work.

Useful.

Boring.

I looked farther down board.

**COACH YARD / LOAD STRAP CHECK / 2C / SEATED POSSIBLE**

Different enough.

"What's seated possible?" I asked yard master.

"Inspect removed straps. Mark fray, buckle damage, stretch. Don't climb coaches."

Good.

Two copper.

I took it.

Yard master **Sena Brakk**.

Big woman.

Long braid.

Talked enough for three people.

Excellent.

"Listen, they're going to hand you straps and every driver swears theirs is fine because if you mark bad they have to go requisition another and requisition window is all the way across yard, which is stupid, yes, but don't let their laziness become your eyesight. Red chalk if fibers cut, white if buckle bent, blue if stretched past gauge. If two problems, mark both. If unsure, put question knot here and I'll look. Do not invent fourth chalk because you feel artistic."

"I won't."

"Good. Last boy made green category. There is no green category."

"What did green mean?"

"He forgot."

Amazing.

I sat at dry yard table.

Straps came.

Simple work.

Not trivial.

First five fine.

Sixth had buckle tooth bent inward.

White.

Seventh frayed edge but not load fibers.

Question knot.

Sena looked.

"Surface. Pass."

Good.

Ninth visibly stretched.

Blue.

Driver argued.

Sena gave him requisition-window directions with enough detail to become a lecture.

He left.

Twelfth had cut fibers plus buckle bend.

Red and white.

No green.

I worked.

At midpoint coach arrived from High Shelf.

Mud gray.

One wheel rim chipped.

Passengers stiff.

Driver climbed down and said north road had frost pockets above Stone Mouth.

Frost.

New environment.

Grey Post cool.

High Shelf cold.

I listened.

Sena asked road status.

"Open. Slow after marker twenty-two. Quarry convoy coming down tomorrow."

High Shelf coach tomorrow still plausible.

Then strap pile included one strange silver-thread webbing.

I touched only because it had been handed to me.

Fibers shimmered.

"Charged?" I asked.

Sena said, "Memory weave."

"What remembers?"

"Stretch direction. Pull too hard one way, silver threads stay dark there until reset steam. We use it on instrument crates, not people."

Artifact-adjacent ordinary infrastructure.

I inspected.

One side had dark silver band.

Blue stretch.

Sena checked.

"Correct."

"Can it quantify?"

"No. Tells you where abuse happened, not how much unless you know weave lot."

Everything here loved directional residue.

Interesting regional technology family.

Residual Field Witness back Dorrin suddenly made more cultural sense.

Not isolated magical gadget.

A whole measurement tradition around preserving peak direction after event.

Good.

No purchase.

Half shift ended.

Two copper.

Eighteen to twenty.

Better.

Now High Shelf five leaves fifteen.

Likely lodging still leaves me above floor.

Thin but workable.

I walked board.

Berths four.

Two sold since morning.

Decision.

Return south remained possible.

High Shelf voluntary.

No one asked.

No job waiting.

Why go?

Because Shelf Road kept going.

Because quarry resonance stones.

Because weather yard.

Because old me wanted strange places.

Because current me had built enough cash through work to buy one more step without violating floor.

Because if I didn't, I could sit at Grey Post convincing myself prudence was destination.

Could still turn later.

I bought High Shelf ticket.

Five copper.

Twenty to fifteen.

Again fifteen.

My favorite miserable number.

Departure second ring tomorrow, weather permitting.

Ten floor intact.

Five discretionary.

Road House tonight still two copper if closure crowd persisted.

That would leave thirteen.

Still above floor.

I needed sleep.

Could cheaper worker loft exist?

Asked.

Survey clerk said weather crowd clearing, old coach shed floor one copper after night bell if no freight team took it.

No guarantee.

I waited.

Risk one-copper savings.

Not dramatic.

Night bell.

Coach shed had space.

One copper.

Fifteen to fourteen.

Good.

Four above floor.

Food bits.

Trial limb had rested since Brineward.

Coach shed floor dry.

Task: repack for colder road.

Actual reason.

I put trial limb on.

Medium foot.

Temporary ash shaft.

Still shop's.

Still not mine.

Cold-weather packing meant both hands useful.

I laid shirt, coat, wrap, notebook pouch.

Standing.

Crutches against bench.

First ten minutes easy.

Foot placement quiet.

Twelve.

Fifteen.

I carried folded coat seven steps to pack.

Returned eight with empty hands.

No issue.

At nineteen, one turn around crate happened without active foot-placement checklist.

I noticed after.

Funny.

Progress noticed by missing thought.

At twenty-three, hip fatigue.

Twenty-six, mild heat.

I wanted thirty.

Because round number.

Bad reason.

Stopped at twenty-seven.

Crutches.

Removed limb.

Skin check.

Upper pink mild.

Lower-front faint line.

At twenty minutes lower gone, upper mostly gone.

At thirty upper gone.

No pain.

Logged:

**Grey Post coach shed. 27 min dry board. Cold-road repack. Coat carry 7 steps; empty return 8. One clean crate turn around ~19 without active foot-placement checklist. Hip fatigue 23. Mild heat 26. Stopped 27. Lower clear 20; upper mostly clear 20, clear 30.**

Not farther than 28.

Better in another way.

Good.

No deliberate magic.

Tempting after tool bench.

Very tempting.

Lot 67 could distinguish delayed intervals under external release now.

That did not mean I should point it at magic the same day.

Need separate characterization.

Maybe repeat bench another day.

Maybe external physical trigger first.

Training brain wanted pipeline.

Road brain wanted sleep.

Road won.

Before bed I checked ticket.

**GREY POST -> HIGH SHELF / 5C / EIGHTH DAY SECOND RING / WEATHER PERMIT**

Cash fourteen.

Vale twenty-three.

Ten floor.

Four discretionary.

High Shelf lodging unknown.

Still not calibrated in seconds and not using it on magic/body yet. Earned 2c checking coach straps. Bought High Shelf ticket 5c. Cash 14c, Vale 23c. Ten floor intact. Trial build 27 min dry, fine. - Greg**

Then:

**I am apparently going somewhere colder on purpose. Please judge quietly.**

Postage bits.

High Shelf tomorrow.

# CHAPTER 453

## THE CLIMBER

High Shelf coach left on time.

I distrusted it immediately.

Grey Post had spent enough time teaching me weather possessed administrative authority.

But eighth-day second ring arrived cold, clear, and rude.

Coach board still said:

**HIGH SHELF / OPEN / FROST POCKETS ABOVE STONE MOUTH / SLOW M22-M31**

Paid ticket.

Fourteen copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

Ten-copper don't-be-stupid floor.

Four copper currently available for stupidity.

Not much adventure budget.

I boarded by lower step on crutches.

Trial leg wrapped.

Coach smaller than Survey six-wheel.

Four wheels, low roof, iron drag shoe mounted under rear.

Driver was woman named **Rell Tann**.

No relation questions.

I had matured.

Maybe.

"Pack inside?" she asked.

"Yes."

"Anything breakable?"

"One timing demonstrator. One borrowed leg. My dignity."

"Last one's not insured."

Good.

Passengers:

older quarry cutter with wrapped hands;

young man carrying two cages of gray moths;

woman with rolled maps;

me.

No party.

We left Grey Post north.

Road climbed.

Not metaphorically.

Actual sustained grade.

Shelf country rose in steps, each broad enough road and scrub, then another dark stone lip above.

Sea disappeared behind lower ridges within first bell.

That felt important.

First time since coast expansion I couldn't find water by turning head.

Land had swallowed horizon.

Cold came next.

Not snow.

Thin frost in shaded cracks.

Scrub silver at edges.

Breath visible when coach slowed.

Moth cages clicked.

"What are those?" I asked young man.

"Dust moths."

"Quarry thing?"

"Crack thing."

He elaborated after deciding I wasn't mocking.

Dust moths hated certain charged stone dust. Release one in sealed test hood with sample; wing pattern changed if dust carried active resonance.

"Precise?"

"No."

Excellent region.

"Useful?"

"Enough to not breathe bad dust."

Better.

Name **Toma Senn**.

He was delivering moths to High Shelf cutting sheds.

At marker nineteen road narrowed between black walls.

Stone Mouth.

Literal enough.

Rell stopped before shaded bend.

Got down.

Checked road with boot and short hooked tool.

Frost.

Not much.

Enough.

She fitted drag shoe lower.

Quarry cutter said, "Could walk faster."

Rell said, "Do."

He did not.

We continued slow.

At marker twenty-two frost became patches across full road.

Coach wheels had iron rims.

Bad combination.

Rell kept one side on rough gravel shoulder where possible.

No one chatted.

At marker twenty-five, uphill freight cart ahead stopped.

Two draft animals steamed.

Load covered with gray canvas.

Road too narrow to pass safely.

Rell stopped well back.

Driver ahead waved.

Something wrong with rear wheel.

Not broken.

Chocked.

Cart had slid backward half a wagon length on frost and driver had jammed wheel chocks.

Now couldn't restart without risking another slide.

High Shelf road problem.

We waited.

Rell walked up.

Quarry cutter followed because apparently knew freight driver.

I stayed.

Driver owned movement.

Good lesson retained.

Map woman got out and stretched.

Toma checked moth cages.

Five minutes? Don't use local minutes. Short while.

Rell returned.

"They're unloading two blocks."

"To where?" map woman asked.

"Shoulder. Then cart takes grade lighter. Yard wagon comes back for blocks."

"How long?"

"Longer if everybody asks."

Fair.

Workers unlashed canvas.

Under it were two pale stone blocks with black seams.

Each maybe person-sized but flatter.

As first block shifted, air made low tone.

Not block itself exactly.

My teeth felt it.

Tiny vibration.

Toma's moths all turned same direction in cages.

Interesting.

"Resonance stone?" I asked.

He nodded.

"Low grade."

"That is low?"

"You can stand near it."

Excellent scale.

Workers used screw jack and rollers to lower first block onto stone shoulder.

Second block caught.

One roller skated on frost.

Block tilted.

Everybody moved fast.

Not panic.

Training.

Quarry cutter shoved chock under lower corner with long bar.

Rell grabbed rope already tied uphill.

Freight driver yelled clear.

Block settled against chock.

No injury.

I had stood up instinctively.

Crutches under me.

Then stopped.

What exactly would I contribute?

Nothing useful in icy resonance-block rigging.

Sat back down.

Growth again.

Old me could have done more physically.

Current me knew that didn't grant certification.

Map woman said, "You looked ready."

"I was preparing to become obstacle."

She laughed.

Workers reset roller with sand and rough mat.

Second attempt clean.

Cart unloaded.

Moved uphill.

Our coach followed after road cleared.

Two blocks stayed shoulder under red marker flags.

Temporary problem waiting for yard wagon.

No Greg solution.

At marker twenty-nine, terrain opened.

High Shelf appeared.

Not one town.

A cut valley in gray rock with buildings stacked along terraces.

Quarry faces west.

Smoke low because wind tore it sideways.

Tall wooden frames crossed some cuts, hung with bronze bells and glass strips.

Stone carts moved on narrow rail grooves.

I stared.

Not trains.

Probably gravity haul lines or guided carts.

Ask later.

At entry gate, sign:

**HIGH SHELF CUTTING DISTRICT**

**PUBLIC ROAD / LOWER TERRACE**

**ACTIVE CUTS / PERMIT**

**RESONANCE HOURS POSTED DAILY**

**HEARING WRAPS REQUIRED BEYOND BLUE LINE**

Good.

Town had rules because stone could apparently hum your teeth.

Coach stopped lower terrace.

I got off.

Dry stone but uneven.

Crutches.

Trial leg stayed wrapped.

First concern money.

Lodging board.

**CUTTER HOUSE FLOOR 2C**

**OLD SCALE SHED BUNK 1C / NO HEAT AFTER SECOND BELL**

One copper.

Excellent.

I asked whether available.

Three spaces.

Paid.

Fourteen to thirteen.

Three above floor.

Very thin.

I put pack at Old Scale Shed.

It was exactly what name promised.

Long stone room behind retired weighing platform.

Six bunks.

One iron stove that apparently became decorative after second bell.

Floor dry.

Good.

Work board next.

I needed money before anything else.

Listings:

**CUT THREE / SPOIL CART / 5C / STRONG BACK**

No.

**LOWER SCALE / TICKET SORT / 2C HALF / SEATED**

Possible.

But another paper sort.

**MOTH HOUSE / CAGE WASH / 2C / WATER + BRUSH / SEATED BENCH**

Different.

Wet but indoor.

Could do on crutches seated.

**RESONANCE BELL WALK / 4C / BLUE LINE / HEARING WRAPS / UNEVEN CUT ROAD**

No.

Not today.

I took moth-house tab.

Toma Senn was there.

Of course.

Not because destiny.

Because he delivered moths to moth house.

He looked at tab.

"You followed me."

"I followed two copper."

"More believable."

Supervisor **Della Orr** showed work.

Cages were palm-sized wooden frames with fine wire mesh and removable clay trays.

Dust moths moved to clean cages.

Dirty empty cages washed with warm water and stiff brush.

No charged solvent.

No touching moths.

No opening occupied cages.

"Can sit?" I asked.

"That's why bench."

Good.

"Leg?"

"What about it?"

"I have trial work leg. Dry floor. Could use for standing transfers if okay."

She looked at floor.

"Don't kick cages."

"Strong policy."

I could wear.

Rested since Grey Post previous day? Ch452 wore 27 min, Ch453 next day travel. Not full rest day. Road sheet allowed non-consecutive wear, so consecutive days not ideal. Original max morning/evening non-consecutive means wear sessions same day non-consecutive? But progression has rested. Better don't wear. Preserve recovery.

"Actually no," I said. "Crutches."

Della shrugged.

Work.

I sat.

Washed cages.

Simple.

But moth house itself interesting.

Occupied cages lined wall by stone sample tags.

Some moths gray.

Some wings developed dark spots.

One cage moths pressed flat against mesh away from sample tray.

"That bad?" I asked.

Della looked.

"Active dust."

"Meaning?"

"Meaning cutter shouldn't breathe it until washed or bound."

"Bound?"

"Clay mist. Makes dust heavy and quiet."

Magic occupational safety.

Nice.

A bell sounded outside.

Low.

Then second bell different pitch.

Workers in room paused.

Della counted.

Third bell.

She said, "Cut Six starting resonance cycle."

Everyone resumed.

"Posted hours?"

"They ring before charged cutting. Blue line closes during cycle."

"How long?"

"Depends seam."

"Timing fixed?"

"Start fixed. End when bell reader clears."

Interesting.

Not Lot 67 today.

Actual system.

At midpoint? Don't know. Later.

Man rushed in carrying sealed sample tin.

"Six east face."

Della took.

"Why here?"

"Moths went wall-side."

Della's face changed.

Not panic.

Attention.

She put tin into empty test hood, transferred three moths with sleeve tool.

Moths immediately climbed upward and pressed against far mesh.

"Shit," Toma said.

Della rang small handbell twice.

Outside big resonance bells stopped.

Cycle aborted.

That fast.

Workers knew signal.

Della wrote tag.

"East face dust active above expected. Nobody resumes until wash crew and reader clear."

Man said, "Foreman says seam map had it low."

"Moths don't read maps."

Good line.

"Could be sample contamination."

"Then second sample will tell us."

Procedure.

No Greg involvement.

I washed cage.

Then Toma said, "You like instruments."

"Unfortunately."

He nodded at my pack.

"You have one?"

"Timing demonstrator."

"Can it time resonance?"

"No."

Good immediate answer.

"Why?"

"Because I have not established what its marks mean in this environment, it isn't certified, and timing cycle length doesn't tell dust safety anyway."

Della looked over.

"You can stay."

High praise.

Second sample arrived.

Same moth response.

Cut Six remained closed.

Workers outside grumbled because closure cost production.

Nobody argued with moths exactly.

One foreman did come in.

Big man, red ears.

"We lose half shift if wash crew takes east face."

Della said, "Then lose half shift."

"Map says low."

"Two fresh samples say active."

"Moths can react to iron binder."

"These are dust moths from new batch. Iron control passed this morning."

Foreman looked at Toma.

Toma nodded.

"Passed."

Foreman hated facts.

Not because stupid.

Because facts cost him.

"Can we cut west side?"

Della thought.

"If reader clears separate airflow and blue line stays east. Ask yard. Not me."

He left.

Again system not clean but functional.

Work resumed around interruption.

I finished cages.

Two copper.

Thirteen to fifteen.

Five above floor again.

Good.

Della signed tab.

"Tomorrow if you want another half, maybe."

"Maybe."

No automatic job.

I left moth house near late afternoon.

High Shelf cold sharpened.

Public lower terrace had market smaller than Dorrin.

Tools.

Wool wraps.

Stone samples.

Hearing cloth.

Food.

One stall sold retired quarry indicators.

I did not approach.

Growth.

Maybe cowardice.

Cash fifteen.

Ten floor.

Five discretionary.

Do not shop artifacts in mining town with five copper.

Strong rule.

I bought hot food with bits.

Then looked at route board.

This mattered.

Beyond High Shelf:

**NORTH CUT ROAD / MERE RIDGE / 3C / CARTS WHEN CLEAR**

**WEST QUARRY / CONTRACT ONLY**

**OLD GLASS PASS / CLOSED AFTER FIRST FROST**

Mere Ridge.

Three copper.

Another door.

Cheaper than expected.

But lodging there unknown.

Return High Shelf to Grey Post five? Board south:

**GREY POST / 5C / TENTH DAY**

Not daily.

I had fifteen.

Could buy Mere Ridge three, leave twelve.

Then one cheap bunk maybe eleven.

One above floor.

Stupid.

Could work another half tomorrow first.

Maybe earn two.

Then fourteen after fare.

Still thin.

Road kept offering arithmetic.

I missed when adventure was monsters.

Actually no.

Monsters expensive too.

At Old Scale Shed, stove warm before second bell.

I unpacked.

Trial leg stayed wrapped.

One full rest night after prior wear.

Tomorrow maybe.

Lot 67 stayed packed.

No reason.

Magic?

Dry room.

People around.

No.

Could find safe outdoor corner, but cold, unfamiliar, no need.

No forced attempt.

I wrote home.

**High Shelf. Colder. Quarry district. Cash 15c, Vale 23c. Earned 2c washing dust-moth cages. They use moths to flag active resonance dust before cutting. One cut shut down today after two samples reacted. Trial build stayed wrapped. Ten floor intact. - Greg**

Then:

**There is apparently a place called Mere Ridge farther north for 3c. I have not bought ticket. This sentence is for accountability.**

Postage bits.

I imagined Cala reading that days from now.

Maybe saying, "Then don't."

Tavi asking what dust moth looked like.

Lyssa circling `not bought` like contract clause.

Good.

At bunk, quarry cutter from coach was two beds over.

He recognized me.

"You made it."

"Coach did most."

"You going north?"

"Maybe."

"Mere Ridge?"

"Maybe."

He scratched wrapped hands.

"Nothing there."

Dangerous phrase.

"What's nothing?"

"Sheep. Stone. Ridge weather. Old signal house. Couple inns. North road splits after."

"Splits where?"

He pointed vaguely north as if geography lived in ceiling.

"One road goes Crown Vale. Other bends east toward lake country."

Vale.

Of course.

Could be place, not surname.

Still annoying.

"Crown what?"

"Crown Vale. Valley. Not person."

He had seen my face.

"I know."

"You looked offended."

"Personal history with noun."

He decided not ask.

Good.

Lake country.

That was new.

Not coast.

Not quarry.

Actual inland.

I lay down.

High Shelf wind pushed at shutters.

Climber.

I had climbed mostly sitting in coach.

Technically dishonest.

But road had gone up enough my ears had popped once.

Counts.

Tomorrow money first.

Then maybe north.

Maybe not.

I had fifteen copper and a ten-copper floor.

Adventure had five copper left.

Adventure needed a job.

# CHAPTER 454

## THE EARNER

Adventure needed a job.

High Shelf had jobs.

Most of them wanted two good legs, strong lungs, hearing wraps, quarry certification, or a personality capable of waking before dawn cheerful.

I had one of those.

Barely.

Work board first ring:

**CUT THREE / SPOIL CART / 5C / STRONG BACK**

Still no.

**LOWER SCALE / LOAD TICKET CHECK / 2C HALF / SEATED**

Possible.

Boring.

**BELL STORE / RESONANCE FLAG FOLD + CASE PACK / 3C HALF / DRY BENCH / CAREFUL HANDS**

Better.

**MOTH HOUSE / CAGE WASH / 2C**

Already did.

No becoming local moth janitor.

I took Bell Store tab.

Cash fifteen.

Ten floor.

Three-copper Mere Ridge fare.

If I earned three, then bought fare, eighteen to fifteen.

Could still pay one-copper bunk and sit fourteen.

Four above floor.

Not luxurious.

Less stupid.

Good enough.

Bell Store sat behind lower cutting terrace, outside blue line.

Long stone room.

Shelves of cloth flags, bronze clappers, glass strips, padded cases, hearing wraps, and things I did not touch because labels included words like **phase** and **return-tone**.

Supervisor was **Pell Ardin**.

Pell again.

I refused to react.

Growth.

He had enormous eyebrows and spoke like every sentence had been revised twice before leaving mouth.

"Your task is not technical," he said. "I am stating that first because the room looks technical and people become ambitious when surrounded by nouns."

"I understand deeply."

"Good."

He pointed bench.

"Flags come back from cut lines. You check for tears, stiffness, obvious glaze contamination. Fold by color stripe. Pack in numbered cases. Anything questionable gets question tag. You do not decide whether contamination is active. You do not scrape glass dust off with fingernail. Someone did that."

"Still have finger?"

"Yes."

"Disappointing lesson design."

Pell blinked.

Then continued.

"Cases are light. Some require two-handed fold because signal stripe must not crease across conductive thread. Can you stand at bench?"

"For a while. I have a trial work leg. Dry floor?"

"Dry unless roof fails."

I looked up.

"Does roof fail often?"

"No. But I dislike guarantees."

Good man.

Trial limb had rested a full day.

Stable stone floor.

Useful two-handed task.

Crutches within reach.

Yes.

I fitted liner.

Temporary ash shaft.

Medium foot.

Still shop property.

Still not mine.

Still dramatically useful.

Standing with both hands free while folding signal cloth was exactly why I wanted the damn thing.

First ten minutes easy.

Flags mostly wool-like cloth with thin metallic threads along one edge.

White stripe.

Amber.

Blue.

Black.

Some smelled faintly metallic.

One had clear gray dust embedded along hem.

Question tag.

Pell checked.

"Correct. Cut Two glass residue. Wash crew."

"Dangerous?"

"Unknown until washed/tested. Therefore not yours to classify."

Beautiful.

I folded.

Packed.

Carried one empty case four steps to shelf.

Returned four.

No issue.

At about fifteen minutes, foot placement had gone quiet again.

Not absent.

Quiet.

Body knew enough that brain could pay attention to stripe alignment instead of narrating ankle-that-wasn't-there.

That was weird.

Good weird.

One flag had black stripe but case label said amber set.

I almost assumed worker mixed pile.

Stopped.

Tag said **B3 BLACK / WEST CUT**.

I asked.

Pell came.

"Good catch. Wrong return bin, not wrong flag."

He moved entire pile.

"If you'd silently corrected one flag, we'd have found rest later and wondered why one wandered."

"Systems hate helpful people."

"Systems hate undocumented helpful people."

Better.

At twenty minutes hip began reminding me arrangement was temporary.

Not pain.

Fatigue.

I shifted weight.

Kept folding.

At twenty-four, mild heat.

I finished current flag.

Sat.

No chasing twenty-eight because last time twenty-seven.

Today task different.

Cold outside.

Stone floor harder.

Enough.

Twenty-four minutes.

Crutches back.

Pell noticed.

"Done standing?"

"Yes."

"Good. Bench works seated."

No inspirational speech.

Perfect.

I logged later:

**High Shelf Bell Store. 24 min dry stone. Flag fold/case pack. Empty case carry 4 steps out + 4 return. Foot placement quiet by ~15. Hip fatigue 20. Mild heat 24. Stopped. Skin check pending.**

Work continued seated.

Then bell outside rang four short.

Pell froze.

Not alarm exactly.

Inventory attention.

Second sequence: two long.

He walked door.

"What is that?" I asked.

"Cut Four lost return tone."

"Meaning?"

"Means signal loop heard outbound bell and no confirmed return. Could be broken line, bad clapper, wind, worker missed pull, or actual cut condition."

"And?"

"And nothing until bell crew tells us."

Good.

He pulled two spare flag cases from shelf.

Worker arrived running.

"Need yellow return set and two hearing wraps."

Pell handed cases.

"Line break?"

"Unknown. West post not answering."

"Take question kit too."

Worker left.

I watched.

"Do lost return tones stop cutting?"

"Depends line. Cut Four is shared-face warning. So yes, until they know whether warning failed or condition changed."

Production interrupted again.

Different mechanism.

High Shelf built redundancy because rock could apparently lie in several dialects.

Pell sighed.

"Yesterday Six. Today Four. Cold wakes old faults."

"Mechanical?"

"Sometimes. Sometimes charge thread contracts. Sometimes moisture freezes in housings. Sometimes people tie knots like criminals."

"Technical category?"

"Very."

He returned bench.

No investigation Greg.

I folded cloth.

Half shift ended.

Three copper.

Fifteen to eighteen.

Good.

Skin check in washroom.

Lower-front faint line.

Upper pink.

At twenty minutes, lower gone, upper mostly gone.

At thirty, upper gone.

No pain.

Fine.

I added:

**Lower-front clear 20. Upper mostly clear 20, clear 30. No pain.**

That was enough limb today.

Now decision.

Mere Ridge cart board.

**NORTH CUT ROAD / MERE RIDGE / THIRD RING / 3C / OPEN / FROST SHADE ABOVE M6**

Third ring.

Today.

Could go.

Cash eighteen.

Three fare leaves fifteen.

Likely bunk one or two leaves thirteen-fourteen.

Floor intact.

I bought ticket.

Eighteen to fifteen.

There.

Again.

No prophecy.

No job contract.

Just enough margin after work.

I collected pack from Old Scale Shed.

No second bunk payment.

Checked stove ash.

Left.

At coach yard, Mere Ridge vehicle wasn't coach.

Covered cart with narrow benches and high wheels.

Driver **Iven Ro**.

Ro.

Fine.

Three other passengers.

Shepherd woman with folded crook.

Boy carrying sack of wool combs.

Man with two sealed clay jars strapped chest-to-back like unfortunate armor.

I asked lower step.

Iven pointed small rear ladder.

Not ideal.

Then side loading board.

"Use freight board."

Wider.

Less steep.

Good.

Trial limb wrapped again after morning wear.

Crutches primary.

Boarded seated transfer with driver holding pack after asking.

North Cut Road narrower than Shelf Road.

Less infrastructure.

Stone markers farther apart.

Quarry noise faded behind us.

That mattered.

High Shelf had been industry loud.

North road became wind and sheep bells.

Actual sheep.

White-gray animals on slopes with dark horns curling close to skull.

Some had little glass beads braided into neck wool.

"Decoration?" I asked shepherd woman.

"Storm beads."

"Warning?"

"Sort of. They click when fleece charge gets high."

"Sheep become weather instruments."

She looked offended.

"Sheep become sheep that don't surprise shepherd."

Better.

Her name **Hessa Pell**.

Pell.

I refused universe reaction.

Storm beads clicked softly when wool rubbed.

Road climbed less now.

Mostly followed ridge contour.

Frost shade at marker six was real.

Iven stopped once to scatter grit from bucket on inside turn.

No drama.

At marker nine, road split around shallow sink.

Left marked freight.

Right marked light cart.

Iven chose right.

Shepherd said, "Freight side broke?"

"Soft shoulder."

"Since when?"

"This morning."

"Will they fix?"

Iven shrugged.

"After somebody owns it."

Infrastructure politics universal.

We continued.

Mere Ridge appeared near evening as line of stone houses hugging broad saddle between hills.

Not impressive.

Exactly as quarry cutter promised.

Sheep.

Stone.

Wind.

Old signal house on highest knob, squat round tower with three empty-looking arms sticking roof.

Two inns.

One smithy.

One store.

Small road office.

That was town.

I liked it immediately.

Dangerous.

Cart stopped at **Ridge Post**.

Route board:

**SOUTH / HIGH SHELF / NEXT CART ELEVENTH DAY / 3C**

**NORTHWEST / CROWN VALE / 4C / ROAD OPEN**

**EAST / LARKEN LAKE / 5C / WEATHER HOLD POSSIBLE / DEPART TENTH DAY**

Larken Lake.

There.

Lake country got name.

Five copper.

Too much now without work.

Cash fifteen.

Ten floor.

Five discretionary.

Buying lake fare would put exactly ten before lodging.

No.

Crown Vale four leaves eleven before lodging.

Also no.

Money first again.

Inn prices.

Wind Lamb floor 2c.

Ridge Cot shared loft 1c.

Ridge Cot.

One copper.

Fifteen to fourteen.

Four above floor.

Again.

I was becoming very good at arriving places with fourteen copper.

Maybe business model.

Shared loft warm enough.

Low ceiling.

Smelled wool and onions.

I put pack down.

No limb again.

Already wore today.

Work board downstairs tiny.

**WOOL COUNT / MORNING / 2C**

**SIGNAL HOUSE CLEAN / HALF DAY / 3C / STAIRS**

No.

**ROAD OFFICE COPY / WEATHER RETURNS / 2C HALF / WRITING**

Paper again.

**SHEARING SHED TOOL TAGS / 2C / SEATED**

Maybe tomorrow.

Then separate handwritten card:

**OLD SIGNAL HOUSE: ONE-BIT PUBLIC VIEW / SECOND BELL / WEATHER PERMIT**

One bit.

Old signal house.

Artifact-ish infrastructure.

Cheap.

Could see.

Not buy.

Fine.

I ate stew with bits.

Hessa Pell from cart was there too, arguing with innkeeper about whether storm beads counted as livestock equipment for storage fee.

They did.

She lost.

No friendship.

At table beside me, old man complained lake road had been holding carts because east mist froze on marker glass.

Someone else said Crown Vale lower road warmer.

Route choice already forming without me asking.

I wrote home note.

**Mere Ridge. Earned 3c folding/packing quarry signal flags, then paid 3c north cart. Cash 14c, Vale 23c. Trial build 24 min dry work use, fine. Ten floor intact. Roads now split toward Crown Vale or Larken Lake. No ticket yet. - Greg**

Then:

**Adventure remains mostly accounting with scenery.**

Postage bits.

After dinner I went outside.

Cold but less industrial than High Shelf.

Stars clear.

Signal house on ridge dark.

Three roof arms pointed different directions.

No lights.

Retired maybe.

Wind moved sheep bells below.

I found sheltered wall behind inn.

Dry stone.

No one nearby.

Magic.

Maybe.

Long rest since external 136.

Safe bounded condition.

No Lot 67.

No timing obsession.

Simple external movement.

I placed small dry wool tuft on flat stone.

Light.

Not dangerous.

One pace.

Attempt 137.

Breathe.

Old pathway.

Current body.

Push.

Wool trembled.

Could be wind.

Sheltered, but not zero.

Ambiguous.

Discard.

Second attempt would make 138 if done.

Did I need?

Maybe one controlled comparison.

I put second tuft under deeper corner where air stiller.

Same distance.

Attempt 138.

Push.

Tuft shifted visibly maybe one finger-width.

No gust felt.

Clean enough to log bounded.

Not breakthrough.

Not rank.

Just another external response.

I stopped.

No third.

Protocol now 59 / 53 / 138.

I wrote:

**Mere Ridge inn wall. External 137 wool tuft ambiguous, possible residual air. Discard. External 138 deeper shelter, ~1 pace, visible ~1 finger shift, no felt gust. Bounded positive. Stop.**

Good.

Magic had not disappeared.

Also had not become destiny.

Back inside.

Fourteen copper.

Ten floor.

Four discretionary.

Lake fare five.

Crown Vale four.

One exact edge.

Tomorrow needed job again if lake.

Could choose Crown Vale now if wanted and still hit floor after fare, but not lodging.

So no.

Earn first.

Again.

Earner.

That title actually fit.

I earned three.

Spent three.

Ended farther north with same general financial problem.

Capitalism but scenic.

Goodnight.

# CHAPTER 455

## THE SHEPHERD

Sheep were louder than expected.

Not individually.

Collectively.

One sheep sounded stupid.

Forty sheep sounded political.

I woke at Ridge Cot to a wall of offended bleating outside.

Wind too.

Mere Ridge apparently believed mornings should contain multiple complaints.

Cash fourteen.

Vale twenty-three.

Ten-copper floor.

No Crown Vale ticket.

No Larken Lake ticket.

No magic today.

Probably.

I dressed.

Trial leg stayed wrapped.

Twenty-four minutes yesterday. Rest day.

Crutches.

Breakfast bits.

Work board first.

Money before scenery.

The road office had four tabs.

**WOOL COUNT / LOWER PEN / 2C / HALF**

**SHEARING SHED / TOOL TAG CHECK / 2C / SEATED POSSIBLE**

**SIGNAL HOUSE / CLEAN LOWER ROOM / 2C / STAIRS FOR UPPER ONLY**

**RIDGE FLOCK MOVE / EAST FENCE TO LOWER PEN / 3C / WALKING / UNEVEN GROUND**

No.

Three copper tempting.

Uneven ground not.

Tool tags sounded like more tags.

Signal House cleaning had potential, but public viewing second bell existed anyway.

Wool count.

I took it.

Lower pen sat behind Wind Lamb inn in a shallow stone hollow.

Woman from cart yesterday was there.

Hessa Pell.

She recognized me.

"Carrow man."

"Mere Ridge man for at least another day."

"That's not how being from somewhere works."

"Temporary citizenship."

She ignored that.

Good.

Work was not counting sheep by looking at sheep.

Thankfully.

Fleece bundles came from morning skirt table, each tied with colored cord by flock.

My job:

count bundles by cord color,

check storm-bead braid still attached to fleece sample tag,

note missing bead sets,

stack by buyer mark.

Do not grade fleece.

Do not decide bead condition.

If braid missing or beads cracked, question mark.

Signal system historically sent ridge condition by mechanical arm positions plus lightly charged wire confirmation between posts.

Bren.

Caro Olt.

"Good. Means you know."

Green.

Green.

No lore vending machine.

Not sea huge.

But large enough opposite shore existed as dark blue line only where air cleared.

South shore curved out of sight.

Fresh water smell different.

Less salt.

More mud, leaves, cold stone.

That landed harder than expected.

Carrow smelled like harbor even inland streets when wind turned.

Duskport salt.

No salt.

Good.

Paid.

Two above floor.

Excellent.

I hated it.

No salt crust.

Could expose lake-specific infrastructure.

Not guaranteed.

Apply first ring.

Good.

Trial leg done for day.

Broad hull.

A thin line of blue light ran along edge.

Not propulsion.

Maybe depth or submerged reed warning.

Interesting.

No asking while docking.

Two.

# CHAPTER 457

## THE LAMPLIGHTER

Fog Bowl House smelled like hot glass and wet wool.

Good start.

I had twelve copper.

Ten of it belonged to emergency me.

Current me had two.

Current me needed employment.

The work board still had:

**FOG BOWL HOUSE / WICK GLASS SORT / 3C HALF / DRY BENCH / CAREFUL HANDS**

I took the tab before somebody with more money discovered careful hands.

Fog Bowl House sat behind South Landing's second tower.

Low stone building.

Two chimneys.

Windows clouded white from inside.

Supervisor was **Mera Venn**.

Venn.

Again.

Not related until proven.

I had learned.

She was broad-shouldered, gray braid, left sleeve rolled above elbow, and spoke like every sentence had another sentence waiting behind it.

"Wick glass is not lamp wick," she said before hello. "People hear wick and think flame. No flame. If you put flame near charge glass, I throw you in lake. Not literally because paperwork, but spiritually yes."

"Strong orientation."

"You sort. You do not polish, scrape, re-seat, warm, bend, charge, compare brightness by eye, or decide what a weird one means."

"Question bin."

"Question tray. Bin sounds like we don't care. We care aggressively."

I liked her.

Bad sign.

She showed table.

Thin curved strips of cloudy glass, each about two fingers long.

Some clear at edges.

Some smoky.

Some carried a pale thread inside, like frozen lightning too tired to perform.

Three trays:

**CLEAR RETURN**

**DULL RETURN**

**QUESTION**

My actual job was simpler.

Match each strip to stamped return card.

Check for obvious chip, cloud spread, hairline fracture, darkened anchor end.

Do not grade brightness.

Do not decide serviceability.

"Why sort clear and dull if I don't grade?"

Mera pointed at card marks.

"Because crew already marked behavior before removal. You match marked condition to physical strip. Clear-return card goes clear-return tray unless physical damage makes question. Dull-return card goes dull-return tray unless mismatch makes question."

"So I verify identity and visible condition."

"Exactly."

"Not performance."

"Exactly."

I sat.

Trial leg rested today.

Crutches against bench.

Good.

First ten ordinary.

Then one clear-return card with dark anchor end.

Question.

Mera checked.

"Good. Burn line."

"Burn?"

"Not heat burn. Charge staining where anchor contact carried wrong pulse too long."

"Meaning bowl failed?"

"Meaning strip shows stain. Don't marry evidence too early."

Excellent.

She took it to technician table.

I kept sorting.

Fog bowls, I learned slowly, were not lamps.

They were shore references.

Each tower had one or more hanging bowls with wick-glass assemblies inside.

When lake fog thickened, keeper charged bowl at known low pulse.

The bowl scattered that pulse through suspended moisture and made a pale glow visible from water.

Not a beam.

Not a lighthouse.

More like luminous volume.

"So brighter means thicker fog?" I asked.

Mera made face.

"No."

Good.

"Brightness depends bowl, wick, charge, moisture, angle, distance, contamination, whether some idiot cleaned outer glass with fish oil, and probably moods of dead ancestors."

"Last one measured?"

"Extensively."

"Useful conclusion then?"

"Pilots don't use brightness as number. They use whether expected shore glow is present, absent, distorted, doubled, or displaced relative to route markers."

There.

"Comparative navigation."

"Yes."

"Not fog measurement."

"Yes."

I sorted faster once system settled.

Not too fast.

Careful-hands job paid for not being interesting.

A young technician at next table, **Risa Pell**, was fitting a wick strip into bronze fork.

Pell.

Names everywhere.

She caught me looking.

"You want one?"

"Can't afford lunch emotionally."

"Good. Retired wick glass is mostly decorative unless you own bowl housing."

"Mostly?"

"People make jewelry. Bad jewelry."

"Dangerous?"

"Usually just ugly."

Reassuring.

A runner came in from South Tower.

"Two bowl showing double edge."

Mera stopped talking.

"In fog?"

"Lower bank rolling in. Pilot Three reported split glow from west approach."

Mera looked to technician table.

"Risa. Bowl Two return set from yesterday?"

"Bench three."

They moved.

I did not.

Sort.

Runner waited.

Mera checked yesterday's returned wick set against service card.

One strip had been marked dull but no fracture.

Risa said, "Could be bowl suspension."

Mera: "Could be outer skin condensation. Could be second reflection off wet causeway. Could be wick seat. We don't get to pick favorite."

Runner asked, "Do we close west approach?"

Mera said, "Keeper decides navigation status. Tell tower we have no bench cause yet. Ask if double edge persists after bowl shield reset."

Runner left.

Again.

People with actual authority did authority.

I matched cards.

Fifteen more.

Question strip with hairline crack.

Question strip whose stamped number didn't match card.

Question card marked clear but glass physically smoky at one edge.

Mera checked mismatch.

"Wrong return pouch."

She did not silently fix.

Logged transfer.

Systems hate undocumented helpful people.

High Shelf again.

Regional religion.

Second runner came.

"Shield reset fixed split. West approach remains open."

Mera nodded.

"Then likely external reflection or shield seat. Bowl Two stays in service pending afternoon physical check."

"Not wick?"

"Not proven wick."

Good.

No dramatic failure.

No ferry disaster.

Just navigation system producing ambiguous symptom and people narrowing it.

I finished half shift.

Mera counted trays.

"Forty-eight matched. Six question."

"Is that good?"

"It's forty-eight matched and six question."

Perfect.

She paid three copper.

Twelve to fifteen.

Five above floor.

Breathing room.

Small.

Real.

I did not immediately buy ferry ticket.

Growth.

Outside, fog had moved across lower lake in pale sheets.

South Tower bowl glowed softly.

Now that I knew what it wasn't, it looked more useful.

Not a beacon saying HERE.

A reference saying expected shore glow currently behaves like this.

Pilots built rest from experience.

Same as bottom-change plates.

Same as half the tools I liked.

No absolutes.

Just better questions.

I went to landing board.

Routes unchanged except one new note.

**NORTH SHORE / REEDMARK / 3C / AFTERNOON FOG HOLD POSSIBLE**

**EAST CROSSING / LAKEWARD / 4C / NOON WINDOW CLOSED / NEXT REVIEW TOMORROW**

**LOWER LAKE / TOLLEN / 2C / DEPART SECOND RING**

Too late for Tollen today.

Good.

Forced patience.

I asked ticket clerk what each place actually was.

"Reedmark?"

"North-shore reed-cutting town. Ferry yards, marsh road, uplake carts."

"Lakeward?"

"East-side market town. Bigger. Cross-lake freight, glass works, road east toward orchard country."

"Tollen?"

"Lower lake fish town. Cheap beds. South road reconnects valley routes."

There.

Different choices.

Reedmark opened north shore and marsh road.

Lakeward opened glass works and orchard country east.

Tollen looped lower lake and south roads.

Lakeward strongest expansion.

Also four copper.

At fifteen, paying four leaves eleven.

Then lodging could hit ten exactly.

Too thin.

Could work tomorrow again.

But not same sort job by default.

Could maybe earn another small job.

Or take Reedmark three, leave twelve.

Still one-copper lodging leaves eleven.

Thin but better.

Or stay South Larken another day and build buffer.

This was not fake choice.

I sat by public quay with bowl tower behind me.

Freshwater gull equivalent? Small black lake birds fought over peel.

One won.

Capitalism but feathers.

Caro Olt passed carrying repaired net panel.

"Still here."

"Financially."

"Where going?"

"Maybe east. Maybe north."

"East costs more."

"I noticed."

"North smells worse."

"Useful metric."

"Reedmark marsh mud gets in boots."

He looked at crutches.

"Or... whatever you've got."

"Currently one boot and two sticks."

"Then mud gets in stick ends. Worse."

Good point.

"Lakeward?"

"Too many people. Glass workers think every piece of clear junk deserves ceremony. Market decent."

"Road beyond?"

"Orchards east. Hill towns after. Never went."

"Why not?"

"Lake here."

Simple.

His world did not need expansion.

Mine apparently did.

That thought bothered me slightly.

Not because wrong.

Because easy to turn movement into virtue.

Staying wasn't failure.

Going wasn't courage automatically.

I was traveling because I wanted to.

Important.

At South Landing, a ferry crew began unloading baskets of long silver fish.

One basket had glass tag with pale green thread.

Another amber.

I asked worker after load clear.

"Temperature?"

"Freshness interval."

"Meaning?"

"Tag changes after enough warm exposure. Not clock."

Of course.

"Resettable?"

"Some. These no. Cheap freight tags."

"Accurate?"

Worker shrugged.

"Accurate enough buyer rejects amber if contract says green."

Economic instrument.

Not scientific absolute.

Interesting.

Artifact market everywhere, just embedded in labor.

I did not ask price.

Wallet thanked me.

Afternoon I stayed local.

Actual life.

Bought stew with bits.

Watched fog bank cross without drama.

Helped Net House keeper move three empty stools because I was there and had hands? Crutches complicate. Could carry one stool one-handed awkwardly. Better not.

I sharpened pencil.

Wild adventure.

Later, public tower keeper gave short explanation to two apprentice pilots.

I listened from public side.

Expected pattern:

South Bowl visible first on west approach.

Causeway bowl second.

If both visible but spacing looks wrong, suspect lateral drift or reflection.

If one absent, do not infer bowl failure immediately because fog wall can hide local reference.

Pilots compare with route memory and bottom-change plates.

Multiple weak instruments together create stronger navigation.

That was beautiful.

Not because magic.

Because system.

I wrote it down:

**Lake navigation does not trust one instrument. Bowl gives shore relation. Bottom plate gives bottom-change relation. Pilot gives route memory. Fog posts constrain road approach. None sufficient alone.**

Then underlined:

**Useful systems overlap imperfect evidence.**

Probably true beyond boats.

Could apply to human conflict too.

Dangerous thought.

I left it.

Evening board updated:

**REEDMARK NORTH SHORE / MORNING WINDOW LIKELY / 3C**

**LAKEWARD EAST / MORNING REVIEW / 4C / CROSS-LAKE FOG POSSIBLE**

No need choose tonight.

Net House second night still 1c.

I paid.

Fifteen to fourteen.

Four above floor.

Same damn number.

But this time I had not moved.

That mattered.

I had spent one copper to learn place instead of three or four to leave it.

Maybe good.

Maybe just slower capitalism.

I sent home note next morning? Could send tonight. Enough time since previous note departed Mere Ridge yesterday. Fine.

**South Larken. Stayed another night. Earned 3c sorting returned wick glass at Fog Bowl House. Fog bowls are shore-reference tools, not fog meters or lamps. South approach had a double-glow report today; shield reset cleared it, no proven wick failure. Cash 14c, Vale 23c after second Net House night. Ten floor intact. No onward ticket yet. - Greg**

Then:

**Lakeward east is 4c. Reedmark north is 3c. I am attempting the radical experiment of not buying transportation immediately after earning money.**

Postage bits.

Trial leg rested all day.

Good.

Lot 67 packed.

Good.

Magic 138 still enough.

Good.

Before bed, fog thickened enough South Bowl disappeared from Net House window.

Then pale glow returned ten breaths later through different patch.

Not signal failure.

Fog geometry.

I understood that now.

Tiny thing.

Still satisfying.

Lamplighter was wrong technically.

No lamps.

But I had spent half day sorting pieces that made shore glow in fog.

Close enough for chapter title.

Tomorrow I could go north.

Or east.

Or work again.

For once, I had enough money to choose badly without immediately becoming broke.

Luxury.

# CHAPTER 458

## THE WAYFINDER

Lakeward ferry posted green at first ring.

That was inconvenient.

I had spent last night hoping weather would make decision for me.

Very mature.

South Larken board:

**LAKEWARD EAST / 4C / CLEAR WINDOW / LOAD CALL FIRST-AND-HALF**

Cash fourteen.

Ten-copper floor.

Four copper fare.

Which would leave exactly ten.

Then lodging would require violating my own rule before I had even found bed.

No.

Reedmark north remained three copper.

Tollen two.

Crown Vale was now west behind ridge.

All sensible.

Lakeward still pulled hardest.

Glass works.

Orchard road.

East country.

Different.

I stared at board until clerk said, "You buying or courting it?"

"Financially incompatible relationship."

"Then move."

Fair.

One small job had appeared below route board.

**LANDING THREE / DEPARTURE TOKEN CHECK / 1C / ONE BELL / SEATED**

Not enough to be interesting.

Exactly enough to be useful.

I took it.

No heroic labor.

No half-day delay.

One bell at a little table beside boarding lane.

Job was stupidly simple.

Passenger handed token.

I matched stamped route notch to clerk sheet.

Green basket Lakeward.

Blue Reedmark.

Yellow Tollen.

Wrong basket went back to clerk.

No fare disputes.

No identity judgments.

No upgrades.

Just stop people getting onto wrong boats before wrong boat became expensive geography.

I caught three.

Woman with Tollen token in Lakeward line.

Man with yesterday's void Reedmark chit.

One child carrying mother's token while mother held child's.

That one technically would have solved itself.

Still counted.

One copper.

Fourteen to fifteen.

Now Lakeward fare left eleven.

One-copper floor bed would leave ten.

Thin.

But not fake.

I bought east crossing.

Fifteen to eleven.

No joy.

A ferry ticket should feel like possibility.

Mine felt like subtraction.

Good.

Trial leg stayed wrapped.

It had rested yesterday, but wet deck, cross-lake fog possibility, and unknown landing surface made crutches correct.

I boarded **Glass Heron** through lower ramp.

Broader than lower-lake boats.

Two shallow hulls joined under a wide deck? No. Not twin hulls. One broad hull with side outriggers? Keep simple.

Broad flat-bellied hull, low cabin, high reed-slat wind screens.

At bow, two bottom-change plates hung folded upward for open water.

Fog bowl mounted aft under hood.

Pilot **Rian Kett**.

Deck crew three.

Passengers maybe twenty.

Freight mostly orchard crates, glass cullet sacks, lamp oil, seed baskets.

I found bench against wind screen.

No one knew me.

Still nice.

Then somebody said, "Peg-Leg Greg?"

Damn it.

I looked.

Teenage boy with apple crate.

"No."

He grinned.

"My aunt came through High Shelf two days ago. Said there was tall one-legged man with trial limb heading lake road."

"That's circumstantial."

"She said he talks annoying."

"Could be anybody."

He laughed.

Nickname had beaten mail somehow.

Fine.

"I don't even have peg."

"Yet."

Kett Arlo's disease spreading by trade routes.

Boy's name **Perrin Sol**.

Orchard apprentice from east side.

He was returning with rootstock.

"Lakeward?" I asked.

"Then east road."

"How far?"

"Home? Half day cart if road dry."

"Where?"

"Morrow Orchard."

New place.

"Big?"

"Enough."

Good answer.

Glass Heron left South Landing.

Shore fog was lifting, but lake still held gray bands farther east.

Open water changed sound.

Sea ferries had constant salt chop and long swell memory.

Larken had shorter motion.

Wind-made.

Patchy.

Surface dark in one direction, silver in another.

Caro's line returned.

Lake wind lies.

Pilot Rian watched water like it was handwriting.

Bottom plates stayed up.

Fog bowl aft dark.

No need.

For first half bell nobody talked much.

Then older woman across aisle started telling story to two children.

Not performance.

Something they had clearly heard before and wanted again.

"Tell the cup one."

"You know cup one."

"Tell it."

She sighed like storyteller defeated by market demand.

"Fine."

I listened because sitting did not require moral justification.

Story was called **The Woman Who Returned the Water**.

Not ancient-god proclamation.

A woman named **Iria** lived in orchard country during a dry year.

Spring above orchards thinned.

People fought over channels.

Upper farms closed gates.

Lower farms accused them of theft.

Village measurers argued whether spring itself was failing or water was being held.

Iria's grandmother told her there was older water path before the cut road.

Not road exactly.

Sequence of places.

Lakeward shore.

White Reed.

Sleeping Stone.

Three Sisters Hollow.

Mirror Spring.

Iria carried a plain cup of lake water uphill.

At each place she poured some out.

Not because lake water magically fed spring.

Because the path marked where water changed.

At White Reed, soil stayed wet though channel was dry.

At Sleeping Stone, moss lived on downhill face.

At Three Sisters, one hollow still held frogs.

At Mirror Spring, she found spring mouth blocked by fallen root and stone after storm.

She cleared enough for water to move.

Then carried a black spring pebble back down and placed it in lake.

Children asked why.

Old woman said, "Because story says water remembers road both ways."

One child said, "Rocks don't remember."

"Then remember for rock."

Good.

That line landed.

I expected ending.

Didn't get one.

Woman continued.

On way home Iria met three farmers arguing at lower gate.

They wanted to know who caused dry channels.

She told them storm blocked spring.

Upper farmer said that proved he had not stolen.

Lower farmer said upper still closed too much gate.

Third said everybody would lie again next drought.

So they set marker stones at channels and agreed each family had to walk spring path once before accusing another.

Children hated administrative ending.

"That's boring."

"That's why you still have apples."

Excellent.

Perrin beside me had gone quiet.

"Your orchard?" I asked softly.

He looked.

"What?"

"Morrow. Is that in story country?"

His expression changed.

Not dramatic.

More careful.

"Yeah."

"Mirror Spring real?"

"Depends which old person you ask."

"That's not answer."

"There's spring called Mirror. Path isn't used now. Cut road goes around north shoulder."

I looked back toward storyteller.

She was correcting child pronunciation of Three Sisters place-name.

"Still water problem?"

Perrin stared at crate.

"Not like story."

There.

Somebody wanted something badly maybe.

"What's like story?"

"Nothing."

Then, after a while:

"East channels are low."

"Drought?"

"Maybe."

"Upper gates?"

"Maybe."

"Spring?"

"Maybe."

"You have excellent evidence."

"I know."

He sounded irritated.

Not at me exactly.

Good.

"Why were you west buying rootstock if channels low?"

"Because my mother already paid."

Human answer.

"And because if water comes back, we need it."

"If?"

He rubbed crate edge.

"District reader says wait three days. Water office wants another channel measure. Upper orchards say lake fog changed recharge. Lower orchards say upper gates are cheating. My grandfather says somebody needs to walk old path."

"And what do you say?"

"I say grandfather can't walk it."

There.

Quest arrived quietly.

Not mine.

Somebody else's.

"You?"

"I have work."

"That's convincing."

He glared.

"You don't know my family."

"Correct."

I shut up.

Good.

Glass Heron crossed gray band.

Fog bowl aft lit pale.

Crew did not panic.

Pilot adjusted east by maybe small angle.

A deckhand called marker number.

Bottom-change plates lowered as shore shelf approached.

Water under us darkened green over weed beds.

One plate gave faint line.

Pilot shifted north.

Layered imperfect evidence.

Worked.

Lakeward emerged through fog in pieces.

First glass towers.

Not towers like castle.

Tall kiln chimneys with pale exhaust.

Then shoreline cranes.

Then long low buildings with roofs of blue-gray tile.

Orchard carts everywhere.

Apples, pears, long yellow fruit I didn't know.

Freshwater reeds bundled by docks.

East side smelled like wet wood and hot glass.

No salt.

Still strange.

We docked.

Perrin lifted rootstock crate.

Too heavy for him alone.

Deckhand helped.

I did not volunteer with crutches on wet ramp.

Growth.

At landing square, story woman disappeared into market.

Perrin waited near road board.

I looked too.

**ORCHARD ROAD / MORROW / 2C CART / SECOND BELL**

**GLASS ROAD / EAST WORKS / 1C CART**

**NORTH SHORE / REEDMARK / 3C FERRY**

**LOWER LAKE / TOLLEN / 2C**

Morrow Orchard.

Two copper.

I had eleven.

Could not pay and preserve floor.

Not without work.

Again.

But now there was reason beyond novelty.

Not obligation yet.

Reason.

Perrin saw me read Morrow line.

"Don't."

"Don't what?"

"Make face like you're thinking."

"My face does that."

"Grandfather will hire anybody who listens to story."

"That's poor hiring practice."

"Exactly."

I almost laughed.

"Hire for what?"

"Walk."

"Old path?"

"He wants somebody to go from White Reed to Mirror Spring and mark wet signs before district crew finishes arguing."

"Why not you?"

He got angry this time.

"Because I have two younger sisters, mother, six rows already stressed, and I am apprentice under east graft house. If I disappear three days because grandfather wants story walk, we lose more than water."

There.

No clean answer.

"How long is path?"

"Nobody knows now."

"That's bad."

"Yes."

"Passable?"

"Grandfather says yes."

"That is not evidence."

"I know."

He was learning my language fast.

"District maps?"

"Old path crosses landslide cut beyond Three Sisters. New road goes around."

"So grandfather wants stranger with one leg to inspect abandoned footpath."

Perrin looked at crutches.

"I said hiring practice bad."

Fair.

I should walk away.

Actually I should crutch away.

Cash eleven.

Ten floor.

No room.

Unknown path.

Unknown lodging.

Unknown work.

Borrowed trial leg.

No reason to inherit orchard family's problem.

Then Perrin said:

"He pays eight copper if somebody reaches Mirror Spring and comes back with route notes. Twelve if they also bring sealed spring sample to district office."

Oh.

Real money.

Not miraculous.

Several days risk for eight to twelve.

Could matter.

Could fund road.

Could maybe feed leg money indirectly.

Could also cost more than paid.

"Why twelve for sample?"

"District office wants it. Grandfather says if we're walking anyway, bring sample."

"Who provides bottle?"

"District."

"Authority?"

"Sample only. No gate authority."

Good.

"Why hasn't district sent somebody?"

"They will. Eventually."

"How eventually?"

"Three days maybe. Five if reader team stays north channels."

"And orchard?"

"Lower rows start losing fruit if channels stay this low another two or three days."

There.

Clock.

Not world ending.

Fruit.

Work.

Family.

Money.

Old story path.

Modern measurement delay.

Somebody needed something.

I hated how well it fit.

"Grandfather name?"

"Teren Sol."

"Morrow Orchard?"

"Yes."

"Does he know you are recruiting one-legged strangers at ferry docks?"

"No."

"Excellent."

"He will like you."

"That worries me."

I still did not agree.

Important.

First lodging.

Lakeward cheap board showed:

**GLASS YARD FLOOR / 1C / WORKERS + TRAVELERS**

I paid.

Eleven to ten.

Exactly floor.

No discretionary copper.

There.

Adventure officially required income.

I put pack at Glass Yard floor.

Dry stable stone.

Trial limb had rested all day? No wear today. Could use later, but not necessary. Keep rest.

Then I went to district water office.

Not because quest accepted.

Because check claims before becoming idiot.

Office clerk **Aven Rusk** found Morrow notice after some searching.

"Low east channels."

"How low?"

"Enough for watch, not emergency."

"Mirror Spring sample requested?"

"Requested if field team reaches source."

"Can private person bring one?"

"Sealed district bottle, witness mark at fill, accepted as supplemental. Not authoritative source reading by itself."

Good.

"Old path?"

Aven made face.

"Story path."

"Real?"

"Parts."

"Passable?"

"Depends what you call path."

"Accessible with crutches?"

She looked at me properly.

"I would not promise that."

Finally.

"Landslide?"

"Three Sisters north shoulder. New road bypasses. Old footpath may cross lower bench. We do not maintain it."

"Any map?"

"Public archive sketch. No current condition."

She pulled thin sheet.

White Reed.

Sleeping Stone.

Three Sisters Hollow.

Mirror Spring.

Same sequence.

Story was map memory.

Not exact map.

But map.

Hawaiian-story nudge in world's own grammar: story holds geography, practical observation, social rule.

I copied route names.

"Bottle?"

"We don't issue unless someone signs supplemental sample chain."

"Can I see form?"

She handed.

Simple enough.

Collector name.

Route.

Fill point.

Witness if available.

Seal number.

Observed conditions.

No claim of source flow rate.

No claim of cause.

Good.

"Fee?"

"Deposit one copper on bottle. Refunded when returned intact, sample or empty."

I had zero discretionary.

Of course.

"Can orchard post deposit?"

"Yes."

Interesting.

I returned form.

"Not signing yet."

"Good."

"Why good?"
