But visual flow could be below normal.

Diversion could matter.

Side branch could matter.

Losses between here and White Reed could matter.

Upper gates could matter.

Fog recharge could still matter.

Everything still alive.

Correct procedure had not solved social problem.

Of course.

I inspected from allowed areas.

New diversion flap had fresh wood grain.

Peg hardware newer iron.

A small scratched marking on side, not words, maybe maker tally.

No owner sign.

Main channel stone showed wet line higher than current water by maybe two fingers.

Recent? Could be yesterday. Could be seasonal stain. Record as visible higher wet/stain line, timing unknown.

Right seep area had pale mineral crust and damp moss.

No blockage obvious.

Left seep stronger.

Main mouth clear enough visually.

No fallen root.

No boulder.

Legend not literal.

Good.

Then sound uphill.

Human.

A cough.

I froze.

Not combat readiness exactly.

Attention sharpened.

Another sound.

Foot on stone.

Somebody above catch roof.

I moved away from gate and sample handling area so first impression was not stranger tampering with water.

Crutches planted.

Hands visible.

A person stepped down from narrow upper path carrying bundled brush and short pruning blade.

Middle-aged man perhaps.

No need name yet.

He stopped when he saw me.

Then saw my bottle.

Then crutches.

"Who are you?"

Not friendly.

"Greg. Private Morrow field contract. District supplemental bottle."

"Morrow sent you?"

"Morrow cooperative hired me. District provided bottle procedure. I don't represent district."

His eyes moved to sealed bottle.

"You touched gate?"

"No."

"Why are you here?"

"Lower Morrow channels are low. Contract is route notes plus source sample."

His mouth tightened.

Human conflict arrived at source.

"Of course they sent somebody."

"They didn't send me to accuse you."

"You don't know who I am."

"Correct."

That almost broke tension.

Almost.

He set brush bundle down.

"That flap isn't theirs."

"I didn't say it was."

"They'll say it is."

"Maybe."

"They always do."

There.

Somebody wanted something badly.

I stayed quiet.

He continued because people often did when silence refused to fight back.

"East branch feeds ridge nursery and fire cistern. Has for years. We replaced rotten flap after spring thaw. Morrow knows."

Claim.

Not fact yet.

"Who is we?"

He hesitated.

Name threshold.

This person mattered.

Need registry check before naming, but prose already drafting. Avoid naming until future chapter/state can check. He can say ridge growers without personal name.

"Ridge growers," he said. "Upper shoulder holdings."

"How much does flap divert?"

"Depends basin."

"Today?"

"Less than quarter."

"Measured?"

He bristled.

"By marks."

"Where?"

He pointed to side post with carved notches.

I hadn't noticed.

Five horizontal cuts.

Water near second.

"What do marks mean?"

"Old share heights."

"Old how?"

"Before me."

Not enough.

"Any written schedule?"

"Lakeward office has copy."

Maybe.

"Morrow says lower rows losing water."

"Morrow closes their own orchard gates badly and blames mountain."

Maybe.

"Spring lower than usual?"

He looked at basin.

This answer took longer.

"A little."

"How little?"

"One mark maybe."

"Compared to what date?"

"This time last year."

"Recorded?"

"Not by me."

Good.

He hated that answer.

I recognized feeling.

"I'm writing exactly that."

"Write that Morrow likes sending strangers."

"They sent one because district team delayed."

"Same thing."

"Not really."

He laughed once without humor.

"You paid by them?"

"Yes."

"Then you're theirs."

There it was.

Procedure insufficient.

"They pay me for route notes and bottle. Not conclusion."

"Everyone says that until conclusion costs employer."

Fair enough.

I could not prove independence with sentence.

So I did not try.

"You can inspect my notes if you want."

Risk.

But useful.

He looked suspicious.

"Why?"

"Because I'm writing your claims as claims. If you think I misheard, better now than after I walk back."

He came closer slowly.

No weapon posture despite pruning blade at belt.

I turned notebook where he could read without taking it.

He read.

**Main visible flow present. Visual normal unknown. Secondary east flap half closed. Identity/authority unknown.**

Then lower:

**Ridge grower states east branch feeds ridge nursery + fire cistern and has for years; replacement flap installed after spring thaw; estimates current diversion less than quarter based on old share marks; says spring about one mark lower than same time last year, not personally recorded. Claims Lakeward office has written schedule.**

He stared.

"You wrote I didn't record it."

"You said you didn't."

"Makes me look unreliable."

"Makes statement bounded."

"Same thing to clerks."

There.

Painful value choice.

If I removed uncertainty to make him feel respected, notes became worse.

If I kept it, he had reason to believe system discounted oral local knowledge.

"I can add how you know."

He looked at me.

"How?"

"You said compared to last year. What are you actually looking at?"

He pointed to moss line behind left side of catch wall.

"When basin sits above third cut for more than a week, moss browns there. This year never did."

Now information.

I looked.

Could see band difference.

Not enough to validate timing.

But actual observation basis.

I wrote:

**Basis offered: seasonal moss-color band near left catch wall associated by local experience with sustained higher basin level. Current year reportedly never above third share cut long enough to brown same band. Not independently calibrated.**

He read.

"Better."

"Still not measurement."

"I know."

No sarcasm.

Just tired.

I asked, "Did you clear southern bypass?"

He nodded.

"After slide. Upper route killed a mule two years ago. We use lower shelf to reach spring."

That mattered.

"Why isn't Morrow using it?"

"Because they stopped coming."

"Garrick Sol walked old route nine years ago."

Recognition.

"Garrick."

Not warm.

"You know him."

"Everybody knows Garrick."

Could mean anything.

"He said no one permanent beyond White Reed."

"I'm not permanent. I come every few days in season."

Fair.

"Why no marker at southern descent?"

"Because every idiot with boots then thinks lower shelf is road."

Excellent reason.

"It's not?"

"It's water-maintenance access. Stable enough when stream low. Bad when ridge dumps."

Yesterday pulse.

"Does stream pulse?"

"Yes."

"Why?"

"Upper seep chambers."

I waited.

He gestured uphill.

"Rock pockets fill and spill. Not clockwork. More after fog."

Local explanation.

Plausible.

Not proven.

I wrote it as claim.

That made yesterday less mysterious without magically settling it.

Then he said, "You're going back today?"

I looked at sky.

Late morning nearing midday.

Three Sisters crossing.

Shoulder.

Food.

Return to Sleeping Stone maybe by dark if clean.

Morrow impossible today.

White Reed maybe tomorrow.

Three-day supply officially exceeded by overnight at Sleeping Stone again.

Could ration.

He saw calculation.

"There's an old dry hut half bell below spring on east maintenance cut."

Free lodging information, not gift.

"Public?"

"No owner. Water crew uses it. Roof mostly works."

"Food?"

He shook head.

Good.

"I have enough if careful."

He looked at my crutches.

"Shoulder?"

"Functional."

"That's not answer."

"Bruised."

He nodded like person who knew stubborn workers.

"Lower shelf crossing worse afternoon. Ridge seep pulse grows after fog lifts sometimes. If you're leaving, go before late light."

That was actionable local knowledge.

Could be self-serving if he wanted me gone.

Still useful.

Decision.

Return immediately across Three Sisters, maybe Sleeping Stone by dark.

Or use dry hut near spring, rest shoulder, then leave early when ford likely lower.

Food margin thinner but crossing timing better.

Sample safer if not rushed.

I chose hut.

Not because adventure wanted more chapter.

Because today's route back included stream behavior likely worse later.

Better to leave first light.

I told him.

He shrugged.

"Your neck."

"Generally."

He collected brush.

No name exchanged.

Interesting.

Maybe next time.

He climbed upper maintenance path.

I did not follow.

I spent another half bell observing permitted spring area.

No gate changes.

No tasting.

No magic.

No Lot 67.

Could Lot 67 time pulses? Maybe real timing problem now.

But pulses not periodic enough established. Using it without reference setup wouldn't answer much.

Packed.

District bottle now full, sealed, padded.

Quest objective technically achieved at source.

Return remained half the job.

I found east maintenance cut from his directions.

No need route name.

Old dry hut existed.

Low stone walls.

Wood roof patched mismatched.

One corner open to wind.

No food.

No person.

Fine.

I set pack down.

Shoulder throbbed enough that removing strap felt better than any bed I'd paid for.

I counted food.

Enough dinner.

Enough morning.

Emergency portion after that.

Not enough to linger.

Good.

At hut entrance I could see pale cliff above trees.

Mirror Spring hidden.

I wrote final source notes.

Then a separate heading.

**WHAT THIS DOES NOT PROVE**

Spring visually running does not prove normal flow.

New east diversion flap does not prove improper diversion.

Ridge grower's `less than quarter` is local estimate, not measured volume.

His claim that written schedule exists should be checked Lakeward.

One-mark-low seasonal comparison is memory supported by moss-band practice, not calibrated record.

Morrow lower-channel loss can occur between source and orchard.

Upper gates remain separate.

White Reed subsurface behavior remains separate.

Good.

Then another heading.

**WHAT CHANGED**

I had a sealed source sample.

A passable current lower route existed around Three Sisters under some conditions.

Someone from upper ridge actively maintained spring access.

The source was not obviously blocked like story version.

There was active diversion infrastructure with disputed social meaning.

That was enough to make return difficult in a new way.

Not physically.

Socially.

Helena wanted water.

Garrick wanted old route remembered.

Jonah wanted his family not to lose everything while adults argued.

Ridge growers apparently believed Morrow blamed them by habit.

District wanted supplemental evidence.

I now had evidence.

Evidence did not choose who deserved water.

Of course it didn't.

I lay back against blanket roll.

Shoulder hurt.

Right boot finally dry.

Magic stayed 139.

Cash stayed twelve.

Vale stayed twenty-three.

Sample stayed sealed.

Tomorrow I had to cross Three Sisters again with something everyone would want to interpret in their favor.

That felt more dangerous than the stream.

Not immediately.

But eventually.

The quest had changed shape.

I had crossed the broken path.

Now I had to cross back carrying an answer that wasn't one.

# CHAPTER 464

## THE COURIER

The sample made everything heavier.

Not physically.

Physically it was a bottle.

Padded.

Sealed.

Maybe half a pound.

Socially it weighed about six arguments.

Morning at the maintenance hut came cold and blue through the broken shutter slats.

My right shoulder had improved enough to disappoint me.

If it had been dramatically worse, decision easy.

Turn around slowly.

If magically healed, also easy.

Continue normally.

Instead I had soreness, dark bruise, full enough load-bearing, and a sharp little protest when I reached too high.

Useful body.

Annoying body.

Mine.

I ate what counted as breakfast and re-divided remaining food.

One small portion for the road to Three Sisters.

One emergency portion.

Nothing left that deserved optimism.

Water better.

I had both flasks.

The district bottle did not count.

I checked seal before anything else.

Intact.

Wax strip unbroken.

Number matched form.

Bottle padded center-pack, not outer pocket.

If I fell on it, larger problems already happening.

Good.

The spring grower was gone.

No note.

No dramatic warning.

Just fresh boot marks outside hut heading uphill.

I preferred that.

No companion.

No guide.

No convenient witness.

I left before full sun.

The lower shelf looked different from return direction.

Everything did.

Outbound travel had built a ladder in my head:

ford

steps

bench

hollow.

Now I knew where the bad plank was.

Where shelf narrowed.

Where one root caught blanket roll.

Where water channel crossed under old retaining wall.

That knowledge mattered more than confidence.

At first bend I stopped.

Stream sound louder than yesterday morning.

Not dramatically.

Enough.

I walked bank before reaching crossing.

The cracked plank remained cracked.

Good consistency.

The southern ford spread wider than yesterday afternoon.

Water maybe similar depth to yesterday morning.

No abrupt pulse while I watched.

I waited anyway.

One quarter bell.

Then another.

Food margin said move.

Shoulder said don't rush.

Sample said don't be stupid.

I crossed.

First crutch.

Second.

Right foot.

Pause.

Water cold enough to sharpen everything.

Halfway, current pressed harder on left crutch shaft.

Not surge.

Steady.

I angled shaft slightly upstream and shortened next move.

Old body knew rivers.

Current body knew compromise.

Three more placements.

Bank.

Done.

No magic.

No slip.

No heroism.

Excellent.

I sat immediately after crossing and checked seal.

Still intact.

Courier behavior.

That was what I was now.

Not investigator anymore.

Not really.

I had information.

My job was to stop adding damage to it.

Three Sisters Hollow was empty.

Morning shadows long.

The three fins did not share one shadow yet.

Story wasn't wrong.

Story was asleep.

I climbed from southern descent into hollow and took longer rest under rock lip from previous night.

Shoulder warm.

Not worse.

Good.

I considered route back.

Old lower bench still cut.

Southern bypass known.

Sleeping Stone beyond.

Then slide bypass.

Then White Reed.

Morrow after.

Could reach Sleeping Stone today easily enough if shoulder held.

White Reed maybe late.

Morrow unrealistic.

Food problem.

At minimum one more night.

Employer gave three days.

Quest had become four.

No tragedy.

But planning debt.

I ate half road portion.

Saved half.

Then started west.

Three Sisters route was easier backward because I knew gravel fan was wrong line.

I stayed upper track until old water wall.

No searching.

Faster.

Not fast.

There was difference.

By midday haze burned off.

Sleeping Stone overhang visible through trees earlier than expected.

Good.

Then I heard voices.

Two.

Human.

Not close enough for words.

I stopped.

No reason for danger assumption.

Recent twine had already shown people used route.

Still, sample changed stakes.

I moved off center trail to wider ground beside old channel wall and waited until voices resolved.

Two orchard workers came around bend carrying pruning bundles and a short-handled saw between them.

No need names.

They stopped when they saw me.

One looked at crutches.

Then pack.

Then my face.

"You came from spring?"

Question too quick.

"Why?"

Worker with saw said, "Because nobody else comes down that way with that much mud on one boot."

Fair.

"I came from above Three Sisters."

Technically true.

Other worker glanced at pack again.

"Morrow sent you?"

There.

Not random hikers.

"Who are you with?"

"North channel crews."

Not ridge growers exactly.

Not Morrow.

"Names?"

They gave them.

I did not need them yet.

I wrote roles only mentally.

Saw-worker said, "Did you sample source?"

Direct.

"Why do you ask?"

He sighed.

"Because everybody knows Morrow hired somebody. Because Garrick talks loud. Because Lakeward runner took bottle east. Because if you got one, people will spend tonight pretending bottle says whatever they already decided."

Correct.

I liked him slightly.

"Then you understand why I'm not discussing contents."

"Contents are water."

"Observations."

"Fine. Observations."

Other worker said, "Was east flap open?"

There it was.

"I'm not giving field findings on trail."

"We're not asking you to change paper."

"You're asking me to preview evidence before employer and district receive it."

Saw-worker rubbed face.

"We're asking because if Morrow thinks ridge branch is taking too much, they'll close lower exchange gate before district gets there. Then upper nursery loses pressure."

Human consequence.

"Can Morrow do that?"

"They can close their exchange gate. Whether they should is whole argument."

"Would that affect fire cistern?"

Pause.

"Maybe."

Not enough.

"How much?"

"Depends level."

"Recorded?"

He laughed without humor.

"You really are him."

"Who?"

"The sentence man."

Damn Helena.

Nickname spreading sideways now.

"That isn't improvement over Peg-Leg Greg."

Neither laughed.

Fine.

Other worker stepped closer.

Not threatening.

Urgent.

"Look, if spring is low, say spring is low. If flap is wrong, say flap is wrong. But don't walk back into Morrow with silence if they're about to close water on us."

There.

Correct procedure hurting somebody before procedure finishes.

My contract said return evidence.

Not mediate on trail.

But silence had consequence too.

What could I truthfully say without leaking findings?

"I can tell you this," I said. "Nothing I saw authorizes anyone to treat one observation as full cause. If you think Morrow may close an exchange gate before district review, send your own runner now. Tell them that consequence explicitly."

Saw-worker stared.

"That's it?"

"That's useful."

"It's annoyingly useful."

"My specialty."

He looked toward east.

"We can send one from White Reed."

"Do that."

Other worker said, "You didn't answer flap."

"Correct."

"You're protecting them."

"No."

"Then us?"

"Also no."

"Then who?"

I touched pack strap.

"Evidence."

That sounded dramatic.

I disliked it immediately.

But true.

Saw-worker finally nodded.

"Fine."

They moved past me toward east.

No confrontation.

No names needed.

But social pressure arrived before Morrow.

Good.

Bad.

I continued.

Sleeping Stone shelter showed new soot.

Very new.

Maybe those workers had used it outbound.

That solved one tiny mystery without making mystery.

I checked old twine.

Still there.

No need.

Shoulder wanted rest.

I gave it one quarter bell.

Then another.

Could stay here overnight.

Food one emergency portion plus half road portion.

White Reed reachable before dark if pace held.

Mabel had keeper house, likely water, maybe not food.

Could ask.

Hospitality not guaranteed.

But reaching maintained route reduced risk.

Continue.

Slide bypass was worst part.

Not because new danger.

Because shoulder remembered every root.

The active scar looked settled relative yesterday.

Meaningless timeframe.

I used same uphill rooted bypass.

This time descending.

Harder in different way.

I lowered pack separately twice.

Sat three times.

At one root step, right shoulder sharpened suddenly under crutch load.

I froze.

Waited.

Pain eased to strong ache.

No pop.

No weakness.

But threshold changed.

I shortened route expectations.

White Reed only.

No Morrow push even if light remained.

Good.

Below slide, old wire hazard still partly visible.

No grazer.

No need callback.

At split oak, dead trunk looked deader.

Alive trunk still yellow leaves.

Useful landmark.

By late afternoon I heard White Reed before saw it.

Reed hiss.

Water.

Then keeper house roof.

Relief landed harder than expected.

Not home.

Not even Morrow.

But maintained bridge.

Human roof.

Known person.

I crossed bridge.

Mabel Crowe was outside shaking mud from reed mat.

She looked at me.

Then shoulder.

Then pack.

"You look worse."

"I have data."

"That's usually why."

Good woman.

"Jonah?"

"Back Morrow. Came through yesterday asking whether you'd returned."

"No."

"I noticed."

She pointed at bench.

"Sit before you fall for dramatic effect."

I sat.

First thing:

sample seal.

Still intact.

Mabel watched.

"Got it?"

"Yes."

"Spring?"

"I'm returning findings to district and Morrow first."

She rolled eyes.

"Gods preserve paperwork."

"They've tried."

"Did you at least find path?"

That I could answer.

"Southern lower maintenance shelf crosses below Three Sisters slide under current conditions. North upper track remains terrible idea."

"That old shelf still there?"

"Yes."

"Huh."

"You knew?"

"Knew there was one. Didn't know it survived."

Good.

"Recent cuts. Ridge crews use it."

"That tracks."

"Why didn't you mention?"

She gave me look.

"Because I haven't been past Sleeping Stone in eleven years and didn't want to murder you with nostalgia."

Excellent.

That was exactly right.

"Thank you."

"You're welcome."

I asked about food.

Not delicately.

"I am one emergency portion away from becoming stupid. Do you sell anything?"

"No."

Damn.

"I do have stew."

"Cost?"

"You can split wood tomorrow."

"Shoulder."

"Then you can write me a route note copy after district gets original."

Fair exchange.

Not free.

"Done."

She fed me thick reed-root stew with dried meat and bread.

I did not ask magical nutritional composition.

It was hot.

Enough.

Food crisis reduced.

Not erased.

Mabel had one spare floor pallet.

"Cost?"

"Same note copy."

"You're overpaying me."

"Then make handwriting legible."

Deal.

I stayed.

Shoulder needed it.

Before dark, runner arrived from Morrow.

Jonah.

Of course.

Not secretly following quest.

Actual maintained-road runner.

He came across bridge sweaty and angry.

"You're alive."

"Barely in accounting sense."

He saw pack.

"Bottle?"

"Intact."

His face changed.

Relief.

Then urgency.

"Grandfather wants to know now."

"He gets report when I reach Morrow."

"I know."

"Then why are you here?"

"Because Helena sent me."

Ah.

"Why?"

Jonah sat across from me without asking.

"Lower exchange gate got reduced this afternoon."

There.

"By Morrow?"

"Cooperative vote. Temporary."

"Why?"

"Because Row Four dropped fruit this morning and because north crews supposedly increased draw and because everybody is furious."

"Did they know my findings?"

"No."

Important.

"Any district order?"

"No."

"How much reduction?"

"One notch."

"Effect?"

"Lower rows keep more local feed. Upper nursery gets less exchange pressure."

The workers' warning already happening.

"Fire cistern?"

Jonah shook head.

"Nobody agrees."

"Excellent."

"Not excellent."

"No."

He leaned forward.

"Mom wants you to come straight Morrow tomorrow. Grandfather wants you to tell council at dawn if spring is running. North crew sent runner saying nobody should touch another gate until district sees sample."

"They're right about last part."

"Can you tell me whether spring is running?"

There.

Jonah.

Person I knew enough to care.

Not district.

Not employer exactly.

Family.

"Visible flow was present," I said.

His shoulders dropped.

Relief too fast.

I raised hand.

"That does not mean normal flow. I do not have calibrated normal."

Relief changed into frustration.

"Greg."

"No. Listen. This is exactly where people hurt each other. `Water exists` becomes `source is fine`. `source is fine` becomes `upper gates stole it`. That's not supported."

"But it's flowing."

"Yes."

"That matters."

"Yes."

"Then why do you make every answer feel smaller?"

Good question.

Not technical.

Human.

I took time.

"Because small true answer is better than large false one."

Jonah stared at bridge boards.

"Sometimes people need larger answer before fruit falls."

There.

"I know."

"Do you?"

That one landed.

Maybe I didn't enough.

I had spent first life being right under pressure because wrong killed people.

Here, delay also hurt people.

Different problem.

"I'm coming tomorrow," I said. "Early. Sample intact. Notes intact. I'll say exactly what I saw. And exactly what I couldn't establish."

"Grandfather will hate that."

"Your mother might too."

"She will less."

Probably.

Jonah looked at my shoulder.

"What happened?"

"Rock."

"Of course."

"I won."

"You look like you lost."

"Technical draw."

He finally smiled.

Good.

He had brought no food for me because he didn't know I was here.

Good continuity.

Mabel gave him leftover bread anyway and charged him one chore tomorrow? No, don't make hospitality transactional every bite. Ordinary kindness allowed.

He ate.

Then said he had to return before full dark.

"Why come yourself?"

"Because if runner said `he's alive` I'd still wonder if runner meant mostly."

Human.

"Also Grandpa would've come if I didn't."

"Bad knee."

"Exactly."

I gave Jonah his loaned flask back.

"Finally."

He shook it.

"You drank my good water."

"Criminal."

"I'm billing you."

"Send invoice."

He left west toward Morrow maintained track.

No permanent companion.

I stayed White Reed.

Before sleep, I copied notes into two sets.

One full district sequence.

One Morrow contract summary.

Important distinction.

District gets chain-form facts.

Morrow gets contracted route/water observations.

Same underlying truth.

Different scope.

I added a third page for gate consequence because now situation changed during my absence:

**Return note: Morrow lower exchange gate reportedly reduced one notch afternoon Day 4?** Need day numbering. Day 1 to Sleeping Stone, Day2 Three Sisters, Day3 Mirror Spring, now return Day4. Yes.

**Report source: Jonah Sol at White Reed. No district order reported. North crews reportedly object and request no further gate changes pending sample review. Verify in Morrow/Lakeward.**

That mattered.

Evidence did not freeze world while I walked.

Exactly.

Cash still twelve.

Vale twenty-three.

Magic 59 / 53 / 139.

Sample sealed.

Shoulder sore.

Food no longer emergency-thin because Mabel fed me, but field provisions effectively exhausted except emergency portion.

Tomorrow Morrow.

Then district.

Maybe council.

Maybe argument.

Probably argument.

Courier.

That was what I was being.

Not because I carried bottle.

Because every person I met wanted me to open it before delivery.

Not literally.

Worse.

They wanted me to open the meaning.

Tomorrow they would all be in same room.

I suspected carrying water had been easier.

# CHAPTER 465

## THE WITNESS

Mabel woke me before sunrise by putting a bowl near my head.

Not on my head.

Near it.

Hospitality had limits.

"Eat."

I opened one eye.

"Good morning."

"It'll improve after the bowl."

The bowl held oats, chopped dried apple, and enough salt to make the whole thing taste intentional.

My shoulder had stiffened overnight.

Not worse exactly.

Stiffer.

I sat up against the White Reed wall and tested it before breakfast because apparently I had become a man with morning procedures.

Forward lift.

Fine.

Side lift.

Ache.

Overhead.

Still no.

Crutch load.

Stable.

Good enough for maintained road.

Mabel watched me press into the right crutch.

"You always do that?"

"Recently."

"Comforting answer."

"I have others."

"Keep them."

I ate.

The district sample sat wrapped in spare cloth inside the pack, seal checked again before I closed it.

Filled.

Sealed.

Intact.

Still capable of disappointing everyone.

Jonah had already left for Morrow before dawn.

Mabel said Helena sent him back because apparently sleeping indoors was now an inefficient use of young men with legs.

I returned Jonah's flask to him last night.

One less thing.

Mabel handed me a folded heel of bread when I stood.

I looked at it.

"Route-note copy," she said.

"You already fed me for that."

"That was floor and stew. This is breakfast theft prevention."

"Different contract."

"Very."

I put it in my pocket.

"You're ruthless."

"I've lived at a ford for twenty-six years."

Fair.

I left White Reed in gray light.

The maintained road felt obscene.

Flat enough that I could see wheel ruts.

Wide enough for two carts in places.

No slide scar.

No hidden lower shelf.

No need to ask basalt formations what time it was.

I made better speed and hated my shoulder less for it.

Morrow appeared after second bell as rows first, then roofs.

The lower channels were visible before people.

Water still moved.

That mattered.

How much?

Not from road.

I did not turn moving water into answer because I'd apparently developed a personality defect.

At the first cooperative gate, two orchard workers looked up from a channel board.

One recognized me.

Not personally.

By crutches.

And probably because Morrow currently had one traveling amputee associated with its water problem.

"That's him."

I considered asking which part.

Didn't.

Helena met me outside the packing shed.

She had flour on one sleeve and no interest in greeting ceremony.

Her eyes went to my shoulder, then pack.

"Bottle?"

"Intact."

She exhaled.

"Good."

Then, after half a beat:

"You?"

"Also mostly intact."

"Mostly wasn't in the contract."

"It was implied by the payment structure."

She closed her eyes briefly.

"Come inside before my father finds you."

Garrick found me first.

Of course.

He came from the channel side with his wrapped knee stiff and one hand on a walking stick he had not used when I left.

"You reached it."

Not a question.

"Yes."

"Water?"

"Visible flow present."

His face changed.

Hope first.

Then irritation because apparently Jonah had already warned everyone about my answer.

"Greg."

"I know."

"Was there water or not?"

"Yes."

"Thank you."

"That does not tell us whether flow is normal."

"There he is," Helena said.

Garrick looked at her.

"Don't encourage him."

"I'm not sure anyone could."

That almost became a family joke.

Then a bell rang at the lower shed.

Not alarm.

Meeting bell.

Helena's mouth tightened.

"They've been waiting."

"Who?"

"Everyone who thinks your bottle belongs to their argument."

Excellent.

The cooperative room had been designed for fruit tallies.

Long table.

Wall boards.

Hooks for scales.

Not enough chairs for politics.

Morrow had solved this by making angry people stand.

Jonah was near the back with Ada and June absent, thankfully.

Two lower-row growers stood beside Helena's brother-in-law? No. I did not know that. A man with stained cuffs stood beside them. Unnamed.

Across the room were three north-channel workers.

One was the saw carrier I had met on the return road.

Good.

No new name needed.

At table end sat a woman in Lakeward district gray with a brass seal case.

She was new.

She mattered.

Naming guardrail internally: check before naming. I had not checked registry here in prose, so avoid name. She could remain district examiner.

The district examiner looked at me.

"Greg?"

"Yes."

"Contract field walker?"

"Yes."

"Bottle first. Conversation second."

I liked her immediately.

I put pack on table carefully.

Shoulder thanked me.

Unwrapped cloth.

Showed seal without handing it across until she cleared space.

She checked number against form.

Checked wax edge.

Checked cap.

Asked where filled.

"Main center outflow immediately below Mirror Spring lip. Bottle did not contact basin or stone."

"Witness?"

"None at sampling. An upper-ridge grower arrived afterward."

"Did he handle bottle?"

"No."

"Did anyone?"

"No."

She took it.

That was the first moment since Mirror Spring when the bottle stopped being my problem.

Not entirely.

But physically.

Wonderful sensation.

She placed it in a padded district box and closed the latch.

"Chain accepted for transport. Sample goes Lakeward."

Helena leaned forward.

"Accepted means his contract is complete?"

The examiner looked at Garrick.

"Contract says valid district supplemental source sample returned with completed seal form. This is returned. Form is complete. Seal is valid on inspection. Analysis isn't a condition of his field pay unless you wrote something different on your copy."

Garrick said, "We didn't."

"Then pay him."

Twelve copper.

Simple sentence.

Beautiful sentence.

Garrick reached for purse.

One north worker said, "Before we start paying people, can we hear what he found?"

Helena turned.

"The pay isn't a vote."

"Didn't say it was."

"Sounded like it."

The examiner tapped one finger on table.

"Pay the contract. Then field statement. Then arguments. In that order if you'd all like me to remain here."

Nobody tested her.

Garrick counted twelve copper onto table.

I counted it again because I respected money more than theater.

Twelve.

Cash twenty-four.

Vale twenty-three.

Ten-copper floor suddenly looked less like floor and more like a thing beneath me.

Not rich.

Not even close.

But the quest had paid.

Real money.

For real value.

That mattered.

I put coins away.

The examiner opened my district notes.

"Now. Start with route."

I did.

White Reed.

Sleeping Stone.

Slide.

Rooted bypass.

Three Sisters lower bench cut.

North-face upper track unused under explicit safety warning.

Southern stone descent.

Lower maintenance shelf.

Cracked plank rejected.

Shallow ford.

Observed pulse one day, lower/stabler next morning.

Recent branch cutting.

Rejoin with old lower bench.

Mirror Spring.

No embellishment.

The room got quieter as route became less abstract.

The saw carrier interrupted once.

"You used the lower shelf?"

"Yes."

"Both ways?"

"Yes."

"Morning return?"

"Yes."

He nodded to another north worker.

"Told you."

The examiner looked up.

"Told him what?"

"That lower shelf still passes if the seep is down."

"Recorded anywhere?"

He grimaced.

"Not district. Water crew knows it."

The examiner wrote something.

He watched her write and looked like a man seeing his own irritation become permanent.

I understood.

Then spring observations.

"Main center outflow visibly flowing. Left side seep visibly stronger than right by eye only. No calibrated flow measurement. No known normal available to me."

Garrick shifted.

"But water was there."

"Yes."

"The old path was right."

Helena said, "Father."

He ignored her.

"White Reed, Sleeping Stone, Three Sisters, Mirror Spring. All there."

"The route memory was materially useful," I said.

His face brightened.

"There."

"That does not establish every explanation attached to it."

His face unbrightened.

Helena rubbed forehead.

"He did warn you."

One north worker said, "What explanation?"

Garrick turned.

"That water above Morrow existed before your damned share boxes."

"Nobody said it didn't."

"You act like paper invented the hill."

"And you act like your grandfather personally dug the spring."

There.

Human beings.

Finally escaping evidence into identity.

The examiner raised voice slightly.

"Field statement."

It worked.

Not because they agreed.

Because she could leave with bottle.

I continued.

"A newer secondary diversion flap sits below catch basin, half closed during observation, directing some water east."

Morrow side of room changed.

North side changed differently.

Helena's eyes sharpened.

Garrick said, "How much?"

"I did not measure volume."

"Quarter?"

"Upper-ridge grower later estimated less than roughly a quarter by old share marks. I did not verify that."

Saw carrier spoke.

"That's about right."

A lower-row grower said, "Of course you'd say that."

"Because I can count marks?"

"Because your trees drink them."

"Our nursery keeps half your replacement stock alive."

"Not if our fruit drops first."

The room went again.

Examiner let it this time.

Interesting.

Maybe because argument now concerned choice, not field fact.

Helena looked at north workers.

"Is the fire cistern actually on that branch?"

"Yes."

"Directly?"

"Nursery line feeds the cistern reserve after second split."

"How full?"

Pause.

"Yesterday morning, about two-thirds."

"Recorded?"

The worker's jaw tightened.

"Board at nursery."

Examiner wrote.

Helena nodded once.

"Good. That's useful."

Garrick looked at her.

"Useful for what?"

"For not accidentally solving fruit by emptying fire water."

"One notch isn't emptying anything."

"You don't know that."

"Neither do they."

"Exactly."

Jonah, from back:

"That's what I've been saying."

Everyone looked at him.

He regretted participation instantly.

I almost smiled.

The examiner turned to me.

"Upper-ridge grower statement."

I read it.

East diversion existed for years according to him.

Current flap replaced after spring thaw.

Less than quarter estimate by share marks.

Lakeward office supposedly holds written schedule.

Spring looked one share-mark lower than same season last year according to his memory.

Basis: moss-color band and seasonal experience.

Not personally recorded.

Not calibrated.

Garrick seized one piece.

"Lower."

North worker seized another.

"Diversion existed for years."

Helena said, "You're both doing the thing."

"What thing?" Garrick asked.

"Taking the sentence you like."

Good.

I liked Helena.

The examiner apparently did too.

"Correct."

That annoyed Garrick more than when Helena said it.

The district woman pulled a second sheet from her case.

"Lakeward schedule copy. I brought it because your runner reached us before dawn."

Room changed again.

Good runner.

The examiner flattened paper.

"Mirror east branch is authorized as seasonal nursery and fire reserve draw. Maximum share is written as one quarter of marked catch division under ordinary condition."

Morrow grower said, "So they can take a quarter while we're losing fruit?"

"Under ordinary condition," examiner repeated. "Drought restriction can alter share. I do not have authority in this room to declare restriction."

"Who does?"

"Lakeward water panel after current readings, or emergency local action under immediate safety provisions. Fruit loss alone is not written as emergency safety. Fire reserve can be."

That was not going to make anyone happy.

Excellent law.

The north saw carrier folded arms.

"Then Morrow's gate change was unauthorized."

Helena said, "Probably."

Garrick looked at her.

"Helena."

"What? We changed it without district order."

"We changed our lower exchange gate."

"Which affects shared channel."

"Because our trees are dropping fruit."

"Yes. And it can still be unauthorized."

Garrick's face went red.

Not rage exactly.

Humiliation.

Different.

Important.

He had hired me because he wanted evidence.

Now evidence was arriving and refusing to behave like ally.

I knew that feeling.

The examiner asked, "Who ordered the one-notch reduction?"

Silence.

Then Garrick said, "I did."

Helena turned fully toward him.

Jonah stared.

I had suspected cooperative action.

Apparently cooperative meant somebody made decision while everyone else was shouting.

Examiner: "Under what authority?"

"We were losing fruit."

"That's reason. I asked authority."

Garrick leaned on stick.

"The lower exchange gate sits on Morrow stone."

"Shared water agreement?"

"Old agreement."

"Still active."

"According to Lakeward."

"According to signed schedule."

There it was.

Garrick looked older suddenly.

Not defeated.

Cornered by a system he thought had forgotten the land.

He said, quieter, "When I was Jonah's age, if lower rows started dropping fruit, you changed water before you asked somebody a day's ride away whether the trees were thirsty."

Examiner didn't mock him.

"And when that change reduced another family's cistern?"

"We knew who lived uphill."

"Do you now?"

Garrick looked at north workers.

One of them looked away first.

That was answer enough.

Social density lost over time.

Paper replaced some of it.

Not all.

Maybe badly.

But something had changed beyond bureaucracy.

People no longer knew each other's channels intimately enough to improvise without collateral damage.

Helena said, "Can we put it back one notch until panel?"

Garrick snapped, "And lose another day?"

North worker said, "Yes."

Lower grower said, "Easy for you."

"Our nursery supplies your graft house."

"Next year doesn't help this year's fruit."

"Fire doesn't wait for fruit either."

Everyone had a sentence.

All of them true enough to hurt.

The examiner looked at her schedule.

"I can order no further changes pending panel review. I can also recommend restoring prior exchange setting because current change appears outside standing agreement."

"Recommend," Garrick said.

"Yes."

"Not order."

"Not under my field authority absent immediate safety finding."

He looked at Helena.

Then north workers.

Then me.

Why me?

Because he'd hired me.

Bad reason.

"You walked it," he said. "What would you do?"

No.

There it was.

The trap disguised as respect.

I could say not my authority.

True.

Cowardly if used alone.

I had information.

I had judgment.

I just didn't have mandate.

Different things.

"I wouldn't close anything farther," I said.

North side nodded.

Morrow side frowned.

"I would restore the one notch until you know what that change does to nursery and fire reserve."

Now Morrow side frowned harder.

Garrick's face shut.

I continued before anyone mistook this for verdict.

"That's not because spring proves north is entitled to more water. It doesn't. It's because you changed a shared control before you knew downstream and upper consequences, and now you know one claimed consequence includes fire reserve. Restore known setting while district checks current flows and share condition."

Lower grower said, "And Row Four?"

"Gets worse maybe."

He stared at me.

"That's your answer?"

"It's part of it."

"Easy when they aren't your trees."

"Yes."

That stopped him.

Not because good answer.

Because I didn't pretend otherwise.

"If there were a painless setting," I said, "you wouldn't need this room."

Garrick looked at sample box.

"I paid twelve copper for that?"

"You paid twelve copper to know more than you knew four days ago."

"And we still don't know what to do."

"You know the spring isn't visibly blocked. You know a legal seasonal east diversion exists up to written limit under ordinary condition. You know the lower route still passes under some conditions. You know your one-notch change may affect a nursery and fire reserve. You know spring flow may be lower than one grower's seasonal memory, though that isn't calibrated. You know you need current channel readings between source and Morrow before blaming one gate. That's more."

"Not enough."

"No."

He looked furious.

Then tired.

"No," he repeated.

Helena put hand on table.

"Father. Put the notch back."

He didn't answer.

"For one day," she said. "District checks. We count Row Four drop properly instead of shouting about it. North sends cistern board reading. If panel says restriction, then we fight about restriction with numbers."

North saw carrier said, "We'll send the board reading."

Lower grower said, "And if we lose another cart of fruit?"

Helena looked at him.

"Then we record that too."

He laughed without humor.

"Paper fruit."

"No. Dead fruit with a count. So nobody gets to tell us later it wasn't happening."

That landed.

Garrick stared at her.

Something changed there.

Not agreement.

Recognition maybe.

His daughter wasn't choosing paper over orchard.

She was trying to make orchard legible enough that distant authority couldn't flatten it.

Same problem as ridge grower's moss band.

Different direction.

Garrick finally said, "One notch back. Until tomorrow's reading."

North worker exhaled.

Lower grower swore softly.

Examiner wrote.

No one won.

Good.

The meeting dissolved into tasks.

That was more useful than agreement.

North crew would send nursery/cistern board copy.

Morrow would restore exchange gate one notch and record time.

Lower rows would count fresh fruit drop by row instead of estimating by mood.

District examiner would take sample to Lakeward and request same-day channel readings at two intermediate boxes plus current Mirror branch setting verification.

Garrick would complain.

Not formally assigned.

Likely.

I sat once room thinned.

Shoulder had begun throbbing from standing too long.

Helena noticed.

"You can stop being useful now."

"Dangerous precedent."

"Sit."

I sat.

Jonah came over.

"So that's it?"

"For me?"

"For the spring."

"No."

He made face.

"I knew you'd say that."

"You're learning."

"I hate it."

"Also learning."

He leaned against table.

"Did you like it?"

"The meeting?"

"The trip."

Ah.

Different question.

White Reed.

Sleeping Stone.

Rock slide.

Three Sisters.

Cold ford.

Mirror Spring under white cliff.

A road that stopped being road.

Three shadows touching.

The moment Jonah turned back and I kept going alone.

I looked at him.

"Parts."

"That's not an answer."

"It's a large answer made smaller."

He groaned.

Good.

Then I said, "Yes."

He stopped.

"Yeah?"

"Yeah."

I had.

Even hurt.

Even hungry.

Even while being reminded that adventures were mostly inconvenient terrain between arguments.

Something in me had liked waking up and not knowing exactly what the day would require.

Not because danger.

Danger was expensive.

Because distance changed the questions.

Carrow had become a place where I knew how things worked.

This route kept handing me systems I didn't own yet.

Old stories that were maps but not instructions.

Water that could be both shared and fought over.

People who were right at different scales.

I liked that.

That was potentially a problem.

Helena returned with small cloth pouch.

"Your twelve is counted?"

"Twice."

"Of course it is."

She set another folded paper beside me.

"What's that?"

"Lakeward examiner copied the route note receipt. Proof sample accepted into district transport. Keep it until they close file."

I took it.

Not money.

Useful.

"Garrick's mad at you," she said.

"I noticed."

"He'll recover."

"Will the fruit?"

She looked toward lower rows through open door.

"Some."

Not all.

There was the cost.

No speech.

No lesson.

Some fruit would drop while people measured.

Maybe restoring gate would save nursery.

Maybe it wouldn't matter.

Maybe Morrow's problem lived somewhere between spring and lower rows.

Maybe tomorrow's readings would make one theory stronger.

Maybe not.

Quest completed.

Problem not solved.

That felt more true than treasure.

I checked purse later behind packing shed.

Twenty-four copper.

Twenty-three with Vale.

Bits unspecified.

Ten-copper floor intact by fourteen.

For first time since South Larken, I had enough cash to choose a route without first finding a two-copper bench job.

That changed things.

Not dramatically.

Enough.

The trial limb was still wrapped in pack.

Shoulder still hurt.

Magic still sat at one hundred thirty-nine external attempts, last one emergency and unexplained.

Lot Sixty-Seven remained packed.

I had no reason to test anything today.

I had reason to sleep.

Helena offered packing-shed loft again.

Paid contract included field lodging? Last night return ended at White Reed, but Morrow hospitality after completion could be ordinary. I offered a copper.

She looked offended.

"You slept in our loft before because we hired you. You're sleeping there tonight because you came back injured carrying our bottle. Keep your copper."

"That sounds dangerously like kindness."

"Don't spread it around."

Fair.

Before evening I made Mabel's promised route copy.

Not full district statement.

Route only.

White Reed to Sleeping Stone.

Slide bypass.

Three Sisters.

Southern shelf.

Ford caution.

Mirror maintenance access.

I marked date and observed conditions.

No guarantee.

Jonah said he'd take it down tomorrow.

Then he hovered.

"What?"

"Are you going back to Lakeward?"

I looked east.

Road there.

Lake beyond.

Routes beyond that.

Carrow much farther behind.

"Probably."

"Then what?"

I didn't know.

That used to bother me more.

"Find out."

He smiled.

"That's a terrible plan."

"Worked once."

"You got hit by a rock."

"Plan had weaknesses."

He laughed.

From channel side, Garrick shouted his name.

Jonah left.

I stayed by packing shed a while.

Morrow smelled like cut fruit, wet soil, wood smoke, and channel water.

Not sea.

Still not sea.

I noticed that again.

Home belonged to different air.

But for first time, distance did not feel like something happening to me.

I had gone somewhere because somebody needed a thing.

I had crossed the old route.

I had come back.

Tomorrow there would be readings and arguments.

After that, another road.

Maybe another story somebody insisted was only a story.

I hoped not.

Which was probably how I knew I did.

# CHAPTER 466

## THE SEEKER

I slept late.

Not dramatically.

One bell later than useful people.

Still counts.

Morrow packing loft had morning light through slats and fruit crates below making small wooden noises as workers moved them.

My shoulder remained bruised.

Less stiff than yesterday.

Better.

Not good.

I tested it anyway.

Forward lift.

Fine.

Side lift.

Ache.

Overhead.

Still insulting.

Crutch load.

Stable.

Trial limb remained wrapped.

No reason to prove anything to anybody.

Cash twenty-four copper.

Vale twenty-three.

Bits unspecified.

Ten-copper floor.

Fourteen copper of room.

That felt strange.

Not wealth.

Choice.

Choice was expensive enough that I noticed when I had some.

Below, Morrow had already started counting fallen fruit.

Actual counting.

Baskets by row.

Chalk marks.

Helena's idea survived sunrise.

Good.

I did not join.

My contract was done.

This mattered too.

Not every problem became mine because I understood it a little.

Garrick was at lower gate with two workers when I came down.

Gate had been restored one notch as agreed.

He saw me.

I expected argument.

Got a nod.

Small.

Tired.

Enough.

"Leaving?" he asked.

"Yes."

"Lakeward?"

"Probably."

"Probably is bad road planning."

"I've improved."

He almost smiled.

Almost.

Then he looked toward rows.

"District reading cart comes before noon."

"Good."

"You aren't staying for it."

Not question.

"No."

He looked at me again.

"Good."

Interesting.

Maybe he didn't want me there.

Maybe he understood what contract ending meant.

Maybe both.

"Route held," he said after a while.

"Parts did."

"Enough."

There was old path pride in that sentence.

I let him keep it.

Helena gave me a paper-wrapped breakfast from packing shed.

Paid? She waved away question.

"You made us twelve copper worth of trouble. Eat."

"I thought trouble was included."

"Apparently not."

Jonah arrived carrying Mabel's route-note copy under arm.

"I'll take this down."

"Thanks."

"She'll complain about handwriting."

"It's legible."

"That's not same thing."

Fair.

He looked at pack.

"You're actually leaving."

"That was the idea."

"Where?"

"Lakeward. Then I don't know."

"That's still terrible plan."

"Consistent branding."

He grinned.

Then became serious.

"Come back sometime."

Not dramatic.

No friendship oath.

Good.

"Maybe."

"That's not answer."

"You'll survive."

He rolled eyes.

I left Morrow by midmorning cart.

Two copper.

Twenty-four to twenty-two.

Still twelve above floor.

No anxiety spike.

Progress.

Danton Pike was not driving this one. Good. Fewer named people.

Driver was driver.

Cart rolled west through orchards.

Water boards had people around them now.

Not shouting.

Mostly looking.

Better use of eyes.

At one shared box, district gray coat crouched by scale marks while two growers waited.

I did not stop.

Morrow became rows.

Rows became scattered trees.

Lakeward glass roofs appeared near afternoon.

Shoulder liked cart more than crutches.

Everything liked cart more than crutches.

At Lakeward gate I had three immediate choices.

Cheap floor again.

Better room.

Or keep moving before shoulder convinced me otherwise.

I picked cheap room anyway.

Not because broke.

Because bed was bed.

**GLASS YARD BUNK / 2C**

Twenty-two to twenty.

Still ten above floor.

Luxury apparently was paying twice the floor rate and feeling smug.

I dropped pack.

Then did something I had not been able to do last time.

Went to market without needing job board first.

Dangerous behavior.

Lakeward afternoon market was louder than I remembered.

Glass workers ending shifts.

Orchard carts coming in.

Reed bundles.

Kiln heat from east works.

Fresh bread.

Metal polish.

Wet rope.

No salt.

Still no salt.

I noticed every time now.

At district office, sample file had not returned yet.

Expected tomorrow or later.

Fine.

I left home note at post counter.

Bits.

No exact count.

**Morrow job done. Reached Mirror Spring by old route through White Reed, Sleeping Stone, and Three Sisters. Route partly broken but a lower maintenance bypass still exists under some conditions. Brought sealed sample back. District accepted it for transport. Water dispute not solved. Contract paid 12c. Cash now 20c after travel and bunk, Vale 23c. Shoulder bruised from route accident but improving. Trial build healthy, not used on rough path. Not heading home yet. I liked the trip more than I expected. - Greg**

I stared at last sentence.

Could remove it.

Didn't.

Post clerk took note.

That was that.

Then market.

No objective.

I could afford curiosity now.

Not purchase, necessarily.

Curiosity.

Important difference.

Artifact stalls near glass quarter were better than South Larken.

Not richer exactly.

More specialized.

Kiln tools.

Fog glass.

Heat-mark tiles.

Retired orchard charms that mostly seemed designed to tell farmers things farmers already knew.

One stall had old route hardware.

Bell pins.

Channel tags.

Glass markers.

A dented brass frame shaped like half a wheel.

I stopped.

Seller looked up.

Older man, unnamed because not yet important.

"Broken," he said.

"Good sales technique."

"Saves time."

I pointed.

"What was it?"

"Road memory frame. Maybe."

Maybe.

Good word.

"What does that mean?"

"Means estate lot said road memory frame and I didn't argue with dead man's nephew."

Excellent provenance.

I leaned closer without touching.

Half-wheel brass frame.

Three slots.

One still held cloudy glass strip.

Tiny tooth track around inside rim.

No obvious charge.

No dramatic glow.

"Price?"

"Nine copper."

I laughed.

He didn't.

"Broken maybe-road thing for nine?"

"Brass is worth three. Old glass another two if cutter wants it. Teeth are fine. Nine if you want whole question."

That was actually better pricing logic than most sellers deserved.

Market could see salvage value.

Not dumb.

"Where from?"

"Estate lot out of east ridge. Cartman's family."

"Which road?"

"Didn't ask."

"Maker mark?"

He turned frame with rag.

Tiny stamped shape near hinge.

Not letters.

Three short lines over a circle.

I did not know it.

"Recognize?"

"No."

"Then why nine?"

"Because you stopped."

Fair.

I moved on.

Nine copper was not impossible now.

That was dangerous too.

But I didn't know enough.

Good.

A second stall sold old orchard measuring pieces.

Nothing pulled.

Third had retired ferry depth tongues.

Interesting, not needed.

Fourth had a glass case with small black stone disk under wire.

Label:

**ECHO STEP / INCOMPLETE / NO PAIR / 18C**

I stopped harder.

Seller noticed.

Different seller.

Woman with magnifying lens on cord.

"Incomplete," she said.

"I can read."

"Most interested men can't."

"What does echo step mean?"

"Old trail pair."

"Pair of what?"

She tapped case.

"Two stones. Set one at known place, carry one. Under charge they answer distance? Direction? Delay? Depends maker and generation. This one's missing mate. No useful response alone that I know."

Interesting.

Not miracle.

Not useful enough.

"Why eighteen?"

"Maker school."

She pointed underside mark through glass.

Same symbol.

Three short lines over circle.

There.

Connection.

"That mark."

"Old east-road school."

"Name?"

"Depends who you're arguing with. Lakeward catalog calls them **Sunwheel Roadworks**. Orchard people call them **Three Rays**."

Not a person name.

Good.

"How old?"

"Old enough that complete paired pieces sell to collectors before workers."

"This one?"

"Incomplete. Eighteen because mark is clean and stone body isn't cracked."

Market knows provenance.

Price reflects collector value.

No bargain yet.

"What roads used them?"

"East ridge mostly. Older orchard roads before current post glass. Some north passes."

I thought of half-wheel frame.

"Road memory frame?"

Her eyebrows moved.

"Where?"

"Other stall."

"Brass half-wheel?"

"Yes."

"Three slots?"

"Yes."

Now she was interested.

"That might be marker reader."

"Might?"

"Three Rays made too many things and labeled almost none."

"What would it read?"

"Old road disks maybe. Or station strips. Depends teeth."

"Useful now?"

"If you have matching route pieces."

"Which I don't."

"Then decorative question."

Good phrase.

I liked her.

Still didn't name her.

"Any surviving matching route?"

She leaned back.

"Maybe east beyond orchard country."

There.

"Where?"

"You buying information?"

"Depends price."

"One bit."

Fair.

I paid one bit.

She said:

"Old **Sunroad Cut**. East of Lakeward, past Bracken Vale road split. Most modern traffic goes south around ridge now. Old cut closed after bridge wash years ago. Collectors still find Three Rays bits there sometimes."

New place.

"Closed how?"

"Depends section. Old bridge gone, upper road partly walkable from west according to people who like making bad decisions recreationally."

"That seems specific."

"I sell to collectors."

"Any reason to go besides broken artifacts?"

"View."

"Terrible reason."

"Then don't."

I looked at Echo Step disk.

"Story?"

She smiled slightly.

"There's always story."

Of course.

"What one?"

"Old orchard saying. Sunroad never lost a traveler who kept three shadows."

I waited.

"That's it?"

"Mostly. Some versions say road could remember where you were if you carried the right stone. Some say bridge bells answered before fog. Some say people made all that up after road closed because broken infrastructure is more romantic than maintenance budgets."

Excellent.

"Which do you believe?"

"I believe complete Three Rays route sets sell for silver."

Best answer.

Silver.

There was economic pull.

Not free artifact.

Not cheap miracle.

A real collector market.

"How complete?"

"Reader plus matched route disks or strips. Working pair sometimes more. Depends condition and provenance."

"You seen one?"

"Complete? Once."

"Price?"

"Two silver and change."

I whistled.

Leg money territory.

Above leg money.

Potentially.

"Did it work?"

"Enough that buyer paid."

"What did it do?"

"Reader held strip. Disk placed across hall. Needle moved to one of three bands when charge pulse sent. Seller claimed route-position confirmation. Buyer claimed more. I claimed commission."

Good.

No omniscient artifact reveal.

Market-visible capability existed.

"Sunroad Cut still has pieces because?"

"Because if everybody knew where complete ones were, I wouldn't be telling you for one bit."

Fair.

I went back to first stall.

Half-wheel still there.

Nine copper.

Now I knew likely school.

Possibly marker reader.

Still incomplete.

Still question.

Could buy.

Cash twenty.

Nine would leave eleven.

Only one above floor.

Bad.

Could break floor intentionally because object potentially valuable.

But evidence weak.

No.

This was exactly why money mattered.

I wanted ability to buy question without becoming broke.

I did not have that yet.

"Seven," I said.

Seller snorted.

"Nine."

"Eight."

"Nine."

"You negotiate beautifully."

"You came back."

Correct.

I left it.

Painful.

Good.

I wrote in notebook outside market:

**Three Rays / Sunwheel Roadworks. Old east-road school. Matching route systems collector-valued. Half-wheel possible marker reader, incomplete, 9c. Echo Step orphan disk 18c. Complete sets reportedly silver+. Sunroad Cut east past Bracken Vale split; old bridge washed, upper road partly walkable from west per collector traffic. Saying: `Sunroad never lost a traveler who kept three shadows.` Meaning unknown.**

Then:

**Do not buy incomplete hardware just because it has a story.**

Then:

**But maybe go look at road.**

There.

Adventure door.

Not contract.

Not somebody begging.

Curiosity and possible value.

Different grammar.

I liked that immediately.

Dangerous.

At route board east of market:

**BRACKEN VALE / 4C CART / MORNING**

**EAST WORKS / 1C**

**LOWER ORCHARD LOOP / 2C**

No Sunroad.

Closed roads did not get boards.

Bracken Vale four copper.

Cash twenty.

After fare sixteen.

Still six above floor.

Could lodge farther east maybe.

Shoulder needed another day maybe.

Could leave tomorrow morning.

Reasonable.

I checked weather.

Dry tomorrow.

Fog east ridge early.

No rain watch.

Of course fog.

Sunroad saying involved shadows.

Three shadows.

Could be timing like Three Sisters.

Could be artifact reference.

Could be bullshit.

All acceptable.

I bought Bracken Vale morning seat.

Four copper.

Twenty to sixteen.

No panic.

That alone felt like progression.

Then I went back to bunk and did absolutely nothing useful for a while.

Shoulder deserved it.

I unwrapped trial limb to inspect.

No damage.

Dry.

Liner good.

I did not wear it.

Not yet.

Rough route unknown tomorrow after Bracken Vale.

Maybe stable town road later.

Magic remained 59 / 53 / 139.

I considered external attempt.

No.

Still no need.

The emergency deflection could remain itself without becoming homework.

Lot Sixty-Seven stayed packed.

Load tile.

Current reader.

Quietener.

All packed.

The strange thing was I now had too many potentially relevant tools for a road I knew nothing about.

That was new.

Before dinner I went back to the artifact quarter one last time.

Not to buy.

To look at half-wheel.

Still there.

Seller saw me.

"Nine."

"I know."

"Tomorrow it'll be ten."

"Then you're bad at inventory turnover."

He smiled this time.

I studied maker mark.

Three rays over circle.

Sunwheel.

Three Rays.

Three shadows.

Maybe related.

Maybe people built story around symbol.

Maybe symbol built around road phenomenon.

Maybe coincidence.

Good mystery shape.

I left it.

That was the real win.

Not buying because I wanted it.

Wanting enough to travel for better information.

Capital preservation as plot.

At bunk I wrote another home note draft but didn't send.

No need two notes same day.

Instead:

**Tomorrow Bracken Vale. Looking into old closed road called Sunroad Cut. Possible Three Rays roadwork artifacts. No contract. No promise. Just curious.**

I stared at `just curious.`

That was how trouble introduced itself when it wanted plausible deniability.

Shoulder throbbed.

Twenty-four copper had become sixteen through travel, lodging, and ticket.

Still above floor.

Vale twenty-three.

Leg balance still enormous.

And now I had seen an incomplete broken artifact for nine copper that I actively wanted but correctly did not buy.

Good.

Want created direction.

Direction created road.

Road created problems.

Apparently I had learned nothing.

Or exactly enough.

Tomorrow I was going east.

# CHAPTER 467

## THE PROSPECTOR

The Bracken Vale cart left before the lake decided what color it wanted to be.

Gray first.

Then silver.

Then one long strip of pale yellow where the morning sun found a gap between ridges.

I watched it from the back bench while Lakeward became roofs, then smoke, then nothing behind wet fields.

Sixteen copper.

Twenty-three with Vale.

One paid seat already spent.

No contract waiting at the other end.

That was new.

I had paid four copper because a broken road sounded interesting.

Financial discipline remained alive but had begun making questionable friends.

The cart carried sacks, two empty milk cans, a woman with a basket of green onions, and a man who slept through every rut with professional commitment.

Nobody needed a name.

Good.

My shoulder was better again.

Not healed.

Better.

Right crutch load no longer produced immediate complaint. Overhead still pulled the bruise sharply enough that I avoided it.

The trial limb stayed wrapped behind my pack.

Road was dry stone and packed earth, but a moving cart was not where I wanted to experiment with a shop's unfinished property.

We climbed east.

Lakeward country changed slowly.

Reed flats gave way to low orchards, then scrubby slopes where black rock showed through red soil.

Fog sat in the folds between hills.

Not thick enough to hide road.

Enough to make distance dishonest.

The driver stopped once at a ridge post to turn a small square plate from white side to black.

I watched.

"Fog mark?"

"East face."

"Meaning?"

He pointed without looking at me.

"Black toward Bracken means carts coming west know this side was fogged before first ring. White means clear. Gets turned back at midday if it burns off."

Mechanical information.

Human-maintained.

No magic required.

"Useful."

"That's why we do it."

Excellent conversation.

We continued.

Bracken Vale appeared as a dark green cut between two ridges.

Not a town exactly.

A road village stretched along one side of a narrow valley, houses and workshops tucked behind hedges of shoulder-high fern. Water wheels turned in a shallow stream. Roofs were steep. Stone chimneys taller than Lakeward's.

The place smelled like damp leaves, hot iron, and something sweet being boiled.

No sea.

Still.

The cart stopped beside a long shelter with three route boards nailed under its eave.

One board pointed south:

**LAKEWARD / DAILY**

One east:

**LOW RIDGE ROAD / CINDER CROSS / 5C**

One older board had been painted over twice.

Under the newer paint I could still make out:

**SUNROAD**

Arrow northeast.

No fare.

No service.

There we go.

I got down carefully.

Shoulder fine.

Pack heavier than memory because memory was a liar.

The driver unloaded sacks.

"Sunroad Cut?" I asked.

He looked at the old board.

"You walking?"

"Considering it."

"Don't use bridge."

"I heard it washed out."

"Then you heard correctly."

"Upper road?"

He shrugged.

"People go up."

"People come back?"

He looked at me for first time.

"Usually."

Useful distinction.

"Three Rays pieces?"

Another shrug.

"People come looking."

"Find them?"

"Sometimes they come back dirty."

I smiled.

He didn't.

Bracken Vale had a small public board beside the shelter.

Work notices.

Lost goat.

Wheelwright apprentice wanted.

Road closure south after heavy rain, crossed out.

One hand-lettered card:

**OLD SUNROAD: WEST WALK ACCESS OPEN TO MARKER 7. BEYOND 7 NOT MAINTAINED. BRIDGE ACCESS CLOSED. RIDGE OFFICE ACCEPTS HAZARD NOTES. NO SALVAGE REMOVAL FROM ROAD STRUCTURES.**

There.

Much better than collector hearsay.

Marker Seven.

Walk access open.

No salvage removal from road structures.

That last line mattered.

If I found Three Rays hardware bolted to a wall, it belonged to road structure until somebody said otherwise.

Loose object?

Unknown.

Do not build legal theory from sign punctuation.

I copied notice.

Then found ridge office.

It occupied half of a stone building beside stream, other half apparently a cooper's shop because barrels were everywhere and the whole place smelled of wet oak.

Inside, one clerk sat behind a desk covered in route slips.

No reason to name him yet.

"Sunroad walk access?"

He looked at crutches.

Then at me.

Not rude.

Assessment.

"To Seven is public at your own risk. Surface gets rough after Four."

"Bridge?"

"Closed means closed."

"Upper road past Seven?"

"Not maintained. Not prohibited. Ridge office doesn't certify it."

"Any map?"

He pulled a sheet from drawer.

Not free.

"Two bits for route copy."

I paid.

Worth it.

The map was simple.

Bracken Vale.

Old Sunroad climbing northeast.

Markers One through Seven.

At Seven, old road forked.

Lower branch descended toward washed bridge.

Upper branch continued along ridge, then disappeared from current maintained map after a symbol shaped like three short lines over a circle.

I stared.

"That's Three Rays."

The clerk looked down.

"Old roadworks mark."

"What did it mean here?"

"Depends which old book you believe."

Good.

"What books?"

"One says service station. One says route-check station. One says marker house. There's no house now."

"Anything left?"

"Stone pad. Maybe some iron. Collectors dug around it enough that office posted the salvage rule."

"So people did find pieces."

"People found things they sold as pieces."

Better answer.

"Any verified?"

He leaned back.

"You buying?"

"Not yet."

"Then you're already smarter than half of them."

I liked Bracken Vale.

"Verified Three Rays brass came out of Sunroad twenty years ago when bridge repairs exposed an old storage box. That was before my time. Ridge record has it. Since then? Mostly fragments. Teeth. Glass chips. Stamped fittings. Some real, some probably dropped by people who came looking for real ones."

"Dropped intentionally?"

"If you can sell a three-copper brass scrap for twelve because it came from a famous road, eventually somebody notices brass is portable."

Human beings remained the strongest magical ecology.

"What about the saying? Three shadows."

He frowned.

"Which version?"

Excellent.

"Sunroad never lost a traveler who kept three shadows."

"Collector version."

"There's another?"

"My grandmother said, `Don't trust Sunroad until your third shadow points home.`"

Different.

"What does that mean?"

"She said it meant leave late enough that morning fog has burned but early enough to be off ridge before evening fog comes back."

That was plausible.

"Third shadow?"

"She also said I asked too many questions."

"Strong family tradition."

He almost smiled.

"There are three standing stones before Marker Five. People say your shadow touches each one as sun moves. Or used to. Trees changed. Road changed. Could be that."

So:

collector version made Three Rays sound magical;

grandmother version made it weather timing;

standing stones offered physical route image;

maker symbol happened to be three rays.

No clean answer.

Good.

"Any reason not to walk to Seven today?"

He looked outside.

"Fog lifting. Dry yesterday. No rain watch. Shoulder?"

"Bruised. Functional."

"You alone?"

"Yes."

"Then I'd turn at Seven."

"Because?"

"Because Seven is where office stops maintaining road and you don't know road."

Fair.

"Anything dangerous before?"

"Loose outer edge after Four. Don't lean on old rail. Marker Six has a spring crossing that makes stone slick. No bridge issue until lower branch after Seven."

"Animals?"

"Mostly goats."

"Hostile?"

"Only morally."

There it was.

One dry line.

He was allowed one.

I copied hazards.

Then asked salvage.

"Sign says no removal from road structures. What about loose surface finds?"

He sighed.

This question had history.

"If it's obviously road hardware, leave it and report location. If it's loose common scrap, ridge doesn't care unless marked. If it carries Three Rays stamp, leave it and report. If you're not sure, leave it and report."

"Finder's fee?"

"Sometimes, if office recovers something useful or catalogable. Don't plan your retirement."

"I already have a retirement plan."

"Good."

It was mostly not getting killed.

The ridge office sold no adventure permit.

Charged no access fee.

Offered no guide.

Just information.

I left with map and less ignorance.

Before walking Sunroad, I needed somewhere to leave unnecessary weight.

Bracken Vale had a bunkhouse behind the cart shelter.

One copper for floor space and locked pack cage until next morning.

I paid.

Sixteen to fifteen.

Ten-copper floor left five discretionary.

I kept notebook, water, food, repair wrap, one spare liner, road map, line quietener, current reader, and HT-LP-4 tile.

Lot Sixty-Seven stayed locked with main pack.

Trial limb stayed locked too.

That took weight off shoulder.

Good.

No need to carry every interesting object I owned into every interesting place.

I ate bread and cheese from a street stall for bits.

Then started Sunroad.

Marker One was boring.

A waist-high stone with a carved numeral and newer white paint.

Marker Two was also boring.

Wonderful.

Road climbed through fern and low trees.

Old paving appeared in patches under dirt, broad flat stones fitted tightly enough that cart wheels once had an easier life than I currently did.

At Marker Three, I found first old rail.

Iron posts along outer edge with nothing between them.

Maybe once cable.

Maybe timber.

Rust deep.

I did not lean.

The valley dropped away to my left.

Bracken roofs looked smaller than expected.

Shoulder remained manageable.

I rested.

Then Marker Four.

Road narrowed.

Outer edge had slumped in two places.

Not dramatic.

Enough that I stayed inside.

No action scene required.

Sometimes hazard's job was to make you walk correctly.

Beyond Four stood the first of three stones.

Not marker stones.

Tall narrow slabs on inside edge, each with a shallow polished face angled differently.

First was nearly black.

Second visible farther up.

Third maybe another hundred paces beyond that.

Three shadows.

I checked sun.

Late morning.

My shadow fell behind me and slightly downhill.

It did not touch first slab.

I moved along road.

At one point, shadow of my crutch crossed slab face.

Probably not what grandmother meant.

I wrote nothing yet.

Second standing stone had a groove near base.

Not natural-looking.

Horizontal slot.

Empty.

Third had same.

Interesting.

Could have held signboards.

Could have held brass frames.

Could have held drainage guides.

No stamp visible.

At Marker Five, road bent sharply east.

Fog had mostly burned away.

From there I could see farther ridge and, below it, a pale scar where something large had cut through forest.

Old bridge valley maybe.

The three standing stones behind me were no longer visible together.

I walked back twenty paces.

Two visible.

Another twenty.

All three.

Sun angle made each cast a separate narrow shadow across road.

Three shadows.

Not one.

Could simply be why phrase existed.

Then I noticed the polished faces.

Each faced a slightly different direction.

If you stood at a particular stretch between Four and Five, reflected sunlight from the first was visible.

Move farther.

Second flashed.

Then third.

Not simultaneously.

Sequential.

Three bright flashes as position changed.

I stopped.

That was interesting.

Not magic.

Could be deliberate sighting system.

Polished stone faces as daytime route confirmation through patchy fog?

Maybe.

But trees now different. Original sight lines unknown.

I recorded:

**Three standing slabs between M4-M5. Polished angled faces. From westbound/eastbound approach? Current late-morning position gives sequential reflected flashes while moving. Base slots on at least 2 and 3. Could be route/timing markers. Could be unrelated later modification.**

I turned around and walked approach again.

One flash.

Then second.

Then third.

Repeatable at current sun angle.

No need for twenty trials.

This was road, not laboratory.

Marker Six had spring crossing exactly where clerk said.

Water ran over paving in a thin sheet.

Stone green at edges.

I used inside rough strip.

Slow.

No drama.

Then Seven.

Marker Seven was taller than others and older.

New white paint covered one face.

Behind it, road forked.

Lower branch descended steeply into trees.

A newer board blocked it:

**BRIDGE CLOSED. NO THROUGH ACCESS.**

Upper branch climbed.

No barrier.

No maintenance paint.

And twenty paces up, half hidden by fern, sat a rectangular stone pad.

Three short lines over a circle were carved into its front edge.

There.

Actual Three Rays site.

I stayed at Seven first.

Office advice: turn here.

I had daylight.

Shoulder good enough.

Weather clear.

Upper road not prohibited.

But unknown.

I could inspect the stone pad without committing beyond.

Twenty paces.

Reasonable.

I went.

Pad was roughly room-sized.

No house.

Four post holes.

Rust stains.

Broken ceramic bits.

Old dig scars around two corners.

Collectors had absolutely been here.

One shallow channel crossed pad center.

At its end was a brass-colored fleck.

I crouched carefully.

Not brass hardware.

Just mineral stain.

Good lesson.

The carved Three Rays mark was clean enough to trace with finger without damaging anything.

Three lines.

Circle.

The three lines were not parallel.

They spread slightly.

Rays.

Maybe sun.

Maybe road branches.

Maybe branding.

I looked back toward standing stones.

Could not see them from pad.

So no immediate magical alignment revelation.

Excellent.

Then I saw something under fern on uphill side.

A piece of black stone.

Round edge.

My heart did one stupid thing.

I moved fern aside with crutch tip.

Half a disk.

Black.

Palm-sized.

One drilled hole near edge.

No visible stamp.

Could be Echo Step fragment.

Could be roof tile.

Could be someone's dinner plate if people here hated ceramics.

It was loose.

Marked?

I turned it only enough to see underside without lifting away.

Three tiny incised lines near broken edge.

Not full maker mark.

Maybe.

Salvage rule:

If not sure, leave and report.

Damn responsible governance.

I sketched position.

Pad uphill side, two handspans from northeast post hole, under fern.

Did not remove.

Then noticed the drilled hole had a polished inner rim.

Use wear.

Maybe mounted.

Interesting.

Worth reporting.

Could earn nothing.

Could be catalogable.

Could confirm black stone disks actually belonged at site.

That was enough.

I stood.

And looked up upper road.

Past the pad, paving narrowed into two wheel tracks swallowed by grass.

Maybe fifty paces ahead, an old iron frame leaned beside road.

Beyond that, ridge curved out of sight.

No marker eight visible.

No current sign.

No obligation.

I had come to inspect road.

I had reached maintained limit.

Found real site.

Found possible fragment.

Learned three-shadows story had at least one plausible nonmagical road-marker interpretation.

Enough for one day.

Turn around.

I did.

That was the interesting part.

Not because I lacked curiosity.

Because I had more now.

Curiosity was not an instruction to spend every remaining margin immediately.

On return, the three standing stones flashed in reverse order.

Third.

Second.

First.

I stopped after first.

Reverse sequence.

Could matter.

Could simply be geometry.

Notebook:

**Return westward near midday: reflected flashes occur reverse order. Potential directional confirmation? Need original sight lines / old roadworks description before stronger claim.**

At Bracken Vale ridge office, I reported black half-disk location.

The clerk pulled a small form.

"You didn't take it?"

"Sign said not to."

He looked genuinely surprised.

"That does simplify things."

"Disturbing."

He copied my sketch.

"We'll send somebody tomorrow or next ridge round."

"If it's real?"

"If it's marked road hardware and catalogable, there may be a finder payment. Usually one to three copper depending usefulness."

"If it's roof tile?"

"You get spiritual growth."

Two dry lines.

Bracken Vale was corrupting him.

"Can I see the old record you mentioned? The storage box from bridge repairs."

He considered.

"Public copy costs one bit to pull."

I paid.

He disappeared into back room and returned with a copied inventory sheet.

Twenty years old.

**SUNROAD LOWER BRIDGE WEST ABUTMENT / REPAIR EXCAVATION**

Items recovered from sealed stone box:

three brass toothed frames;

six black route disks, paired by stamped numbers;

three cloudy glass strips;

one folded copper instruction plate, corroded;

miscellaneous fasteners.

Disposition: transferred Lakeward district archive, then two frames and four disks later deaccessioned to roadworks collection after duplication of markings.

There.

Real system.

Not collector fantasy.
