The shape.

I knew that shape.

Very well.

Support had always been full of it.

Not financial arbitrage necessarily.

Resource arbitrage.

One person had time.

Another had force.

Someone had information nobody else understood.

A healer had three minutes before overload.

A striker had one opening.

A retreat route had capacity nobody was using.

A useless artifact in one fight became decisive in another.

My entire old profession had been identifying mismatches and moving capability to where it mattered most.

I had been treating that instinct like a personality quirk.

It was probably my most expensive skill.

"Where are private commission notices?"

The clerk pointed.

I went.

The board was smaller.

Less standardized.

Worse handwriting.

Better numbers.

**SEEKING BULK DRY REED GLASS / LAKEWARD OR NORTH SHORE / 1 SILVER CEILING / COMMISSION BY AGREEMENT**

**BUYER SEEKS MATCHED HARBOR TENSION TILES / CONDITION SECONDARY / QUOTE REQUESTED**

**CINDER CROSS TOOL HOUSE SEEKS RETIRED ROUTE HARDWARE / NO SUNROAD PROVENANCE PREMIUM WITHOUT RECORD**

**PRIVATE HOUSE REQUIRES THREE SEASONAL FIELD CREWS / SOUTH ROAD / ORGANIZER FEE AVAILABLE / REFERENCES REQUIRED**

**WANTED: TWO VERIFIED QUIETENERS FOR COPPER LINE WORK / FUNCTION TEST REQUIRED**

I stopped.

I owned a retired line quietener.

One.

Not selling it automatically.

But now the object in my pack had a market context I hadn't asked about.

Then the Harbor Tension tile.

I owned one of those too.

HT-LP-4.

Bought for two copper.

Retired.

Last-peak load tile.

Useful enough that I'd kept carrying it.

Was somebody buying matched ones?

Maybe mine was worth three.

Maybe twelve.

Maybe nothing without its pair.

The point was I did not know.

And I had been congratulating myself for knowing the price of bunkhouse floors.

I copied every private notice that touched something I understood.

Then went to market.

Not artifact market first.

Freight market.

Lakeward had three adjacent lanes of sheds where goods changed from lake boats to road carts.

Reed glass.

Dried fish.

Wool.

Oil.

Salt substitutes from inland mineral beds.

Tool parts.

Ceramic pipe.

Fog-wick material.

Things I'd been walking past for chapters because they were scenery.

Now I looked at prices.

Not all posted.

Enough.

Dry reed glass strips sold retail by bundle.

North-shore bundles were cheaper when stained or mismatched.

Fog Bowl House used wick glass that had to meet narrower visual consistency.

Cinder Cross tool dealers paid more for retired route hardware if function could be demonstrated, but explicitly refused folklore premiums.

Different places valued different attributes.

Of course they did.

I sat on a public bench and felt stupid in a productive way.

The world had been screaming arbitrage at me.

Dorrin kelp.

Black Reed drying.

High Shelf resonance work.

Mere Ridge wool.

Lakeward glass.

Sunroad collector hardware.

Carrow theatre fabrication.

Harbor salvage.

Different specialists.

Different constraints.

Different markets.

And I had been earning two copper to wash the table they negotiated over.

That was harsh.

Also fair.

I had lacked capital.

I had lacked confidence.

I had lacked route knowledge.

I had lacked proof.

I had lacked mobility.

Some of those were still true.

But not all.

I knew more people now.

A lot more.

And I'd been underpricing that too.

Not because they owed me favors.

Because I knew who knew what.

That was different.

Sori understood commercial artifact judgment.

Hark & Venn understood work limbs and mechanical fitting.

Hessa understood field behavior and magic better than almost anybody I had met in this life.

Mara Fen knew old signal infrastructure.

Mera Tonn had repaired Lot Sixty-Seven correctly when I couldn't.

Della Orr knew resonance moth handling.

Lakeward archive staff knew Three Rays records.

Bracken ridge office knew Sunroad.

Morrow growers knew water pressure in ways district paper didn't.

Theatre people knew fabrication, labor, audiences, and how to make something convincing without making it real.

I knew people who did not know each other.

That mattered.

A lot.

I had spent my old life managing specialists stronger than me in their own lanes.

God-tier support did not mean I personally performed every function.

It meant I knew who should be doing what, what they needed, what they were missing, and when the plan needed to change.

Apparently I had decided reincarnation meant I should become a one-legged day laborer with excellent note-taking.

Interesting choice.

I opened notebook.

Yesterday:

**LEVER: EARNING POWER**

Today I crossed out the second line underneath it.

Not the lever.

The method.

**STOP SELLING DAYS.**

Then:

**EARN ON JUDGMENT, CONNECTION, SPREAD, OWNERSHIP.**

That was closer.

I did not have enough capital for ownership at scale.

So start with judgment and connection.

Brokerage.

Commission.

Arbitrage where risk was bounded.

I went back to the private board.

The dry reed glass notice had a contact desk number.

I found it in a merchant hall two streets over.

The buyer was a woman in a dark green vest with ink on three fingers.

No name needed yet.

"You have glass?"

"No."

Her face closed immediately.

"Then why are you here?"

"I know a dry house at Black Reed that sorts wick glass and pays careful hands to grade it. I know Lakeward Fog Bowl House uses narrower consistency. I don't know whether Black Reed has the grade you want, whether your ceiling is sane, or whether transport kills the spread. I'm asking what specification you're actually buying."

Her face reopened slightly.

"Not wick glass. Reed glass. Different product."

Good.

Wrong first assumption.

"Then I don't know enough."

"Apparently."

"What makes a useful introduction worth paying for?"

That changed conversation.

She tapped notice.

"I need six matched dry bundles, low warp, no salt bloom, delivered Lakeward inside twelve days. North shore has stock. North shore also has three buyers ahead of me and ferry delays. If someone finds equivalent inland stock or gets me ahead of delay without paying stupidity prices, I pay commission."

"How much?"

"Depends what you solve."

"Range."

"One to six copper for information or introduction. More if you take purchase risk yourself."

Still copper.

But not for a day.

For a connection.

And the ceiling on the purchase was one silver.

There.

Scale.

"What counts as equivalent?"

She gave me dimensions, acceptable warp, edge clouding limits, bundle count, bloom rejection.

I wrote them exactly.

"Can I take a copy?"

"One bit."

I paid.

Then I asked the important question.

"If I find six qualifying bundles at a seller you don't know, and you buy them directly, what's the introduction fee?"

"Three copper if they pass inspection. Five if landed price beats my current north-shore quote by ten percent."

"And if I negotiate seller down?"

"If I'm buying directly, your fee is still five. If you buy and resell, your margin is your problem."

Good.

Clear.

"No exclusivity?"

"Not for five copper."

Fair.

I left with specification.

No job.

A hunt.

Different feeling.

I did not know where equivalent reed glass sat.

But I knew something else.

The Fog Bowl House.

They sorted wick glass, not reed glass.

Still glass infrastructure.

People who handled one material often knew who handled adjacent material.

I went there.

The foreman from the board wasn't working front bench. Another sorter was.

I showed specification.

"Not ours," he said.

"I know. Who sells it?"

He read again.

"Low warp dry bundles? Lakeward buyers usually pull north."

"Usually."

"There's a kiln yard at Tollen that does reed backing for window shutters. Not same grade."

"Could they make it?"

"Maybe. They hate small orders."

"Six bundles."

He laughed.

"They'll hate you personally."

"Excellent. Who else?"

He thought.

"Old marsh works east of Cinder Cross used to dry it for signal houses. Don't know if they still do."

Cinder Cross.

The next road east.

There it was.

Adventure and leverage touching.

Not because quest marker.

Because markets had geography.

"Name of works?"

"Don't know current. Used to be called East Fen Glassyard."

I wrote it.

"Anything else?"

"If your buyer needs no salt bloom, inland makes sense. But inland reed tends thicker. Depends why she wants it."

I had not asked why.

Good catch.

Back to merchant hall.

The buyer looked annoyed to see me again.

"What is it for?"

"You don't need to know."

"Then I need functional constraints instead. Flex? Heat? Optical? Structural?"

She stared.

Then gave me a better answer.

"Lamp-house shutters. Needs to flex in frame without edge powdering. Optical quality irrelevant. Low warp because bundles are cut into paired strips."

Not fog bowls.

Not collector glass.

Functional.

"Thicker inland stock acceptable if it flexes?"

"Within gauge."

She added gauge tolerance.

Now I had something.

Not money yet.

Something.

I checked road board.

Cinder Cross.

Five copper.

That would take me from fifteen to ten.

Exactly floor.

Bad if I arrived and found nothing.

Unless the trip also had other value.

Private board already had:

**CINDER CROSS TOOL HOUSE SEEKS RETIRED ROUTE HARDWARE / NO SUNROAD PROVENANCE PREMIUM WITHOUT RECORD**

I had Sunroad records.

Not hardware to sell.

But I knew Lakeward market had an eighteen-copper orphan disk nobody should buy alone.

Could Cinder Cross Tool House want it as a component?

Maybe.

Could broker that too.

Now one route had two possible spreads.

Better.

Then I noticed the private crew notice again.

**THREE SEASONAL FIELD CREWS / SOUTH ROAD / ORGANIZER FEE AVAILABLE**

Managing people.

I almost ignored it because organizer sounded like somebody with a clipboard and a louder voice than mine.

Then I remembered first life.

Rooms full of specialists.

People stronger than me.

People smarter than me in narrow domains.

People with egos large enough to affect weather.

I had coordinated them while things tried to kill us.

Maybe I could manage three seasonal field crews.

Maybe not.

But the idea was not absurd.

That was new.

I copied notice.

Not applying yet.

Need scope.

Need references.

Need to know whether organizer meant hiring, scheduling, provisioning, field supervision, or being blamed when forty people hated lunch.

But I stopped dismissing it.

People leverage.

Not someday.

Potentially now.

I returned to bunkhouse with no additional copper.

Fifteen.

Same as morning.

Seven-copper job declined.

By ordinary accounting, I had made negative progress.

I disagreed.

I had found the scale transition.

Copper labor was not wrong.

It had kept me alive.

It had taught me roads.

It had introduced me to systems.

It had built proof.

But it was not where I should stay.

The next level was not simply bigger wages.

It was leverage.

Information before other people had it.

Knowing two markets.

Knowing two specialists.

Seeing when an object was mispriced because one buyer valued history and another valued function.

Taking commission for solving mismatches.

Eventually taking ownership risk when I had enough capital to survive being wrong.

And managing people when the value came from coordination rather than my hands.

Silver stopped feeling like fantasy currency.

It became scale target.

I wrote:

**NEXT ECONOMIC TIER: THINK IN SILVER.**

Not `have one silver tomorrow.`

Not `refuse copper.`

Think in silver.

Seek transactions, contracts, commissions, lots, or coordinated work where total value moved was silver-scale even if my first cut was still copper.

That was how you learned the market above you.

Then:

**FIRST ARBITRAGE TEST: CINDER CROSS.**

Potential lanes:

Reed glass introduction.

Route-hardware buyer.

Tool-house market information.

New inland suppliers.

Maybe nothing.

Five-copper travel cost.

Current cash fifteen.

Would hit ten-copper floor exactly.

Too tight unless I secured something else first or negotiated travel/work together.

Good.

Constraint.

Not stop sign.

I added:

**Find way east without paying full fare from capital.**

Work passage.

Cargo escort.

Commission advance, unlikely.

Shared cart.

Route task that paid in same direction.

There.

Highest leverage did not mean abandon adventure.

It meant make the road do more than one job.

I closed notebook.

For months I'd been proud that I could survive on copper.

I was still proud of it.

I just wasn't interested in proving it forever.

# CHAPTER 473

## THE FIXER

The legal way to Cinder Cross cost five copper.

The illegal way paid six.

That was not how the morning started.

The morning started with me standing at Lakeward east freight board pretending I was a respectable person.

Fifteen copper.

Twenty-three with Vale.

Ten-copper floor.

One reed-glass specification folded inside notebook.

One possible inland source near Cinder Cross.

One tool-house buyer there asking for retired route hardware.

And no interest in spending a third of my liquid cash just to discover East Fen Glassyard had become somebody's goat shed.

The public transport board offered exactly what I already knew.

**CINDER CROSS / 5C / MIDDAY CART**

The freight board was messier.

**BACKHAUL SPACE EAST / HALF RATE / BULK ONLY**

**DRIVER WANTED / TWO DAYS / OWN TEAM**

**CINDER TOOL FREIGHT / HANDLER RIDES FREE / LOADING REQUIRED**

Loading eliminated that one.

My shoulder was better, not forgiven.

Then a woman beside the board said, "You looking east?"

"Depends what east costs."

"You have two hands?"

I looked at crutches.

She looked at crutches.

"Fair."

Good start.

She was maybe forty, maybe fifty, with a wool vest full of chalk marks and a ledger tied shut with cord.

No name yet.

"What do you do?" she asked.

A week ago I might have said whatever was paying.

"Route notes. Field witness. Some artifact screening. Brokerage badly, so far."

"Brokerage badly?"

"No profit yet. Very clean record."

That got half a laugh.

She pointed at a covered freight wagon beyond the shed.

"I'm taking surplus repair stock to Cinder Cross Tool House. Buyer there keeps changing what he calls acceptable route metal. You know road hardware?"

"Some. Enough to know I don't know most of it."

"Better than collectors."

"Low standard."

"Can you tell Three Rays from ordinary bridge brackets?"

"Usually if stamped. Not necessarily from shape alone."

Her eyes sharpened.

"Good. Come look."

I did not move.

"Why?"

"Because I might pay you."

"Better."

The wagon carried four narrow crates and two bundled iron frames.

Everything looked old.

Nothing looked glamorous.

One crate had a Lakeward municipal surplus tag.

The second had a warehouse sale seal.

Third and fourth had both.

The iron frames were generic shutter or gate fittings.

Maybe.

She opened no crate.

Instead she handed me the sale inventory.

That mattered.

Ownership first.

All six lots came from a district warehouse closure on the south quay.

Declared categories:

retired lamp-house shutter fittings;

obsolete road-maintenance clamps;

miscellaneous iron and brass repair stock;

two lots marked **PRE-MERGER ROUTE HARDWARE / RELEASE STAMP REQUIRED BEFORE DISTRICT EXPORT**.

There.

"Those two stamped?" I asked.

"No."

"Then they can't legally leave Lakeward."

"They can if I wait nine days for heritage desk to inspect them and pay seven copper for release processing."

"Why didn't you?"

"Because Cinder buyer closes his repair lot in four days."

"Why does he care?"

"He melts ugly pieces, keeps useful fittings, and resells anything functional. He pays function. Not history."

"Sale price?"

"One silver for the whole mixed load if he takes it as listed."

There it was.

Silver.

Not mine.

Still real.

"And you want me to tell you if the route hardware is secretly collectible enough that smuggling it is stupid."

She did not flinch at word.

"Partly."

"Other part?"

"I need someone on the east end who can tell him what is and isn't Three Rays nonsense before he discounts the whole load."

"And the release stamps?"

"I was hoping you'd be less interested in those."

"Bad luck."

I crouched by crate labels.

No opening because seals intact.

Inventory gave piece counts and old warehouse categories.

Two route-hardware lots included six clamp bodies, three toothed arcs, four shade-glass frames without glass, and assorted anchor plates.

Three Rays?

Could be.

Could also be later compatible roadwork.

The old route system had broader infrastructure than one maker.

"Do you know where these were removed from?"

"Warehouse records say recovered stock."

"That's not answer."

She sighed.

"I bought them at municipal surplus auction. I have sale receipts. I did not strip a road."

"Could whoever supplied warehouse have stripped road?"

"Probably twenty years ago, if they did. Half this stock predates me."

"Any current road claims?"

"None on sale notice."

"Any safety reason the heritage release exists?"

She leaned on wagon.

"Originally? Yes. People were pulling marked hardware off active roads and selling it to collectors. District made all pre-merger road hardware export-controlled until somebody stamped provenance. Now it also catches warehouse junk the district itself sold me."

That sounded plausible.

Not proven.

"Show me receipts."

She did.

Municipal sale.

Lot numbers matched.

Buyer name matched her ledger initials, which I did not need to repeat.

Paid.

No obvious theft.

No active-road removal in sale description.

No hazardous material.

No person being coerced.

The law's purpose made sense.

The application here looked stupid.

That did not make violation safe.

"What happens if east gate checks?"

"Confiscation hold. Fine. Maybe charge if they think I concealed marked stock deliberately."

"Which you would be doing."

"Yes."

Good.

No euphemism.

"Driver know?"

She looked toward wagon.

"He knows there's export-controlled road stock."

"Does he get paid extra for that risk?"

Pause.

There.

"He gets normal day rate."

"Then no."

"No what?"

"No deal with me. You want me attached to something illegal while the person holding reins eats the same risk for ordinary pay. Fix that first."

She stared.

"You're worried about his morals?"

"His incentives."

"He's driven gray freight before."

"Then he knows what extra risk costs. Ask him."

She did not like being managed by a man she had met three minutes ago.

Reasonable.

But she called the driver over.

He was older, broad, missing two front teeth.

Also unnamed.

"You know the two route lots don't have export release?" I asked.

"Yep."

"You know load can be held?"

"Yep."

"You getting risk premium?"

He looked at freight woman.

"Apparently we're discussing that now."

I liked him.

She swore.

Then said, "One copper extra if it clears."

He said, "Two."

"One."

"Then take your own wagon."

She looked at me as if this were my fault.

It was a little.

"One and six bits."

He considered.

"Fine."

Good.

Now my turn.

"What are you offering me?"

"Ride east. Three copper if buyer takes full load."

"No."

"You haven't done anything."

"I'm already improving payroll."

Driver laughed.

She did not.

"Four."

"Six."

"Absolutely not."

"Your sale is one silver and misses window if you wait legal release. I am not asking percentage because we don't have a conversion I trust and I'm not pretending we do. Six copper plus ride if buyer accepts full load. Two copper plus ride if he rejects route lots but takes remainder because my screening helped preserve sale. Zero if I materially misrepresent what I know."

"Four full. One partial."

"Five full. Two partial."

She looked at driver.

He said, "Pay him five so we can leave."

"Nobody asked you."

"I'm paid by day."

Support network forming beautifully.

"Five," she said. "Ride included."

"And I do not lie about provenance to buyer."

"Fine."

"I do not open sealed crates."

"Fine."

"If gate asks me directly whether marked road hardware is aboard, I answer."

That one bothered her.

"Then what's the point?"

"I'm willing to participate in moving legitimately owned surplus without the release stamp. I'm not agreeing to invent a false story if questioned. Different risk profile."

"You have strange criminal standards."

"They're new. Give them time."

She rubbed face.

"Fine."

There.

Choice.

Knowingly illegal.

Morally acceptable enough by my current information.

Five copper upside.

Free five-copper ride.

Potential confiscation, fine, charge, damaged relationship with Lakeward road offices.

And if seller had lied about ownership, much worse.

I checked receipts again.

Still matched.

I checked load seals against inventory.

Still matched.

I wrote the relevant lot numbers in notebook.

Evidence discipline did not become less useful just because law was now obstacle instead of shield.

We left before midday.

No dramatic hidden compartment.

No night road.

No masks.

The freight woman simply listed the wagon at departure desk under the sale inventory's broad category:

**retired repair stock**.

True enough to be incomplete.

The clerk stamped road departure.

Did not inspect every crate.

I stood beside wagon and knowingly let the incomplete category pass.

That was the crime.

Smaller than stories made crime sound.

Still crime.

My heart did almost nothing.

That bothered me less than it probably should have.

Old life had contained worse.

Much worse.

I had not been a monster.

I had also not been clean.

Apparently some parts survived reincarnation better than mana.

Road east climbed out of Lakeward basin.

Cinder Cross lay beyond a long saddle where dark soil gave way to pale stone and scrub.

The driver let me ride front bench because climbing in and out of freight bed with shoulder was stupid.

We talked route.

Not crime.

He had run east road for eleven years.

Cinder Cross was not named for ash.

It was named for old black cinderstone mile blocks at the intersection of three freight roads.

People still argued about whether the blocks were volcanic, fired slag, or something magical.

Driver's opinion:

"They're rocks."

Strong scholarship.

Halfway, we passed a roadside inspection shelter.

Closed.

Nobody cheered.

Nobody said lucky.

We kept moving.

I noticed how quickly success could become ordinary before success had technically happened.

Dangerous.

At east district line, there was a gate.

Open.

One clerk.

One bored guard.

The wagon papers were checked.

Retired repair stock.

Destination Cinder Cross Tool House.

Municipal surplus receipt.

The clerk looked at total crate count.

Looked at seals.

Looked at us.

"Any charged glass?"

"No," freight woman said.

True.

"Active signal parts?"

"No," she said.

Also likely true.

The clerk stamped entry.

No one asked about pre-merger export release.

We rolled through.

Five copper saved.

Five copper potentially earned.

No lightning bolt from ethics.

Interesting.

Cinder Cross appeared by late afternoon.

Three roads met around a broad stone square with black rectangular blocks set at each corner.

Rocks.

Probably.

Tool House occupied an old mill building with half its windows replaced by shutters.

Functional people.

Good.

The buyer at loading yard was a short man with magnifying lens hanging on cord and a habit of tapping every piece twice before speaking.

He remained unnamed for now.

He checked sale inventory.

Then route lots.

Seals were opened by owner and buyer together.

Not me.

Inside:

clamp bodies.

anchor plates.

three toothed arcs.

four empty glass frames.

No black disks.

No shade glass.

No obvious collector-grade intact unit.

Buyer picked up first toothed arc.

"Sunroad?"

Freight woman looked at me.

There was my fee.

"Maybe compatible with that family," I said. "Not enough to claim Sunroad. No location provenance in sale record. Three Rays infrastructure used related toothed adjustment hardware, but these pieces aren't a complete system and I don't see a maker stamp on this one."

Buyer grunted.

Second arc had stamp.

Three lines over circle.

There.

"That one is Three Rays marked," I said. "Still no site provenance."

"Collector premium?" buyer asked.

"Not from me."

He smiled.

"Good."

He did not care about history.

He cared whether tooth spacing matched repair jigs.

He measured.

First arc wrong.

Second close.

Third exact to one jig.

The empty glass frames fit two standard shutter widths after minor redrill.

Buyer wanted function.

Freight woman had been right.

Then he found municipal sale tag wired under one clamp.

"Release stamps?"

Silence.

There it was.

He knew.

Freight woman said, "Municipal sale."

"I can read. Release?"

"No."

He looked at me.

"Broker?"

"Today."

"You know that's controlled export."

"Yes."

"And you brought it."

"I rode with it."

"Difference?"

"Probably not to a magistrate."

He laughed.

Not kindly.

Not cruelly.

Professionally.

Then he looked at seller.

"One silver was for clean paper."

Ah.

Risk repricing.

Good.

She said, "You posted no provenance premium."

"I posted no provenance premium. I did not post `bring me district trouble.`"

"They're legally purchased."

"Lakeward legally sold you half the problem."

Excellent line.

Negotiation began.

Not mine at first.

He offered lower terms.

Not denomination I could cleanly compare because mixed lot and no silver-copper conversion established.

Freight woman rejected.

They argued over whether release defect followed ownership or only export.

I did not know law enough.

So I asked what actually changed his value.

Buyer looked at me.

"If Lakeward comes asking, I need to prove I didn't strip road stock. Municipal sale receipt helps. Missing release means I may have to hold pieces instead of resell until paper is cleaned. Capital sits."

"How long?"

"Could be days. Could be season if offices fight."

"Which pieces create that problem?"

"Only marked route stock. Rest clean."

There.

Split transaction.

"Then price clean lots at full functional value and hold back route lots under conditional purchase."

Both looked at me.

"Meaning?" freight woman asked.

"He buys clean stock now. He takes custody of route lots only if he wants, but price/payment for those depends on whether municipal receipt plus later release can clear them. Or you keep them and solve paper yourself. Don't discount whole wagon for two dirty-paper lots."

Buyer said, "I'm not warehousing her problem for free."

"Then charge holding against route lots only."

Seller said, "And lose sale window."

"You already moved clean goods into his yard. Save what can be saved."

They hated it.

Then started calculating.

Good.

That was management too.

Not commanding.

Separating problems so one failure did not contaminate everything.

Eventually:

clean lamp/shutter repair lots sold now.

One unmarked route lot also sold as generic repair stock after buyer verified fit.

The marked Three Rays lot stayed with seller, still sealed after inspection? It had been opened. Buyer refused current purchase without release or deeper discount she would not accept.

So not full-load acceptance.

My contract said two copper if buyer rejected route lots but took remainder because my screening helped preserve sale.

That was what happened.

Two copper.

Plus free ride worth five.

Not five cash.

Not nothing.

Freight woman paid two.

Fifteen to seventeen.

Then buyer stopped me before I left.

"You know other marked route stock?"

"Some locations. Not ownership."

"I don't pay collector stories."

"I know."

"I pay matching tooth spacing and usable frames."

"I know now."

"Bring me clean paper and functional measurements before you drag metal here."

That was almost a relationship.

Not trust.

A specification.

Useful.

Freight woman was less happy.

"You cost me the route lot."

"Missing release cost you route lot."

"You could've called it Sunroad collector junk and pushed him."

"He doesn't pay history."

"Could've tried."

"Then next time he assumes I'm selling stories."

She looked at me.

"You care about next time already?"

Yes.

That was the point.

"Unfortunately."

She shook head.

But before leaving she said, "If I clean the release, you want another cut?"

"If I find buyer or improve terms."

"Three Rays lot is mine."

"I know."

"Don't broker around me."

"Then don't broker around me either."

Pause.

She smiled once.

"Maybe you're not terrible at this."

Dangerous sentence.

I went to Cinder Cross lodging board.

One copper floor.

Seventeen to sixteen.

Still six above survival floor after arriving east.

If I had taken normal cart, I would have arrived with ten.

Instead I arrived with sixteen.

Illegal choice had improved my position by six copper relative to the legal baseline.

That was material.

No punishment.

No immediate consequence.

No lesson that rules existed for secret reasons I'd failed to respect.

Just a better purse and a new buyer relationship.

Temptation worked better when rewarded.

I wrote that more carefully.

Not those words.

**GRAY FREIGHT RESULT:**

Municipal ownership supported by receipt.

Export release knowingly absent.

Driver informed and paid extra risk premium.

No active/safety hardware identified.

East gate did not ask controlled-export question.

Buyer discovered missing release independently.

Clean lots sold.

Marked route lot rejected pending paper or discount.

My fee: 2c + transport.

Cash: 17 before lodging, 16 after.

Then:

**Important: illegality did not create profit. Mispriced delay created profit. Law was part of spread.**

That felt precise.

The law made clean surplus slower to move.

That delay had value.

So did willingness to carry enforcement risk.

So did knowing when not to lie further.

Could have gone worse.

Could go worse next time.

Still worked.

I was not sorry.

That answer came too quickly.

Old me would have recognized it.

Maybe approved.

Maybe laughed at how long it took.

Tomorrow: East Fen Glassyard.

Reed glass.

Actual first sourcing hypothesis.

And now Cinder Cross Tool House had given me functional measurements for route hardware.

One road.

Two markets.

Six copper better than legal baseline.

Silver-scale goods beginning to move around me.

This felt less like climbing a ladder.

More like finding the side door.

I had always been good at side doors.

# CHAPTER 474

## THE SOURCER

East Fen Glassyard existed.

That was the first good news.

It had not become a goat shed.

It had become three sheds, two kilns, one leaning drying house, and a yard full of cut reed frames that looked like somebody had tried to build a fence out of bad decisions.

I stood at the west gate with sixteen copper, twenty-three still with Vale, one Lakeward specification, and exactly enough confidence to be irritating.

A woman in a leather apron looked at me through the gate.

"What do you want?"

"Dry reed glass."

"Everybody wants dry reed glass."

"Six matched bundles. Low warp. No salt bloom. Lamp-house shutter gauge."

That changed her face.

Not friendlier.

More specific.

"Who sent you?"

"Nobody. I have a buyer."

"Then you have paperwork."

I took out the copied specification.

She opened the gate.

Good start.

East Fen sat maybe half a mile off the Cinder road where the land flattened into pale marsh grass and low drainage cuts. No salt smell. That mattered, maybe. The buyer had rejected bloom, and the Fog Bowl sorter had said inland made sense for that reason.

The yard itself was old enough that newer work had grown around older bones.

Brick kiln mouths.

Timber drying racks.

A narrow water channel running under two sheds.

Glass bundles stacked on slatted shelves with cloth between them.

Most of it was not the translucent sheet glass I had expected the first time somebody said reed glass months ago.

These were long narrow strips made from fused reed-silica composite, cloudy amber to pale green, flexible enough to bow slightly without cracking.

Fantasy world.

There you are.

I'd spent three days talking about money until the world had started sounding suspiciously like accounting with magic nearby.

The apron woman handed my specification back.

"Six matched is the problem."

"Why?"

"We can make six."

"That wasn't the question."

She looked at me again.

"You're annoying."

"I've been told it scales well."

A voice from inside the nearest shed said, "Lena, if he's buying, let him be annoying."

There.

Name.

Lena.

Not necessarily owner.

I did not need to assign her a biography because somebody had used one syllable.

A man came out carrying a bundle gauge under one arm. Older than Lena, gray at beard, left thumb wrapped in linen.

He held out hand for the paper.

"Lakeward lamp shutters?"

"Yes."

He read.

"Whose order?"

"Buyer in Lakeward merchant hall. I have introduction terms if qualifying stock passes."

"Name?"

I hesitated.

I had not written the buyer's name because she had not given it in a way I trusted myself to remember from state.

Better not invent one.

"Desk contact is on the copy."

It was.

He looked at the mark.

"Knew that house."

Not the person.

Good.

He read gauge tolerance twice.

"We have four that pass this now."

"Four bundles?"

"Four matched dry."

"Buyer needs six."

"Then buyer can need things somewhere else."

Lena smiled.

Apparently that was the customer-service department.

"How far are the next two from passing?"

The man tapped the paper against his leg.

"Depends what you mean."

"Gauge, warp, bloom, edge powdering. Which thing fails?"

That earned me entry into the shed.

Not trust.

Entry.

Four bundles sat on center racks with slate tags.

Two more sat separately near a wall where warm air moved through slatted vents.

The man took a strip from first four.

Bent it against a curved wooden form.

Not much.

Enough.

No white edge dust.

He checked thickness at three points with a split gauge.

Then laid it against a straight board.

Low warp.

The fifth bundle looked similar until he put it to the board.

One end lifted.

Not terrible.

Enough.

"Drying pulled it," he said.

"Can it settle?"

"Maybe."

"How long?"

"Properly? Five or six days."

Buyer had twelve days from yesterday.

Travel back to Lakeward.

Inspection.

Margin.

Still possible.

"What about sixth?"

He showed me.

Gauge good.

Warp good.

Then rubbed one edge with thumb.

Fine pale powder came off.

"Bloom?"

"No. Wrong failure."

Good correction.

"What is it?"

"Outer reed binder ran hot. Edge skin too brittle."

"Can it be recut?"

"Bundle width would fall under spec."

"Can it be re-faced?"

He gave me the look people gave when a non-specialist used a specialist verb.

"Not without making it worse."

So four.

One maybe in six days.

One dead.

Not a deal.

Unless there was more stock.

"Anything not dry yet?"

Lena laughed.

The man did not.

"Plenty."

"Anything already within gauge?"

"Some."

"Enough for one bundle?"

"Probably."

"Could you dry it inside six days?"

"No."

"Why?"

"Because wet glass doesn't care about your buyer."

Fair.

"What controls drying time?"

That was the useful question.

Not "can you hurry."

Not "what if we pay more."

Process first.

Airflow.

Rack spacing.

Humidity.

Initial water content.

Binder composition.

Thickness.

Temperature, but not too much because uneven heat warped strips or hardened edges.

The old drying house used passive cross-draft through high vents.

Six to nine days for this gauge depending weather.

Current weather was damp.

Worse.

I walked outside and looked at the roofline.

Two black iron vanes sat above the drying house.

One moved.

One did not.

"What's that?"

The man looked up.

"Draft pair."

"Artifact?"

"Old yard tool."

There it was.

He did not sound impressed.

Good sign.

"What does it do?"

"Pulls air through when the channel runs."

"One isn't moving."

"Yes."

"Broken?"

"Not exactly."

Excellent answer.

"What does not exactly mean?"

Lena said, "It means don't touch it."

Also excellent.

The man led me around back.

A bronze spindle ran from the water channel into a boxed housing under the drying wall. The channel turned a small paddle wheel. Inside the box, something hummed faintly enough that I felt it more in the crutch tips than heard it.

One roof vane rotated in short steady turns.

The other remained angled east.

"Draft pair used to alternate," he said. "One pulls upper vents, one pulls lower. Keeps the room from drying one face harder than other."

"And now?"

"Upper works. Lower locks after three or four turns."

"Why?"

"If I knew, I'd have fixed it."

"Who services it?"

"Used to be Fenwright shop."

"Used to?"

"Closed."

"How long has lower been bad?"

"Two years."

"Then how do you dry?"

"Slower. Rearranged racks. More hand turning. Works."

There.

The glassyard was not failing.

It had adapted.

Important distinction.

"Would a working lower draft shorten six-day window?"

The man crossed arms.

"For wet stock? Maybe. If it worked correctly."

"Not enough confidence."

"No."

"What about the warped fifth bundle?"

Pause.

"That one might."

Because it was already dry enough.

The problem was not make new glass in impossible time.

The problem was relax and finish one nearly qualifying bundle, then find one more from current semi-dry stock close enough to finish.

Maybe.

Still one short.

I asked to see semi-dry inventory.

Lena said, "You planning to buy the yard?"

"No. I currently have sixteen copper."

"Then definitely not."

The man laughed once.

We went to second drying shed.

The semi-dry stock was less orderly.

Different orders.

Different gauges.

Different buyers.

And, crucially, different ownership status.

Some bundles were already committed.

Some were production overage.

Some had been rejected for cosmetic color variation that did not matter to my buyer because optical quality was irrelevant.

That last category caught me.

"These why rejected?"

He lifted one.

Pale green with darker streak down center.

"Window backing order. Visible through outer lattice. Customer wanted even color."

"Structure?"

"Fine."

"Gauge?"

He checked.

Inside tolerance.

"Warp?"

Inside.

"Edge?"

Clean.

"How dry?"

"Another two days at least."

"How many?"

"Two bundles."

There.

Not matched by appearance.

Buyer did not care about optical quality.

Matched meant functional dimensions and performance, not color.

I reread specification.

Low warp.

Gauge tolerance.

No salt bloom.

Flex without edge powder.

Six bundles.

It did not say color matched.

I had assumed matched meant visual matching because glass.

Stupid.

Useful stupid.

"Does 'matched' on this trade usually mean color too?"

The man read contact mark again.

"For lamp-house shutter work? Usually gauge and batch behavior. Depends buyer."

"Then I need to ask."

I did not pretend.

Cinder Cross had a message counter.

Not instant.

Still better than riding two days because I was shy about clarification.

I could send a paid road message back toward Lakeward merchant hall.

Cost?

One copper for next-run commercial note, according to Cinder posting I had passed.

A copper mattered.

Not crisis.

This was what capital was for.

Information before movement.

I went back to Cinder Cross that afternoon.

Shoulder tolerated the road because I hired the cheap local cart seat from the fen junction instead of proving I could crutch six miles for free.

Bits.

Fine.

At message counter I wrote:

**EAST FEN HAS 4 PASSING DRY BUNDLES. POSSIBLE 2 ADDITIONAL COSMETICALLY MISMATCHED BUNDLES WITHIN APPROX 2 DAYS, GAUGE/WARP/EDGE EXPECTED TO SPEC. DOES MATCH REQUIRE COLOR/OPTICAL CONSISTENCY OR FUNCTIONAL GAUGE/BATCH ONLY? ALSO ONE DRY WARPED BUNDLE MAY BE RECOVERABLE WITH CONTROLLED DRAFT. PLEASE CONFIRM ACCEPTABLE MIX BEFORE I NEGOTIATE.**

Then paid one copper.

Sixteen to fifteen.

Still five above floor.

I disliked that less than I would have a month ago.

Not because fifteen was more money.

Because the copper was buying decision quality on a silver-scale transaction.

That was a different species of expense.

I stayed in Cinder because there was nothing useful to do at the yard while waiting.

Tool House buyer saw me pass.

"More illegal metal?"

"Glass."

"Worse."

"Why?"

"Breaks."

Strong expertise.

I asked whether he knew anyone who serviced Fenwright draft pairs.

He stopped tapping a hinge.

"East Fen's?"

"Probably."

"Lower vane still sticking?"

There.

"Yes."

"Then no."

"You know the problem?"

"I know everybody asks after they decide a dead shop owes them service forever."

"Anyone local?"

"There's a mill tuner south edge who works water-drive governors. Draft pair isn't governor."

"Close enough to inspect?"

"Close enough to charge you for being wrong."

Good.

"What does inspection cost?"

"Ask her."

Her.

I went.

The mill tuner operated from a shed beside a grain wheel, mostly belts, bronze teeth, regulator arms, and three small stones that floated in a vertical cage to show load.

Her name was Tessa because a customer called it while I waited.

Again, no biography required.

I described the draft pair without pretending I understood mechanism.

One upper vane worked.

Lower locked after three or four turns.

Water drive active.

Two-year adaptation by rack turning.

Could she inspect without promising repair?

She said, "Two copper plus ride."

"Ride from Cinder to East Fen?"

"You provide."

"One copper inspection if you're already going that direction?"

"I'm not."

"Then two."

Reasonable.

Too much for me to speculate casually.

Could ask yard to split.

Better.

I returned East Fen next morning after one-copper message had not yet returned because roads remained roads.

The gray-bearded man was turning racks by hand with Lena.

I asked his name because if I was going to propose money, "the man" was becoming stupid.

"Orin."

Good.

"Orin, local mill tuner will inspect the draft pair for two copper plus ride."

"I know Tessa."

"Then why haven't you?"

"Because she told me two years ago she doesn't service Fenwright draft."

"Inspection, not service."

"What good is inspection if she won't fix it?"

"Could tell you whether the failure is mechanical enough for somebody else to fabricate."

He gave me a look.

"Somebody else who?"

"Don't know yet."

Correct answer.

"You paying?"

"No."

"Then no."

Also correct.

I sat on a low beam.

That was the commercial problem.

Everybody wanted uncertainty removed.

Nobody wanted to fund removing it because the immediate value belonged partly to somebody else.

Lakeward buyer wanted six bundles.

East Fen could maybe produce them.

Tessa could maybe identify a fault.

Some fabricator could maybe repair it.

I could spend my own copper and become a philanthropist with excellent notes.

No.

Broker.

Move value, not absorb everyone's risk.

"What if inspection cost comes out of sale only if buyer takes six?"

Orin stopped.

"If no sale?"

"No inspection."

"Tessa won't agree."

"Then yard pays one copper, I pay one, and if buyer takes six you reimburse my one from price."

Now I was taking bounded risk.

One copper.

Potential five-copper commission if landed terms beat north quote materially.

Also market relationship.

Potential future yard output.

Worth considering.

Orin said, "Why would I reimburse your copper?"

"Because if her inspection gets you one qualifying bundle inside window, you get a sale you do not currently have."

"And if she says useless old junk?"

"I lose one too."

"That supposed to comfort me?"

"No. It's supposed to make me less annoying."

Lena said, "Didn't work."

Progress.

Orin thought.

Then shook head.

"Not yet. Wait for buyer answer."

Good.

No need to solve a machine before knowing whether we needed it.

That was Hessa's influence and basic adulthood working together.

The reply came late that day.

Commercial message slip.

Buyer had written only:

**MATCH = GAUGE / FLEX / DRYNESS / BUNDLE LENGTH. COLOR IRRELEVANT. MIXED COLOR ACCEPTABLE. SIX MUST PASS INSPECTION. SEND ASK + EARLIEST READY + TRANSPORT TERMS.**

Beautiful.

No need for draft repair.

Four dry.

Two cosmetic rejects semi-dry, two days expected.

Six.

Potentially.

I took the reply to Orin.

He checked those two again.

One had dried enough overnight to show slight edge pull on three strips.

Not failure yet.

Risk.

The other looked better.

"So five?" I asked.

"Four certain. One likely. One maybe."

Not six.

I hated honest tradespeople.

They made optimism expensive.

"What makes maybe become likely?"

"Turn twice today. Keep lower rack off warm wall. Dry slower."

"That costs time."

"One extra day."

Buyer had time if transport worked.

"What is earliest honest ready?"

"Three days from now."

"Not two?"

"Three."

I wrote three.

"Ask?"

He named a price for six bundles that I did not have a useful denomination conversion framework to compare against Lakeward's north-shore quote.

It was below one silver ceiling.

That was enough to continue.

"Does that include road to Lakeward?"

"No."

"To Cinder?"

"Yes if our cart is free."

"Which day?"

He looked at Lena.

She answered without checking paper.

"Fourth morning."

Different expertise.

"Can six be at Cinder fourth morning if they pass third-day check?"

"Yes."

"Then Lakeward transport from Cinder is my problem?"

"Your buyer's."

"Could be mine if solving it increases fee."

Orin shrugged.

Correct.

I went back to Cinder freight board.

Lakeward backhaul existed daily, but prices varied.

One carrier had space fourth morning.

Reed glass required covered slat rack, no top stacking.

He had that.

He wanted payment from shipper on delivery.

Good.

I gave him bundle dimensions.

He quoted.

I wrote it, did not bargain yet.

Then I did the actual broker work.

I sent one more commercial message to Lakeward.

Another copper.

Fifteen to fourteen.

Four above floor.

The old me would have started getting twitchy.

Current me noticed the floor.

Then asked what the copper bought.

It bought a live quote with source, readiness, and transport.

Worth it.

Message:

**EAST FEN CAN OFFER 6 FUNCTIONALLY MATCHED BUNDLES SUBJECT FINAL THIRD-DAY PASS. 4 DRY PASS NOW. 2 COLOR-REJECT OVERAGE CURRENTLY DRYING, COLOR ONLY OUTSIDE PRIOR CUSTOMER PREFERENCE. EARLIEST CINDER FOURTH MORNING. COVERED SLAT BACKHAUL AVAILABLE SAME MORNING. SELLER ASK + CARRIER QUOTE ATTACHED. I HAVE NOT COMMITTED BUYER OR SELLER. CONFIRM WHETHER LANDED TERMS QUALIFY 3C OR 5C INTRODUCTION CONDITION IF FINAL INSPECTION PASSES.**

Then I waited.

That was the worst part of brokerage.

Not bargaining.

Waiting while other people remained independent organisms.

I spent the afternoon at Tool House, not working for them, just watching a buyer reject a crate of old latch springs because half had been annealed badly.

He saw me watching.

"You're learning to enjoy people saying no."

"Useful market function."

"Expensive hobby."

"Have you ever bought from East Fen?"

"Glass? No."

"Why not?"

"I sell tools."

"Strong boundary."

He tapped a spring twice.

"Try one."

"What?"

"Reject one."

He handed me two springs.

One looked cleaner.

I flexed both.

The cleaner one stayed slightly open.

The ugly one returned.

"Clean one bad."

"Why?"

"Looks overworked?"

"Maybe. Again."

I did.

He said, "You don't know."

"I don't."

"Good. Most brokers become appraisers after three successful introductions."

"How long before they become surgeons?"

"Usually after lunch."

I liked Cinder Cross.

The Lakeward reply arrived next morning.

Longer this time.

**IF FINAL SIX PASS SPEC AND SELLER ASK REMAINS AS ATTACHED, LANDED QUOTE BEATS CURRENT NORTH-SHORE PURCHASE ENOUGH FOR 5C INTRODUCTION FEE. BUYER WILL PURCHASE DIRECT FROM EAST FEN AFTER RECEIVING FINAL PASS MARKS. CARRIER MAY BILL BUYER ON DELIVERY IF SEALS / BUNDLE TAGS MATCH. NO PURCHASE RISK REQUESTED FROM BROKER. DO NOT SUBSTITUTE BUNDLES WITHOUT CONFIRMATION.**

There.

Five copper.

Not guaranteed.

But earned by solving source plus logistics.

More important, the goods moved at silver scale.

Not my silver.

That was fine.

I was finally standing near transactions larger than my purse.

I returned East Fen with buyer confirmation.

Orin read it.

"So she buys direct."

"Yes."

"You get five."

"If your six pass."

"You get five for talking."

"I've done an offensive amount of walking for this talking."

Lena said, "Mostly riding."

"Strategic mobility."

She ignored me.

Third-day inspection took almost an hour.

No magic spectacle.

No miraculous last-minute save.

Four original bundles passed.

First color-reject bundle passed gauge, warp, flex, edge.

Five.

Second bundle had one strip with edge powder.

My stomach dropped.

Orin removed it.

Checked replacement strips from same overage batch.

One passed.

Another warped.

Third passed.

Bundle count allowed individual strip replacement before final tying because it had not been sealed as a finished bundle yet.

Good.

He rebuilt the bundle with two replacement strips.

Then checked the entire bundle again.

Gauge.

Warp.

Flex.

Edges.

Six.

He did not smile.

Professionals were exhausting.

Lena tied bundle cords and marked each with yard lot, dry date, gauge, and inspection initials.

I copied marks onto my sheet.

No claim beyond what we saw.

At Cinder next morning, the carrier checked six tags against paper.

Orin's cart had brought them exactly when Lena said.

The carrier loaded them onto covered slat rack with cloth spacers.

No top stacking.

Buyer in Lakeward had agreed to direct purchase and delivery billing.

I was not buying inventory.

I was not taking title.

I was not pretending I had capital I did not.

The carrier gave me departure receipt.

"Want to ride back?"

I considered.

Lakeward.

Home eventually.

Pending letters.

Work limb.

Vale.

But Cinder still had market value.

Also my commission was not payable until buyer inspected delivery.

If I rode with freight, I could be present.

Could help prevent a stupid handling failure.

Could also look like I was supervising goods I did not own.

Still useful.

"Is ride included?"

He looked at me.

"No."

"Then suddenly I trust your professionalism from afar."

He laughed.

I stayed.

Not every deal required Greg physically attached to it like a barnacle.

That was part of leverage too.

Let capable people do their jobs.

The wagon left.

I had fourteen copper.

Five-copper commission pending, not earned yet.

Twenty-three with Vale.

Bits unspecified.

Ten-copper floor active.

Protocol still fifty-nine, fifty-three, one hundred thirty-nine.

No magic.

No trial limb.

Shoulder better.

And one silver-scale purchase moving west because I had asked three people better questions than the questions they were already answering.

That felt familiar.

Not merchant familiar.

Support familiar.

One market had a delayed supply problem.

One yard had usable stock misclassified by color for a different customer.

One carrier had the right rack at the right time.

Nobody was stupid.

Nobody gave me a miracle price.

Nobody needed me to be the best glassmaker, carrier, buyer, or appraiser.

I just had to see the mismatch and keep the parts from contaminating each other.

That was old work in a new coat.

I opened notebook.

**EAST FEN: 6 shipped, direct sale pending Lakeward inspection.**

**4 existing dry. 2 recovered from cosmetic-color reject overage because buyer's match requirement was functional, not visual.**

**No draft-pair repair required. Do not invent a machine problem when specification clarification solves the deal.**

I underlined that one.

Twice.

Then:

**Carrier: covered slat backhaul, no top stack, buyer bills on delivery.**

**My risk: 2c messages spent. No inventory/title risk. 5c fee pending successful inspection.**

Cash fourteen.

Potential seventeen if fee came through.

Not life-changing.

That was not the point.

The point was one silver-scale transaction had moved through my hands without requiring one silver of my own.

There.

Leverage.

Actual leverage.

I had spent two copper to create enough information that other people's capital, goods, equipment, and labor could line up.

If it failed inspection, I lost two and learned where the mismatch really was.

If it passed, I made three net copper over message costs plus a buyer and seller who both had reason to answer me next time.

That was better than seven copper for a day.

Not always.

But differently.

The difference mattered.

At Tool House, the buyer with the magnifying lens asked, "Glass gone?"

"West."

"Paid?"

"Pending inspection."

"So you're still poor."

"Temporarily between revenue recognition events."

He stared.

"I regret asking."

"That's growth."

He pointed toward the yard.

A small cart had arrived with two iron housings and a crate of broken water-drive parts.

"There's your next education."

"Do I get paid?"

"No."

"Then my education has boundaries now."

"Good."

I turned away.

Then stopped.

One of the housings had a black iron vane bracket shaped almost exactly like East Fen's lower draft assembly.

Not identical.

Similar family.

I looked once.

Did not declare provenance.

Did not buy it.

Did not make it a quest.

But I wrote the lot mark down.

Because information compounded.

Then I went to find lunch.

Bits.

Ordinary.

Not a philosophical crisis.

The silver was moving without me.

That might have been the most important part.

# CHAPTER 475

## THE COORDINATOR

The first thing I learned about managing people was that nobody wanted to be managed by a stranger with two crutches.

Reasonable.

The second thing I learned was that they were perfectly willing to yell at me when nobody else had decided what happened next.

Also reasonable.

Cinder Cross Tool House opened before breakfast had finished becoming breakfast.

The front doors were still barred when I arrived, but the yard gate stood open because freight apparently had different religious beliefs about morning.

I had fourteen copper.

Twenty-three still with Vale.

Five copper possibly traveling toward me from Lakeward if six bundles of reed glass survived a road, an inspection, and at least one person with authority finding a reason to be difficult.

I did not count it.

Pending money was not money.

It was hope wearing paperwork.

The buyer with the magnifying lens stood in the yard beside yesterday's broken water-drive lot.

Two iron housings.

One crate of snapped drive teeth.

One black vane bracket that still looked irritatingly similar to East Fen's lower draft assembly.

I looked at it once.

Then looked away.

Growth.

The buyer noticed.

"Not buying it?"

"I don't know what it is."

"Yesterday that didn't stop you looking."

"Looking is free."

"Not if you do it long enough."

I respected that.

A wagon rattled into the square outside.

Then another.

Then a third cart came around the north corner too fast, braked hard, and made everybody nearby discover religion.

The Tool House buyer swore.

"That's early."

"What is?"

"Everything."

Excellent.

The first wagon carried a long cast frame wrapped in rope and sacking. The second held two squat iron drums and a crate stack. The third cart carried six men, three timber rollers, and enough chain to imprison a minor god.

None of them belonged to the same crew.

I knew because all three groups started arguing before the wheels stopped.

A narrow man in a blue cap jumped down from the first wagon.

"We were told west bay."

A broad woman from the chain cart said, "West bay's blocked."

A Tool House worker shouted from the loading door, "It isn't blocked."

She pointed.

"It has a fucking axle stand in it."

"That's not blocked."

"It is if you're moving an eight-span frame."

"Move the stand."

"With what hands?"

"Yours?"

Promising workplace.

The buyer closed his eyes.

I said, "You seem busy."

He opened them.

"You seem unemployed."

"Temporarily between revenue recognition events."

"I remember regretting you."

"Strong continuity."

He pointed the magnifying lens at me like a weapon.

"Can you organize an unload?"

I almost said no.

Not because I could not.

Because the question was dangerously broad.

"Organize how?"

"Three deliveries. Two receiving crews. One yard lead who was supposed to be here."

"Where is he?"

"At home vomiting."

"Good reason."

"Terrible timing."

"What's moving?"

He pointed in order.

Long wrapped frame.

Two drums.

Crates.

Then yesterday's broken water-drive lot near the inner wall.

"The frame goes to west bay for inspection. Drums stay under north awning. Crates go inside after seal count. Yesterday's lot needs to shift three paces east so the frame can turn."

"Weight?"

"Too much for me to guess safely."

"Who knows?"

Blue-cap driver raised a hand without being asked.

"Frame's marked eleven hundred."

"What unit?"

He told me.

I knew the broad scale.

Heavy enough that my opinion became mostly geometric.

"Drums?"

Second driver answered.

"Four hundred each."

"Who owns the rollers and chain?"

Broad woman.

"Us."

"Who is 'us'?"

"South Chain."

"Who has stop authority during lift?"

She stared.

"Me, if they're using my chain."

Good.

"Who has yard authority?"

Buyer said, "Me."

"Who has receiving record?"

A Tool House clerk at side table lifted slate.

"Me."

"Who decides final placement?"

Buyer.

"Who is paying me?"

Silence.

Important question.

Buyer said, "Two copper."

I laughed.

He did not.

"You're asking me to coordinate three independent crews moving enough iron to pulp somebody."

"You're not lifting."

"That makes the job cheaper?"

"It makes your shoulder less relevant."

"Three copper."

"Two."

"If the long frame clears west bay before second bell and nothing gets put in the wrong place, four."

He squinted.

"Why would I pay more for the same morning?"

"Because you care about the second bell."

He hated that I had noticed.

Blue cap said, "We do too. Road levy starts if our wagon is still in square after second bell."

There.

Delay cost.

Buyer looked at him.

Then me.

"Three if everything clears."

"Four if frame clears west bay and receiving record closes before second bell. Two if we finish later but clean."

The broad South Chain woman barked a laugh.

"He's charging you for your own urgency."

"Quiet."

She did not.

"Four," buyer said. "No injuries."

"That's not a fee condition. That's a baseline."

"Fine."

I held out hand.

He looked at crutch.

Then shook hand anyway.

Good.

I had just sold part of a morning again.

Not ideal.

But this time six other people's labor multiplied what my hour could affect.

Different enough to test.

First problem.

The west bay was not blocked by an axle stand.

It was blocked by three separate truths.

The stand occupied the middle.

A rack of finished brackets occupied the inside wall.

The long frame could enter only if the wagon backed nearly square to the door, but the north awning posts prevented that angle while the drum wagon remained where it had stopped.

Everybody had been arguing about the stand because the stand was easiest to point at.

I walked the route once.

Crutches on hard cinderstone.

Dry.

Slight slope toward street.

No reason to wear trial limb in a freight yard full of chain, rollers, and people who had not met my balance.

The frame wagon had to move first.

The drum wagon had to move before the frame wagon could angle.

The chain cart had to unload rollers before either.

Which meant the smallest cart moved first.

Nobody liked that because the long frame had arrived first.

Arrival order was emotionally powerful and mechanically irrelevant.

I said, "Chain cart unloads rollers at west edge. Then clears north. Drum wagon pulls forward and waits by black block. Frame wagon backs to bay. Tool House moves axle stand during that. Crates stay sealed until frame is inside."

Blue cap said, "My team can move the stand now."

Tool House worker said, "Your team isn't touching our stand."

Blue cap opened mouth.

I pointed at buyer.

"Yard decision."

Buyer said, "My people move stand."

Good.

I pointed at South Chain woman.

"Your chain, your lift calls."

She nodded once.

"Drivers keep their own teams."

Both drivers nodded.

"Anybody can call stop for danger. Only chain lead calls movement once load is under chain."

That got a reaction.

A younger Tool House worker said, "Anybody?"

"Yes."

"What if they're wrong?"

"Then we lose ten seconds."

South Chain woman said, "He's right."

Better authority.

She had earned it in this yard before I arrived.

Use it.

I asked her, "What do you need kept clear?"

She walked the long-frame route with me.

"Nobody inside this side when we turn. Nobody behind wagon wheel on slope. And if I say slack, I mean slack. Last crew heard 'back' and nearly took a finger."

"Good."

She looked at me.

"Good?"

"Specific failure mode."

"You write those down for fun?"

"Unfortunately."

She shook head.

"Strange little man."

I looked down at six-foot-three worth of me.

"Little?"

"Spiritually."

Fair.

We started.

The chain cart unloaded rollers.

Not elegantly.

One man carried a timber roller on shoulder.

Another tried to carry two.

The broad woman slapped one out of his arms.

"One."

"I can carry two."

"You can drop two too."

He carried one.

Different people.

Different competence.

Useful.

The drum wagon moved forward.

One horse disliked the black cinderstone corner block and sidestepped.

Driver corrected without drama.

I stayed out of it.

The axle stand took four Tool House workers and a pry bar.

One of them complained continuously.

Not because he was weak.

Because complaining seemed to be part of how he generated torque.

"Of course we put the bastard here. Best possible place. Middle of the only fucking bay. Beautiful. Art."

Nobody answered.

He kept going.

"Could've put it anywhere. Roof. Street. Buy it a room."

The stand moved.

West bay opened.

Second problem.

The frame wagon could make the angle.

Barely.

Blue cap knew his wagon.

The South Chain woman knew the chain.

The Tool House buyer knew where he wanted the frame.

I knew enough to stop trying to be all three.

That was the harder part.

Old S-class rooms had trained me out of a particular kind of stupidity.

If someone was better than you at the exact thing happening, shut up early.

I stood where I could see wagon rear, bay edge, and the slope.

"Blue cap," I said.

He looked offended.

"Name's Joren."

Damn.

A named person.

"Joren, you own wagon movement."

"Obviously."

"Good. Chain lead owns load movement."

"Also obviously."

"Then we are having a beautiful morning."

He almost smiled.

The South Chain woman said, "Mave."

There.

Fine.

"Greg."

"I know. Buyer called you strange before you got here."

"Excellent network."

Mave looked at the ground.

"Two rollers on approach. Third inside threshold."

One of her men said, "Third's split."

She turned.

"What?"

He showed her.

Long crack along one timber roller.

Mave swore.

No spare.

Joren said, "Use mine."

"You have one?"

"Under tool box."

Different crew.

Useful resource.

Mave inspected it before accepting.

Not trust.

Verification.

Good.

They set.

Joren backed wagon.

Slow.

Horse steps.

Wheel.

Stop.

Correct.

Again.

The frame's wrapped end cleared north awning by less than handspan.

Tool House worker at door said, "More south."

Joren said, "Can't."

Buyer said, "You can."

I said nothing.

Mave stepped back, sighted the angle, and said, "He can't. Rear wheel drops gutter if he goes south."

Buyer frowned.

Then walked out and saw it.

"Fine."

Good.

Nobody needed me.

Progress.

They rigged chain.

I watched hands.

Not because I knew rigging better.

Because hands disappeared first when too many competent people focused on the big thing.

Mave did too.

She slapped a wrist away from a pinch point without looking.

"Keep fingers."

"Had it."

"You still do."

That was probably old conversation.

The frame lifted enough to free wagon bed.

Rollers under.

Chain took weight.

Joren's crew eased wagon forward.

The frame settled onto rollers.

Eleven hundred units of somebody else's bad day.

Mave said, "Take."

Her crew pulled.

Tool House workers guided with bars.

The frame moved half a pace.

Stopped.

Again.

Half a pace.

It was boring.

Good heavy work often was.

The dangerous part usually arrived when boredom convinced somebody nothing bad was happening.

We were three pulls from threshold when the rear roller walked.

Not failed.

Walked.

Its left end shifted downhill maybe three fingers.

Tiny.

The frame followed.

Tiny.

Mave saw it same time I did.

"STOP."

Everybody froze except one Tool House worker who took one more pull because his back was turned.

The frame shifted again.

"STOP MEANS STOP," Mave roared.

He dropped line.

Silence.

The frame sat angled.

Not falling.

Not safe enough to pretend.

One roller now visibly off square.

One guide bar trapped under lower edge.

The complaining Tool House worker was on wrong side of it.

Not crushed.

Too close.

I pointed.

"You. Out."

He looked at frame.

"I can get bar."

"No."

"It's ours."

"Congratulations. Leave it."

Mave said, "Out."

He came out.

Good.

No debate after expert confirmed.

Mave crouched outside load path.

"Ground's pushing left."

The yard slope.

Not much.

Enough.

Joren said, "Chock right."

Mave said, "Not while it's loaded like that."

Buyer started, "What if we just pull the nose-"

"No," three of us said.

He looked offended.

I enjoyed that more than I should have.

Mave rubbed jaw.

"We need weight back a finger, reset roller square, then chock downhill."

"Can chain take reverse?"

"Yes, but not from current hook angle."

Joren pointed to wagon.

"Use rear tree as redirect."

Mave looked.

"No. Too high."

Joren frowned.

Then nodded.

Actual disagreement.

No sarcasm academy.

No one-line genius.

Good.

One of Mave's men said, "What about door post?"

Buyer said, "Absolutely not."

"Post's stone."

"Arch isn't."

Useful information.

I looked at available geometry.

Three independent teams.

Three separate tools.

Nobody had complete answer yet.

Old me stirred.

Not power.

Arrangement.

"What needs to be true before you can reset the roller?" I asked Mave.

"Load needs to come right and back without lifting."

"What can create controlled rightward pull?"

"Chain from north anchor."

Buyer said, "North anchor's behind drum wagon."

There.

I pointed toward street.

"Move drum wagon."

Driver said, "We're already staged."

"Yes."

"We move, we lose slot."

"What slot?"

"North awning."

"You're not unloading until frame clears anyway."

He stared.

Then looked at buyer.

Buyer said, "Move."

Driver swore under breath.

Not at me specifically.

Maybe at logistics as concept.

Drum wagon moved.

North wall anchor exposed.

Old iron ring.

Mave inspected it.

"Rated?"

Buyer said, "For mill housings."

"Recent?"

"Used last month."

She still checked masonry.

Good.

Chain rerigged.

Joren supplied two wheel chocks.

Tool House supplied another pry bar.

I supplied almost nothing physical.

That was the point.

Mave directed.

Small tension.

Stop.

Chock.

Reset roller.

The trapped guide bar came free without anyone reaching under.

Good.

Then frame moved again.

This time with downhill chock repositioned after each pull.

Slower.

Nobody complained.

Except the complaining man, who had moved from existential to technical.

"Chock's chewing cinder."

Mave said, "Then move it flat."

"Flat slips."

"Other face."

"Other face is chipped."

Joren handed him a different chock.

He took it.

No thank you.

Workplace intimacy.

The frame crossed threshold.

Second bell had not rung.

Not yet.

Inside bay, final placement belonged to Tool House.

I stepped away.

Buyer said, "Where are you going?"

"Crates."

He looked toward street.

Right.

We were not done.

The drums moved under north awning.

No chain issue.

No drama.

The sealed crates were easier.

Which did not mean casual.

Receiving clerk counted seals.

Two matched.

Third had wax chipped but cord intact.

Carrier record already noted chipped wax at departure.

Good.

No mystery.

No Greg investigation.

Record matched.

Crates moved inside.

Second bell rang while last slate entry was being signed.

Buyer looked at bell tower through open gate.

Then at clerk.

"Closed?"

Clerk finished line.

Stamped.

"Closed."

Joren's wagon was already rolling out toward road levy post.

Mave's crew gathered chain.

Drum driver had feed bag over horse.

Nobody applauded.

Excellent.

Buyer gave me four copper.

Fourteen to eighteen.

I counted because transaction required counting, not because eighteen copper had become a spiritual condition.

He said, "You didn't do much."

"Correct."

"You mostly told people the order."

"Correct."

"Mave solved the frame."

"Yes."

"Joren solved the turn."

"Yes."

"My people moved the stand."

"Eventually."

He glared.

"Then why did I pay you four?"

I looked across yard.

Three deliveries.

Three crews.

One closed receiving slate.

No one injured.

No levy delay.

No crate put in wrong bay.

"Because none of them owned the whole morning."

He considered that.

Mave walked past carrying chain hook.

"He kept your mouth out of lift calls."

Buyer said, "That alone might be worth two."

"Put it on my reference."

He looked at me.

"You want a reference?"

"Specific one."

"For what?"

"That I coordinated a multi-crew freight unload under your yard authority and completed receiving before second bell. Not that I know rigging. Not that I know wagons. Not that I know water-drive parts."

He laughed once.

"You're collecting paper again."

"Paper gets me into rooms."

"Money gets you into better rooms."

"Working on both."

He went inside.

I assumed that meant maybe.

Five minutes later the receiving clerk brought out a narrow Tool House slip.

Not glowing praise.

Better.

**CINDER CROSS TOOL HOUSE**

**GREG**

**COORDINATED THREE INBOUND FREIGHT TEAMS FOR WEST/NORTH RECEIVING. NO LIFT AUTHORITY. NO APPRAISAL AUTHORITY. RECEIVING CLOSED BEFORE SECOND BELL.**

Signed by buyer's mark and clerk witness.

I read it twice.

Mave leaned over.

"That say you know chain?"

"No."

"Good."

"You worried I'd steal your career?"

"You'd last half a morning."

"Generous."

She looked at my crutches.

Then at frame inside bay.

"Maybe whole morning if you stayed out from under things."

That was almost affection.

Or professional tolerance.

Different species.

I folded the slip into notebook.

Eighteen copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

Five commission still pending.

Ten-copper floor.

No sudden wealth.

No obligation-clearing montage.

Not yet.

But the day had answered another question.

Could I manage people?

A little.

Under a narrow scope.

When the people already knew their jobs.

When I did not mistake coordination for expertise.

When I could make the interfaces cleaner.

That sounded familiar enough to hurt.

Old support work had rarely meant telling the strongest person how to swing a weapon.

It meant making sure the healer knew when the striker would commit.

Making sure the retreat route was clear before anyone needed it.

Making sure somebody had authority to stop a bad move.

Making sure three specialists were not each solving a different version of the same problem.

I had not become that person again.

One freight morning did not earn mythology.

It earned a slip.

Good.

Slips were useful.

At the post counter, there was still no Lakeward commission notice.

Reasonable.

The glass wagon had left yesterday.

Distance remained stubbornly physical.

There was, however, a message from Lakeward district routing.

Not money.

Not Hessa.

Not Hark & Venn.

A short copied notice attached to my earlier Bracken report number.

**RIDGE INSPECTION RECEIVED. WEST-ACCESS CLOSURE MAINTAINED PENDING REPAIR. PRIOR REPORT TIMESTAMP CONFIRMED. FINAL HAZARD REFERENCE AVAILABLE ON REQUEST.**

There.

Another old piece becoming usable.

Not cash.

Capacity.

I paid postage in bits for the reference request.

No copper.

Then I looked at the private freight board.

One notice had been added since morning.

**SOUTH ROAD SEASONAL CREWS / INTAKE + ROUTE STAGING / ORGANIZER NEEDED / THREE CREWS / REFERENCES / FEE BY SCOPE**

I knew that shape.

Not this exact job.

The shape.

Yesterday I might have copied it.

Two days ago I might have told myself I needed more experience.

Today I had a Tool House slip in my notebook and enough sense to know one good unload did not make me a field boss.

Both could be true.

I copied the contact.

Not applied.

Not yet.

First ask scope.

How many people?

Whose hiring authority?

Who handles wages?

Who owns safety calls?

Who provisions?

Who can fire somebody?

Who absorbs delay?

Questions before confidence.

Good.

I bought lunch with bits.

Sat on black cinderstone edge outside the square.

My shoulder was tired.

Not injured.

Crutch palms sore.

Right leg had done a full morning of standing, shifting, backing out of other people's way, and refusing to enter crush zones because pride was expensive.

No trial limb.

No magic.

Fifty-nine.

Fifty-three.

One hundred thirty-nine.

Still.

The world had moved anyway.

That mattered too.

I opened notebook.

Wrote:

**14c -> 18c.**

Then:

**5c Lakeward reed-glass fee still pending. Do not count.**

Then:

**Tool House coordination reference secured. Scope narrow.**

Then:

**Bracken final hazard reference now available. Request sent.**

I stared at the money line.

Eighteen.

A few months ago I would have felt rich.

A few chapters ago I would have felt safe.

Now I felt capable of making another move.

Different.

Better.

Not because eighteen was a bigger emotion.

Because it had stopped being the only number that mattered.

I had cash.

Pending commission.

Vale balance.

Work-limb money already sunk into something useful.

References.

Two markets.

One seller relationship.

One buyer relationship.

One yard that had now seen me coordinate people without pretending I could rig eleven hundred units of iron.

Optionality.

That was the word.

Not wealth.

Not yet.

I closed notebook.

Across the square, Mave's crew was arguing about whose turn it was to buy beer.

Joren had already left.

The Tool House buyer was yelling at somebody about a crate label.

Nobody needed me.

Good.

I had spent the morning making myself useful in a system that continued after I walked away.

That might be the closest thing to support I'd done in this life.

I got up.

South-road notice in one pocket.

Tool House slip in another.

Eighteen copper in purse.

Five more maybe coming later.

And, for once, no desire to turn any of that into a speech.

There was work to price.

# CHAPTER 476

## THE BIDDER

Eighteen copper was enough to buy something stupid.

That was different from being enough to buy anything I wanted.

Important distinction.

I had eighteen copper in my purse, twenty-three still sitting with Vale, a five-copper reed-glass commission somewhere west of me in the dangerous metaphysical state called probably, and one Tool House reference proving I could tell people not to stand under eleven hundred units of iron.

Progress.

Also, apparently, enough confidence to walk into an artifact sale.

Cinder Cross held it in the back room of a cooper's hall because the actual auction house had a roof leak.

That was reassuring.

Nothing said rare magical equipment like barrels and damp plaster.

The notice had been on the private board under three ordinary freight postings.

**SURVEY / ROAD / WORKSHOP LOTS**

**DEACCESSION + ESTATE GOODS**

**VIEWING SECOND BELL**

**BIDS AFTER THIRD**

**NO FIELD GUARANTEE UNLESS WRITTEN**

That last line was why I came.

Not because I expected a miracle.

Because people wrote warnings where money had taught them to.

The viewing cost one bit.

Fine.

Bits could remain ordinary.

I paid and went in.

Nine tables.

Maybe thirty people.

Enough that nobody needed Greg specifically.

Better.

Most of the goods were tools with magic somewhere in their family but not necessarily enough to matter.

Two fog lamps.

A cracked line-reader.

Three heat stones in a fitted box.

A road compass that apparently pointed toward the last registered marker instead of north, which was useful if you were on the right road and deeply confusing if you were not.

A pair of vibration wedges.

A sealed brass thing nobody was allowed to activate indoors.

Healthy rule.

I looked slowly.

Not because I was pretending not to care.

Because old me had learned that the easiest way to buy the wrong artifact was to fall in love with the first one that did something.

Then I reached table seven.

Black iron half-ring.

Three pale ceramic plates set around the inside.

Leather hand strap.

Short chain.

Flat hook.

Brass thumb stud.

One ceramic plate cracked cleanly through the center.

The tag read:

**SURVEYOR STILLHOOK**

**ROAD OFFICE RETIREMENT LOT**

**TWO PLATES RESPOND / THIRD FAILED**

**ANCHOR-DEPENDENT LATERAL HOLD**

**NO CURRENT BODY-RATED CERTIFICATE**

**OPEN 4C**

I stopped.

Not visibly, I hoped.

Internally, very much.

Stillhook.

I knew the class.

Probably.

That was dangerous word number one.

I knew later stillhooks.

Old-life support crews used them around unstable casting platforms, cliff work, breach teams, rescue lines, and sometimes people who were too important to let a shockwave move six feet to the left.

Not because the hook made you immovable.

It did not.

Nothing useful was ever that simple.

You anchored it.

You held activation.

The field resisted displacement relative to the anchor.

Different generations handled angle, load, and rebound differently.

Some were brilliant.

Some broke wrists.

One famous design had killed three surveyors because the anchor held and the cliff did not.

This one was older.

Much older.

Maybe.

Pattern familiarity.

Not exact recognition.

Good.

Do not become an encyclopedia because object is interesting.

A man beside me picked it up.

The table attendant immediately said, "Stud only over test rail."

The man rolled eyes.

"Wasn't pressing."

"Everyone says that with thumb on stud."

He moved thumb.

Good attendant.

The test rail ran along back edge of table.

Heavy iron bolted into floor.

The man clipped hook to rail.

"Leather strap?"

"Wrist only for display," attendant said. "Old body certification expired. If you wear it, that's your problem."

"Useful."

"Accurate."

I liked her.

He put hand through strap anyway, not tightening.

Thumb on stud.

Ceramic plates dulled from pale cream to gray.

Two of them.

The cracked third flickered once and stayed pale.

The man pulled sideways.

His arm stopped.

Not frozen.

Resisted.

He leaned farther.

The hook held him relative to rail strongly enough that his shoulder moved before his hand did.

Then he changed angle.

Resistance fell off.

He nearly overbalanced.

He released stud.

"That's narrow."

"Yes."

"How narrow?"

"Written test says strongest inside thirty degrees either side of plate axis. Falls fast outside. Third axis unavailable."

There.

Visible utility.

Visible failure.

No mystery discount from stupidity.

A surveyor standing on unstable platform could brace against sway from two useful directions if anchor geometry cooperated.

A rigger might use it.

A person with one free hand and a fixed anchor could hold position against lateral force.

A person needing to move could not.

A person with both hands occupied by crutches had a more immediate design problem.

Me.

Excellent.

I wanted it.

Obviously.

That was irritating.

I read the attached sheet.

Cinder road office retirement.

Used equipment.

Service mark scratched near hook.

Last certified body inspection six years ago.

Third plate failure noted three years ago.

No repair record after that.

No documented catastrophic failure.
