No proof the two working plates still met original load rating.

Seller made no claim beyond demonstration.

Four copper opening.

I could afford four.

I could afford eight without crossing ten-copper floor.

Eight was not one silver.

Eight was also not nothing.

More importantly, the object did not deserve eight merely because I had eight available.

I asked attendant, "Any maker mark?"

She pointed to underside.

Three tiny stamped squares around a diagonal line.

My stomach did a small unpleasant thing.

Not because I knew exact artifact.

Because I knew mark.

Later.

Much later.

Third-Line Survey Works.

I thought.

Maybe Second-Line before rename.

The diagonal changed by generation.

I could not remember which.

Wonderful.

Old memories had chosen now to become decorative.

I asked, "Road office know manufacturer?"

"Retirement sheet says Third-Line pattern. No workshop authentication."

"Why no repair?"

"Office replaced class."

"Meaning?"

"Newer braces have wider hold and automatic release."

Of course they did.

"Repair quote?"

"None current."

"Nobody checked?"

"Two local shops refused ceramic replacement without original pattern gauge."

There.

Repair bottleneck.

Price rational.

The man who had demonstrated it asked, "You bidding?"

I looked at him.

"Maybe."

He looked at my crutches.

Then the stillhook.

"You need a hand to use it."

"Excellent assessment."

He shrugged.

"Just saying."

"So was I."

He moved away.

Not hostile.

Not stupid.

He was right.

Current me had two crutches.

Stillhook needed one hand on brass stud.

That meant if I used it standing, one crutch had to go somewhere.

Unless work limb.

There.

The unfinished leg changed the artifact.

The artifact changed the value of the leg.

That was what I wanted from tools.

Not another object that sat in bag waiting for plot.

A thing that made other infrastructure more valuable.

I kept moving.

Table eight had a genuine better object.

A compact road barrier plate.

Demonstrator threw a wooden bolt at it.

The bolt stopped two feet away and fell.

Everyone made appropriate noises.

Tag opened at one silver.

Of course it did.

Visible miracle.

Visible price.

I did not waste time being offended by economics.

The barrier also required a charged base stone replaced every fifty activations.

Seller had two.

Good artifact.

Expensive.

No hidden bargain apparent.

I moved on.

Table nine held a copper warming bowl and a ridiculous self-leveling cup.

The cup got more attention than half the magical tools.

People understood priorities.

I went back to stillhook only after looking at everything.

The attendant noticed.

"Questions?"

"Can you demonstrate load from both working axes separately?"

"Yes."

She did.

Test rail.

Hook.

Strap loose.

Thumb stud.

First plate axis.

Strong.

Second.

Strong, maybe slightly weaker.

Diagonal between them.

Still useful but uneven.

Toward cracked plate axis.

Almost nothing.

"Any rebound?"

"Release under tension and you'll feel it."

"How much?"

"Depends how stupid you were before release."

Good answer.

"Can you show without body?"

She clipped strap around a weighted leather block instead.

Pulled it with rope against field.

Released stud remotely using a wooden lever arrangement.

Block jumped a handspan toward rope.

There.

Stored tension.

Not huge at test load.

Could be ugly at higher load.

Known downside.

"Does it resist magical displacement?"

Her eyes narrowed.

"Tag says lateral hold."

"That wasn't my question."

"No certified magic-class test in lot papers."

"What kind of force did road office certify?"

"Platform sway. Wind rig. Cart-bed vibration. Manual pull."

"Nothing else?"

"Nothing written."

Good.

Do not tell her why I asked.

Old Greg memory supplied fragments.

A breach team.

A healer anchored to masonry.

A shockwave hitting.

A stillhook holding long enough for a cast to finish.

Was that this generation?

No idea.

Could have been a later military derivative that looked similar.

Could have been a completely different maker.

Could have been story inflation after twenty years.

But the class had potential beyond survey comfort.

Potential.

Not fact.

I checked the crack.

Clean through ceramic.

No burn discoloration.

No melted metal.

No obvious overload scar.

Could be age.

Impact.

Internal failure.

Unknown.

I did not touch it.

"Can plate be isolated?" I asked.

"Current two already operate without third."

"I mean physically removed."

"Seller doesn't authorize disassembly before sale."

Correct.

"Fair."

I left table again.

Then found a woman selling tea in hallway and bought a cup with a bit.

Artifact analysis improved with tea.

Most things did.

I sat against wall.

Eighteen copper.

Ten floor.

Five pending.

Twenty-three Vale.

Work-limb deposit already sunk.

Stillhook opening four.

Unknown repair.

Unknown magical interaction.

Known two-axis function.

Known one-hand activation.

Known anchor dependence.

Known rebound.

No body certificate.

No reason to believe anybody else was missing obvious value.

Exactly what I wanted.

A real artifact problem.

Not free treasure.

Third bell.

People gathered.

Auctioneer stood on old keg.

The cooper's hall had committed to theme.

Lots moved quickly.

Heat stones sold.

Fog lamp did not meet reserve.

Self-leveling cup sold for embarrassing amount.

I respected buyer more than I should have.

Stillhook came seventh.

Auctioneer read sheet.

No poetry.

"Road survey stillhook. Two live plates. One failed. No current body certification. Demonstrated only to posted test. Opening four copper."

Silence for half breath.

Then:

"Four."

The man from viewing.

Of course.

"Five," said a woman near back.

I did not know either.

Good.

"Six," man said.

Now price had information.

At six, two people wanted current visible utility.

Not just me.

Auctioneer looked around.

I had planned ceiling.

Eight.

Not because eight was value.

Because eight was maximum I could deploy without crossing floor, and artifact was not worth violating reserve on uncertain memory.

Those were different ceilings.

My private value might be higher than eight.

My available risk was not.

I raised hand.

"Seven."

The woman in back looked at me.

Not surprised.

Evaluating.

Auctioneer pointed.

"Seven."

Man from viewing said, "Eight."

There went ceiling.

Good.

Walk away.

That was a skill too.

I did nothing.

Auctioneer called eight once.

The woman in back said, "Nine."

Interesting.

Man frowned.

"Ten."

No.

Definitely no.

My artifact remained not mine.

Mildly painful.

Healthy.

Auctioneer called ten.

Woman hesitated.

Then: "Eleven."

The man stopped.

Eleven.

More than I could spend while preserving floor.

Also still below obvious new barrier-plate tier, whatever silver meant relative to copper.

Damaged survey gear with two useful axes had a market.

As it should.

Auctioneer called eleven twice.

Then the woman said, "Condition on sale sheet includes test rail hook?"

Attendant answered, "Hook and wrist strap included. Rail not included."

She nodded.

Auctioneer called.

No higher bid.

Sold eleven copper.

I felt stupid.

Not because I had lost.

Because I had spent half an hour mentally redesigning my life around an object I did not own.

Useful correction.

The woman came to table after sale.

Older than me by maybe fifteen years.

Brown coat.

Short hair.

Hands with pale pressure scars around thumb and index finger.

Surveyor, probably.

Or someone who used tools like one.

She inspected hook before paying.

Not gloating.

I asked, "You know the class?"

She looked at me.

"Enough to buy broken."

"Repair?"

"Maybe."

"Third-Line?"

"Maybe."

Good.

I liked her immediately and distrusted that.

"Why eleven?"

"Because two working plates are worth eight to me, hook assembly two, and I can gamble one on ceramic."

That was clean enough to hurt.

No secret.

No idiot.

She had her own use.

"What use?"

"No."

Fair.

I smiled.

"Had to try."

She looked at crutches again.

Everybody did eventually.

"You were bidding for yourself?"

"Yes."

"Why?"

"No."

She laughed.

Fair again.

Then she said, "You asked magic displacement."

I had not realized she heard.

There.

Visible interest as information.

Bad poker.

"Yes."

"Why?"

"Old story."

"What story?"

"A later stillhook holding under something that wasn't wind or platform sway."

"Later where?"

"Different place. Different time. I don't know this unit does it."

That was more information than ideal.

Also not enough to create a lie.

Her expression changed from amused to professional.

"You know who told story?"

"No one useful to you."

Technically true in a reincarnation way.

"Documented?"

"Not here."

She looked at the hook.

Price already paid.

My information could not hurt my purchase because I had no purchase.

Could still alter future market.

Annoying.

She said, "If I test that, where do I find you?"

I should have said nowhere.

Instead I thought about information networks.

If she owned object and had reason to test a layer I cared about, her result had value.

"Tool House knows Greg."

"Greg what?"

"Just Greg has worked surprisingly often."

She waited.

"Carrow when I'm home. Cinder right now."

"You're not from here."

"No."

"You're very tall."

"Excellent field work."

She ignored that.

"Name's Elian."

I stored it.

She lifted stillhook.

"I test anything strange, information costs."

"Obviously."

"What do you pay?"

"Depends whether strange is useful."

"Broker."

"Recent diagnosis."

She left.

I did not own artifact.

But I had learned something almost as important.

People with enough expertise to buy broken artifacts also created information.

And they charged.

The market was not only objects.

It was people who could reduce uncertainty.

I hated how every road returned to networks.

At Tool House, the buyer looked up from crate slate.

"Buy anything?"

"No."

"Good."

"Why good?"

"You have the face of someone who would buy a cursed spoon if it had an interesting hinge."

"That is slander."

"You bought that old line thing."

"Quietener."

"Same problem."

I leaned against counter.

"Stillhook sold eleven."

He grunted.

"Too much broken."

"Maybe."

"What did you bid?"

"Seven."

He looked at me.

"How much cash you have?"

"Enough that seven wouldn't kill me."

"That's not answer."

"Then stop asking accounting questions."

He smiled.

I asked, "You know anyone who works Third-Line ceramic?"

"Old survey gear?"

"Yes."

"Not here. Maybe South Road maintenance. Why?"

"Curiosity."

"Expensive disease."

"Starting to notice."

He went back to slate.

I did not ask about vane bracket.

Growth continued.

At the post counter, still no Lakeward commission.

Also reasonable.

No Hessa.

No Hark & Venn.

No home reply.

Distance remained annoyingly consistent.

I checked private board.

South-road organizer notice still there.

So was a new line:

**ARTIFACT TEST ASSIST / CONTROLLED SHOP / STRONG MANA REQUIRED**

Not me.

Good.

I left it.

Outside, Cinder afternoon had gone windy.

Not dangerous.

Enough to make hanging shop signs complain.

I found a bench near black corner block and opened notebook.

Wrote:

**STILLHOOK**

Then stopped.

Not mine.

Changed heading.

**STILLHOOK MARKET / CLASS NOTE**

Better.

**Visible:** two-axis lateral hold relative fixed anchor. thumb-held activation. third plate dead. narrow angle. rebound if released under tension. no current body cert.

**Market:** opened 4c, sold 11c. Multiple bidders. Broken does not mean worthless.

**Recognition:** class familiar from later-life use. Third-Line mark maybe. Exact generation unknown. Possible later nonphysical-displacement use remembered, NOT established for this unit/class.

**Buyer:** Elian. likely experienced user from hand marks / behavior, profession unestablished. May test. Info will cost.

Then:

**I lost because my reserve mattered more than my theory. Correct result.**

I stared at that.

It did not feel like losing anymore.

Not much.

I had wanted artifact acquisition to become real.

Real meant somebody else could buy the thing.

Real meant price could exceed my safe capital.

Real meant knowledge was not automatically ownership.

Real meant I needed money before opportunities appeared, not after.

There.

Economic pull without another manifesto.

I still wrote one more line.

**Need deployable capital above floor. Not just purse total.**

Eighteen copper sounded better than it was if ten had a job.

Eight was my actual flexible copper.

That was the number artifacts cared about.

And maybe that five coming from Lakeward.

Maybe.

Still not money.

I closed notebook.

Wind shoved at my coat.

For one stupid second I imagined the stillhook clipped to black cinderstone block, one hand on stud, body held against a force nobody else expected it to resist.

Then I corrected myself.

Two crutches.

One occupied hand.

One unfinished work limb.

One unverified memory.

One artifact now owned by somebody else.

Good.

Problems.

Problems were shape.

Back at bunkhouse, I checked shoulder.

Tired from yesterday.

Better today.

No new pain.

Hands fine.

Right leg fine.

No trial-limb wear.

No magic.

Fifty-nine.

Fifty-three.

One hundred thirty-nine.

Eighteen copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

Five pending.

Same as morning.

Financially, I had accomplished nothing.

That was okay.

Not every chapter of a life needed income.

I had found a category of tool I wanted.

Not a trophy.

A support tool.

Something that could let position become a resource.

Something whose limitations were as important as its effect.

And I had watched somebody else pay more than I safely could.

That part mattered most.

Old Greg had known powerful artifacts existed.

Current Greg had finally started meeting them at prices that hurt.

Good.

Pain was information.

Not injury.

Different thing.

I lay down with the bunkhouse roof creaking above me and thought about the work limb.

One free hand.

Not independence as slogan.

Not walking like everyone else.

One free hand meant a stillhook became usable while standing.

A lamp became usable.

A line became usable.

A shield tool.

A latch.

A person.

A weapon, if needed.

The leg was not merely mobility.

It was hand economy.

I had written that before.

Now an artifact had priced the idea.

One silver and eighteen copper for the finished work limb.

Twenty already down.

I did not know when the remainder came due.

Do not invent terms.

But for the first time, the quote did not feel like an obligation hanging over me.

It felt like infrastructure between me and a class of things I actually wanted to use.

That was different.

I slept badly because the man above me snored like structural damage.

Magic remained unsolved.

Money remained insufficient.

The artifact remained somebody else's.

Still.

For the first time in a while, insufficient felt directional.

# CHAPTER 477

## THE WAYFARER

I paid five copper to go west.

Nothing clever happened first.

That was the clever part.

Cinder Cross public road board still had the same Lakeward fare I had refused three chapters ago.

**LAKEWARD / 5C / MIDDAY CART**

No freight work attached.

No gray-market repair stock.

No broker fee.

No driver who needed somebody to classify old route metal.

Just five copper.

A seat.

A road.

I stood in front of the board with eighteen copper and experienced the unfamiliar sensation of not needing to invent a second job before traveling.

Ten stayed floor.

Five bought the seat.

That left thirteen.

Not comfortable enough to become stupid.

Comfortable enough that time could have a price.

I bought the seat.

The clerk did not ring a bell.

No one congratulated me for economic development.

He gave me a stamped card and said, "West cart. Third bay."

"That's it?"

He looked at me.

"What else were you expecting?"

"Honestly? A character lesson."

"Third bay."

Good institution.

I went to third bay.

The cart was larger than Daro Venn's Bell Ford freight wagon and less interesting.

Four wheels.

Canvas roof.

Bench seats.

Two chestnut road striders with dull copper braid worked through their manes.

The braid caught my attention.

Not decoration.

Thin metal threads ran from collar rings into little ceramic beads tied near shoulder.

Grounding.

Probably.

I knew enough not to announce that.

Driver was a woman with a red scarf and one sleeve rolled above elbow.

No name needed.

She took my fare card, looked at crutches, and pointed to front-side bench.

"Least climbing."

"Thank you."

"Don't thank me until we get there."

Strong salesmanship.

Other passengers accumulated.

A merchant with two square cases chained together.

A young man carrying a rolled sleeping mat and talking before anyone asked him anything.

An older woman with a basket full of sealed clay jars.

Two road workers with yellow cord at wrists.

A mother with a girl maybe eight.

And me.

The talking young man sat opposite.

He had not yet finished describing why he was traveling.

"I wasn't supposed to be going Lakeward today, see, except my brother says if I wait until tomorrow the room is gone, and I told him that's ridiculous because rooms don't go anywhere, people go somewhere, but apparently the landlord has a cousin coming in from Doss Hill and the cousin has a wife and maybe a baby, he wasn't clear about the baby, which I think matters because babies are small but somehow require more room than adults, so now I'm paying five copper because nobody in my family understands planning."

He inhaled.

I said, "Sounds difficult."

"It is."

He continued.

Good.

Different speech.

The driver climbed up.

"Road rule."

Talking man stopped.

Miracle.

She pointed along the cart roof.

"Metal stays down through High Cinder stretch if weather bells sound. Nobody touches ground braid while beads are lit. If I tell you off, you get off. If I tell you stay seated, stay seated. If road wardens stop us, don't make their day interesting."

The merchant said, "Weather's clear."

Driver looked west.

"So is your face. Neither tells me what the ridge is doing."

He shut up.

I liked her.

We rolled out.

Cinder Cross shrank behind us in black blocks, tool roofs, and the square where three roads disagreed about what direction counted as important.

I had thirteen copper.

Twenty-three with Vale.

Five still potentially coming from Lakeward.

Protocol fifty-nine, fifty-three, one hundred thirty-nine.

No new artifact.

No stillhook.

No reason to reopen that wound every half hour.

I failed at that immediately.

The first time the cart leaned through a turn, I imagined clipping a stillhook to the bench frame and letting the road move under me.

Then remembered Elian owned it.

Useful discipline.

The road west climbed for an hour before leveling across pale scrub.

Cinderstone markers stood every so often.

Not the big black corner blocks from town.

Smaller.

Dark rectangular posts sunk waist-high beside road.

Some had copper caps.

Some did not.

Most had shallow grooves down one face.

The little girl beside her mother asked, "Are those magic?"

Her mother said, "Some."

"Which?"

"The ones that work."

Excellent taxonomy.

One of the yellow-cord road workers laughed.

"They all work. Just not all for same thing."

The girl looked at him.

"What thing?"

He opened mouth.

His partner said, "Don't."

"What?"

"You'll explain until Lakeward."

"I can explain short."

"No."

The girl waited.

He failed to resist.

"Old ones mark distance. New caps bleed ridge charge. Side grooves show flood level. Some hold lamp hooks. Three east of here used to carry fog line but that's gone. One near split road has funeral names scratched on back but that isn't official."

His partner stared.

"Short."

"That was short."

The talking young man leaned in.

"My uncle says one of them hums before somebody dies."

Road worker said, "Your uncle needs hobbies."

"I told him that."

He did not seem like somebody who told anyone less than six paragraphs at a time.

The world moved.

I let it.

That was harder than it sounded.

For several chapters I'd been turning places into problems and problems into leverage.

Source.

Coordinate.

Bid.

Price.

Move.

Useful.

Also dangerous if it became the only way I knew how to look.

Sometimes a road was a road.

Sometimes five copper bought the right not to improve it.

We reached the first rise.

Wind changed.

Not stronger.

Different.

Dry air from west became cold across my right cheek.

The road striders' ears turned at the same time.

Driver sat straighter.

The copper braid in their manes gave a tiny clicking sound.

One bead near left animal's shoulder flashed pale blue.

Then went dark.

Nobody else reacted.

I did because I was watching.

The driver looked up at canvas edge.

There were four tiny brass bells tied under roof lip.

All still.

We continued.

Half a mile later the first bell rang.

Not from cart motion.

A single clean note.

The talking man stopped mid-sentence.

That was how I knew it mattered.

Second bell answered.

Then third.

The road workers swore at same time.

Driver said, "Metal down."

Merchant already had one square case upright between knees.

Driver pointed.

"Chain flat."

He lowered it.

One road worker took a narrow iron measuring staff from his pack and laid it on floorboards.

The older woman pulled the wire handle down against her basket.

I had crutches.

Metal tips.

Wood shafts.

Not fully metal.

Still.

I laid both as flat as space allowed.

Driver looked back.

"Good."

The little girl whispered, "Is it lightning?"

Her mother said, "Not exactly."

That was usually a worse answer.

The sky remained mostly clear.

No storm wall.

No thunder.

Only thin high cloud over ridge and that cold crosswind.

Then grass beside road began to shine.

Not glow.

Catch light wrong.

Silver threads moved across seed heads in waves that did not match wind.

The road worker who liked explanations pointed but kept voice low.

"Charge grass."

His partner said, "She can see it."

"I know."

"Then stop naming things."

"It has a name."

Everything did.

That didn't make it useful.

Ahead, one cinder post lit along cap.

Blue line ran down copper seam into ground.

Then next.

Then another.

The third post did not.

Driver saw it.

So did both road workers.

She slowed.

No panic.

No heroics.

She pulled cart toward a widened gravel pocket on left.

A black iron hoop stood there over packed earth.

Ground layby.

Probably.

One of the road workers stood.

Driver said, "Sit."

"I'm route crew."

"Not on my cart."

He sat.

Authority clarity remained beautiful.

We rolled into layby.

The black hoop had three chains dropping to iron plates buried in earth.

No visible glow.

Driver set brake.

"Stay seated."

Talking man whispered, "I don't like that."

Nobody had asked.

Fair.

The cart roof bells rang again.

All four now.

A faint blue crawl appeared across the copper braid in the road striders' manes.

They stamped but did not bolt.

Driver kept reins loose.

Interesting.

Not restraining harder.

Letting animals move their heads.

The merchant reached toward chain case without thinking.

The older road worker slapped his wrist.

"Don't."

He jerked back.

"Why?"

"Because she said metal down."

"I was just moving it."

"Move yourself later."

Driver said, "Thank you."

No sarcasm academy.

Just irritation.

I liked that too.

Across road, silver threads in grass intensified.

Then I saw why metal mattered.

A tall weed stalk leaned toward one cinder post.

No contact.

Blue-white thread jumped from seed head to copper cap with a snap.

Tiny.

Sharp.

Another jumped lower.

Then a dozen faint threads flickered between grasses.

Charge moving toward post.

The post that had not lit sat thirty paces farther west.

Around it, silvered grass moved more violently.

Not because wind.

Field.

The failed post was creating a gap.

The road workers both leaned forward.

Younger one said, "Cap's dead."

Older said, "Or ground break."

"Same result."

"Not same repair."

Good.

Driver said, "Can we pass?"

Older worker watched.

"No."

Simple.

No explanation.

Driver nodded.

We waited.

There was something deeply satisfying about competent people saying no before disaster.

Old life had contained too many people whose definition of bravery was refusing to let information alter a plan.

This road had built a layby for exactly that species.

Maybe civilization was learning.

The girl whispered, "What happens if we go?"

Her mother started to answer.

Road worker said, "Don't scare her."

Mother said, "I was going to say we wait because waiting is easier."

"Good answer."

The girl looked unconvinced.

So was I.

The younger road worker scratched jaw.

"If cap's dead, nearest field cut is next live post. That leaves whole gap riding raw."

Older said, "Unless break's only marker lead."

"Grass says not."

"Grass says charge isn't draining here. Doesn't tell you why."

I knew that argument.

Observation versus mechanism.

Hessa would have approved in a deeply unpleasant way.

I kept mouth shut.

Protocol stayed fifty-nine, fifty-three, one hundred thirty-nine.

Not my test.

Not my road.

Not my instrument.

Good.

Five minutes.

Ten.

The bells rang less often but grass remained silver.

Another cart approached from west.

Driver stood on brake and raised red paddle from seat side.

Other cart stopped before failed post.

Its driver saw layby, saw our bells, and backed into smaller shoulder pocket.

Shared system.

No messenger.

No Greg.

Nice.

Then from east came two wardens on low two-wheeled road skiff pulled by one narrow gray animal.

The skiff had a copper cage mounted over rear axle.

Inside, something blue pulsed.

Not a lantern.

Too dim.

More like trapped heartbeat.

The girl said, "Magic."

Her mother said, "Yes."

Finally.

Wardens wore yellow cord like road workers but with black caps.

One got down carrying a hooked wooden pole.

The other stayed by skiff.

Driver called, "Third cap west of layby didn't take."

Warden said, "We see."

No gratitude.

No insult.

Work.

He approached failed post from ditch side, not road.

Used wooden pole to touch copper cap.

Nothing.

Then lowered pole toward groove.

A blue thread crawled from grass to pole hook, ran down to a little ceramic bulb near handle, and died.

He did it twice.

Then yelled to partner.

"Ground's open."

Younger road worker muttered, "Told you."

Older one said, "You said cap."

"I said cap or ground."

"You said cap's dead."

"Spiritually."

Driver shushed them.

Warden opened small hatch at base of post with another wood tool.

Inside I saw braided copper dropping into dark ceramic sleeve.

He did not touch it.

Instead he connected a temporary chain from post base to a spike carried from skiff.

Not ordinary chain.

Every third link was ceramic.

He drove spike into ditch earth using wooden maul.

Then signaled partner.

The skiff's blue cage brightened.

For one second the whole roadside changed.

Silver grass flattened away from failed post.

Blue line snapped from dozens of seed heads toward temporary spike.

The bells under our roof screamed.

Not metaphor.

All four rang so hard the brass blurred.

The talking man yelled.

The little girl laughed.

Children were insane.

Driver covered one road strider's eye with hand and kept other rein loose.

I gripped bench.

Not because danger reached us.

Because the field changed pressure in my teeth.

Just enough.

A deep metallic taste.

Then gone.

The failed post lit blue.

Not bright.

Alive.

Warden watched for half minute.

Raised green paddle.

Driver waited.

Older road worker said, "She'll wait second cycle."

Driver glanced back.

"Correct."

One minute later the post lit again when silver grass moved.

Temporary ground held.

Driver lifted brake.

We rolled out only after wardens waved.

No applause.

No chapter-ending insight.

Just road opening.

I looked back.

The warden was already writing tag for post.

The other cart crossed after us.

Infrastructure.

That was what magic looked like when people got tired of dying from it.

Not spells.

Procedures.

Laybys.

Wooden poles.

Ceramic links.

A driver who knew when not to be brave.

I liked it more than I expected.

The talking man recovered first.

"That happens often?"

Road worker said, "No."

His partner said, "Often enough."

"Which is it?"

"Both."

"That's not possible."

"It absolutely is."

Then the younger worker started explaining seasonal ridge charge, and his partner gave up on stopping him.

I listened for a while.

Not because I needed to turn it into profit.

Because it was interesting.

Important distinction.

High Cinder stretch lasted another hour.

The bells rang twice more.

No stop.

Then copper beads in the road striders' manes went dull.

Driver said, "Metal up if you want."

Merchant lifted chain case.

Talking man resumed family history mid-thought like he had only been briefly paused by magic.

"My brother also thinks five copper is nothing, which is easy for him because he has a room already..."

I looked out road.

Five copper.

Yesterday I had refused to spend eleven on an artifact because eleven crossed reserve.

Today I had spent five on travel because five bought time, rest, and return to places where unresolved useful things actually existed.

Same purse.

Different decision.

That was the point.

No deprivation performance.

No rich-guy performance.

Price depended on what it bought.

By late afternoon the road descended toward Lakeward basin.

Water appeared first as light.

Then roofs.

Then docks.

My shoulder appreciated every mile I had not crutched.

My right leg appreciated it too.

Public cart was not laziness.

It was body infrastructure rented by the day.

That thought sounded irritatingly like something I'd write down.

I did not.

Growth.

We entered Lakeward before dusk.

Driver handed bags down.

I climbed carefully.

She passed crutches after me.

"Still thanking me?"

"Yes."

"Then trip went well."

"Your standards are inspiring."

"My standards are arrival."

Fair.

I paid nothing else.

The five was already gone.

Thirteen copper remained.

Above floor by three.

Selective constraint.

Not crisis.

I could have found cheapest floor and eaten.

Instead I went to post counter first.

Because timing was now plausible.

The clerk checked name slate.

"Greg?"

"Unfortunately."

"Two slips."

There.

My stomach did stupid little thing again.

First was Bracken reference.

Not money.

I opened that first because I had apparently learned restraint in one domain.

**BRACKEN RIDGE ROAD OFFICE**

**PRIOR HAZARD REPORT RECEIVED BEFORE WEST-ACCESS TEMPORARY CLOSURE. SUBSEQUENT RIDGE INSPECTION CONFIRMED CONTINUED CLOSURE PENDING REPAIR. THIS NOTICE CERTIFIES REPORT TIMING AND RECEIPT ONLY.**

Perfect.

Not "Greg was right."

Not "Greg diagnosed hazard."

Timing and receipt.

Useful.

Narrow.

Second slip had merchant hall mark.

Lakeward reed-glass buyer.

I opened it.

**SIX BUNDLES RECEIVED. SIX ACCEPTED TO CONTRACT SPECIFICATION. INTRODUCTION FEE 5C PAYABLE AT HALL DESK.**

There.

Actual money.

Not pending.

Not hoped.

Not probably.

I read it twice.

Then walked to merchant hall before closing.

Buyer in dark green vest was still there.

Ink on three fingers again.

She looked up.

"You came back."

"Eventually."

"The glass passed."

"I heard."

"Four were clean. Two had ugly color."

"Color was irrelevant."

"Yes."

She reached under desk and counted five copper.

No ceremony.

I took it.

Thirteen became eighteen again.

Interesting.

I had paid five copper to return to Lakeward and then received five copper because earlier work had matured while I was traveling.

Net purse unchanged from morning.

Position changed.

Cinder behind me.

Lakeward here.

Bracken reference in notebook.

Glass commission closed.

East Fen buyer relationship now real.

Stillhook still gone.

South Road notice still copied.

Work limb still unfinished.

Vale still twenty-three.

No debt paid.

No victory montage.

Just motion.

Buyer said, "East Fen can make more?"

"Probably."

"Probably is expensive word."

"Orin can tell you better."

"You have contact?"

"Yes."

"Good. I'm buying direct next time."

There.

Brokerage success reducing need for broker.

Also real.

I smiled.

"Then I did my job too well."

"You got paid."

"Once."

She shrugged.

"If I need another source, I'll ask."

Not recurring income.

Not guaranteed lane.

A completed introduction.

Good.

I asked, "Any complaint from carrier?"

"None. Rack terms were right."

"Good."

That was enough.

I left.

Outside merchant hall, Lakeward evening smelled like water, hot oil, wet rope, and food I could not identify.

Home was still not here.

Hark & Venn still not here.

Hessa still not here.

The work limb was somewhere west beyond more road and timing.

But Lakeward was closer to those lanes than Cinder.

I could decide tomorrow.

Maybe home.

Maybe wait one day for mail.

Maybe inspect a better road route.

Maybe pay again.

That last thought no longer felt like failure.

Useful.

I bought dinner with bits.

Ordinary.

Then a bunk.

Ordinary.

No philosophical crisis.

I sat on cot and updated notebook.

**18c start.**

**-5c Cinder -> Lakeward public cart.**

**13c arrival.**

**+5c East Fen / Lakeward introduction fee after 6/6 acceptance.**

**18c end.**

Then:

**Bracken reference received. Certifies timing + later closure only.**

Then:

**High Cinder charge-weather infrastructure observed. Failed ground at third capped marker west of layby. Wardens installed temporary ground. Do not generalize mechanism beyond observation.**

I paused.

That sounded like Hessa.

Terrible.

Then:

**Travel can be purchased when time/body/location value exceeds fare. Stop forcing every road into a job.**

That one I underlined once.

Not twice.

Protocol fifty-nine, fifty-three, one hundred thirty-nine.

No magic test.

No limb wear.

Shoulder better after sitting most of day.

Cash eighteen.

Vale twenty-three.

Bits unspecified.

Ten floor.

No debt closure.

No artifact.

No new employer.

No new friend.

I had spent a chapter going somewhere.

That was enough.

For once, the road had done the interesting part.

# CHAPTER 478

## THE CORRESPONDENT

The letter was waiting under my name.

That was inconvenient.

Not because I did not want it.

Because I wanted it enough to become stupid.

Lakeward post opened before the breakfast stall beside it, which felt like an institutional mistake. Nobody should have to receive emotional information without fried dough.

I went anyway.

Eighteen copper.

Twenty-three with Vale.

Bits unspecified.

Fifty-nine.

Fifty-three.

One hundred thirty-nine.

All the numbers still where I had left them.

The clerk had three bundles of route notices, two merchant receipts, one narrow parcel wrapped in gray cloth, and a folded cream sheet with **GREG** written on the outside in handwriting I knew immediately.

Lyssa wrote my name like she was annoyed the letters had to take up space.

I stared at it.

The clerk stared at me.

"Taking it?"

"Considering whether that's wise."

"It's yours."

"That does not answer the question."

He pushed it closer.

"Take your problems off my counter."

Good public service.

I took it.

There was no seal beyond a dab of ordinary wax pressed flat with the back of something round. No family mark. No merchant stamp. No dramatic blood.

Just Lyssa.

I went outside.

Then did not open it.

That was stupid too.

Across from the post, Lakeward was already becoming itself.

A fish cart came through too fast.

Two dock women argued about baskets while loading the same baskets.

A boy swept water off a stone step directly toward another boy, who looked down at the water, looked up at him, and said, "I know where you sleep."

The first boy kept sweeping.

Strong relationship.

I bought breakfast with bits.

Ordinary.

Sat on a low wall.

Letter in my coat.

Ate half the bread.

Still did not open it.

This was ridiculous.

Old Greg had opened battlefield casualty lists with steadier hands.

That was not useful perspective.

Casualty lists had rules.

Lyssa did not.

I finished chewing.

Opened the letter.

Her first line was:

**You are terrible at telling me where you are.**

Fair.

Second:

**Your last note said Lakeward, then maybe east, then "probably back before this reaches you," which is not an address and also turned out to be wrong.**

Also fair.

I kept reading.

**I sent this to Lakeward because the post woman said if you were still wandering somebody might know where to send it, which sounds fake, but apparently this is how your life works now.**

I smiled.

That annoyed me.

**The room is fine. I am fine. Before you start doing that thing where you turn "fine" into a diagnostic category, I mean I am busy, I am sleeping enough, I ate yesterday, and nobody has threatened me.**

There.

Relationship language.

Specific enough to stop me.

Not sentimental.

Better.

**Work has been stupid. Two people wanted the same green thread after I told both of them there was only enough for one. One of them said she had asked first. She had not. The other one said she did not care which green. She absolutely cared which green. I solved this by making both of them leave and come back tomorrow.**

I could see it.

Not the customers.

Lyssa's face when somebody said they did not care about color while visibly caring about color.

**Jessa says I should start charging people for changing their minds. Marra says then I would become rich and unbearable. I told her Greg is already working on the unbearable part for the household.**

I stopped.

Read that again.

There were people in the room with her.

Work continuing.

Jessa.

Marra.

Nothing waiting for Greg to activate it.

Good.

Also rude.

**Your cup is still here. The bad one. I almost threw it away because the crack is worse. Then I remembered you get weird about objects when they have survived too long. It is on the high shelf. Do not tell me this is proof of love. It is proof I did not want to listen to you complain.**

I looked out at Lakeward water.

The cup.

I had forgotten the cup.

That hurt more than I expected.

Not because it was important.

Because it had become unimportant in a place where I lived.

Those were different things.

A gull landed three feet away and stared at my breakfast paper.

"No."

It took one step closer.

"Absolutely not."

It took another.

I moved the paper.

The gull looked offended.

Good.

Lyssa continued.

**Cala stopped by. She borrowed the small chair again. She brought it back this time without me asking. One leg is still shorter. I am not fixing it. If you fix it, I will know you were bored.**

I laughed.

The gull left.

Maybe judgment.

**The theatre sent somebody by for you once. I do not know what for. I told him you were east. He said, "Again?" like this was my fault. I told him no. He left.**

That was all.

No urgent theatre job.

No plot summons.

Just someone expecting me enough to knock.

I liked that too much.

Then:

**I got one note from Hark & Venn addressed to you. I did not open it. It is with your papers. Before you ask, no, I do not know what it says, and yes, I know you are going to make a face when you read this. Make it there.**

I made the face there.

Good.

There was work-limb information at home.

Not here.

Not conveniently copied into the letter.

Excellent.

I resisted the urge to build six theories.

Barely.

**If you are coming back, come back. If you are not, send a useful note. I am not asking for an itinerary. I am asking for a city. Maybe two if you are feeling ambitious.**

Then one blank line.

Then:

**I miss you. This is not permission to become dramatic.**

I sat very still.

Lakeward remained rude enough to keep operating.

Cart wheels.

Water.

Somebody shouting about eels.

Hammering from dock repair.

A woman behind me saying, "No, the blue rope. The blue one. That is gray. Do you have eyes?"

Useful world.

I read the last line again.

Then the one above it.

Then the whole letter.

Not because I had missed anything.

Because paper did not get annoyed when you repeated a conversation.

I folded it carefully.

Unfolded it.

There was more on the back.

Of course there was.

**Also your other shirt is missing a button. I put it on your pillow because apparently I enjoy creating future work for myself. Do not wear it like that.**

I closed my eyes.

There.

Lyssa.

Entire person.

I put the letter inside notebook behind the Bracken reference.

Then moved it.

Bracken reference was official and stiff.

Lyssa's letter did not belong behind a road closure.

I put it inside coat.

Better.

The next question was obvious.

Go home.

My mind immediately tried to improve it.

Check road board.

Compare fares.

See if another freight seat moves west.

Ask merchant hall whether anyone needed a document carried.

Maybe Hark & Venn had Lakeward routing.

Maybe theatre note meant work.

Maybe I could stop somewhere and turn travel into another commission.

No.

I had just learned this.

Apparently lessons did not become permanent merely because I wrote them in notebooks.

I stood.

Shoulder fine.

Right leg fine.

Crutch palms ordinary.

No trial limb.

No magic.

Lakeward westbound board was two streets over.

I went there.

Three departures listed.

One toward Carrow district next morning.

One late freight route that did not name Carrow but passed the south branch.

One water packet with final stop west basin.

Prices posted.

I read them.

Then stopped myself before turning the board into a thesis.

The Carrow route left at first bell.

That was enough.

I asked the board clerk, "Seats?"

"Two."

"Can I hold one?"

"Until dusk."

"Cost to hold?"

"One bit."

Fine.

I paid the bit.

He wrote **GREG / FIRST BELL** on slate.

No copper moved.

The fare would be due on boarding.

I did not ask him to explain the entire route economy.

Growth continued.

"How long?"

He gave me the normal answer.

Long enough that if everything behaved, I would be home after one more road day and part of another.

Good.

Physical.

Not teleportation.

I nodded.

"Hold it."

"Already did."

Right.

I left.

Now I had most of a day in Lakeward.

This created a dangerous amount of unscheduled time.

Old Greg would have used it to inspect equipment.

Current Greg was also tempted to inspect equipment.

At least I was consistent.

Instead I went to the public washhouse.

Not because of character development.

Because I had slept in Cinder bunks, freight clothes, road dust, auction rooms, and one Lakeward cot, and my shirt had reached the point where it deserved independent legal representation.

Washhouse attendant took bits.

I rented a basin, soap, drying rail.

Crutches leaned within reach.

Coat stayed with me because Lyssa's letter was inside and I had apparently become twelve.

I washed shirt.

Then undershirt.

Then myself as much as public decency allowed.

A man two basins down was trying to remove black resin from his sleeves and losing.

He had the defeated posture of someone who had already been told the correct method and ignored it.

The attendant walked past.

"Cold first."

"I did cold."

"You did warm."

"It felt cold."

"Water disagrees."

He stared into basin.

"Can water disagree?"

"Yours is."

She kept walking.

Different people.

Good.

I scrubbed road grit from cuff.

One seam near elbow had loosened.

Nothing dramatic.

I could wear it.

Lyssa would see it.

That changed classification.

I found a needle seller outside washhouse.

Bits.

Ordinary repair thread.

I bought enough to close seam myself badly or bring home and let Lyssa tell me I had done it badly.

I considered fixing it.

Did not.

Not because helplessness was romantic.

Because sometimes the person who knew how to do the thing was also the person whose work I respected.

And because I wanted to see her.

That was becoming inconveniently central.

I spent midday near water.

No deals.

No investigations.

I watched a dock crew use a pulley frame with a blue-glass brake bead that darkened whenever load swung too fast.

Magic.

Ordinary.

Not mine.

A boy carried tally boards.

One woman ate half a pear while shouting weights.

A man on barge roof sang three lines of same song until someone threw a rag at him.

He caught rag.

Kept singing.

The brake bead darkened twice.

Crew adjusted.

No accident.

No Greg.

Good.

I bought lunch.

Bits.

Sat with letter again.

This time I did not read entire thing.

Only:

**If you are coming back, come back.**

And:

**I miss you.**

Then I put it away before language became syrup.

I needed to write back.

This was almost pointless because I might beat the letter home.

That was not the point.

She had asked for a useful note.

So she got one.

Lakeward post sold folded sheets.

I bought one.

Sat at high writing counter.

The first version began:

**Lakeward. Going home tomorrow.**

Good.

Useful.

Then I stared.

What else?

I wanted to tell her Cinder had been strange.

That I had moved illegal freight.

No.

Not in a casual letter through public post.

I wanted to tell her I had found East Fen glass.

Fine.

I wrote:

**Lakeward. Going home tomorrow. I found the glass source and the buyer accepted all six bundles, so that worked. I got paid.**

Then:

**I also lost an auction for a broken artifact I wanted because someone else had more money than I could safely spend. This was correct and I hated it.**

That sounded like me.

Then:

**The road from Cinder had a charge-ground failure and the wardens fixed it while we waited. Nobody died. I did nothing useful. I enjoyed this.**

Also good.

Then I stopped.

I had written her a field report.

Lyssa had written me about thread, a chair, a cup, work, and missing me.

I had answered with transactions and infrastructure.

Excellent boyfriend.

I scratched none of it out.

Instead added:

**I miss you too.**

Looked wrong.

Not false.

Just naked.

I added:

**Do not become dramatic about this.**

Better.

Then:

**Tell Marra I am already rich in unbearable. Tell Jessa charging for changed minds is correct and dangerous. Do not throw away the cup yet. I need to inspect whether the crack has achieved historical significance.**

I smiled.

Then:

**Leave Hark & Venn note sealed. I will read it when I get there.**

There.

No theory.

No request to transcribe.

No making Lyssa become my administrative assistant because she happened to share a room with my paper.

Then I added one last line.

**First bell route tomorrow. Carrow. That is a city. I did what you asked.**

I folded it.

Addressed home.

Paid postage in bits.

The post clerk took it.

"Could arrive after you."

"I know."

"You still sending?"

"Yes."

He shrugged.

Good institution.

I walked away lighter by one letter and no copper.

Eighteen stayed eighteen.

That mattered less than it used to.

By late afternoon Lakeward had turned gold around water edges.

I did one useful thing.

Not profitable.

Useful.

I copied the morning westbound seat mark into notebook and checked that my hold was still on slate.

It was.

Then I bought dinner.

No purse recount.

That was getting easier.

At bunkhouse, I cleaned crutch tips.

One had collected black grit in rubber edge.

Picked it out with blunt needle back.

Checked shafts.

Fine.

Hands.

Fine.

Shoulder.

Better.

Residual limb.

Ordinary.

Right leg.

Tired enough to know I had walked city all day, not enough to create a problem.

Protocol stayed fifty-nine, fifty-three, one hundred thirty-nine.

No magic.

No artifact.

No work.

No income.

No debt payment.

Good.

I put Lyssa's letter under pillow.

Then immediately moved it because that was sentimental and also because somebody could steal it.

Notebook pocket.

Safer.

I lay down.

The man in next bunk turned over and said, "You snore?"

"Not according to me."

"That is useless."

"Ask me while I'm asleep."

He thought about that longer than necessary.

"I don't think that works."

"Then we're trapped."

He grunted.

Different roommate.

Different city.

Tomorrow home.

That word had changed quietly.

Home used to mean where my things were.

Then where Lyssa was.

Then where work could find me.

Then where people got annoyed when I left without saying exactly where.

Now all of those were true at once.

I did not write that down.

The scene had already made the point.

Good.

Before sleep, I checked the seat hold slip.

First bell.

Carrow.

Then Lyssa's letter one more time.

Not whole thing.

Just the end.

**I miss you. This is not permission to become dramatic.**

I smiled in dark.

"Terrible woman."

The man beside me said, "What?"

"Nothing."

"Good."

Lakeward creaked around us.

Tomorrow I would go home.

Not because a quest ended.

Not because I had failed abroad.

Not because the city had run out of things to teach me.

Because someone had written:

**If you are coming back, come back.**

So I would.

# CHAPTER 479

## THE HOMECOMER

The fare home was six copper.

I paid it.

That was becoming a habit.

Not the six copper part.

Paying for the thing I wanted instead of demanding that every inconvenience prove my cleverness.

The Lakeward board clerk took my hold mark, checked my name, and said, "Six."

I gave him six copper.

Eighteen became twelve.

Ten stayed floor.

Two remained above it.

That was narrow.

It was also fine.

I was going home.

The cart left at first bell with no magical weather, no broken grounding post, no illegal freight, no glass bundles, and no artifact auction happening within conversational distance.

I considered this suspicious.

The driver did not.

He had gray hair, a green coat, and the settled expression of a man whose professional ambition was to arrive where the route slate said he would.

I respected him immediately.

The Carrow cart was covered but open at the sides. Two passengers slept before Lakeward had fully disappeared behind us. One woman shelled peas into a cloth bag for almost an hour. A boy across from me kept trying to balance a copper washer on his knuckles until his father finally took it away and said, "You have demonstrated gravity."

The boy said, "I can do it."

"You have demonstrated gravity six times."

"Seven."

"Thank you."

Different family.

Different problem.

Good.

I rested.

That was most of the first day.

The road did ordinary road things.

It narrowed.

It widened.

It became bad near a drainage cut.

We stopped once because a freight wagon ahead needed room to turn.

The driver swore at a milestone because someone had painted the west face blue.

Nobody knew why.

At midday I bought food with bits and did not calculate whether hunger had positive return on capital.

Growth.

My shoulder stayed quiet.

Right leg got stiff from sitting.

Hands appreciated not doing ten miles of work.

The residual limb had ordinary end-of-day pressure from the socket liner I wore for shape and protection, not from a trial limb.

No new skin problem.

No magic.

Fifty-nine.

Fifty-three.

One hundred thirty-nine.

The numbers remained boring.

I wanted boring.

By evening we stopped at a road house where the fare included floor space but not dinner.

Fine.

I ate.

Slept badly.

Someone near the door talked in his sleep about onions.

Not metaphorically.

He had strong opinions.

Morning came.

We moved again.

The second day felt shorter because Carrow began appearing before Carrow appeared.

That was how home worked.

First the road markers changed.

Then the carts.

Then people used names I knew without explaining them.

A produce wagon had Marrow Lane chalked across its side.

A courier complained about South Steps.

A woman getting off at a junction told the driver she could cut through Cooper Row if the paving crew had finally moved.

I knew all three places.

Not as map labels.

As distances in my body.

South Steps meant one route if tired and another if carrying something awkward.

Cooper Row meant uneven stones near the drain.

Marrow Lane meant the bakery two corners over sold out earlier than it claimed.

That was home too.

Accumulated inconvenience.

By late morning the city stopped being ahead of us and became around us.

Carrow.

I had expected something.

Not a revelation.

Maybe recognition.

Maybe the old strange feeling that I had crossed from temporary road-life back into the place where my current life actually happened.

Instead my first thought was:

That gutter still leaks.

Excellent.

The cart dropped us two districts from home.

I climbed down slowly, took my crutches from the driver, and adjusted bag.

Twelve copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

Bits unspecified.

One sealed Hark & Venn note somewhere in my room.

One girlfriend who had written that she missed me and then immediately prohibited emotional consequences.

I started home.

Carrow did not care.

A delivery man almost backed into me with cloth rolls.

A dog barked at one crutch and ignored the other.

Two theatre handbills had been pasted over an older soap advertisement.

A woman I recognized from somewhere nodded at me, then looked uncertain about why she recognized me.

I nodded back.

Partial Greg.

Good.

I passed the little paved triangle where Senna had once drawn me.

She was not there.

The bench was.

Somebody else sat on it eating an apple.

Also good.

Not every piece of my life needed to remain staged for my return.

Closer to home, the streets changed from familiar to specific.

The corner where Lyssa had once made me wait because she needed thread and came out with buttons instead.

The doorway where Cala had argued with a vegetable seller about whether bruised tomatoes were cheaper or merely sadder.

The wall I had leaned against the first time my right palm split from too much crutch pressure.

Nobody had put plaques up.

Civilization survived.

I reached our building around midday.

Then stopped outside like an idiot.

Window open.

Our window.

A strip of pale cloth hung over sill.

Not mine.

Voices upstairs.

One Lyssa.

One woman I did not recognize.

Work voice.

Not intimate.

Not angry.

Fast, material, occupied.

I could have gone up.

Obviously.

I lived there.

Instead I stood on street for ten seconds because I had been away long enough that entering felt like interrupting.

That was stupid.

Then the upstairs window opened wider.

Lyssa leaned out.

She looked down.

Saw me.

Her face changed.

Not dramatically.

First surprise.

Then relief.

Then annoyance arrived quickly enough to preserve structural integrity.

"You."

I looked up.

"Me."

"You said tomorrow."

"It is tomorrow."

"You said first bell."

"I left at first bell."

"That was not the complaint."

The woman inside said something I couldn't hear.

Lyssa looked back.

"One minute."

Then at me:

"Come upstairs."

Excellent.

Permission to enter my own home.

I went.

The stairs were still stairs.

I hated them with renewed affection.

By the time I reached landing, door was open.

Lyssa stood there with three pins in her mouth.

She took them out.

"Don't hug me."

I stopped.

She held up both hands.

Blue chalk on one.

Needle in the other.

"Customer."

"Right."

Then she stepped forward and hugged me anyway, carefully, one arm around shoulders and the other held out to side so she did not stab me.

I put one arm around her.

Crutch trapped awkwardly between us.

Perfect.

She smelled like soap, cloth dust, and home.

I closed my eyes.

She said into my shoulder, "Do not."

"I didn't do anything."

"You were about to."

"I was breathing."

"Dramatically."

"That seems medically necessary."

She let go first.

Looked at me.

Really looked.

Face.

Shoulder.

Coat.

Crutches.

Hands.

Bag.

The quick inventory she would deny was an inventory.

"You look tired."

"I traveled."

"You look less terrible than last time."

"Thank you."

"Not compliment."

"I took it anyway."

From inside, the customer said, "Can I move?"

Lyssa turned.

"No."

"Still?"

"Especially now."

I looked past her.

A woman stood on our little fitting platform wearing a half-pinned brown overskirt and the expression of someone who had been warned repeatedly about movement and was building a legal case against the restriction.

Lyssa pointed at the chair.

"Sit."

I sat.

Apparently home had hierarchy.

The customer looked at me.

"You're Greg?"

I looked at Lyssa.

Lyssa said, "Unfortunately."

Good.

I said, "I've been called worse."

Customer said, "She talks about you."

Lyssa, without looking up: "No, I complain about him. Different service."

The woman nodded like this distinction made sense.

Lyssa went back to pinning hem.

And that was my homecoming.

I sat in my chair while my girlfriend worked on somebody else's skirt.

No music.

No reunion audience.

No immediate sex.

No emotional confession.

She had a customer.

I had been gone.

Both facts survived contact.

The room looked almost exactly the same.

Which meant it was completely different.

The small chair Cala borrowed had returned beside wall, one leg still shorter.

My cracked cup sat high on shelf.

Actually high.

Lyssa had not exaggerated.

My other shirt lay folded on pillow with one missing button beside it in a tiny dish.

Papers were stacked under window.

A sealed note sat on top.

HARK & VENN.

There.

I looked at it.

Then away.

Lyssa saw anyway.

"Don't."

"I wasn't."

"You were."

"I looked."

"You just got here."

"Correct."

"Then be here."

That stopped me.

Not because it was profound.

Because she said it while holding three pins between lips and trying to make a brown skirt stop fighting gravity.

I leaned back.

"Fine."

Customer said, "Is this where you tell him you missed him?"

Lyssa looked at her.

"No."

The woman smiled.

"I would."

"Then write him."

"I don't know him."

"Excellent boundary."

I laughed.

Customer moved.

Lyssa slapped side of her own thigh.

"Stop moving."

"Sorry."

"You said sorry last time."

"I mean it every time."

"That is not helping."

The fitting took another twenty minutes.

I watched.

Not like inspector.

Like man sitting in his own room while someone else's work happened.

Lyssa had changed something while I was gone.

A narrow hook rail had been screwed under shelf.

Three bundles of thread hung there.

The table had shifted half a foot toward window.

A second lamp stood near fitting platform.

Not dramatic.

Work accumulating.

The room adapting to her because she used it more than I did.

Good.

The customer finally escaped with instructions I did not understand and a warning not to pull one seam until tomorrow.

At door she looked at me.

"Welcome home."

"Thank you."

Lyssa said, "Leave."

The woman laughed and left.

Door shut.

Silence.

Then Lyssa turned.

No pins.

No needle.

No customer.

I stood.

She crossed room fast and hit me in chest with both hands.

Not hard.

"Your notes are terrible."

"I improved."

"You sent me a letter from Lakeward that may arrive after you."

"That is technically useful location information."

She stared.

I smiled.

She kissed me.

Hard enough that one crutch slipped half an inch.

I caught it.

She pulled back.

"Careful."

"Mixed signals."

"Sit down."

"I just sat for two days."

"Then stand responsibly."

She kissed me again.

Slower.

My hand found her waist.

Hers went behind my neck.

For a little while there was no economy.

No leverage.

No road.

No Vale.

No artifacts.

No protocol.

Just Lyssa warm against me and the small stupid relief of touching someone who had been paper yesterday.

She broke kiss first.

"You smell like road house."

"I used washhouse."

"Then road house fought back."

"I had a roommate with onion dreams."

She paused.

"What?"

"I don't know."

"Did you ask?"

"He was asleep."

"That has not stopped you before."

Rude.

Accurate.

She touched side of my face.

Then softer:

"I missed you."

"I know."

"You don't get to say that like a victory."

"I missed you too."

"I know."

There.

Enough.

She looked down at bag.

"Did you bring me anything?"

I froze.

She watched my face.

Then started laughing.

"Oh, no."

"I brought thread."

"For me?"

"No."

Worse.

"What is wrong with you?"

"My seam is loose."

She laughed harder.

I took the little thread bundle out.

She held out hand.

I gave it to her.

She inspected it.

"This is bad thread."

"It was ordinary repair thread."

"It is ordinary bad repair thread."

"I paid bits."

"You overpaid."

"How much?"

"I don't know. I wasn't there."

"Then your appraisal lacks evidence."

She threw thread at me.

I caught it against chest.

"Fix your own seam."

"There it is."

"What?"

"Home."

She almost smiled.

Then did.

"Idiot."

I looked at high shelf.

"My cup."

"Still cracked."

"Historical significance?"

"Trash with tenure."

"Strong classification."

"You are not drinking from it."

"We'll see."

"We will not."

I crutched over to shelf.

Could not reach comfortably without leaning.

Lyssa watched.

"Do not fall for cup."

"I'm evaluating access."

"Evaluate from floor."

I left it.

Growth.

She began clearing pins from table.

I took off coat.

Bag down.

Crutches against wall in familiar angle.

The room accepted my stuff without ceremony.

That might have been best part.

I did not need to decide where bag belonged.

I knew.

Lyssa knew.

There was already a hook.

She said, "Eat?"

"Yes."

"I have bread. And lentils."

"Marry me."

"No."

"Harsh."

"You say that whenever food is already cooked."

"Pattern recognition."

She put pot on low heat.

I sat again.

The sealed Hark & Venn note remained on papers.

I could see it from chair.

White fold.

Workshop mark.

My name.

Lyssa followed my eyes.

"You can open it."

"I know."

"But?"

I looked at pot.

At her.

At cup.

At shirt on pillow.

At one-short-leg chair.

At the little new lamp.

At my bag back on hook.

"Later."

She nodded.

No praise.

Good.

We ate.

She told me the green-thread story with more detail than letter had included and got angry again at exact same point.

Apparently one customer had claimed "green is green" while holding two shades against daylight for ten minutes.

Lyssa considered this a personal attack on language.

I told her about East Fen.

Not the whole transaction ledger.

Orin's yard.

Reed-glass with ugly color that passed because color didn't matter.

She liked that part.

"See? Sometimes people actually mean they don't care which color."

"That was glass."

"Still color."

"Different profession."

I told her about stillhook.

She listened while tearing bread.

When I finished with eleven-copper sale, she said, "Good."

I frowned.

"You don't know what it did."

"You didn't spend eleven."

"I couldn't safely."

"Exactly."

"I wanted it."

"I gathered."

"It might have been very useful."

"It is currently very useful to somebody else."

Cruel.

I loved her.

Then I told her about High Cinder road ground.

She asked if I had touched anything.

"No."

"Good."

"You and Hessa would get along."

"I don't want that curse."

Fair.

I did not tell her about illegal freight.

Not yet.

Not because I planned to lie forever.

Because warm lentils after returning home did not need a customs confession stapled to them.

Some conversations deserved chosen timing.

After food, she took my coat to inspect loose seam.

I said, "That's shirt."

"This one is also loose."

"What?"

"Here."

She showed me a thread near pocket.

I stared.

"Road is predatory."

"Cloth experiences use."

"Less poetic."

"More true."

She handed coat back.

"Tomorrow."

I nodded.

Tomorrow.

That word felt good.

Not because anything was solved.

Because there was one.

I checked body before bed mostly from habit.

Hands fine.

Shoulder better.

Residual limb ordinary.

Right leg stiff from travel but settling.

No skin break.

No magic.

Fifty-nine.

Fifty-three.

One hundred thirty-nine.

Cash twelve copper after fare.

Vale twenty-three.

Bits unspecified.

Ten floor.

Two flexible copper.

No panic.

No debt payment.

No artifact.

No income.

Home.

The Hark & Venn note stayed sealed on papers.

I moved it once so cup of water would not spill on it.

That was all.

Lyssa got into bed beside me and immediately stole more blanket than physics allowed.

I said, "I was gone for over a week."

"And?"

"I thought absence might reset territorial claims."

"No."

"Unreasonable."

"You can leave again."

I pulled blanket back.

She pulled harder.

We negotiated through violence.

Eventually I had enough.

Barely.

She turned away from me.

I put arm around her.

She backed into me without comment.

Outside, someone dropped something metal in alley and swore.

A cart passed late.

Somewhere farther away, somebody laughed too loudly.

Carrow.

No fanfare.

No quest completion.

No return reward.

Just the city continuing around a room that had kept my cup, my shirt, my papers, and one person who was irritated I had been difficult to locate.

I had spent six copper to come home.

Worth it.

# CHAPTER 480

## THE WEARER

The Hark & Venn note was still on the table in the morning.

Lyssa had put a cup beside it.

Not the cracked cup.

Apparently there were limits to symbolism before breakfast.

I sat down, looked at the fold, and did not immediately open it.

Lyssa was already dressed for work, hair tied back badly enough that one side had escaped. She was sorting dark thread into three piles and muttering at one of them.

I said, "You know the letter is watching me."

"It is paper."

"Hostile paper."

"Open it or eat."

"Those are not mutually exclusive."

"They are if you drip lentils on it."

Good point.

I opened it.

No dramatic seal.

No bill folded inside.

No tiny drawing of a leg with a knife through it.

Strong start.

The note was short.

**HARK & VENN WORK ORDER**

**RETURN FOR FIT REVIEW BEFORE FINAL SOCKET WORK.**

**BRING CURRENT TRIAL COMPONENTS, LINER, AND WEAR NOTES.**

**EXISTING QUOTE UNCHANGED. NO ADDITIONAL PAYMENT REQUESTED FOR THIS REVIEW.**

**DO NOT INCREASE UNSUPERVISED WEAR ONLY BECAUSE TRAVEL SESSIONS WERE TOLERATED.**

I read the last line twice.

Lyssa looked over.

"Did they write specifically to insult you?"

"Apparently."

"Good shop."

"Everyone is joining Hessa."

"Maybe because you make the same face before doing something stupid."

"I have several faces."

"You have one face and too much confidence in it."

I folded the note.

That was the entire financial event.

No surprise balance.

No due date.

No threat.

Existing quote unchanged.

Twenty copper deposit still paid.

One silver and eighteen copper remained the quoted total for the work order, but the note did not tell me what portion was due when, and neither did I.

Excellent.

I had twelve copper.

I kept twelve copper.

Progress could occur without money leaving my hand.

That felt suspicious after the last month.

Lyssa held up two spools.

"Which is darker?"

"The left."

She swapped hands.

"Now?"

"The right."

"Wrong."

"You cheated."

"I moved them."

"Exactly."

She stared at me.

I stared back.

Then she said, "Take your leg and leave."

Romance.

The trial components were still stored where I had left them.

Socket.

Liner.

Temporary ash shaft.

Medium test foot.

Nothing glamorous.

The whole assembly looked less like a replacement body part than a workshop argument that had learned to stand upright.

I checked everything before packing it.

No split in temporary shaft.

No obvious socket crack.

Foot attachment tight by hand check.

Liner dry.

Straps intact.

I did not improve anything.

I put the pieces in the carrying cage, tied it closed, and discovered again that carrying a leg while using two crutches was an excellent advertisement for owning a leg.

The route to Hark & Venn was familiar enough that I stopped thinking about turns.

That was another kind of progress.

Not important enough to write down.

The workshop smelled the same.

Wood dust.

Oil.

Hot leather.

Something sharp and mineral from the rear room.

A man at the front bench was filing a metal brace while talking to a customer who kept answering questions he had not been asked.

"No, because I can walk stairs. I told the other fellow I can walk stairs, and he said that does not mean the hinge is correct, which I understand, obviously, but I can walk stairs, so the hinge cannot be completely wrong, can it? Because if it were completely wrong, stairs would become more difficult, and they are difficult, but stairs are always difficult, especially the narrow kind, which is why I think the hinge is mostly correct."

The bench man kept filing.

"Sit."

"I can sit."

"Good."

The customer sat.

Different speech.

Excellent.

I waited.

Nobody rushed because Greg had returned from the east with exciting stories about capitalism.

Also excellent.

A woman from the rear room came out, saw the carrying cage, and pointed at the fitting bench.

"Work order?"

I handed her the note.

She read my name.

"Trial set?"

I lifted cage slightly.

"Unless somebody replaced it with bread."

She did not laugh.

Better.

"Bring notes."

I gave her the notebook.

That hurt more.

She read while I sat.

Not everything.

Only wear entries.

Short sessions.

Skin response.

Surface.

Crutch use.

Shoulder state.

Travel rest.

She stopped at Lakeward courtyard entry.

"Twelve minutes."

"Yes."

"One crutch brief."

"Yes."

"Two-hand basin carry."

"Empty."

"Good."

She turned page.

"Why no wear after that?"

"Road travel. Then Cinder. Then I did not want to increase while shoulder was recovering."

She nodded once.

Not praise.

Just information fitting into place.

"Socket off when you stopped?"

"Yes."

"Pink where?"

I pointed.

Upper contact, slightly front and outside.

She pressed the liner there.

"Any heat?"

"No."

"Deep ache?"

"No."

"Skin open?"

"No."

"Phantom change?"

"No."

"Residual pain after?"

"No."

She looked at me.

"You rehearsed."

"I have had a lot of people ask me questions."

"Good."

That one might have been praise.

Dangerous.

She took the test socket from cage and ran two fingers along inside.

Then she compared it to my residual limb without fitting, eyes moving between shape and old pencil marks on socket edge.

"You changed."

I looked down.

"Usually."

"Volume."

"Ah."

She pressed lightly around upper tissue.

Not painful.

"Travel?"

"Several weeks."

"More walking on crutches. Some trial wear. Lots of sitting. Bad food."

"Bad food does not help me."

"It helped me."

"No."

Fair.

She brought a narrow cloth tape.

Measurements.

Three points.

Then again.

Then checked old workshop card pulled from a drawer.

No announcement.

No dramatic "your body has transformed."

Numbers.

That was better.

She said, "Old front pad is too much now."

"Too much pressure?"

"Too much correction for current shape."

"Difference?"

"If I leave it, you may tolerate ten minutes and hate thirty."

That was concrete enough.

She removed a thin temporary pad from inside test socket.

Replaced it with a narrower wedge from a tray.

Then added a small soft strip higher on the inside wall.

I watched.

Did not ask why every six seconds.

Growth.

She handed liner over.

"Fit."

I did.

Socket on.

Temporary shaft.

Foot.

Both crutches.

Stand.

The first load felt wrong.

Not painful.

Wrong.

My left side arrived half a beat earlier than expected.

I shifted.

She said, "Don't fix it."

I stopped.

Annoying.

She crouched and watched.

"Again. Weight off. Then on."

I did.

"Where?"

"Upper inside catches first."

"Pressure or contact?"

"Contact."

"Then?"

"Lower front."

She marked socket.

"Again."

Three cycles.

Then she took it off.

Adjusted strip.

Back on.

Standing again.

Better.

Not perfect.

Better enough that my body stopped trying to solve it before I asked.

She said, "Walk rail."

Parallel rails ran along one side of fitting room.

I had used them before.

Crutches stayed with me first.

Ten steps.

Turn.

Ten back.

She watched foot.

Then hip.

Then shoulder.

Not face.

Specialists had priorities.

"Again slower."

I did.

At step six she said, "You're shortening right."

I stopped.

"Because left is long?"

"Maybe."

That answer pleased me.

She checked height.

No change.

"Not long."

"Then why?"

"You learned to protect it."

I frowned.

"The left?"

"Whole pattern."

She gestured.

"Crutch habit. Right leg takes certainty. Left gets permission after."

That sounded true enough to be dangerous.

"Can I correct it?"

"Yes."

"How?"

"Not by thinking harder."

Hostile profession.

She put one finger against rail.

"Stand. Both feet where I put them."

I did.

"Weight right."

Done.

"Center."

Done.

"Left enough to feel socket."

Done.

"Center."

Again.

No walking.

Just transfers.

After six, she said, "Now step."

I stepped.

Right side still wanted to hurry.

Less.

"Again."

We did that for several minutes.

Not training montage.

Nobody became beautiful.

The test foot made a small dull knock every time I placed it badly.

Useful feedback.

Annoying feedback.

At one point the customer from front room leaned around partition.

"I also shorten one side."

The fitter said, "You are not in this fitting."

"I know, I was only saying."

"Don't."

He disappeared.

Good.

After about eight minutes total standing and walking, she stopped me.

"Off."

"I feel fine."

"Wonderful."

"That sounded hostile."

"It was eight minutes."

Right.

Socket off.

Skin check.

She looked before I did.

Mild pink at upper inside contact.

Nothing sharp.

No lower front hotspot.

She pressed.

"Pain?"

"No."

"Heat?"

"No."

We waited.

Not exciting.

That was the point.

Five minutes.

Pink already fading.

She wrote on workshop card.

Then looked at my notes again.

"You want hands."

I looked up.

"Yes."

"Not distance."

"Distance too, eventually."

"But work order priority."

That had apparently survived.

"Hands."

She nodded.

"Good. Then stop treating one-crutch walking as the test."

I thought about stillhook.

One hand on stud.

One support hand free if leg worked.

Or two hands briefly if standing geometry allowed.

"Standing tasks?"

"Yes."

"Carrying?"

"Light, controlled."

"How light?"

She looked around.

There was a wooden tray on shelf.

Empty.

She put it in my hands.

"That."

Scientific units remained difficult.

I stood with trial limb and both crutches.

Then she took left crutch away.

One crutch right.

Tray left.

Fine.

Then she took right crutch too.

I looked at her.

She said, "Rails."

Both rails within reach.

Right.

Not free standing in middle of room.

I held tray with both hands for maybe five seconds.

My body stiffened immediately.

Not falling.

Preparing too hard.

She tapped tray.

"Put down."

I did.

"Again. Don't perform standing."

"I was standing."

"You were auditioning."

Theatre had infected medicine.

I tried again.

This time knees softer.

Weight mostly right, some left.

Tray in both hands.

Three breaths.

Four.

I reached one hand to rail.

No drama.

She said, "Better."

I said, "Could carry a lamp."

"Not yet."

"A very light lamp."

"No."

"An empty lamp."

"No."

"Hostile."

"Alive."

We repeated with tray from one shelf to another two steps away.

Both hands only during the actual lift.

One hand to rail before moving feet.

Then place.

Then reset.

The movement was absurdly small.

It was also more useful than another hundred feet of hallway walking.

I could imagine work.

Not imaginary heroic work.

Real work.

Carry folded cloth.

Move a plate.

Hold a ledger while standing.

Open a door with one hand while the other steadied.

Use an artifact that demanded continuous contact.

That last one stayed in my head.

I did not tell the fitter about a stillhook I did not own.

Strong discipline.

She stopped the session at fifteen minutes total wear.

I had forgotten to count.

Excellent.

Socket off again.

Skin.

Upper inside pink.

Fading.

No heat.

No pain.

She wrote.

Then she took the socket back to bench.

"Leave trial socket."

I looked at it.

"Why?"

"Final work needs new shape."

There.

Progress.

"New mold?"

"Not full."

She held up the old work card.

"Measurements changed enough I don't want final socket cut from old front correction. We adjust model from today and keep this trial here so shop can compare."

"Final socket work starts now?"

"Prep."

Specific.

Good.

"Does that change quote?"

"No."

"Payment?"

"Not today."

Also specific.

Excellent.

"When do I come back?"

"When shop sends note, or if we ask you to test another temporary fit first."

No invented date.

No fantasy scheduling certainty.

"What do I wear until then?"

"Crutches."

I looked at empty left trouser.

"I had guessed."

"Good."

"Any home trial?"

"Not with this socket. It's staying."

Ah.

That changed something.

The trial limb had been mine in the practical sense for weeks.

Not ownership.

Availability.

Now it was not coming home.

I felt oddly disappointed.

She saw.

"That a problem?"

"No."

"Good."

"I liked having it."

"Then finish it."

Simple.

I almost said I was trying.

Did not.

The work existed whether I narrated effort or not.

She packed liner separately and handed it back.

"Keep liner clean. Bring it next time."

"Anything else?"

"Do not make your crutch gait worse because you miss the test leg."

I stared.

"Was that likely?"

"Yes."

Rude.

Probably correct.

I asked one more question.

"Today changes what?"

She answered immediately.

"Fit model. Current load pattern. Work priority."

"Not final clearance."

"No."

"Not finished limb."

"No."

"Not payment."

"No."

"Good."

She looked at me.

"You need every sentence boxed?"

"Sometimes."

"Then box that."

I did.

Outside, I had the carrying cage without the socket, shaft, and foot.

Much lighter.

Mostly liner and straps.

That should have felt good.

Instead it felt like walking home after leaving part of a future body on somebody else's bench.

Not sad.

Not exactly.

Just strange.

I stopped at a food stall and bought lunch with bits.

No copper.

Twelve remained twelve.

Vale twenty-three.

Ten floor.

Two above.

Economic position narrow.

Earning capacity better than the purse suggested.

Same story.

I did not need to repeat it into submission.

At home, Lyssa was not there.

Good.

She had a life.

The room was quieter without her work noise.

I put liner where it stayed clean.

The high shelf cup watched me.

"Don't."

It said nothing.

Strong relationship.

I opened notebook.

**H&V REVIEW**

Then exact note terms.

Existing quote unchanged.

No additional payment requested for review.

Trial socket retained by shop.

Today's measurements changed enough that final socket prep should not use old front correction unchanged.

No final clearance.

No pickup date.

No new payment schedule.

Bring liner next time.

Wait for shop note or requested temporary refit.

Then body.

Fifteen minutes total supervised test wear.

Adjusted upper inside contact.

No sharp pressure.

Pink fading after removal.

Right-side shortening appears protective habit, not established length issue.

Standing-task priority remains hands, not distance.

Two-hand tray hold only within rail reach.

No lamp.

I underlined that last one because apparently adults required rules.

Then I wrote:

**Trial equipment leaving home is progress, not loss.**

I stared at it.

Too neat.

I crossed out **progress, not loss**.

Wrote:

**Shop kept trial socket because final prep needs current fit. I miss having it. Both can be true.**

Better.

The door opened.

Lyssa came in carrying cloth under one arm and a paper sack in the other.

She looked at empty cage.

"Where's the leg?"

"Shop kept it."

She stopped.

"Bad?"

"No."

"Then why?"

"Final socket prep."

Her face changed slightly.

Interested.

Not celebration.

"Good?"

"Probably."

She pointed at me.

"That was a question."

"Yes. Good."
