"Then say good."

"Good."

She put cloth down.

"Did you pay?"

"No."

That got more reaction.

"Excellent."

"Existing quote unchanged."

"Even better."

"You're very romantic about money."

"I live with you."

Fair.

She opened paper sack.

Bread.

Onions.

I looked at onions.

Then at her.

"Road house."

"What?"

"Nothing."

She began putting food away.

I told her fitter said my right side was shortening because I had learned to protect the left.

Lyssa said, "You do that."

I frowned.

"You've noticed?"

"You limp differently when tired."

"Why didn't you say?"

"Because you already know you limp."

"Different pattern."

She shrugged.

"I am not Hessa."

"Everyone keeps saying that while acting like Hessa."

"I said you move differently. I did not write a report."

Fair.

She looked at me again.

"Does it hurt?"

"No."

"Then good."

That was her evidence ceiling.

Simple.

Human.

Enough.

I did not turn dinner into a biomechanics conference.

We ate.

The sealed Hark & Venn note was gone now because it had become an opened Hark & Venn note folded inside my notebook.

One old uncertainty converted into three narrower ones.

Final socket prep had begun.

No date.

No payment due today.

No guarantee.

Good.

The limb was not finished.

Also good, in a more irritating way.

I had twelve copper.

Vale had twenty-three.

Protocol stayed fifty-nine, fifty-three, one hundred thirty-nine.

No magic.

No artifact.

No income.

No debt payment.

But the body lane had moved.

Not because I spent more.

Because I returned to the specialist, let them tell me the old fit was no longer the right fit, and left part of the equipment behind so they could make the next version.

That was less satisfying than buying something.

Probably healthier.

Lyssa finished eating and pointed at my coat.

"Tomorrow I fix that seam."

"I can do it."

"No."

"You told me to fix my own seam."

"That was before I saw how you bought thread."

"I have been professionally insulted all day."

"Good."

I looked at her.

At the room.

At the liner drying clean.

At the empty place where the trial cage had been heavier yesterday.

Tomorrow still existed.

So did the leg.

Just not here.

For once, that was exactly where it needed to be.

# CHAPTER 481

## THE SUBSTITUTE

The theatre came back for me after breakfast.

Not metaphorically.

There was a knock on the door while Lyssa was trying to convince a customer that a sleeve could not be both tighter and easier to move in.

The customer said, "I only mean here."

Lyssa said, "That is where your arm bends."

"Yes."

"So if I make it tighter there, bending becomes worse."

"I understand that."

"No, you don't."

Knock again.

I looked at door.

Lyssa pointed at it with two pins.

"Useful."

Apparently I had a household role.

I opened the door.

The man outside wore theatre black, though his shirt had faded enough that one shoulder was almost brown.

I knew his face.

Not name.

He knew mine.

"Greg."

"Unfortunately."

He looked relieved anyway.

"We came by."

"Lyssa mentioned."

"You're home."

"Observant institution."

He looked past me.

Lyssa said from table, "If you want him, take him."

The customer said, "Can I move now?"

"No."

The theatre man looked back at me.

"We need a substitute this afternoon."

There.

Not summons.

Not destiny.

A shift.

"What kind?"

"Prompt side."

That mattered.

"Not rail?"

"No."

"Not load?"

"No."

"Not trap?"

"No."

"Not running stairs?"

He looked at crutches.

"No."

Good theatre.

I said, "What happened?"

"Renn is sick."

A name I knew enough to place.

Prompt-side support.

Not friend.

Not stranger.

"How sick?"

"Enough that he sent word instead of arriving."

Reasonable threshold.

"What do you need covered?"

He had answer ready.

"Rehearsal reset, entrance holds, prop table, cue copy. Second half into evening if we keep schedule."

"Scene changes?"

"Light ones. Anything heavy gets two other hands."

"Who owns calls?"

"Stage lead."

"Who owns props?"

"Prop mistress until handoff."

"Who owns fly?"

"Fly crew."

"Then why me?"

He looked genuinely confused.

"You've done the room."

Right.

Not because I was secretly the best support specialist alive.

Because I had worked there before.

Useful distinction.

"Pay?"

"Three copper if you stay through evening close. Two if we release before supper."

There it was.

Three copper.

Not silver.

Not leverage.

Not life-changing.

Also not one-copper desperation work.

And I was twelve copper with a ten-copper floor.

Three mattered.

I said, "Food?"

"Staff pot if you're there through supper."

Better.

"Anything I need to bring?"

"Yourself. Pencil if you distrust ours."

"I distrust everyone's pencils."

"Then bring one."

Lyssa said, "He'll come."

I looked back.

She was pinning sleeve.

"You negotiate for me now?"

"I want you out of room before you explain elbows."

Customer said, "I would listen."

"No, you wouldn't."

The theatre man waited.

I thought about Hark & Venn.

Trial socket gone to shop.

Liner home.

Two crutches.

Shoulder improved.

No new skin problem.

No magic issue.

No reason not to work.

Also no reason to pretend this was capital strategy.

Sometimes three copper was three copper.

And sometimes returning to a place that had known you before you left was useful for reasons that did not fit under MONEY.

"I'll come."

"Second bell after midday."

"I know."

He nodded.

"Good."

Then left.

I closed door.

Lyssa said, "Three?"

"Three if evening."

"Good."

"You heard all of that."

"Small room."

"Privacy remains theoretical."

"Pay rent somewhere larger."

Cruel economic policy.

I got my notebook.

Not the road one.

The theatre pages were older.

Folded cue copies.

Prop notes.

One page with a stain I still hoped was soup.

I found enough blank paper.

Lyssa saw.

"You're smiling."

"No."

"You are."

"Bad lighting."

"It's morning."

"Exactly."

She smiled without looking up.

I hated that.

The walk to theatre was familiar enough to be annoying.

Carrow streets had already resumed demanding things from my body.

Uneven curb.

Delivery cart blocking half lane.

Two boys carrying a board sideways like civilization had not invented turning.

I arrived early because old habits had survived amputation, travel, capitalism, and several poor decisions.

The theatre smelled right.

Dust.

Lamp oil.

Old cloth.

Hot rope.

Paint.

A little mildew where the back wall always lost an argument with rain.

Nobody had fixed that while I was gone.

Comforting.

The front house was dark except for work lamps.

Onstage, three actors were arguing over a bench.

Not emotionally.

Literally.

One wanted it two feet left.

One wanted it where it was.

Third was sitting on it and refusing to participate in the geometry.

A stage lead I recognized by role more than friendship stood in aisle with cue book.

She looked at me.

"You're alive."

"Mostly."

"Good enough."

Excellent welcome.

She pointed prompt side.

"Renn's table."

I went.

The table was a crime scene.

Cue copy.

Two chalks.

Bell pull.

Prop handoff sheet.

Cup with three pencils.

One pencil broken.

A little wooden marker carved with ACT TWO.

And half an apple.

I looked at apple.

Stage lead said, "Not his."

"That creates more questions."

"Later."

Good.

I sat on the tall prompt stool.

Then got off immediately.

Too high for crutches.

I pulled a low chair instead.

That was the first adaptation.

No speech.

No dignity loss.

Chair.

The stage lead came over.

"Need anything?"

"Clear path from here to prop table. No loose cable across it."

She looked.

There was loose cable.

"That wasn't there yesterday."

"Still here today."

She kicked it aside, then stopped.

"Not enough."

Called a hand.

They rerouted it properly against wall.

Good.

I asked, "What changed?"

She opened cue copy.

"Act two entrance order. Third scene now starts with Nella already onstage. Door two sticks again. Bench moves after supper scene, not before. Bell cue seven moved under line instead of after it. We lost one lantern because somebody dropped it."

From stage, an actor yelled, "It slipped."

Stage lead yelled back, "Gravity remains undefeated."

The actor said, "I maintain the handle participated."

Different room.

Same species.

I wrote changes.

Not all theatre work needed mystical significance.

Sometimes the job was knowing which door was broken.

Rehearsal started.

For the first twenty minutes, my contribution was almost nothing.

Hold door two.

Mark missed entrance.

Reset letter prop.

Hand bell rope to stage lead when she came over.

Write down that the bench had moved too far downstage because one leg caught a floor seam.

Then the room became a room.

Actor in blue coat missed entrance because he was still arguing with wardrobe.

The woman playing Nella entered early because she had been told yesterday's order and nobody had reached her before call.

Prop cup appeared on wrong table.

One scene started without the red scarf because somebody had moved it to drying rail.

Nobody died.

Nobody was incompetent.

The system had changed in six small places and people were carrying different versions in their heads.

Ah.

There it was.

I knew that problem.

The stage lead stopped scene.

"Again from bell seven."

Actor on bench said, "From line?"

"From bell."

"Before line?"

"Under line."

"Which line?"

The stage lead closed her eyes.

I said, "Your line about the widow's garden."

He looked at me.

"Oh."

Nella said, "That is not my line."

"It is his."

"I know. I'm just saying nobody calls it that."

Actor said, "I call it that now."

Stage lead pointed at me.

"Write widow's garden in margin."

I did.

The cue copy became marginally worse and operationally better.

Good theatre.

At second run, Nella held entrance correctly.

Door two stuck.

I braced one crutch, pulled latch with free hand, and got it open.

Not elegant.

But I could do that with two crutches because I was not moving at same time.

Hands target.

There.

The work limb was absent and still changing how I thought about work.

I did not narrate that aloud.

Progress.

A prop hand arrived with red scarf.

"Where?"

"Second hook until supper scene."

"Why?"

"Because first hook is letter now."

He stared at hooks.

"There are three."

"Correct."

"Which is second?"

I looked.

Someone had removed little chalk numbers.

Of course.

I marked wall.

One.

Two.

Three.

Prop hand said, "That seems obvious."

"It becomes obvious after someone does it."

He hung scarf.

Thirty seconds later another hand tried to put cup on hook two.

I stopped him.

"Table."

"Renn uses hook."

"Not today."

"Why?"

"Scarf."

He looked at scarf.

"Who put scarf there?"

The first prop hand yelled from across room, "Me."

"Why?"

"Because Greg told me."

Now several people looked at me.

I raised pencil.

"Temporary government."

Nobody respected it.

Good.

By midday, the room had stabilized.

Not because I had solved theatre.

Because the current cue copy and current physical room finally matched.

That was enough.

We ate staff pot behind scene shop.

Beans.

Onion.

Something green that had surrendered identity.

Free.

I accepted.

One actor sat near me and talked for most of meal.

Not to me specifically.

To the air around us.

"I think the problem is the widow's garden line is too early because if I say it there then Nella's reaction belongs to information she has not emotionally received yet, which I know sounds like nonsense because she hears the line at same time, but emotionally the scene hasn't caught up, and yes I understand the bell is practical, but practical things still have dramatic consequences, and I am not saying move bell, I am saying maybe I need to arrive at the line differently."

He ate one spoonful.

Continued.

I said, "Tell the stage lead."

"I did."

"What did she say?"

"She said stop arriving differently every run."

Fair.

He frowned into beans.

"She may have a point."

"Terrible."

"I know."

He kept talking.

I listened enough.

Not every conversation needed winning.

After meal came scene-change rehearsal.

This was where my body mattered more.

The old version of my theatre work had involved carrying things because carrying things was available.

Current version did not.

I owned that before someone else had to.

Stage lead said, "Bench reset."

Two hands moved bench.

I did not.

"Lantern stand."

One hand reached for it.

I said, "I can place marks."

Stage lead nodded.

I chalked where feet belonged.

Crutch.

Bend carefully.

Mark.

Stand.

Slow.

Still useful.

One stagehand glanced at me, then at bench.

"You want lighter side?"

"No."

"You sure?"

"Yes."

He shrugged and kept moving.

That was good too.

No inspiration speech.

No pity contest.

The job changed.

At one point a narrow side table needed to shift only a foot.

I could have done it by hooking one hand under edge and hopping.

I did not.

Because that was stupid.

I called, "Table."

A hand moved it.

Then I reset papers on it.

Shared work.

Theatre did not require every person to prove maximum independence.

By late afternoon, the stage lead had stopped checking whether I knew what she meant by reset.

She would call:

"Garden."

I reset letter and bench marks.

"Door."

I checked latch.

"Seven."

I set bell rope and cue line.

"Red."

Scarf.

Compact language.

Earned language.

Not universal sarcasm.

Good.

The rehearsal itself got better.

That mattered.

The actors stopped tripping over changed entrances.

The bench stopped fighting floor seam.

Nella's early presence changed the scene in a way I actually liked.

She sat before the others entered and mended something small while listening to conversation she was pretending not to hear.

That gave the whole scene weight.

Not tactical weight.

Not foreshadowing weight.

Theatrical weight.

The audience would understand that she had been there before anyone acknowledged her.

Nice.

At one point I forgot to watch cue sheet because I watched her performance.

Bell seven came late.

Stage lead looked at me.

I rang.

Too late.

Scene stopped.

She said, "Greg."

"My fault."

Actor in blue said, "I thought it was emotionally interesting."

Stage lead said, "You think weather is emotionally interesting."

"It is."

"Again."

I wrote **WATCH THE DAMN CUE** beside bell seven.

Theatre remained capable of punishing abstraction.

Second attempt clean.

Before evening run, wardrobe sent down a coat with one cuff pinned wrong.

Not my department.

It became my problem because the actor wearing it was due through door two in less than a minute and the loose pin was aimed directly at his wrist.

I said, "Stop."

He stopped.

Good actor.

I pointed.

He looked down.

"Oh."

Wardrobe hand came over at speed, saw pin, and said, "Who did that?"

Actor said, "I assumed you."

She looked offended enough that I almost apologized on his behalf.

"I pin inward."

"Apparently someone pins politically."

"Hold still."

She fixed cuff in twelve seconds.

Then looked at me.

"Thank you."

"Selfishly motivated. Blood complicates props."

She did not laugh.

The actor did.

Door cue came.

He went.

No injury.

No lesson.

Just one more tiny failure that became nothing because someone noticed before it became expensive.

Evening close happened after lamps came on.

No audience.

Just rehearsal.

But the room had shape again.

Stage lead signed the day's call sheet.

Then counted three copper into my hand.

No ceremony.

Twelve became fifteen.

She said, "Renn may be back tomorrow."

"Good."

"You available if he isn't?"

There.

Potential repeat.

Not entitlement.

I said, "Send word."

"Here or home?"

"Home."

She stared.

"I know where that is."

"Apparently everyone does now."

"Your woman explained."

Of course.

"What did she say?"

"That if we lost you again, we should charge postage to your pay."

I nodded.

"Fair."

The theatre man from morning was coiling a cable nearby.

He said, "She said more than that."

"I don't need all evidence."

"Growth."

I looked at him.

He grinned.

Traitor institution.

I put three copper away.

Fifteen.

Five above floor.

Still twenty-three Vale.

Still no debt payment.

Still nowhere near clearing work-limb balance safely.

But the cash floor had room again.

More importantly, I had worked a known place without turning myself back into who I had been before leaving.

I had not carried the bench.

I had not climbed fly stairs.

I had not hidden the crutches.

I had not made the room accommodate me through vague goodwill either.

I had asked for clear path.

Used chair.

Taken prompt-side work.

Called other hands for moving things.

Done the parts I could do well.

That was not lesser theatre.

It was just current theatre.

On walk home, my shoulders were tired.

Not injured.

Hands a little sore.

Right leg had done a full day of city standing and short movement.

Residual limb ordinary.

No trial wear because shop had hardware.

Protocol unchanged.

Fifty-nine.

Fifty-three.

One hundred thirty-nine.

No magic.

No artifact.

No clever deal.

Three copper for one skilled substitution.

Fine.

At home, Lyssa was sewing my coat.

She looked up.

"How much?"

"Three."

"Good."

"How was your sleeve war?"

"She wanted it tighter."

"Did you make it tighter?"

"No."

"Victory."

"She comes back tomorrow."

"War continues."

Lyssa bit thread.

Then looked at my face.

"You had fun."

"No."

"You did."

"I was paid."

"That isn't answer."

I put purse on table.

"Rehearsal improved."

There.

She smiled.

Not huge.

Enough.

"What were you doing?"

"Prompt side."

"Meaning?"

"Stopping adults from putting red cloth in wrong places."

"Heroic."

"At scale."

She threw a scrap at me.

I caught it.

With one hand.

Because the other was on crutch.

I looked at scrap.

Then at her.

She said, "Do not make that meaningful."

"Wasn't going to."

"Liar."

Maybe.

I sat.

Fifteen copper.

Twenty-three with Vale.

Bits unspecified.

Ten floor.

Five above.

Hark & Venn working offscreen.

Theatre maybe tomorrow, maybe not.

Home still home.

I opened notebook.

Wrote:

**THEATRE. PROMPT-SIDE SUBSTITUTE. 3C. CURRENT CUE/ROOM ALIGNMENT. NO HEAVY SHIFT.**

Then:

**Useful work did not require pretending old body.**

I considered that.

Too neat.

Crossed it out.

Wrote instead:

**Door two still sticks.**

Better.

That was enough.

# CHAPTER 482

## THE INTERRUPTER

Hessa's note arrived folded around one sentence.

**COME SHOW ME WHAT YOU DID, NOT WHAT YOU THINK YOU DID.**

Comforting.

There was a second line underneath.

**BRING THE NOTEBOOK. DO NOT PRACTICE FIRST.**

Less comforting.

I showed Lyssa.

She read both lines.

"She knows you."

"Everybody is becoming hostile."

"Maybe you're consistent."

"That is not defense."

"It wasn't."

I had fifteen copper.

Twenty-three with Vale.

Bits unspecified.

Ten floor.

Five above it.

Hark & Venn had the trial socket, temporary shaft, and test foot.

I had the liner.

Theatre might want me again if Renn stayed sick.

Nobody had sent word.

Good.

One day could contain one thing.

Apparently revolutionary.

I packed the magic notebook.

Not the road notebook.

They had become separate mostly because the road notebook was fat enough to injure somebody.

The magic pages were thinner and somehow more embarrassing.

Numbers had that effect.

Fifty-nine supervised minimal draws.

Fifty-three deliberate shaping attempts.

One hundred thirty-nine external-effect attempts.

The last number had become dangerous in my head.

Not because it was high.

Because one of those attempts had mattered outside a room.

Emergency.

Bad footing.

Bad timing.

A motion I had not wanted continuing.

I had pushed.

Something had moved enough differently that I stayed upright.

Useful.

Also terrible evidence.

Hessa's favorite kind of problem.

I left home on two crutches.

No trial limb.

No magic on the way.

No private warm-up in an alley because I had been specifically told not to be stupid in writing.

Growth.

Hessa's workroom looked almost unchanged.

That was reassuring until I noticed the table had moved.

Half a pace left.

I stopped in the doorway.

"Table."

"What about it?"

"It moved."

"Yes."

"Why?"

"Because I moved it."

Excellent investigation.

Hessa held out a hand.

"Notebook."

I gave it to her.

That always hurt.

She opened near the back.

Not the old marked-strip pages.

The newer pages.

Travel.

Attempts.

Failures.

The emergency entry.

She read in silence.

I stayed standing until she pointed at chair.

"Sit."

I sat.

She kept reading.

No greeting.

No how was the east.

No congratulations for not dying.

Good specialist.

After a while she said, "One hundred thirty-nine."

"Yes."

"Why is one hundred thirty-nine not supervised?"

"Because the hill refused scheduling."

She looked at me.

"Again."

"Bad footing. Load shifted. I was already moving. I used the external effect because falling was worse."

"Target?"

"Not a target in the room sense."

"Greg."

"My own relation to the moving load and crutch plant."

"That is still not a target."

"I know."

"What did you intend?"

There.

Harder.

"Interrupt the motion."

"Which motion?"

"The part that was continuing wrong."

"That is a sentence you wrote after surviving."

Fair.

I thought.

The actual moment had been faster.

No philosophy.

No protocol board.

Crutch tip moved.

Weight went somewhere it should not.

Body followed.

Then external effort.

"Rightward continuation," I said. "Relative to where I needed the plant."

"Better."

"Not good?"

"Better."

She turned notebook around.

My emergency entry was narrow.

Good.

No heroic paragraph.

No claim of force.

No claim of distance.

No claim that magic saved me by itself.

She tapped it.

"You wrote deflection."

"Yes."

"Do you know it deflected?"

"No."

"Do you know your body corrected?"

"Yes."

"Do you know the magic contributed?"

I hated this one.

"I know I attempted it."

"Correct."

"And the outcome changed."

"Many things changed."

"Also correct."

She looked pleased.

Terrible woman.

"You have been thinking about this for weeks."

"Yes."

"How often?"

"Enough."

"Have you tested it?"

"No."

"Good."

That sounded like praise.

I distrusted it.

She got up.

The old marked strip was not on the main table.

Instead there was a shallow wooden track about an arm long, set on two blocks.

At one end sat a small round wooden shuttle with a metal washer fixed into its center.

No glow.

No runes.

No theatrical significance.

I pointed.

"New toy?"

"Old drawer."

"What does it do?"

"Rolls badly."

"Specialist equipment."

"Very."

She lifted one end of the track by a finger width.

The shuttle rolled down.

Slow.

Straight enough.

Hit a padded stop.

She reset it.

"Today is not about making that move."

"Good."

"Today is not about stopping it."

Less good.

"Today is not about your emergency."

"Then why am I here?"

"Because you keep naming outcomes before you know which condition matters."

Hostile.

Accurate.

She put a chalk mark halfway down the track.

Another near the end.

Then a small upright card beside the middle.

"External only."

"So one-forty."

"If you attempt."

"Minimal and shaping stay fifty-nine and fifty-three."

"Yes."

Good.

Numbers.

Rules.

I liked rules when they were trying to stop me from lying.

Hessa said, "First condition. Shuttle at rest. Try to move it sideways."

"How much?"

"Enough that I can see it."

"Direction?"

"Toward window."

"Distance?"

"I said enough that I can see it."

Annoying.

I planted crutches beside chair and sat forward.

Shuttle at rest.

External effect had always been the strange lane.

Not touching.

Not shaping a held thing.

Not drawing something into myself.

Changing something out there.

Sometimes.

Badly.

I settled.

Looked at shuttle.

Attempted.

Nothing.

Hessa watched shuttle.

Not me.

Good.

"Count it."

"One-forty."

She wrote.

"Again?"

"No."

I looked up.

"One attempt?"

"Different condition."

Of course.

She reset track level.

"At rest taught us what?"

"Nothing visible under one attempt."

"Good."

She lifted track.

Shuttle rolled slowly.

"Do not touch yet."

It passed first chalk mark.

Stopped at padded end.

"Now what?"

"Same direction. Toward window. Attempt only after it passes middle mark."

Motion already happening.

There.

She reset.

Lift.

Roll.

The shuttle crossed first line.

Middle.

I attempted.

Nothing obvious.

It reached pad.

Hessa crouched.

Looked at track.

"One-forty-one."

"Nothing?"

"Did I say nothing?"

"No."

"Then don't help."

She reset.

This time she put a thin line of charcoal along the shuttle's near edge.

"Why?"

"Because eyes lie."

"Mine?"

"Everyone's."

Better.

She ran it again without magic.

The charcoal edge stayed nearly parallel to track.

Tiny wobble from bad wheel.

Baseline.

Again.

"One-forty-two. Same timing."

I waited.

Middle line.

Attempt.

The shuttle ticked.

Small.

Not dramatic.

Its leading edge angled maybe a finger toward window before padded stop.

I froze.

Hessa did not.

She marked where it struck padding.

Then ran finger along track.

"Again."

"You saw that."

"I saw a different end position."

"That's what I said."

"No. You said that with your face."

Rude.

Reset.

"One-forty-three."

Same track.

Same lift.

Same direction.

Middle line.

Attempt.

Nothing.

Straight to pad.

I exhaled.

Hessa wrote.

"Good."

"That was failure."

"Excellent observation."

"You said good."

"Because now you have two different outcomes under similar conditions."

"Which is worse."

"Which is information."

There was Hessa.

I leaned back.

Shoulders fine.

No body strain worth naming.

Magic effort had that familiar internal thinness.

Not pain.

More like reaching with something that had never learned anatomy.

Hessa watched me.

"Any change?"

"Small fatigue."

"Where?"

I pointed vaguely at center chest, then corrected.

"No. Not chest exactly. General."

"Write general."

I did.

She took a small wedge and changed track angle slightly.

Steeper.

"Why?"

"Speed."

"Same timing?"

"Same mark."

"So less time remaining after attempt."

"Yes."

"Direction?"

"Toward window."

She reset shuttle.

"One-forty-four."

It rolled faster.

Middle.

Attempt.

The magic felt late.

Not because I had missed line.

Because the thing already had more certainty.

That was not technical.

I hated the word.

The shuttle reached end.

Maybe touched padding slightly left of baseline.

Maybe not.

Hessa marked it as **unclear**.

Good.

"No clean claim."

"No."

She flattened track again.

Then did something I did not expect.

Turned the entire track around.

Now shuttle would roll the opposite direction through room.

I looked at her.

"Window direction changes relative to track."

"Yes."

"So you want same room direction?"

"No."

"What direction?"

"Right of travel."

There.

Different reference.

Not toward window.

Relative to moving object.

"Why?"

"Because your emergency description was relative to motion, not room."

That made my spine straighten.

Hessa noticed.

"Do not become excited."

"Impossible instruction."

"Try."

She ran baseline once.

Shuttle rolled opposite direction.

Same poor little wobble.

Reset.

"One-forty-five. After middle. Right of travel."

I waited.

Middle.

Attempt.

Nothing.

Pad.

Hessa marked.

Again.

"One-forty-six."

"How many today?"

"Until I stop."

Strong ethics.

Roll.

Middle.

Attempt.

The shuttle gave a tiny sideways jerk.

Not enough to leave track.

Enough that charcoal edge changed.

Right of travel.

I stared.

Hessa caught it before I said anything.

"Do not celebrate."

"Wasn't."

"You inhaled like a gambler."

"Breathing remains medically necessary."

"Reset your face."

I did not know how.

She measured no distance.

Good.

There was no useful ruler claim here.

Only visible deviation.

She marked **clean visible lateral change / moving / right-of-travel condition**.

One clean.

One.

Out of what?

Not enough.

She reset track level.

Then set shuttle at rest again.

"One-forty-seven. Right relative to what?"

I looked at stationary shuttle.

"Nothing."

"Exactly."

"Room right?"

"Choose."

"Room right."

Attempt.

Nothing.

Hessa wrote.

She folded hands.

"Now tell me what happened."

Trap.

I knew trap.

"Across eight external attempts today, two moving trials showed clean visible lateral changes?"

She raised eyebrow.

I counted.

140 rest no response.
141 moving no obvious.
142 moving clean.
143 moving none.
144 faster moving unclear.
145 reversed moving none.
146 reversed moving clean.
147 rest none.

Eight.

"Two clean moving deviations," I said. "One unclear at higher speed. Rest attempts no visible response. Other moving attempts no visible response."

"Good."

"That suggests motion matters."

"No."

I closed eyes.

"That suggests motion might matter."

"Better."

"Could be timing."

"Yes."

"Could be attention."

"Yes."

"Could be track vibration."

"Yes."

"Could be random variation in whatever the hell I'm doing."

"Yes."

"Could be target construction."

"Yes."

"You enjoy this."

"Immensely."

I believed her.

She pulled the marked strip from a drawer.

There it was.

Old enemy.

The strip from sessions where left, right, whole had behaved badly enough to create several weeks of restraint.

She set it down.

I looked.

"Are we using that?"

"No."

"Then why bring it out?"

"Comparison."

She opened older page.

Attempt seventy-six.

Left marked region, no response.

Seventy-seven.

Right marked region, clean small rotation, right end away relative to left.

Seventy-eight.

Whole strip, no response.

Hessa tapped page.

"What did you want seventy-seven to mean?"

"That I could affect part of object."

"What did it prove?"

"One clean rotation happened under right-marked attempt."

"Anything else?"

"No."

"What did you want one hundred thirty-nine to mean?"

"Emergency deflection."

"What did it prove?"

"That I attempted external effect during a bad motion and the situation ended differently than it might have."

She waited.

I sighed.

"Which proves almost nothing about mechanism."

"Good."

"Today?"

"Today gives me a reason to test moving targets again."

Not Greg.

Her.

Hessa's protocol.

Important.

"You have a next protocol."

"I have a candidate."

I smiled.

She pointed at me.

"That expression is why I use words carefully."

"Candidate protocol."

"Moving target. Fixed track. Known baseline. Variable timing later, not today."

"Direction reference?"

"Both room-fixed and travel-relative eventually."

"Speed?"

"Later."

"Mass?"

"Much later."

"Practical object?"

"No."

"Application?"

"No."

"Very hostile."

"Alive."

I looked at shuttle.

Small wooden disk.

Washer.

Bad wheel.

Two clean deviations.

Eight attempts.

No practical use.

And still.

It was first time in a while the external lane had felt like it was narrowing toward something I recognized.

Not force.

Not throwing.

Not invisible hand.

Interruption.

Maybe.

A motion exists.

A bad line exists.

Change enough that somebody gets another second.

A cart rolls wrong.

A blade travels wrong.

A falling tool misses foot.

A door swings.

A person loses balance.

No.

Too fast.

Too many applications.

I caught myself.

Hessa saw that too.

"What are you doing?"

"Ruining it with usefulness."

"Correct."

"I was imagining cases."

"Stop."

"Stopped."

"Your problem is you have sixty years of uses waiting to colonize one clean event."

"Fifty-nine."

"What?"

"Old age."

She stared.

I stared back.

Then she said, "That was not the important number."

Fair.

I looked at notebook.

External total now one hundred forty-seven.

Minimal fifty-nine.

Shaping fifty-three.

Protocol changed numerically.

Capability did not change nearly that much.

That was healthy.

"Do we call it interruption?"

"No."

"Chapter title problem."

"What?"

"Nothing."

She took notebook back and wrote one line beneath results.

**NEXT CANDIDATE: MOVING-TARGET EXTERNAL TEST. DO NOT GENERALIZE TO STOPPING, DEFLECTION, FORCE, OR PRACTICAL USE.**

Then another:

**NO INDEPENDENT TESTING.**

I said, "You really know me."

"I know literacy is imperfect."

"Cruel."

"Useful."

She closed notebook.

Session over.

That surprised me.

"We're done?"

"Yes."

"Eight attempts."

"Yes."

"I can do more."

"I know."

"That usually isn't reason to stop."

"It often is."

There it was.

Same lesson as work limb.

Same lesson as roads.

Same lesson as money, apparently.

Capacity did not require immediate expenditure.

Terrible universe.

I stood.

Crutches.

Body fine.

Magic fatigue light.

No dizziness.

No headache.

No hand tremor.

No visible residual effect.

Hessa watched the stand anyway.

"Shoulder?"

"Improved."

"Leg?"

"Shop has trial hardware."

"Good."

"You don't know details."

"I don't need details to know you aren't wearing it."

Fair.

"Final work pending."

"Then leave it pending."

Everyone in my life had formed conspiracy against acceleration.

I took notebook.

At door, Hessa said, "Greg."

I turned.

"One hundred thirty-nine."

"Yes."

"Emergency use was not permission."

"I know."

"Do you?"

I thought about hill.

Bad plant.

Motion going wrong.

The fact I would do it again if choice was fall or attempt.

Honest answer.

"If the same emergency happened, I might use it again."

Hessa nodded.

Not approval.

Not anger.

"Then write that as your rule, not mine."

That stopped me.

Institutional boundary.

Ethical boundary.

Evidence boundary.

She was not going to pretend she controlled what I did alone.

And I was not going to pretend my choices were her authorization.

"Fair."

"Good."

I wrote before leaving:

**Outside-session restriction remains. Emergency exception is my judgment/risk, not Hessa clearance. If used, record narrowest possible facts.**

She read over shoulder.

"Better."

"Still hostile?"

"Always."

I left.

Carrow afternoon was ordinary enough to insult magic.

A bread cart blocked half street.

Someone had hung sheets between windows and created a public tunnel.

Two men argued over a wheel.

A child tried to drag a stick longer than he was tall.

No one needed external interruption.

Good.

I bought lunch with bits.

No copper moved.

Fifteen stayed fifteen.

At home, Lyssa had three lengths of brown cloth across table and a customer who was explaining sleeves with both arms.

I stopped inside door.

Customer said, "I want it to fall naturally but still hold shape."

Lyssa said, "Those are different requests."

"I know."

"No, you keep combining them."

"I mean naturally here."

She grabbed his wrist.

"Your arm is not naturally here. You are posing."

He lowered arm.

"Oh."

Lyssa looked at me.

"Magic?"

"Two clean things happened."

Her face became suspicious.

"Out of?"

"Eight."

"That sounds less impressive."

"Thank you."

"What things?"

"Small moving target changed sideways."

"Useful?"

"Hessa says I'm not allowed to ask that yet."

Lyssa nodded.

"I like her."

"Everyone does."

"I didn't say I like you."

Customer looked between us.

"Are you together?"

Lyssa said, "Unfortunately."

I put notebook away.

Not behind road reference.

Not beside Hark & Venn liner.

Different drawer.

Magic could remain its own problem.

I checked the numbers once before dinner.

Fifty-nine.

Fifty-three.

One hundred forty-seven.

That last number was bigger.

The claim was smaller.

Two clean visible changes on a moving wooden shuttle out of eight attempts under controlled conditions.

Rest attempts did nothing visible.

One faster trial unclear.

No stopping.

No force measurement.

No practical use.

No independent testing.

Candidate next protocol existed now.

That was progress.

Annoyingly good progress.

I had spent weeks wanting magic to become useful again.

Maybe useful did not begin with stronger.

Maybe it began with knowing what kind of mistake it was willing to interrupt.

I crossed out **interrupt**.

Too early.

Wrote:

**Motion may matter.**

Hessa would approve.

Which was unbearable.

Cash fifteen.

Vale twenty-three.

Bits unspecified.

Protocol fifty-nine, fifty-three, one hundred forty-seven.

No income.

No expense in copper.

No limb update.

No theatre update.

No artifact.

No debt payment.

Home in Carrow.

One candidate protocol.

Two clean little deviations.

And absolutely no permission to turn either into a spell.

Good.

# CHAPTER 483

## THE VERIFIER

Sori's packet arrived wrapped in oilcloth and tied badly.

That narrowed sender immediately.

Lyssa put it on table beside my breakfast.

"Yours."

"How do you know?"

"It says Greg."

"Could be another Greg."

"It also says `Do not let him lose the second sheet.`"

I looked at handwriting.

Sori.

"Hostile identification."

Lyssa sat across from me with one sleeve half pinned inside out over her wrist.

She had work.

I had fifteen copper.

Twenty-three with Vale.

Bits unspecified.

Ten floor.

Magic fifty-nine, fifty-three, one-forty-seven.

Hark & Venn still had the trial hardware.

No theatre runner.

No Hessa note.

One oilcloth packet.

Good.

I opened it.

First sheet was letter.

Second was folded twice and marked in thick charcoal:

**DO NOT LOSE THIS ONE.**

Third through seventh were copied circulars.

Of course.

Sori's letter started without greeting.

**Merehook customs archive was worse than expected. Filing by issuing office is not a system. It is a private revenge against time.**

I smiled.

Lyssa looked up.

"Bad?"

"Her happiness has become administrative."

"That sounds expensive."

I kept reading.

**I found the transition circulars I wanted. Eleven years, but only six matter unless you have become interested in expired ferry rope law, which you probably have, unfortunately.**

Fair.

**Main thing: retired road, beacon, survey, and customs equipment changes status differently depending on how it leaves service. Some becomes district surplus. Some remains titled equipment even after physical retirement. Some can be sold only after release mark. Some can be transferred between offices without sale. Some is scrapped locally but cannot legally keep official seal plates attached.**

I stopped.

There.

Not artifact mechanism.

Ownership.

Paper.

Sori continued.

**You kept asking why two objects that look equally dead can have different prices. This is one answer. Not only answer.**

I had asked that.

Probably too often.

**A piece with intact service mark but no release record can be more annoying than valuable. A piece with boring release paper can be easier to move than a better piece without it. Collector people still pay stupid money for stories. Working people pay for function. Offices pay for not being accused of buying their own stolen property back.**

I read that sentence twice.

Lyssa said, "That one good?"

"Very."

"Then you are making work face."

"I have several faces."

"You have two."

Cruel.

I unfolded second sheet.

It was a copied table.

Not artifact list.

Status classes.

Old Merehook district language on left.

New language on right.

Examples.

**ACTIVE TITLE / IN SERVICE**

**RETIRED TITLE / HELD**

**TRANSFER RELEASE / OFFICE TO OFFICE**

**SURPLUS RELEASE / PRIVATE SALE PERMITTED**

**SCRAP RELEASE / OFFICIAL MARKS TO BE STRUCK OR REMOVED**

**HAZARD RETAIN / NO PRIVATE TRANSFER**

Different districts used slightly different words.

Sori had added notes in margin.

**Do not assume Carrow uses same labels. Verify local equivalent.**

There.

She knew me.

Third sheet was eleven-year-old circular about retired coast-signal forks.

Fourth about customs scales.

Fifth about survey braces and line hardware.

Sixth about district glass.

Seventh about disputes after old equipment entered private markets with seals still attached.

I ate bread while reading because Sori had not written EAT FIRST but Hessa had damaged me permanently.

The interesting part was not that rules existed.

Of course rules existed.

The interesting part was that market value could split before anyone discussed magic.

Same piece.

Same condition.

Different paper.

Different risk.

Different buyer pool.

I thought about stillhook.

Road office retirement lot.

No current body certificate.

Demonstrated function.

Sold eleven copper.

Had it been surplus released?

Probably.

Auction would be difficult otherwise.

But I had not checked release language because I had been busy wanting object.

Useful failure.

I thought about Three Rays freight.

Marked hardware.

Old road structure rules.

Ownership questions.

Also useful.

Then Road Response Kit from Survey auction.

Retired timing demonstrator.

Five copper.

Did I have release paper?

Receipt, yes.

Specific release class?

I opened pack.

No Road Response Kit here. Stored? Current authority after return maybe at home. Yes.

I found its purchase receipt among travel papers.

Auction line.

Lot number.

Description.

Paid five copper.

No obvious release class written on front.

Back had small stamp.

I had ignored it.

Three short words around a square.

**DISTRICT DISPOSAL CLEARED**

Not Sori's terms.

Local terms.

Maybe equivalent.

Maybe not.

I copied exactly.

Lyssa watched.

"Did you buy illegal thing?"

"Not this one."

She stopped pinning.

I looked at her.

Bad sentence.

"That was poorly phrased."

"Very."

"This one came from public survey auction."

"Good."

She went back to sleeve.

I did not explain Ch473.

Interesting.

Not today.

The packet had not earned confession by proximity.

One lane.

I finished breakfast.

Then went to Carrow district records office.

Not customs.

Carrow was inland enough that customs belonged gates, weights, road permits, freight houses, guilds, and whoever else had fought over jurisdiction for two hundred years.

I needed local equivalent.

District records occupied narrow stone building behind north market.

I had been there before for other paper? Probably, but no need invent exact prior clerk.

Public counter had four windows.

Road records.

Property copies.

Guild filings.

"Other."

I chose other because bureaucracy appreciated honesty.

Clerk was unnamed because I did not plan emotional arc.

I put Sori's copied table on counter.

"Do you have local equivalents for retired district equipment status?"

Clerk looked at table.

Then me.

Then table.

"Where is this from?"

"Merehook customs archive."

"Why?"

"Curiosity."

"No."

"Market curiosity."

"Worse."

Fair.

She read Sori's headings.

"Carrow road district uses different release terms."

"Of course."

"Survey office uses some of these."

"Which?"

She pointed.

"Transfer release roughly same. Surplus release, we call open disposal. Scrap release depends office."

"Can public check status from item mark?"

"Sometimes."

"Good word."

She ignored.

"If item has district serial or disposal docket, yes. If only maker mark, no."

"What if service seal still attached?"

"Depends seal."

"Naturally."

"Active authority seals cannot remain on private sale goods unless voided. Historical maker or inspection marks can."

"How is void shown?"

"Cut, punch, black overstrike, or docket reference depending office and year."

Different grammar by institution.

Excellent.

"Could somebody own something legally and still have bad paper?"

"Obviously."

"Could good paper increase resale value?"

She looked at me like I had asked whether dry grain sold better than wet.

"Yes."

"How much?"

"That is not records question."

Correct.

"Can I get public copy of current disposal categories?"

"Two bits."

I paid.

Bits unspecified stayed unspecified.

No copper movement.

She gave one sheet.

Carrow terms:

**ACTIVE HOLD**

**OFFICE TRANSFER**

**OPEN DISPOSAL**

**MATERIAL SCRAP**

**RESTRICTED RETENTION**

And a note:

**PRIVATE POSSESSION DOES NOT BY ITSELF ESTABLISH RELEASE FROM DISTRICT TITLE.**

Useful sentence.

I asked about auction receipts.

"If district auction receipt lists lot and docket, enough?"

"Usually enough to show that sale."

"Usually."

"If somebody challenges, docket is stronger."

"Can I request docket copy?"

"If public. One bit to inspect, copy extra."

I had Road Response lot number.

Could test.

Not because I feared ownership.

Because I wanted to know process.

I gave number.

Clerk searched ledger.

Slowly.

Not magic.

Three shelves.

Two index books.

One curse under breath.

Excellent system.

She found it.

"Survey office disposal. Open disposal. Lot group twelve through nineteen."

Mine among them.

"Any restrictions?"

She read.

"Demonstration equipment sold as-is. No service certification. Official survey calibration marks voided before sale. Private possession permitted."

There.

Road Response Kit was mine cleanly.

Not more magical.

Not more valuable mechanically.

But cleaner to resell.

I paid one bit for docket copy.

Still no copper.

Clerk stamped copy.

"Anything else?"

"Can public search by category instead of lot?"

"No."

"By district office?"

"Yes, if you know year."

"By disposal docket?"

"Yes."

"By maker?"

"No."

"By serial?"

"Sometimes."

"By current owner?"

"No."

That mattered.

Information existed but badly indexed.

Human friction.

Potential spread.

Not magic.

I thanked her.

Outside, market was loud enough to erase legal philosophy.

Good.

I sat near stone trough and compared Sori's table to Carrow sheet.

Same broad system.

Different labels.

Different void methods.

Different indexing.

That meant someone moving old district hardware between regions could misunderstand status without lying.

It also meant someone who knew paperwork could reduce uncertainty.

Reduce uncertainty had value.

I wrote:

**Artifact / equipment market has legal-provenance layer separate from mechanism and condition.**

Then crossed out artifact.

Too narrow.

**Retired equipment market.**

Better.

Then:

**Need 4 things separated: physical object / function / title-release status / story provenance.**

That was useful.

I thought about Elian.

She had priced stillhook by working plates, hook assembly, ceramic gamble.

She had not mentioned paperwork.

Maybe because auction made title obvious.

Exactly.

Good market hides solved problems.

You notice paper only when paper is bad.

I thought about the half-wheel reader in Lakeward.

Estate lot.

Dead man's nephew.

Nine copper because brass, old glass, teeth, question.

Did seller have release chain?

Unknown.

Could be perfectly legal private estate property.

Could be old district hardware with unclear release.

I had never asked.

Again.

Not error exactly.

Missing layer.

The next question was whether this knowledge could earn money now.

Dangerous.

I could run around markets asking sellers for dockets and become world's least popular teenager.

No.

Need actual claim.

I went to Vale.

Not to pay debt.

Not to ask work.

Antonius's office dealt in paper, obligations, receipts, property, contracts, and people who discovered signatures after consequences.

He would know whether Carrow buyers cared about release proof.

That was adjacent enough.

Vale clerk at front knew me.

No need new name.

"Antonius?"

"In."

"Busy?"

"Always."

"That means no?"

"It means wait."

I waited.

Luxury.

No job board.

No panic about lost bell.

Fifteen copper remained fifteen.

After maybe quarter bell Antonius waved me in.

His desk looked exactly like paper had reproduced overnight.

He looked at my crutches.

Then packet.

"You're carrying evidence. Dangerous."

"Educational."

"Worse."

I sat.

Put Carrow disposal sheet down.

Then Sori's Merehook copy.

"I learned dead government equipment has multiple kinds of dead."

Antonius read headings.

"Yes."

"You could have told me."

"You did not ask."

Fair.

"How much does release proof matter to private buyers?"

"What kind?"

"Retired route hardware. Survey gear. Signal pieces. Working oddities."

"Depends buyer."

Of course.

"Worker buying tool?"

"Wants function, price, safety enough not to die."

"Collector?"

"Maker, age, story, completeness, mark."

"Dealer?"

"Margin."

"Office?"

"Paper."

"Which private buyer cares most about title?"

"The one planning resale."

There.

"Why?"

Antonius leaned back.

"Because uncertainty compounds. A worker may use a questionable tool for ten years and never be challenged. Dealer sells to five people in five districts and each buyer asks where it came from."

"Or one buyer does."

"Yes."

"So clean release expands buyer pool."

"Usually."

"Premium?"

"No fixed premium."

"Because obvious."

"Because market is not a multiplication table."

I liked him less when correct.

He pointed at Sori's copy.

"Who sent this?"

"Sori."

"Archive worker?"

"Not exactly."

"Customs?"

"No."

"What is she?"

"Currently a person angry at filing systems."

"Useful profession."

I explained Merehook archive, briefly.

No group biography.

No road nostalgia.

Antonius read the seventh circular about disputed private sales.

"This is old."

"Eleven-year set."

"Rules may have changed."

"Current Merehook table included."

"Still not Carrow."

"That's why I got Carrow sheet."

He nodded once.

Good.

"Question," I said. "If I find a seller with legitimate object and poor documentation, can I create value by tracing release?"

"Maybe."

"Not buying object."

"Better."

"Charge for provenance packet?"

"Possible."

"Who pays?"

"Seller if packet increases saleability. Buyer if packet reduces risk. Dealer if it saves time. Nobody if object is worth two copper and search costs three."

There.

Scale.

"Meaning only works on enough value."

"Or repeated categories."

I looked at him.

He looked back.

"Repeated categories?"

"One docket can cover a disposal lot. One office transition can explain many serials. One archive search can answer a class of objects."

There.

Leverage.

Not one object.

Information reusable across objects.

That was closer.

I thought of Sori digging through eleven years because she wanted to know how transition systems changed.

She had not done it for me.

But copy could create repeated value.

"Could somebody sell verification service?"

"They already do."

Important.

"Who?"

"Dealers, clerks, auction agents, freight houses, appraisers, lawyers, retired office staff, whoever market trusts."

Good.

No invented empty market waiting for Greg.

"Then advantage?"

"Know something they don't, move faster, charge less, specialize better, or connect markets they do not serve."

Competition.

Better.

"Any Carrow public notice for people seeking release verification?"

"Not generally."

"Why not?"

"Because people usually ask dealer handling sale."

"Meaning service bundled."

"Often."

I wrote.

Antonius watched.

"You trying to become dealer?"

"Not today."

"That answer improved."

"I lost an artifact auction because my deployable capital was eight copper."

"Good."

"Good?"

"You lost because you could not afford risk without crossing your floor. Better than winning badly."

I stared.

"Everyone has Hessa disease."

"I do not know what that means."

"Good."

He continued.

"If capital small, information service is attractive because inventory risk low."

That was exactly my thought.

I disliked him for saying it first.

"But trust matters," he said.

"There."

"Yes."

"Nobody pays stranger teenager to certify district title."

"Correct."

"Even if correct."

"Especially if correct without recognized source."

"Need source."

"Need chain."

"Need somebody else's authority."

"Often."

That was the actual bottleneck.

Not knowledge.

Trust.

Could I sell Sori's archive copies?

No.

Not mine to sell, and copies were not certification.

Could I use them to ask better questions?

Yes.

Could I identify where a dealer was overpricing uncertainty?

Maybe.

Could I connect seller to public docket cheaply?

Maybe.

Could I become provenance verifier instantly?

No.

Good.

Antonius tapped Carrow sheet.

"What do you actually have?"

"One Road Response Kit bought at district survey auction. Receipt. Open disposal docket copy now."

"Planning sale?"

"No."

"Then why verify?"

"Because I wanted to know whether process was real."

"Expensive curiosity?"

"Bits."

"Acceptable."

I put papers away.

He said, "Bring me a real deal next time."

"What counts real?"

"Somebody wants to buy. Somebody wants to sell. There is uncertainty preventing agreement. You can reduce it."

Simple.

"Then I charge?"

"If they agree."

"Revolutionary."

"Greg."

"Yes."

"Do not spend three days proving title on something nobody asked you to move."

There.

That was the chapter's whole useful correction.

Not every information gap was opportunity.

Opportunity required transaction.

Buyer.

Seller.

Friction.

Then reduce friction.

I stood.

"Thank you."

"You owe me twenty-three copper."

"Beautiful ending ruined."

He almost smiled.

Debt remained.

I left without paying any of it.

Correct.

On road home I passed two artifact stalls.

Did not stop.

Growth maybe.

Or hunger.

At home Lyssa had moved from inside-out sleeve to customer coat.

Different work.

I put Sori packet beside my notebook.

She looked at me.

"Did paper make money?"

"No."

"Good."

"Why good?"

"Because if paper made money immediately, you would become unbearable."

"Paper may make money later."

"There he is."

I told her only useful version.

"Sori found that retired official equipment can have different sale status depending how it left service. I checked Carrow version. My Road Response Kit has clean open-disposal docket."

"The thing you already owned."

"Yes."

"So today you proved you own your own thing."

"Legally."

"Powerful."

"Also learned that verification can create sale value."

"Did Antonius say that?"

"Yes."

"Then you learned Antonius knows his job."

Cruel again.

I sat.

My notebook had too many headings.

Magic.

Road.

Money.

Artifacts.

People.

Now paper trying to become artifact.

I wrote one new page anyway.

**PROVENANCE / RELEASE**

Then:

**Do not confuse:**
**1. what object is**
**2. what object does**
**3. who owns it / whether it was released**
**4. what story says**
**5. what market will pay**

Then below:

**Verification is not value by itself. It matters when uncertainty blocks a real transaction.**

That was better.

Then:

**If capital is small, reducing uncertainty can be cheaper than owning inventory. But trust/authority is bottleneck.**

I stared.

Old me had worked around appraisers, quartermasters, salvage teams, expedition clerks, city offices, auction brokers, guild recorders.

I had noticed outcomes.

Rarely labor.

Power had hidden paperwork from me because other people carried it.

Current life made every layer visible.

Not because world changed.

Because my place in it did.

Dangerous thought.

True enough.

I stopped before turning it into speech.

Sori's note had one last line on back I had missed.

**If you are still carrying that weird survey timing thing, check whether the auction voided official calibration before you ever sell it. Merehook had three cases where private buyers mistook old district marks for current certification. Nobody died. Everyone became annoying.**

I looked at docket copy.

Official survey calibration marks voided before sale.

There.

She had been right to flag it.

The Road Response Kit's old marks did not certify current accuracy.

Another clean separation.

Physical function.

Historical mark.

Current certification.

Not same.

I added:

**Road Response Kit: legally open-disposal. Old calibration marks voided. Do not represent as currently survey-certified.**

Good.

No sale.

No profit.

No new artifact.

Just one piece of my existing kit becoming more honestly legible.

I wrote Sori back.

**Carrow uses different terms but same broad problem. My Survey auction lot was OPEN DISPOSAL here, official calibration marks voided before sale. So I own it cleanly and still cannot pretend its old marks certify anything current. Antonius says provenance verification creates value only when actual buyer/seller uncertainty blocks a transaction. Apparently information must have customers too.**

Then:

**You were right about filing by issuing office. Carrow has invented several additional ways to make same mistake.**

I folded note.

Postage bits later.

No copper.

Fifteen copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

Ten floor.

Magic fifty-nine, fifty-three, one-forty-seven.

No work-limb update.

No theatre update.

No magic test.

No debt payment.

No artifact purchase.

And still, something had moved.

Not money.

Not power.

My understanding of what a market actually sold.

Objects, yes.

Function.

Risk.

Paper.

Trust.

Time.

Uncertainty.

Sometimes story.

Sometimes bullshit.

Often both.

I had spent years thinking support meant making stronger people work better.

Maybe markets had support roles too.

Not buying.

Not selling.

Making agreement possible.

That thought was almost grand enough to become annoying.

So I stopped.

For now, I had one clean docket, one rude packet from Sori, and a better question to ask the next time somebody tried to sell me something old.

That was enough.

# CHAPTER 484

## THE INTERMEDIARY

The first useful thing I did that morning was not buy anything.

Progress remained humiliating.

Carrow's north-market equipment board had changed while I was east.

Some notices gone.

Some new.

Most ordinary.

**TWO HANDCART WHEELS / IRON RIM / BUY OR TRADE**

**THREE ROOF LAMPS / ONE NEEDS WICK HOUSING**

**MILL SOUTH / NEED ONE DRY SHAFT COUPLER / CURRENT PATTERN ONLY**

Then:

**NORTH FREIGHT YARD SEEKS 4 YARD SWAY SHOES / OLD DISTRICT PATTERN ACCEPTABLE / OPEN DISPOSAL OR CLEAN PRIVATE CHAIN REQUIRED / CURRENT LOAD RATING NOT REQUIRED / BENCH FUNCTION MUST PASS / OFFER TO 1S 2C FOR FOUR**

I stopped.

Not because artifact.

Because wording.

Open disposal.

Clean private chain.

Current load rating not required.

Bench function must pass.

Buyer knew exactly what problem it was willing to own.

Good buyer.

I copied notice.

Then saw another one three rows lower.

**CROSS STREET SURPLUS / 5 OLD YARD SHOES / DISTRICT MARKS / SELL LOT / 1S 6C / NO SPLIT**

I stared.

Maybe same thing.

Maybe not.

Could be wrong pattern.

Could be stolen.

Could be five dead lumps with district marks and ambition.

Could be exact mismatch.

Buyer wanted four.

Seller had five.

Price gap existed.

Paper gap likely existed.

Function gap existed.

And I had fifteen copper.

Which was not enough to buy seller lot without violating every intelligent thought I had possessed this month.

Good.

No inventory.

I took both notices down only long enough to copy exact wording, then pinned them back.

Ownership of paper remained with society.

Cross Street Surplus was three lanes south.

I went there first.

Two crutches.

No trial hardware.

No magic.

No reason to complicate morning with body experiment.

The surplus stall occupied half an old cooper shed.

Iron hooks.

Cart latches.

Lantern cages.

Old gate rollers.

Three chipped heat stones in a basket labeled **WARM SOMETIMES**.

Honest merchandising.

The yard shoes were under bench.

Five black iron housings, each about two hands long, with thick flat base, paired side jaws, and dull gray ceramic bar inside.

Broad category familiar.

Old freight yards used them under temporary load frames or beside rails to damp lateral sway.

Not magic enough to impress anyone.

Useful enough to prevent expensive things from becoming interesting.

Seller was a woman maybe forty with scar across one eyebrow and absolutely no interest in my life.

Good.

I showed copied notice.

"These yours?"

"Yes."

"All five same lot?"

"Four are. Fifth came with them."

There.

Already worse.

"Which four?"

She pointed.

The fifth had slightly different base casting.

"District source?"

"Old south warehouse clearance."

"Receipt?"

She reached under counter and produced folded sale sheet.

Good.

Not defensive.

I read.

**SOUTH WAREHOUSE DISPOSAL / LOT 31 / FOUR SWAY SHOES / MISCELLANEOUS FASTENERS**

Four.

Not five.

"Fifth isn't on receipt."

"No."

"Where from?"

"My brother bought it with a rail cart years ago."

"Paper?"

"No."

There.

Buyer wanted clean release or private chain.

A brother's rail cart might be clean private chain.

Might not.

"Why sell five together?"

"Because I want them gone."

"Why one silver six?"

"Because four work."

"You tested?"

"Clamped to bench. Pulled frame sideways. Four damp. Fifth barely."

Interesting.

"Which fifth?"

"The one with different base."

"So four receipt-matched ones work?"

"Yes."

"Current rating?"

"No."

"Service marks?"

"Old."

"Voided?"

She looked at me.

"What?"

There.

Not her domain.

I crouched enough to see side plates.

Each of the four matched units carried district oval and three short punch marks crossing lower edge.

Void maybe.

Could be inspection.

Could be damage.

Do not invent.

"Do you know what those punches mean?"

"No."

"Buyer notice says open disposal or clean private chain."

"I have disposal receipt."

"Receipt says four. That's good. But buyer may want docket or void status on district marks."

She folded arms.

"Why?"

"Because if marks look active, their own records people may refuse them."

"They're old iron shoes."

"Yes."

"So?"

"So offices enjoy paper."

She considered.

"What do you want?"

Good.

Real transaction.

"Four copper if I bring a buyer who completes at at least one silver two copper for the four receipt-matched shoes."

Her eyes narrowed.

"Four copper?"

"Only if sale completes."

"You found buyer already."

"I found notice."

"That's not worth four."

"Then you can answer notice yourself."

Silence.

I liked that.

She looked at my copied buyer notice.

"Three."

"Four."

"Three and one bit for copying."

"Four. You keep fifth."

"Why would I keep worst one?"

"Because buyer asked four and your receipt proves four."

She disliked that.

Exactly.

"If buyer takes all five?"

"Then your problem gets worse because fifth has weaker chain."

"Maybe buyer doesn't care."

"Then negotiate it separately. My fee is for four receipt-matched units."

There.

Scope.

She stared long enough that I wondered whether I had become annoying professionally.

Probably.

"Four if they pay one silver three," she said.

"Four if they pay one silver two or more."

"Three if one-two. Four if one-three."

I thought.

Seller incentive.

My incentive.

Could distort me toward pushing buyer one copper higher.

Not automatically wrong.

But buyer should know I am paid by seller.

Important.

"Fine. Three if completed at one silver two. Four if one silver three or more. You pay records bits if we need them."

She nodded.

"Written."

She found scrap paper.

Good seller.

We wrote:

**Greg introduction / transaction assist. Seller pays 3c if four receipt-matched sway shoes sell for at least 1s2c. Seller pays 4c if completed price at least 1s3c. Seller covers agreed public-record copy/inspection bits. No fee if no completed sale. Fifth shoe excluded unless separately agreed.**

She signed.

I signed.

No modern broker license speech.

No ownership transfer.

No promises about function.

I told her one more thing.

"Buyer needs bench function."

"Four work."

"I am not certifying that."

"I didn't ask."

Good.

North Freight Yard was across river-side market.

Buyer notice belonged receiving office.

I arrived before midday.

Showed copied seller sheet, not seller's original receipt.

Receiving clerk read.

"Five?"

"Four are on district disposal receipt. Fifth separate and excluded."

"Price?"

"Seller asks one silver six. She will transact four at one silver two minimum if other terms clear."

That was not exactly seller ask.

But signed fee terms established minimum, not offer.

Careful.

"Correction. Seller has not offered one-two. She agreed my commission only if completed sale reaches one-two or more."

Clerk looked at me.

"Difference?"

"Yes."

"Annoying difference."

"Important difference."

He sighed.

"Buyer is yard quartermaster."

Role, not name.

Good.

Quartermaster came.

Older man with heavy apron and left hand missing two fingertips.

No need explain.

He read notice.

"Four same pattern?"

"Looks same broad pattern. I have not measured."

"South warehouse?"

"Receipt says South Warehouse disposal lot thirty-one."

"Open disposal?"

"Receipt says disposal. I have not verified local status class yet."

"Current cert?"

"None established."

"Function?"

"Seller says four damp under bench pull. Buyer notice says your bench test."

He looked at me properly.

"You seller?"

"No."

"Buyer?"

"No."

"Then what are you?"

There.

Title.

"Intermediary."

That sounded too adult.

He frowned.

"Broker?"

"Recent diagnosis."

Elian's line came back.

Fine.

"Paid by who?"

"Seller. Three copper if sale completes at one silver two. Four if one silver three or more."

His face changed.

Good reason.

"So you want my price higher."

"Yes."

"Why tell me?"

"Because otherwise you should assume worse."

He laughed once.

Not warm.

But fair.

"Good. Then I want one silver one."

"Your posted offer says to one silver two."

"To."

"Not at."

"Correct."

Negotiation.

Excellent.

I had no authority to accept.

"Bench test first?"

"Paper first. I'm not moving yard time for stolen district gear."

There.

Friction.

Actual.

"Receipt enough?"

"Not for our ledger. Need docket or void note."

Exactly Ch483.

I did not smile.

Would have been unbearable.

"Seller agreed to pay public-record bits."

"Good."

"Can your office check?"

"We can, but not today."

"Why?"

"Clerk who knows old south warehouse index is off."

Human system.

"Tomorrow?"

"Maybe."

Maybe was expensive.

"District records can search lot."

"Then do that."

"Fee?"

"Seller pays records bits. My time is on my commission."

He nodded.

"Bring docket copy. If open disposal and official marks voided or sale-cleared, we bench four."

"Price still open."

"Obviously."

Good.

I went back seller.

Explained.

She swore at records.

Longer than necessary.

Not Greg-style.

Actual speech.

"Every time district sells old yard iron they stamp a receipt, punch three holes somewhere no one explains, put the real answer in a book across town, then ten years later everybody acts surprised that nobody remembers which hole means what. I bought the whole cart because the warehouse foreman said cleared. He said cleared. He was standing under a sign that said clearance. What else was I supposed to ask, whether the word clearance had been cleared?"

I waited.

She finished.

"Records bits," I said.

"Fine."

Good.

I did not make records office a chapter again.

I already knew route.

I already knew question.

Lot thirty-one.

South Warehouse.

Disposal docket.

The clerk took one bit to inspect and another to copy.

Seller reimbursed both bits.

Docket:

**SOUTH WAREHOUSE / OPEN DISPOSAL / LOT 31**
**FOUR SWAY SHOES**
**SERVICE AUTHORITY VOIDED BY THREE-PUNCH LOWER-SEAL STRIKE**
**SOLD AS-IS / NO CURRENT LOAD CERTIFICATION**
**PRIVATE TRANSFER PERMITTED**

There.

Three punches.

Not active inspection.

Void strike.

Exactly what buyer needed.

No mechanism claim.

No function claim.

No fifth shoe.

I carried copy to North Freight Yard.

Quartermaster read.

"Good."

One word.

Worth two bits.

"Bench?"

"Bring seller tomorrow second bell."

"Transport?"

"Her problem."

Correct.

I returned seller.

She had a handcart.

Also correct.

Next morning we rolled four shoes to North Freight Yard.

I did not help pull cart.

Two crutches.

Seller had one stall hand pull.

I carried paper in shoulder bag.

Hand economy still irritating.

At yard test bench, buyer's mechanic took over.

Not me.

He checked housings.

Measured jaw openings.

Looked at ceramics.

Tapped one lightly with small wood handle.

No mystical scan.

First shoe clamped under short side frame.

Mechanic hung weighted bar from frame and pulled sideways with rope through floor ring.

Released.

Frame moved.

Shoe resisted and settled motion faster than bare frame baseline.

Useful.

No precise rating.

Second similar.

Third squealed.

Mechanic stopped.

Opened housing cover.

Dirt and old grease.

"Function?"

Seller said, "It worked at my bench."

Mechanic said, "It may. Not like this."

He asked seller permission to wipe old grit.

She said yes.

He cleaned track with rag.

Reclamped.

Third damped, but slower.

Mechanic marked **WEAK / FUNCTIONAL**.

Fourth did nothing first pull.

Seller's face hardened.

"Again."

Mechanic did.

Nothing.

He checked jaw seating.

One jaw had not fully closed due bent latch.

"Latch."

"Repairable?" quartermaster asked.

Mechanic said, "Probably. Not testing repair today."

Good.

Fourth failed current bench test.

Now deal friction changed.

Buyer wanted four.

Seller had three pass, one failed, one excluded undocumented weak unit back at stall.

No sale at posted condition.

This was where intermediary could become liar.

I did not.

Quartermaster said, "Three at nine copper."

Seller said, "No."

I stayed quiet.

"Nine each?" she asked.

"Nine total."

"Get bent."

Good.

He pointed at failed fourth.

"I'm buying three useful shoes and one future repair if I take four."

"You're not taking four at nine."

"Then no deal."

Could end.

My fee zero.

Correct.

I thought.

Not about squeezing.

About transaction.

Buyer needs four.

Seller has fifth weak but working, undocumented.

Fourth failed due latch likely repairable but not proven.

Could seller repair and return later?

Could buyer buy three now, fourth conditional after repair?

Buyer notice wants four.

Maybe quantity timing flexible.

Ask.

"Do you need four today?"

Quartermaster looked at me.

"Need four before next loading frame goes live sixth day."

There.

Time.

"Would you buy three now and fourth after seller repairs latch, same lot chain?"

"Maybe."

Seller said, "I'm not splitting lot."

I looked at her.

"You already brought four."

"Price was for four."

"Yes."

"This isn't price."

Correct.

"What price for three now plus repaired fourth later?"

She thought.

Buyer impatient.

Good.

She said, "One silver four for all four, fourth delivered functioning before sixth day."

Quartermaster laughed.

"No."

"Your notice says to one-two."

"For four passing now."

"Then one-two with fourth later."

He considered.

Mechanic said, "Latch is probably simple."

"Probably isn't pass."

"Correct."

Quartermaster looked at seller.

"One silver one. Three now. Fourth after pass. No fourth by sixth day, three copper withheld."

Seller snapped, "Withheld from what? You already cut price."

Good question.

They argued.

Not elegantly.

Seller talked too much.

Quartermaster repeated same three sentences.

I listened.

Actual gap became structure, not price.

Seller wanted payment enough now to justify repair.

Buyer wanted leverage to ensure fourth arrived.

"Separate the fourth," I said.

They both looked at me.

"Three shoes sale now. Fourth is conditional purchase after pass. Stop pretending one agreement has to carry both."

Seller frowned.

"Then three price?"

"Your decision."

Quartermaster said, "One silver for three. Two copper for fourth after pass."

Seller looked.

"One silver one for three. One copper fourth."

"No."

"One silver for three. Two for fourth. You collect fourth only when it passes here."

Seller thought.

That recreated buyer's posted one silver two total.

But delayed fourth risk on seller.

My commission agreement: completed sale reaches one silver two or more.

Partial three sale did not complete my condition.

Good.

I said, "My fee only if fourth completes and total reaches one silver two."

Seller glared.

"I'm aware."

Good.

She accepted.

Written.

North Freight Yard bought three now for one silver.

Fourth remained seller's.

Conditional: buyer would buy fourth for two copper if it passed same bench before sixth-day frame deadline.

No obligation to buy failed fourth.

No seller penalty beyond no second payment.

Clean.

Seller took failed shoe back.

No fee to me yet.

Important.

I had done work and earned zero so far.

That felt correct.

Two days later seller sent runner to home.

Not because protagonist.

Because she owed potential commission and knew address from written sheet.

"Passes tomorrow," note said.

No detail.

Next morning I went North Freight because if fourth passed, transaction completed and my fee triggered.

Seller arrived with fourth.

Latch had new pin and old bent tongue straightened.

Who repaired it did not matter.

Mechanic bench tested.

First pull.

Damped.

Second.

Damped.

He marked **PASS / CURRENT BENCH ONLY / NO LOAD CERT**.

Quartermaster paid two copper.

Total deal: one silver + two copper.

Seller paid me three copper under agreement.

Fifteen to eighteen.

There.

Three copper.

Again.

But not a three-copper day job.

I had not sold day.

I had connected existing demand and supply, narrowed title uncertainty, protected buyer's test authority, helped restructure a failed four-unit sale into staged transaction, and waited to get paid until actual friction cleared.

Still three copper.

Different engine.

Seller counted my coins.

"Three."

"Yes."

"You should have taken two."

"No."

She snorted.

Quartermaster said, "You cost less than yard clerk losing half day."

That was compliment shaped like accounting.

Better.

"Need anything else?" I asked.

"No."

Good.

No automatic deal pipeline.

Seller said, "I have fifth shoe."

"No."

"Didn't even ask price."

"No paper."

"Brother's rail cart."

"Find chain first."

"It works."

"Buyer needed clean chain."

"You've become boring."

"Professional growth."

She left.

No fifth-shoe side quest.

I stood by yard wall with eighteen copper.

Ten floor.

Eight deployable.

Exactly the same safe artifact ceiling I had carried into stillhook auction at eighteen.

Interesting.

But context had changed.

Then, eighteen had been whole purse after travel work and pending fee.

Now I had reached eighteen again by closing a transaction whose total was silver-scale without owning the goods.

Not rich.

Not even thirty-copper reserve.

Still nowhere near paying Vale responsibly.

Still not buying artifacts.

But direction.

I wrote notebook:

**North Freight / sway-shoe transaction**
**Seller: 4 receipt-matched units + 1 excluded weak/unclear-chain unit**
**Buyer: needed 4 old-pattern shoes; clean release; current load cert not required; bench function required**
**Friction 1: district marks / release**
**Resolved: open-disposal docket + three-punch void strike**
**Friction 2: 1 of 4 failed bench due latch**
**Resolved commercially, not technically: 3 sold now, 4th conditional after repair/pass**
**Final completed price: 1s2c**
**My fee: 3c**
**Cash 15 -> 18**
**I owned none of it.**

Then:

**Do not confuse facilitating with certifying.**
**Records office owned title record. Buyer mechanic owned bench function. Seller owned repair. Buyer/seller owned price. I owned connection + scope + structure.**

That line mattered.

Support.

Actual support.

People doing their own jobs better because boundaries aligned.

Not mystical.

Not heroic.

Profitable enough to notice.

I went home.

Lyssa was eating standing over table because apparently sitting had become optional in our household.

She saw coins.

"Paper made money."

"Eventually."

"How much?"

"Three copper."

"How much stuff?"

"One silver two copper transaction."

She stopped chewing.

There.

Scale.

"Yours?"

"No."

"Good."

"Why does everybody say that?"

"Because you like objects."

Fair.

I explained shortest version.

Four yard shoes.

Paper clear.

One failed.

Split transaction.

Repair.

Pass.

Seller paid three.

Lyssa nodded.

Then said, "So you got paid because two stubborn people needed a third stubborn person."

I thought.

"Yes."

"Useful profession."

Sori and Antonius both would hate that summary because it was better.

I counted money again.

Eighteen copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

Bits unspecified.

Magic fifty-nine, fifty-three, one-forty-seven.

No limb update.

No theatre update.

No Hessa.

No debt payment.

No artifact purchase.

Three copper earned.

Silver-scale value moved.

More importantly, I had learned what part was mine.

Not the iron.

Not the ledger.

Not the repair.

Not the test.

The gap.

Sometimes a gap was just empty.

Sometimes it was where money got stuck.

That was worth learning.

I closed notebook.

For once, being in the middle had paid.

# CHAPTER 485

## THE ORGANIZER

The South Road notice was still there.

That annoyed me more than if it had disappeared.

**PRIVATE HOUSE REQUIRES THREE SEASONAL FIELD CREWS / SOUTH ROAD / ORGANIZER FEE AVAILABLE / REFERENCES REQUIRED**

Same paper.

Different pin.

Somebody had moved it from the lower corner of the private board to eye level.

No new date.

No urgency strip.

No promise that the work still existed.

Just enough persistence to make pretending I had not copied it weeks ago embarrassing.

I had eighteen copper.

Twenty-three with Vale.

Bits unspecified.

Ten floor.

Eight above it.

Magic fifty-nine, fifty-three, one-forty-seven.

Hark & Venn still had the trial hardware.

No Hessa note.

No theatre runner.

No buyer waiting for me to solve a paper problem.

Good.

I took the notice down long enough to read the tiny line at bottom I had ignored the first time.

**INQUIRE SOUTH ROAD FIELD OFFICE / LOWER GATE / THIRD BELL TO FIFTH**

There.

Actual place.

I copied that too.

Then pinned notice back.

I did not apply immediately.

That felt responsible.

Also suspicious.

I went to theatre first.

Not for work.

Rinna was at side table arguing with a supplier receipt.

The supplier receipt was losing.

She looked up when I stopped outside the work lane.

"You're not on call."

"Correct."

"Then why are you standing there?"

"Reference."

Her face became less welcoming.

Fair.

"For what?"

I showed copy of South Road notice.

She read it.

"Field crews."

"Yes."

"You know field work?"

"Not enough to supervise field work."

"Good start."

"Notice says organizer. I don't know what that means yet."

"Then why do you need me?"

"Because if they ask whether I can keep people, timing, objects, and changing instructions from becoming six separate disasters, you have evidence."

Rinna stared at me.

Then at notice.

"You want me to say you can run a field crew?"

"No."

"Good."

"I want you to say exactly what you can support."

That interested her.

She leaned back.

"Prompt-side work?"

"Yes. Theatre calls generally too, if you think true."

"You keep notes."

"I have been accused."

"You follow scope when scope is clear."

I waited.

"Usually."

Cruel.

"You don't move other people's authority around just because you're bored."

"That sounds flattering."

"It isn't."

"Fine."

"You notice when two people are working from different versions."

"Yes."

"And you ask before touching things now."

Now.

Important.

"Would you sign that?"

"Not those words."

Of course.

She found scrap paper.

Wrote:

**Gregory has worked bounded theatre support under changing rehearsal conditions. He keeps current instructions, asks when authority is unclear, and can coordinate cues, entrances, prop-state handoffs, and light work within assigned scope. This is not a reference for fieldcraft, hiring, safety inspection, or crew supervision outside theatre.**

Then signed Rinna.

Perfect.

"Thank you."

"If they hire you to harvest something and you kill it, that is not my fault."

"Strong confidence."

"Go away."

I went to Vale.

Antonius saw the notice and sighed before I asked.

"No."

"You don't know question."

"Reference."

"How?"

"You brought paper."

Fair.

I put Rinna's note beside notice.

He read it.

"Good reference."

"I know."

"What do you want from me?"

"Narrower thing. Written agreements. Scope. Money. Whether I do what I said after I sign."

"You owe me twenty-three copper."

"Excellent evidence of ongoing relationship."

"That is one interpretation."

He did not reach for paper.
