"Why should I reference you?"

Good question.

"Because you can say no."

He almost smiled.

"Better answer."

Then he did reach for paper.

**Gregory keeps unusually detailed records for his age, reads terms before agreeing more often than he used to, and distinguishes observed fact from preferred explanation when forced. I can confirm he has maintained an active account with this office and has honored agreed reporting/credit terms to date. I do not certify field competence, hiring judgment, solvency, or future performance.**

Signed Antonius Vale.

"Solvency."

"You asked for honest."

"I did."

"Try not to regret it."

I folded both references.

South Road field office sat just beyond Lower Gate where city stone gave way to packed road and drainage ditches.

Not countryside.

Not yet.

Carrow still pressed behind me.

Warehouses thinning.

More carts.

More mud.

The office was a long single-story building with three open windows and a porch wide enough for workers to stand without blocking road.

Good design.

I arrived on two crutches halfway between third and fourth bell.

One woman sat behind front table.

No need name.

She wore a brown vest, gray shirt, and expression of someone who had already answered the same question thirteen times.

I showed notice.

"Still open?"

"Maybe."

Strong start.

"What decides?"

"Whether you're an organizer or another person who thinks organizer means shouting at laborers."

Better.

"What does it mean here?"

She looked at my crutches.

Then references.

Then me.

"Sit."

I did.

"Private house has three seasonal crews already contracted. Not yours. You do not hire them. You do not set their wages. You do not fire them. Each crew has its own lead."

Good.

"Then what am I organizing?"

"Four days. Three field sections. Two supply carts. Water. Meal issue. Start order. Crew arrival. Tool issue records. Field handoff. If one section isn't ready, you move sequence without making three crews stand around blaming each other."

There.

That was familiar.

Not fields.

State.

"Who owns work method?"

"Crew leads."

"Safety call?"

"Crew lead for ordinary work. House field steward can stop any section."

"Tool condition?"

"House tool keeper."

"Transport?"

"House carts and one contracted wagon. Organizer tracks, does not drive unless separately agreed."

"Worker disputes?"

"Crew lead unless schedule or house provision caused it."

"Weather?"

"Field steward."

"Pay disputes?"

"Office."

Excellent.

Clear authority.

"How many workers?"

"Depends day. Around twenty across all three crews."

Not my twenty.

Important.

"What are fields doing?"

"Seasonal drainage and late-cut cleanup before wet turn. Ditch clearing. Cut brush. Pull old stakes. Reset access paths. Some bundling. No crop judgment from organizer."

Better.

"Fee?"

She slid a sheet across table.

**ORGANIZER TERM / FOUR DAYS**

**4C ON ACCEPTED START PLAN + CONFIRMED CREW/TRANSPORT CONTACTS**

**10C ON COMPLETION OF FOUR-DAY HANDOFF IF ALL DAILY ORGANIZER RECORDS ACCEPTED**

**NO COMPLETION FEE IF ORGANIZER ABANDONS TERM WITHOUT HOUSE RELEASE**

**HOUSE COVERS AUTHORIZED MESSAGE / RECORD / LOCAL CART COSTS**

Fourteen copper total.

I read again.

Not silver.

Still more than a seven-copper day.

More importantly, I was not being paid to clear a ditch.

I was being paid so twenty people and several moving parts did not waste four days.

"What counts accepted record?"

"Start roster. Actual arrival. Section assignment. Tool issue discrepancies. Cart delay. Work handoff. Anything house steward marks relevant. Not a diary."

"What if crew lead changes sequence for field reason?"

"You record it and adjust shared schedule."

"What if I disagree?"

"If it's field method, you don't matter."

I liked her.

"References required."

I gave both.

She read Rinna first.

Longer than I expected.

Then Antonius.

She looked at me.

"You owe Vale?"

"Yes."

"How much?"

"Twenty-three copper."

"Does this job require you to front house expenses?"

"Sheet says no."

"Good."

No shame.

Just risk.

"Why field crews?"

"Because notice existed."

"Bad answer."

"Because I spent a long time thinking useful work meant personally doing task. Recently I have been more useful connecting people and keeping separate expertise aligned. I wanted to know if that transfers to more people."

She watched me.

"That's a speech."

"Unfortunately."

"Shorter."

I thought.

"I think I can keep three competent crews from waiting on the same cart."

There.

She nodded once.

"Better."

Interview continued.

Not dramatic.

She gave scenarios.

Two crews arrive, third late.

Water cart missing.

Field steward closes lower section.

One crew lead wants to borrow tools issued to another crew.

Contract wagon leaves early because driver claims route changed.

I did not solve everything brilliantly.

On tool borrowing I said organizer could reassign.

Wrong.

"Tool keeper owns issue."

Right.

On closed field I initially moved crew immediately.

She asked whether alternate section was ready.

I had not checked.

Good.

On late crew I said start others if their work independent.

Correct enough.

On missing water cart I asked whether work could begin safely without it.

"Field steward decides."

Right.

On wagon I asked who contracted it and what written route term said.

That got a tiny nod.

At end she said, "You are not first choice."

Good.

"Who is?"

"Woman who did spring turn. She is north with family."

"Second?"

"Man from Miller Lane. He wants hiring authority we are not giving him."

Interesting.

"So?"

"You can have provisional term if references verify today. Start plan due by first bell tomorrow. If plan accepted, four copper. If first morning goes badly enough, house can release you and you keep only accepted-plan payment."

"Fair."

"Don't say fair until you read whole term."

Antonius disease.

I read whole term.

Four pages.

Not exciting.

Useful.

No hidden worker wage liability.

No requirement I guarantee attendance.

No authority to discipline crews.

House could release for material organizer failure.

I could withdraw before start plan acceptance with no fee and no penalty.

After start, abandoning without release forfeited completion fee.

No invented fine.

I signed.

She signed for field office.

"Now references."

She sent a runner.

House expense.

I waited.

Luxury again.

Rinna's answer returned first.

Confirmed.

Antonius's second.

Confirmed.

The woman at table handed me a packet.

Crew contact sheets.

Three leads, names on paper.

I did not need their names in my head yet.

Better categories first.

North crew.

Middle crew.

Lower crew.

Each had meet point, expected headcount range, tool needs already agreed, and one note from prior organizer.

North crew liked to start early and hated waiting for carts.

Middle crew had one older worker who carried their own ditch gauge.

Lower crew had two workers joining only second day.

Not problems.

Facts.

Transport sheet:

Cart A water and meal.

Cart B tools and spoil baskets.

Contract wagon for cut brush from lower turnout afternoon only.

Field sections:

North ditch.

Middle access line.

Lower drainage cut.

I asked for map.

She gave one.

I asked for current readiness.

"Field steward checks at dusk."

"Can I see results?"

"Posted here after sixth bell."

Good.

I spent rest of afternoon meeting crew leads.

Not recruiting.

Not motivational speech.

Just contact.

North crew lead was a compact woman with mud dried to one boot and no patience for introductions.

"You organizer?"

"Provisional."

"Can you make carts arrive?"

"No."

She stared.

"Can you tell me before they don't?"

"Sometimes."

"Better."

Good relationship start.

Middle crew lead talked too much.

A lot too much.

He explained, without apparent need for air, that his people could clear ditch, path, old stake, light brush, medium brush, some heavy brush if the roots were not stonebound, but they were absolutely not hauling wet spoil uphill again because last season somebody had called that "temporary repositioning" and temporary had lasted three hours and two backs and one argument with a mule.

I let him finish.

"Wet spoil uphill is not in your sheet."

"Good."

"If it appears?"

"We discuss before anybody decides language changes weight."

Excellent.

Lower crew lead was formal.

"Our contracted headcount on first day is six. Second day may be eight. I will confirm at evening bell. We require the two long-handled ditch hooks listed. If unavailable, I prefer reassignment before substitution."

"Because?"

"Short hooks change body position."

"Understood. Tool keeper owns issue. I'll flag availability, not invent replacement."

He nodded.

Different speech.

Different people.

By sixth bell I returned field office.

Dusk readiness sheet posted.

North ditch: **READY**.

Middle access line: **READY**.

Lower drainage cut: **HOLD / WATER STILL HIGH / RECHECK FIRST LIGHT**.

There.

Plan changed before plan existed.

Original packet assumed north, middle, lower start together.

Could assign lower crew elsewhere temporarily.

But alternate work needed readiness.

I asked field steward.

Unnamed man with coat tied around waist and mud to knees.

"If lower still held at first light, what work can that crew legally start?"

"North spoil bundling after north crew clears first hundred pace. Or old-stake pull on middle south edge."

"Which creates least interference?"

"Depends pace."

"Can lower lead choose at start based on actual progress?"

"Yes, if I approve section."

Good.

So plan did not force one.

It created decision point.

I wrote:

**FIRST BELL**

North crew -> North ditch.

Middle crew -> Middle access line.

Lower crew -> report Lower Gate, hold assignment until field-steward first-light status.

If lower cut opens -> Lower drainage.

If still closed -> steward selects North bundling or Middle old-stake edge based on readiness; lower crew lead owns work method.

Cart A -> water/meal first North turnout, then Middle, then Lower Gate hold point unless steward redirects.

Cart B -> tool issue at Lower Gate before crews split; long ditch hooks reserved to Lower crew only if lower drainage opens, otherwise remain tool-keeper custody.

Contract wagon -> lower turnout afternoon only, confirm after first-light lower status.

Then communication.

One runner token to North turnout.

One to Middle.

Field office runner held for wagon notice if lower status changes.

I did not need people sprinting because I had failed to decide where information moved.

That felt old.

Not old life exactly.

Old reflex.

I took plan home.

Lyssa was eating over work table.

Bad sign.

"You got job?"

"Provisional organizer term."

She looked up.

"Organizer."

"Apparently."

"People?"

"Around twenty."

She stopped chewing.

I enjoyed that too much.

"You are in charge of twenty people?"

"No."

"Good."

"Three crew leads are in charge of their crews. Field steward owns field decisions. Tool keeper owns tools. Office owns pay. I own schedule and shared failure."

"Shared failure."

"Strong title."

"How much?"

"Four copper if they accept start plan. Ten more after four-day handoff if records accepted."

She did math with face.

"Fourteen."

"Yes."

"You have eighteen."

"Yes."

"So if you don't ruin crops you get thirty-two."

I looked at her.

"There are no crops in my authority."

"You know what I mean."

I did.

Thirty-two.

Above the thirty-copper reserve target I had been circling for months.

If.

Not mine yet.

Important.

"Four first," I said.

"Good."

She went back to eating.

"Also drainage."

"Less romantic."

"Much."

I slept badly because apparently management had discovered anticipation.

First bell came cold and gray.

I reached Lower Gate early.

Two crutches.

Shoulder bag.

Plan.

No trial leg.

No magic.

I was not walking fields if I could avoid it.

Organizer did not mean personally inspecting every ditch.

Field steward arrived with first-light status.

Lower drainage cut: **HOLD**.

Water had dropped, not enough.

Good.

Plan branch B.

Then Cart B did not arrive.

Of course.

North crew arrived.

Middle crew arrived.

Lower crew arrived with six.

No tools.

One cart missing.

Cart A water and meal was there.

I checked time.

Then asked field office.

Cart B axle pin broken three streets north.

Repair underway.

Estimated half bell.

Maybe.

North crew lead said, "We have our hand tools. We need baskets later."

Middle lead began a long explanation about how his crew technically had enough hooks to start but not enough spare handles if one cracked and one always cracked when somebody said there were enough handles.

Lower lead said, "Without long hooks, lower cut is irrelevant. For alternate old-stake work we have personal pry bars."

There.

Capability distributed.

I asked field steward.

"Can lower crew start middle south-edge stake pull now?"

He looked at Middle lead.

"If your crew stays north half until second bell."

Middle lead inhaled for speech.

I raised hand.

"Can you?"

He stopped.

"Yes."

"Then lower takes south-edge stakes until Cart B arrives or lower drainage opens."

Lower lead nodded.

North crew lead said, "We're starting."

"Yes."

They left.

No applause.

Good.

I sent one runner to Cart B repair point for update.

House token.

Then Cart A driver said his route sheet listed Lower first.

My plan said North first.

Old version.

There it was.

Different versions.

Theatre again.

I showed signed start plan.

Driver showed route sheet from yesterday.

Neither of us wrong.

Office had failed update.

I did not yell.

Growth.

"Can you run North first without breaking your cart agreement?"

"Agreement says field office route."

"Then office can correct."

We walked five yards to table.

Office woman wrote amended route.

Driver signed receipt.

North first.

Problem gone.

Not heroic.

Useful.

By second bell, North ditch was moving.

Middle access line moving.

Lower crew pulling old stakes and complaining that whoever drove them last year had hated future generations.

Cart B arrived after repair.

Tool keeper checked pin before releasing cart onward.

Not me.

Good.

Field steward rechecked lower water.

Still hold.

So long hooks stayed with tool keeper.

No reason to issue them just because packet said Lower crew.

By third bell, the lower crew had cleared enough old stakes that Middle lead wanted south edge early.

Conflict.

Not fight.

Timing.

I asked both leads what they needed.

Middle lead gave six sentences.

Lower lead gave two.

Result: lower could finish current thirty paces, then move to North spoil bundling. Middle could expand south afterward.

Field steward approved.

Done.

At midday I realized I had not eaten.

Bad management.

Of self.

I took meal from house issue like everyone else entitled by contract.

Sat on overturned basket near gate.

Hands tired from crutches and paper.

Right leg fine.

Shoulder ordinary.

No magic fatigue because I had not done anything stupid.

North crew lead passed with water cup.

"Cart came."

"I noticed."

"You told us before it came."

"That was goal."

"Good."

Then she left.

That was probably more satisfying than praise should have been.

By fourth bell, field office woman called me over.

She had my start plan.

Marked in three places.

"Accepted."

Four copper on table.

There.

Not completion.

Not fourteen.

Four.

I counted once.

Eighteen to twenty-two.

She said, "Your actual-arrival record is missing Cart A route correction time."

I looked.

She was right.

I had written amendment, not time.

"Fix."

I did.

"Also lower crew alternate assignment changed at second bell. Record who approved."

"Field steward."

"Write it."

I did.

"You're doing fine."

Dangerous sentence.

I waited.

"That does not mean relax."

There.

Safer.

"Three more days."

"Yes."

"Completion fee is not earned."

"I know."

"Good."

I put four copper away.

Twenty-two.

Ten floor.

Twelve above it.

Closer.

Not there.

At fifth bell the lower drainage finally opened.

Too late to move crew there productively that day.

Field steward said tomorrow first bell.

Lower lead agreed.

Middle lead had opinions about whether lower water would rise overnight based on cloud shape, ditch smell, one frog, and his grandmother.

Field steward listened politely.

Then said, "We check at first light."

Hessa would have approved.

I did not tell anyone.

At close I wrote actual day state.

North: ditch clear through marked section, spoil bundling partly done.

Middle: access line clear through north half, south-edge stakes partly cleared by Lower crew before handoff.

Lower: drainage closed until fifth bell, no drainage work performed, alternate stake work completed in bounded section.

Cart A: old route sheet corrected at first bell plus.

Cart B: delayed by axle pin repair, arrived before second bell plus.

No worker injuries reported to organizer.

No claim that none existed outside reports.

Tool keeper retained long hooks.

Contract wagon not called because lower brush haul did not start.

Tomorrow: lower drainage only if steward opens at first light.

There.

Not a victory.

A current state.

I knew this feeling.

Old life before a fight when everyone had different capabilities, different timing, and enough ego to make geometry political.

But this was not battle.

Nobody was dying.

Nobody needed buff timing.

Nobody needed me to make them stronger.

They needed water in right place.

Tools not issued to wrong crew.

A cart route updated before driver left.

A field held because water was high.

Three leads allowed to remain better at their jobs than me.

Support without power.

I almost wrote that.

Did not.

Too neat.

Instead I wrote:

**Organizer day 1: plan is not instruction to reality. Plan is shared version until reality changes. Keep authority attached to person who can actually decide.**

Still a little neat.

Fine.

At home Lyssa was not there yet.

Her work had its own schedule.

Good.

I put four new copper beside the rest.

Twenty-two.

Not thirty-two.

Not yet.

Ten completion copper sat in someone else's future obligation, contingent on three more days and acceptable records.

I did not count it.

Growth.

Then I looked at Antonius's reference again.

**reads terms before agreeing more often than he used to**

Rude.

Accurate.

Tomorrow lower field might flood again.

A crew might come short.

Cart B might break something more expensive than a pin.

Middle lead might explain weather to death.

None of that belonged to me yet.

What belonged to me was current state, current scope, and next bell.

For once, that was enough management.

# CHAPTER 486

## THE COORDINATOR

Day two was mostly boring.

That was excellent.

I had spent enough of my life around people who believed competent coordination should look dramatic.

Usually dramatic coordination meant somebody had failed earlier.

South Road did not need me dramatically.

It needed three crews, two carts, one contracted wagon, a field steward, a tool keeper, an office, and several pieces of paper to keep agreeing about what was happening.

That was harder than it sounded.

It was also less interesting to watch.

North crew started early.

Middle crew started almost early.

Lower drainage opened after first light and stayed open.

Cart A followed the corrected route.

Cart B arrived with both long ditch hooks.

The contract wagon appeared when promised.

Nobody cheered.

I loved everyone.

At second bell the North lead told me their spoil-basket count was wrong by three.

I checked my sheet.

My sheet said twelve.

Her crew had nine.

“Tool keeper?”

“Already told.”

“Then why tell me?”

“So you stop writing twelve.”

Fair.

I changed twelve to nine and added the time.

That was coordination.

Not fixing baskets.

Not finding baskets.

Not asking why baskets had betrayed us.

Current state.

At third bell Middle lead found me near the access line and spent enough words to irrigate a field.

“The south edge is technically open, and when I say technically I mean someone has cut the top brush and declared victory while leaving three root crowns, one buried stone, and a section of old ditch wall exactly where every reasonable person will put a foot. So yes, I can send people there. No, I will not send people there while somebody calls it clear, because if I hear the phrase mostly clear one more time I am going to start applying mostly to other nouns. Mostly fed. Mostly paid. Mostly alive.”

I waited.

He breathed.

“Field steward?”

“Coming.”

“Good.”

Field steward came.

Looked.

Said south edge was not ready.

Middle crew stayed where it was.

I changed the sheet.

Nothing else happened.

Beautiful.

At lunch I ate sitting on an overturned basket because chairs were a city conspiracy.

The North lead passed, looked at my notebook, and said, “Still alive?”

“Against projections.”

“Whose?”

“Mine.”

She kept walking.

Day two ended with no missing workers, no wrong cart, no section hold after start, and only one meal count correction because Lower crew actually had eight that day instead of six.

I did not solve that either.

Lower lead had confirmed eight at evening bell as promised.

I had failed to update meal count before Cart A loaded.

That was mine.

Not disaster.

Still mine.

The field office sent two additional meal portions on the next local cart.

Authorized house cost.

I wrote:

**DAY TWO ERROR: headcount confirmation updated roster, not provisioning count. Same fact existed in two places. I changed one.**

That annoyed me.

Useful.

The office woman read the line at end of day.

“You like writing your own mistakes.”

“No.”

“You do it often.”

“That is different.”

She marked the daily record accepted.

No money.

Correct.

Completion fee lived in future.

Twenty-two copper remained twenty-two.

Third day started clean.

Which should have frightened me.

North had ten.

Middle seven.

Lower eight.

Twenty-five total that morning.

More than first day.

Still not mine.

Crew leads had people.

House had contract.

I had shared state.

Cart A water and meal.

Cart B tools and baskets.

Contract wagon lower turnout after third bell to haul cut brush.

Lower drainage remained open.

Middle south edge finally cleared.

North ditch had moved far enough that their spoil bundling no longer interfered with Lower alternate work.

Everything fit.

Then field office changed priority.

A runner found me just before second bell.

Folded note.

Stamped by field office.

**HOUSE PRIORITY CHANGE**

**CONTRACT WAGON AFTER THIRD BELL: NORTH TURNOUT FIRST. CLEAR NORTH BRUSH STACK BEFORE EVENING HOUSE CART PASSAGE. LOWER TURNOUT AFTER NORTH IF TIME.**

There.

Real instruction.

Not rumor.

Not driver memory.

Not somebody shouting over a ditch.

Field office stamp.

I read twice.

North turnout first.

Lower after if time.

This was inconvenient because Lower crew had been cutting and bundling based on the wagon arriving at their turnout after third bell.

I looked at current board.

Contract wagon line.

Lower turnout.

Crossed it out.

Wrote North first.

Sent runner token to North.

Sent one to contract wagon meet point.

Updated field office copy when I passed.

Good.

Very efficient.

Very wrong.

I did not tell Lower.

Not immediately.

That was the mistake.

It took almost half a bell to become visible.

I was at Middle checking whether their south-edge handoff had been recorded when Lower lead appeared.

He did not hurry.

That made it worse.

“Our wagon has been reassigned.”

“Yes.”

“To North.”

“Yes.”

“Since before second bell.”

I looked at him.

There it was.

“You were not told.”

“No.”

“I changed the route and sent notice to wagon and North.”

“I noticed.”

His voice stayed formal.

Not loud.

“You did not send notice to the crew whose work plan depended on the wagon you removed.”

Correct.

I hated him.

“Correct.”

“We continued cutting brush for the lower haul.”

“How much?”

“Enough that the turnout is now becoming inconvenient.”

Not dangerous.

Not disaster.

Still a problem.

“Why didn’t you stop when wagon didn’t arrive?”

“It is not after third bell.”

Right.

They were still working under valid old schedule.

The wagon was not late yet.

I had changed future state without changing their information.

Worse.

Lower lead continued.

“If I had known at second bell, I would have shifted four people to old-stake pull and kept only two bundling until we knew whether the wagon returned.”

That was a work-method choice.

His.

Exactly.

I had removed the information he needed to make it.

“I should have told you first.”

“You should have told all affected leads.”

“All?”

“Middle uses lower turnout path for basket return after fourth bell.”

I closed eyes.

Of course.

Shared route.

I had updated the vehicle.

Updated receiving crew.

Updated office.

Not updated every person whose local plan touched the route.

Old support memory arrived too late.

Changing one resource could change six people's timing without changing any of their orders.

“I need field steward.”

“He is at North.”

“Of course he is.”

Lower lead did not smile.

Cruel man.

We went to lower turnout.

Cut brush had been bundled along one side of access.

Not catastrophic.

But enough that a cart turning through would lose room.

Middle's basket return could still pass by hand.

A loaded cart would be annoying.

I did not decide whether annoying was acceptable.

Field steward did.

He arrived after runner found him.

Looked at brush.

Looked at road.

Looked at me.

“Why did Lower keep cutting?”

“Because I did not notify them when wagon priority changed.”

Lower lead said nothing.

Good.

No rescue.

Field steward asked, “Current wagon?”

“North turnout first. Lower after if time.”

“North brush stack size?”

“Unknown to me.”

“You sent wagon there without knowing?”

“House priority note ordered North first.”

He took note.

Read.

“Fine. That is not your decision.”

Good.

“But affected crew notification is.”

“Yes.”

He looked at lower turnout again.

“Stop adding brush here. Lower lead?”

“Yes.”

“Your people can move to old-stake pull west edge until fourth bell. Keep two on ditch cleanup if you want. Your method.”

“Accepted.”

There.

Not Greg assignment.

Field steward opened section.

Crew lead chose work split.

I updated.

Then Middle.

I found Middle lead before he found me.

Small mercy.

“Contract wagon changed to North first. Lower brush is staying in turnout longer. Field steward has stopped additional brush stacking there. Does that change your basket return?”

He looked toward lower road.

“Yes, because our return line uses the inside bend, and if those bundles creep another half pace we have either single-file carriers or we go around the stone wall, which is not far unless you are carrying wet spoil and then suddenly distance becomes a philosophical problem.”

“Current bundles are stopped.”

“Stopped where?”

“Lower turnout east edge.”

“Show me.”

We walked.

Well.

He walked.

I crutched.

He looked.

“Fine if they stay there.”

“Good.”

“Do they stay there?”

“Field steward stopped additional stacking.”

“Then yes.”

There.

Actual confirmation.

Not assumption.

I added:

**MIDDLE LEAD CONFIRMED basket return remains workable with current lower bundle position.**

Then I sent notice to North lead too.

She read.

“You changed wagon to us.”

“House did.”

“You knew.”

“Yes.”

“You told us.”

“Yes.”

“Then why are you here?”

“Because apparently I am learning notification is not finished until I know who change hurts.”

She looked at me.

“Expensive lesson?”

“Completion fee currently endangered only emotionally.”

She handed note back.

“Lower mad?”

“Formal.”

“Worse.”

“Yes.”

By third bell contract wagon reached North.

North brush stack actually did need clearing before evening house cart passage.

So priority change was not stupid.

Important.

Sometimes bad coordination came from bad instruction.

This was not that.

Instruction was valid.

My transmission was incomplete.

Wagon loaded North brush.

I did not supervise loading.

North lead and wagon people handled it.

I recorded departure.

At fourth bell wagon returned.

Lower had fewer new brush bundles because lead had shifted people exactly as he said he would have earlier.

They loaded what existed.

Not all planned brush moved.

Lower crew did not fail.

House priority had changed.

Record reflected that.

At fifth bell I went back to field office.

The woman read my day sheet.

Stopped at the priority change.

Read longer.

Then:

“Why is there an organizer-caused delay entry?”

“Because I caused one.”

“How long?”

“Lower crew continued old brush plan for about half a bell after priority changed because I failed to notify them. No worker idle time established. It created excess brush at lower turnout and required field-steward reassignment before the expected wagon window.”

She looked up.

“You could have written priority transition delay.”

“That would be true.”

“And shorter.”

“It would also hide who failed.”

She tapped paper.

“The house does not need confession. It needs useful record.”

Fair.

“What is useful wording?”

She thought.

“Priority change received before second bell. Wagon reassigned North. Lower crew notification delayed approximately half bell by organizer. Lower continued prior plan until notified. Field steward reassigned allowable work. No injury. No worker idle period recorded. Lower brush haul reduced.”

I wrote it.

Better.

Factual.

Less theatrical guilt.

“Do I lose completion?”

“Not from that.”

“Good.”

“If you do it again, maybe.”

“Less good.”

She pointed at communication section.

“You have sender and receiver for wagon.”

“Yes.”

“Add affected-party acknowledgment for priority changes.”

There.

System change.

Not moral lesson.

I added a small column.

**AFFECTED LEADS ACKNOWLEDGED**

Annoying.

Useful.

Day three record accepted.

Still no completion money.

Twenty-two.

That night Lyssa was home before me.

Rare enough to be suspicious.

She had one foot on chair and was pulling thread from a seam with small blade.

“Bad day?”

“Educational.”

“That means bad.”

“I forgot to tell a crew I moved their wagon.”

“You moved their wagon?”

“House moved wagon. I moved information badly.”

She pulled another stitch.

“Did anybody die?”

“No.”

“Did anybody lose money?”

“Not established.”

“Did anybody yell?”

“No.”

“That sounds fake.”

“The Lower lead was formal.”

“Oh.”

She understood.

I ate.

Told her the useful part.

Not every cart.

Not every section.

Just that I had updated the schedule everywhere except one of the people whose work depended on it.

Lyssa said, “That sounds like you.”

“Hostile home environment.”

“You like systems more than people sometimes.”

I opened mouth.

Closed it.

She looked pleased.

“Terrible woman.”

“Yes.”

Fourth day came with rain.

Not hard.

Enough to turn edges dark and make every road decision slightly more expensive.

Field steward kept all three sections open but marked Lower drainage for shorter checks.

His decision.

North crew arrived nine instead of ten.

Middle seven.

Lower eight.

Meal count twenty-four.

I changed roster.

Changed provisioning count.

Both.

Growth.

Cart A had twenty-four meal portions.

I nearly became arrogant.

Then did not.

Cart B arrived on time.

Contract wagon had no fourth-day assignment because the house priority sheet moved final brush haul to its own evening cart.

Good.

Fewer moving parts.

I still used affected-lead acknowledgment column when field steward shortened Lower's work window.

Lower lead signed initial after I told him.

Middle lead initialed when basket-return route changed by one path because rain softened the lower bend.

North lead refused to initial.

“Why?”

“I heard you.”

“Office wants acknowledgment.”

“Then write I heard you.”

“That is acknowledgment.”

“Then why do I need to write?”

I considered.

She was right enough.

The office had told me to add affected-party acknowledgment.

Not necessarily signatures.

I wrote:

**NORTH LEAD verbally acknowledged 2nd-bell Cart A return change.**

Then showed her.

She read.

“Fine.”

Good.

No bureaucracy for its own sake.

By midday nothing dramatic had happened.

By afternoon even less.

North finished its contracted ditch segment.

Middle handed off the south access edge.

Lower completed the open section the steward had kept available and left the remaining wet edge marked for later house work outside these four days.

Not failure.

Scope.

Crew leads reported completion in their own terms.

I did not translate fieldcraft into my language.

I recorded handoffs.

North lead: section complete to marked post.

Middle lead: access line clear to agreed south edge, root crown at final marker left for house removal because it sat outside their contracted line.

Lower lead: drainage cut complete through open section; wet-turn edge excluded under steward hold.

Field steward signed readiness/handoff.

Tool keeper closed issue sheet.

Two long ditch hooks returned.

Basket count still nine at North.

Nobody had invented the missing three.

Good.

Cart A final water count closed.

Meal count closed.

Contract wagon had no unresolved route.

I brought four days of organizer records to field office.

The woman read them.

All of them.

This took time.

I sat.

Did not count future copper.

Tried not to.

Failed.

Ten copper.

If paid.

Twenty-two plus ten.

Thirty-two.

That was more liquid copper than I had held at once in a while.

Not silver.

Not rich.

But the old thirty-copper reserve marker lived there.

I stopped.

Not mine yet.

She kept reading.

Day one.

Two corrections.

Day two.

Meal count mismatch.

Day three.

Organizer notification delay.

Day four.

Rain route acknowledgment.

She tapped day three.

“Lower lead complain?”

“Not formally.”

“Did you ask?”

“Yes. He said record was accurate after correction.”

“Field steward?”

“Signed.”

She checked.

Then day four.

“Why no North lead initial?”

“Verbal acknowledgment. She objected to signing something when office only needed proof she received change.”

The woman looked at me.

“She objected?”

“Yes.”

“And you changed method?”

“I recorded verbal acknowledgment in front of her.”

“That is fine.”

Good.

She stacked papers.

“Accepted.”

I waited.

She looked amused.

“Four-day handoff accepted.”

There.

“Completion fee?”

“Still ten copper.”

“Important question.”

She opened cash box.

Counted.

Ten copper.

Put it on table.

I counted again.

Not because I distrusted her.

Because money deserved witnesses.

Ten.

Cash twenty-two to thirty-two.

No conversion.

No celebration.

Just thirty-two copper.

She said, “You did adequately.”

“High praise.”

“You missed one affected crew on a priority change.”

“Yes.”

“You corrected without arguing that technically the schedule was updated.”

“I considered arguing internally.”

“I can tell.”

“Useful skill.”

“No.”

She slid my copy of accepted term over.

“Keep that. It is a reference.”

That mattered almost as much as copper.

Almost.

“What does it support?”

“Four-day organizer term completed with accepted records.”

“Field competence?”

“No.”

“Hiring?”

“No.”

“Safety?”

“No.”

“Beautiful.”

“You are strange.”

“Reference can include that?”

“No.”

Fair.

I folded paper carefully.

No promise of another contract.

No permanent position.

No crew became mine.

North lead would go wherever North crew went.

Middle lead would continue explaining material reality until language surrendered.

Lower lead would remain formal enough to make mistakes hurt more.

Field office would hire someone else next time if someone better existed.

Good.

The work had still happened.

At home I put ten copper on table.

Lyssa looked at it.

Then me.

“How much?”

“Ten.”

“For today?”

“For finishing four-day organizer term.”

“That sounds better.”

“It is.”

“How much now?”

“Thirty-two cash.”

She stopped with cup halfway to mouth.

Not dramatic.

Just noticed.

“Plus Vale?”

“Vale is the opposite direction.”

“Right.”

Twenty-three copper still owed.

There it was.

For the first time, that number did not feel larger than my entire life.

Twenty-three owed.

Thirty-two cash.

Ten floor.

Work limb still existed.

Other obligations might exist.

Bits existed.

Food existed.

Tomorrow existed.

Thirty-two did not mean twenty-three was free to leave.

It meant I could finally look at old numbers without assuming every answer was no.

That was different.

Lyssa said, “Are you going to hand Antonius all of it tomorrow and come home proud and broke?”

“No.”

“Good.”

“I am going to verify what I actually owe first.”

She narrowed eyes.

“Who are you?”

“Growth is frightening.”

“Very.”

I put the copper away.

Did not separate twenty-three.

Did not pay anything.

Not yet.

First verify.

Vale.

Work limb.

Anything else actually contractual.

Not favors.

Not meals.

Not people being kind to me.

Real obligations only.

Then preserve enough cash that clearing the past did not destroy the future.

That thought wanted to become a manifesto.

I refused.

My right leg hurt.

My hands were tired.

One crutch tip had collected South Road mud in the outer groove.

I cleaned it with an old rag.

Much better use of evening.

Magic remained fifty-nine, fifty-three, one-forty-seven.

Hark & Venn had not sent word.

No theatre runner.

No artifact.

No sudden opportunity.

Thirty-two copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

Ten floor.

A completed organizer reference folded with my papers.

And one management lesson I trusted because it had cost somebody else half a bell of bad information.

A plan was not shared because I had written the current version.

It was shared when the people whose choices depended on it actually had it.

Obvious.

Expensive.

Mine now.

Tomorrow I could start asking which old obligations were real.

Tonight I cleaned mud off my crutch and ate.

That was enough.

# CHAPTER 487

## THE REPAYER

Twenty-three copper used to be weather.

Not literally.

That would have been easier.

Weather did not care what I had done before breakfast.

Twenty-three copper sat in Antonius Vale's ledger because I had borrowed, advanced, gambled, spent, worked, credited, and generally behaved like Future Greg was a wealthy idiot I could bill forever.

He had not been.

Current Greg had thirty-two copper.

That changed shape of problem.

Not enough to erase it cleanly.

Enough to stop pretending it was mountains.

I counted money once.

Thirty-two.

Then again because arithmetic became more trustworthy when coins were physically annoying.

Still thirty-two.

Lyssa was working at table.

Dark blue fabric today.

Long coat maybe.

Or dress.

I had learned not to guess garments while she held scissors.

"You're doing money face," she said.

"I have money face?"

"You have several."

"Yesterday you said two."

"I revised."

Growth.

I separated ten copper.

Not hidden.

Not sacred.

Floor.

Then another ten.

Working room.

That left twelve.

Lyssa looked over.

"You're paying him twelve."

"Maybe."

"Why maybe?"

"Because first I verify."

She put scissors down.

"That sounds responsible."

"Don't sound surprised."

"I didn't."

She had.

I ignored.

The twelve copper went into one pouch.

The other twenty stayed home.

That mattered.

Old Greg would have carried everything because carrying everything made options feel larger.

Current Greg had learned that sometimes largest option was not having all money available to spend.

I took two crutches.

No liner needed.

No Hessa apparatus.

No artifact.

Just account.

Vale office was open by second bell.

Same front clerk.

Different stack of paper.

Commerce reproduced.

"Antonius?"

"In."

"Busy?"

"Yes."

"Can I pay him while busy?"

The clerk looked at pouch.

"That depends whether you need conversation."

"Unfortunately."

"Then wait."

I waited.

Again.

This was becoming luxury habit.

A quarter bell later Antonius waved me in.

He looked at me.

Then pouch.

Then me.

"No."

"I haven't asked."

"You are going to make payment dramatic."

"I was planning restrained competence."

"Worse."

I sat in guest chair.

Still uneven.

I rocked it once.

He stared.

"Continuity matters," I said.

"Fix it yourself."

"Then it stops being your chair."

"Excellent."

I put no money down yet.

That got attention.

"Current balance first."

Antonius leaned back.

"You know current balance."

"Twenty-three copper."

"Then why ask?"

"Because last time I came here to talk about retired equipment you told me I owed twenty-three. I have since done work elsewhere. I have not made payment to you. I want ledger, not memory."

His face changed slightly.

Approval perhaps.

Or indigestion.

He called clerk.

Actual account came.

One ledger this time.

"Only one?" I asked.

"You have become less inconvenient."

"Financial progress."

"Do not get excited."

The clerk opened account.

My name.

GREGORY.

Still offensive.

Entries since old review.

Credits.

Payments.

No mystery new fee.

No invisible interest line invented while I was traveling.

Twenty-three copper outstanding.

I traced latest line.

"Nothing changed."

"You did not pay anything."

"Sometimes institutions discover reasons."

"We already had reasons."

Fair.

"What are current payment terms?"

Antonius looked at me.

I waited.

He said, "Same."

"I want words."

He sighed.

"Cash accepted. Work credit when we agree work in advance. No fixed installment. No penalty for not paying on a schedule that does not exist. I would prefer payment when you have surplus rather than when paying me makes you unable to work."

There.

Exact enough.

"Any minimum reserve you require me to keep?"

"No."

"Any minimum payment?"

"No."

"Any reason not to pay twelve today?"

"Your reason."

"Meaning?"

"You decide whether twelve is surplus."

I hated adult freedom.

"Do you know how much cash I have?"

"No."

"Thirty-two."

His eyebrows moved.

Tiny.

Real.

"Better."

"That is all?"

"What do you want, applause?"

"Confetti."

"No."

I explained only necessary part.

South Road organizer term.

Fourteen total over four days.

Accepted records.

No future job guaranteed.

He listened.

"How much are you keeping?"

"Twenty."

"Why?"

"Ten floor. Ten working room."

"Working room for what?"

"Fare. Food. records. Opportunity. Broken crutch tip. Something boring that costs more than expected."

"Good."

That annoyed me.

"I was hoping you'd argue."

"Why?"

"So I could defend intelligence."

"Pay twelve."

Cruel.

I opened pouch.

Twelve copper.

Not one.

That felt strange.

Not emotionally impossible.

Just heavier on table.

Antonius counted.

Clerk counted again.

Twelve.

Receipt written.

Account updated.

Twenty-three became eleven.

I stared at line.

Eleven copper.

Antonius watched me.

"Do you understand subtraction?"

"Recently."

"Good."

I held receipt.

The first time I paid him one copper, the point had been proving debt was finite.

Now twelve copper disappeared from purse without threatening food.

That was different enough I did not need speech.

So naturally I almost made one.

Antonius interrupted.

"Do not pay the other eleven today."

"I don't have it in pouch."

"You have twenty elsewhere."

I looked at him.

"How did you know?"

"You said thirty-two."

Right.

"I could pay eleven and keep nine."

"That would put you below whatever floor you just claimed mattered."

"Ten."

"Then no."

"You are protecting ledger."

"Correct."

"If I die, debt gets worse?"

"Collection gets annoying."

"Beautiful relationship."

He pushed updated account copy toward me.

"Eleven. No fixed schedule. Same terms."

I folded it.

That was all.

Almost.

"Hark & Venn."

Antonius looked at me.

"What about them?"

"Separate obligation. Work limb quote."

"I am not their clerk."

"Good answer."

"What are you asking?"

"Whether paying one real debt down changes anything with yours."

"No."

"Good."

"Greg."

"Yes."

"Do not use my ledger as permission to invent theirs."

There.

Apparently everyone had developed the same irritating instinct.

"That was already plan."

"Then leave."

I left.

Eleven Vale.

Twenty cash at home.

Bits unspecified.

Magic still fifty-nine, fifty-three, one-forty-seven.

No wealth.

Just less old drag.

Outside, I bought nothing.

This required restraint mostly because a baker cart smelled unfair.

I had bits.

Breakfast had happened.

Still unfair.

I went home first.

Not Hark & Venn.

That surprised me.

Lyssa was gone.

Work.

Good.

I put receipt beside old Vale strip.

One copper old payment.

Twelve copper new payment.

The new strip looked almost identical.

Amount was only different line.

No fanfare.

I liked that.

Then I found Hark & Venn papers.

Quote.

Old notes.

Deposit receipt.

Twenty copper deposit paid.

Total quote one silver and eighteen copper.

Current exact problem remained current exact problem.

Without conversion rate, I could not turn that into a clean copper balance in my head without inventing money rules.

More importantly, I still did not have payment schedule.

Or final completion notice.

Or request.

I could walk there and ask.

That was legitimate.

So I did.

Hark & Venn shop smelled like wood dust, oil, leather, and enough metal filings to season soup.

Bad soup.

Mara was not front.

Another shop worker looked up.

"Greg."

Known enough.

"That concerning?"

"You come often enough."

"I aspire to be forgettable."

"Failed."

Good.

"I need account question."

"Fit?"

"Money."

Worse word.

Worker called someone from back.

Not Mara.

Older bookkeeper maybe.

No name needed.

I gave quote copy and deposit receipt.

"I am not here to pay unless account says payment is currently due. I need exact status."

Bookkeeper read.

Then pulled shop order.

Actual paper.

Good.

"Quote still active."

"One silver eighteen copper."

"Yes."

"Deposit twenty copper received."

"Yes."

"Applied to order?"

"Yes."

Important.

"What is due today?"

"Nothing scheduled today."

There.

I did not improve sentence.

"Final balance timing?"

"On final acceptance unless shop and customer agree otherwise."

New exact term.

Useful.

"Final acceptance means?"

"Completed work presented, fit accepted for release, account settled or alternate payment agreement made."

Good.

"Is completed work presented?"

"No."

"Then no payment due."

"No."

I could have kissed paperwork.

Did not.

"Any storage fee?"

"No."

"Any new fitting fee from last review?"

"No. Existing order."

"Any materials change affecting quote?"

Bookkeeper checked.

"No approved price change on account."

Good.

Not forever guaranteed.

Current.

"Can I make voluntary payment early?"

"Yes."

There.

I stopped.

Could.

Different from should.

I had twenty cash.

Vale eleven.

Ten floor.

Ten working room.

If I paid Hark & Venn now, I would reduce working room.

Maybe rational if it prevented future bottleneck.

But completed work not ready.

No payment due.

No discount established.

No production acceleration established.

No reason.

So no.

"What would early payment change?"

Bookkeeper looked at me.

"Account balance."

"Production timing?"

"No."

"Material priority?"

"No."

"Fit schedule?"

"No."

There.

Information.

I smiled.

"Then I am done."

They seemed disappointed by efficiency.

Mara came through back doorway anyway, carrying leather strap and pencil.

"You're here."

"Apparently."

"Problem?"

"No."

She looked at bookkeeper.

"Money question."

Mara looked at me.

"Did you get rich?"

"Thirty-two copper briefly."

"Briefly?"

"Paid Vale twelve."

She nodded like that made sense.

"I have twenty."

"Good."

"Your shop could have some."

"No."

I stared.

She pointed pencil at me.

"Not until it changes something."

Everyone was conspiring.

"Bookkeeper said same."

"Then why are you asking me?"

"To see if specialist contradicts ledger."

"Specialist says stop trying to pre-pay a socket that isn't ready."

"Hostile."

"Accurate."

She disappeared back.

That was Hark & Venn update.

No fitting.

No secret completion.

No dramatic reveal of work limb under cloth.

Just account.

Useful.

Outside I wrote:

**VALE: verified 23c. Paid 12c. Remaining 11c. Same flexible terms.**

Then:

**H&V: quote unchanged 1s18c. 20c deposit received/applied. Nothing scheduled due today. Final balance due on final acceptance unless alternate agreement. Early payment allowed but does not change production/material priority/fit schedule. No reason to pay early established.**

On way back from Hark & Venn, I stopped at public fountain because shoulder strap on pouch was annoying me.

Not painful.

Annoying.

Important category.

I set both crutches against stone, drank, then checked strap.

One stitch had stretched.

Not broken.

Just stretched enough that coin weight pulled pouch lower than I liked.

Twenty copper was heavier than seven.

Obvious.

Still new.

I retied strap through second loop instead of pretending leather would improve from confidence.

A boy beside fountain watched me.

Maybe ten.

He had wooden hoop.

"Did you lose your leg in war?"

There it was.

"No."

"Monster?"

"No."

"Cart?"

"No."

He frowned.

"What then?"

"Badly."

He considered that.

"That's not a thing."

"It was at the time."

His mother called him from across street.

He ran away dissatisfied.

Good.

I put pouch back on.

Money stayed where I wanted it.

Body stayed where I wanted it.

Mostly.

The route home passed Vale office again if I cut through west market.

I did not.

Not because temptation to pay eleven.

Because I had already made decision.

Repeating decision every street corner was not discipline.

It was anxiety wearing spectacles.

I took longer market route.

Bought two onions with bits.

Not copper.

A woman selling lamp wick tried to convince me her new batch burned twice as long.

"Compared to what?"

"Old batch."

"Whose old batch?"

"Mine."

"Tested?"

She stared.

I bought onions and left wick.

Apparently Hessa had colonized shopping too.

At one crossing, a porter dropped a bundle of reed slats.

Nothing magical.

People helped because slats blocked road.

I could not help much without surrendering both hands and becoming furniture.

So I moved aside, held one rolling slat in place with crutch tip, and let people with free hands do actual work.

A month ago that might have irritated me.

Today it was just geometry.

The porter said thanks.

I said, "My contribution was preventing one stick."

"Important stick."

"Best one."

Then kept going.

No lesson.

No job.

No commission.

It was a city afternoon.

That mattered more than I wanted to admit.

Twelve copper had just left my life and the day had not narrowed around it.

I still bought onions.

Still took longer route.

Still had room for ordinary mistakes.

That was probably what surplus actually meant.

Not money you did not care about.

Money you could spend without making every next decision worse.

That was enough debt audit for one day.

Almost.

I went through notebook for actual obligations.

Not favors.

Not meals.

Not kindness.

Actual.

Vale.

Work limb.

Anything else?

Gauge debt?

I had memory of Antonius/Tere gauge lane.

But memory was not authority.

I did not pay ghosts.

Borrowed money?

Maybe old lines already rolled into Vale.

Maybe not.

Again.

No ghosts.

Unpaid equipment?

Nothing current came to mind with exact number and counterparty.

Advances?

Need proof.

Installments?

Need proof.

So I stopped.

That was discipline too.

At home, Lyssa returned with bread.

Not gift debt.

Bread.

"How poor are you?"

"Twenty copper."

She set bread down.

"That sounded almost proud."

"I paid twelve."

"Vale?"

"Yes. Verified twenty-three first. Eleven left."

She blinked.

"That's it?"

"That's what?"

"You went from twenty-three to eleven?"

"Yes."

"Today?"

"Yes."

She looked toward table where old account papers sat.

"That used to be huge."

There.

I sat.

Right leg tired.

Shoulders normal tired.

Hands slightly sore from crutches and papers and city.

No magic.

No artifact.

No field crew.

Just errands.

"It still exists," I said.

"Eleven."

"Yes."

"And you have twenty."

"Also yes."

"Could pay it."

"Would leave nine."

She pointed bread at me.

"So don't."

"Excellent financial counsel."

"I know."

I told her Hark & Venn terms.

Nothing due today.

Final balance on final acceptance unless alternate agreement.

Early payment changes nothing but account.

She nodded.

"Then why pay early?"

"Exactly."

She tore bread.

"Look at you."

"Do not make this emotional."

"You're the one with a debt notebook."

"I have many notebooks."

"That makes it worse."

We ate.

I expected to feel lighter.

That phrase annoyed me because debt was not physical weight.

Except sometimes it had been.

Jobs chosen because I needed next copper.

Travel delayed.

Artifacts skipped.

Risk avoided because there was no room.

One account did not cause all of that.

But it had been part.

Now Vale was eleven.

Eleven.

I had spent more thought avoiding eleven copper losses than I had just spent paying twelve.

Scale changed.

Not solved.

Changed.

I checked cash once after dinner.

Twenty.

Ten floor.

Ten room.

Vale eleven.

Hark & Venn no current due.

I could chase another commission tomorrow.

Or rest.

Or theatre.

Or answer Hessa if she sent word.

Or buy fare if route mattered.

Options remained.

That was point of not emptying purse.

Lyssa was repairing coat seam near lamp.

I put updated Vale receipt with account papers.

The old one-copper receipt sat beneath it.

I almost threw old receipt away.

Did not.

Not because sentiment.

Because comparison was useful.

One copper had once been enough to make repayment feel real.

Twelve copper now was enough to make old debt look small.

Someday eleven might be line item.

Maybe soon.

Not today.

I wrote final note:

**DEBT CLOSURE IS NOT SAME AS CASH DEPLETION.**

Then crossed out because it sounded like poster.

Better:

**Keep enough to move. Pay what is actually owed.**

Still a little poster.

Fine.

Nobody was reading notebook except future me.

Poor man.

I closed it.

Twenty cash.

Eleven Vale.

One silver eighteen work-limb quote, twenty-copper deposit already applied, nothing due until final acceptance unless terms change.

No other debt verified.

That last line mattered most.

No other debt verified.

Not no other debt existed.

I simply did not know enough to pay it.

For once, uncertainty did not need filling.

It could stay blank until evidence arrived.

I went to bed less indebted than I had woken.

Not cleansed.

Not transformed.

Just twelve copper poorer and twelve copper less obligated.

Good trade.

# CHAPTER 488

## THE ACTOR

Twenty copper lasted through breakfast.

This was less impressive than thirty-two.

It was also more useful than nine.

I decided not to improve it before noon.

Dangerous experiment.

Lyssa had already left for work.

The room was quiet except for street carts, somebody arguing about onions downstairs, and one pigeon that had developed a personal grievance against the sill.

I ate bread.

Used the last of yesterday's onions.

Did not calculate return on onions.

Growth.

Vale eleven.

Hark & Venn nothing due.

Magic fifty-nine, fifty-three, one-forty-seven.

No South Road contract.

No buyer.

No seller.

No one waiting for me to fix movement of anything.

I had become temporarily unemployed in four separate disciplines.

Excellent.

The theatre runner found me before second bell.

Not the same visitor from before.

Actual theatre runner.

Young man, narrow shoulders, damp hair, carrying three folded notices and breathing like he had decided stairs were political.

"Greg?"

"Unfortunately."

"Rinna says come."

"Why?"

"She said not to explain because you'd ask questions."

I looked at him.

"That's insulting."

"She was right."

Strong runner.

I took crutches.

No bag beyond notebook.

No money pouch beyond bits and two copper because leaving home with nothing had become unnecessary performance.

The theatre smelled like dust, glue, old cloth, and somebody boiling something that should not have been boiled indoors.

Home-adjacent.

Rinna stood near stage with script in one hand.

Nella sat on edge of platform wearing rehearsal trousers and one shoe.

The other shoe was beside her.

I pointed.

"Loss?"

"Choice."

"Concerning."

"It hurts."

"Less concerning."

Rinna held script toward me.

"No."

"I haven't asked."

"You came holding pages."

"That is not consent."

"Read."

There it was.

I took pages.

Not prompt copy.

Scene.

Actual scene.

Five pages.

My role marked **HARROW**.

I knew Harrow.

Not personally.

Character.

Older younger brother.

Which was already annoying phrase.

The play had been in rehearsal while I traveled. I had seen pieces before leaving and bits when I substituted prompt-side after returning.

Nella played Mara Vale.

Not Vale related to Antonius.

Probably.

Different Vale.

Need not investigate.

Harrow was her brother, recently home after six years away, pretending he had come back because their father was sick when he had actually come back because the river company had collapsed and taken his money.

Good.

Normal family disaster.

"Where is Pell?" I asked.

"Sick," Rinna said.

"Renn?"

"Back."

Good.

One uncertainty resolved casually.

Renn was apparently alive and working again.

No ceremony.

"Why me?"

"Because you know scene traffic, you're here, and Nella needs another body with enough words to run the second half."

"Enough words."

"Do not become proud."

Nella said, "Pell does it better."

"Thank you."

"He does."

"I believed first time."

She looked at me.

Good.

Not everyone cushioning ego.

Rinna tapped page.

"This is rehearsal. You are not replacing Pell. Pell is still Harrow unless illness grows ambition. We need Nella's scene. You read Harrow."

"How much acting?"

"Some."

"Terrible unit."

"More than table read. Less than costume."

Useful.

I put crutches at side of stage.

That created immediate problem.

Harrow crossed from window to center table, then to door, then back.

I did not.

Current me had two crutches.

No trial limb.

No theatrical miracle.

Rinna watched me look at blocking marks.

"Adapt."

"Authority?"

"Acting blocking for this rehearsal only. Do not redesign scene."

There.

I moved chair closer to center line.

Kept one crutch against it.

Other stayed under right hand.

Could stand, shift, sit, turn.

Not cross quickly.

Harrow would become less mobile in this run.

Fine.

Nella put second shoe back on.

Apparently pain had negotiated.

We started.

Scene opened with her folding father's shirts.

Harrow entered.

My first line:

"I thought you would have burned those."

I said it.

Correctly.

Nothing happened.

Nella looked at me.

In character maybe.

Then said her line.

"He still wears them."

I said, "He still thinks he does."

Good line.

I liked it.

I said it like I liked it.

Rinna stopped us.

"No."

"What?"

"You're showing us line."

"I spoke line."

"You presented it."

Nella said, "You made face before it."

"I have face."

"Yes."

Rinna pointed at start.

"Again. Harrow has been in house ten minutes. He knows father is worse than letter said. He is trying not to let Mara ask why he came home. Stop acting like audience deserves warning when good sentence arrives."

Cruel.

Accurate.

Again.

"I thought you would have burned those."

"He still wears them."

"He still thinks he does."

Better.

Not much.

Nella folded shirt.

She did not look up.

Her next three lines were longer than mine.

She spoke while folding.

Not cleanly.

Character thought changed halfway through.

"Mrs. Pell says he asks for tea and then forgets why it's there, and yesterday he told me Mother would be angry if I left the window open, which would be interesting if Mother had not been dead nine years, and I shut it anyway because I was tired."

The line landed because Nella did not polish it.

She sounded tired.

Actually tired.

I forgot next line.

Not entirely.

It existed somewhere.

Prompt-side instinct looked for cue copy that was not there.

Renn whispered from side:

"Then why write me?"

Right.

I said, "Then why write me?"

Late.

Nella looked up.

"Because I wanted you home."

That was scene.

Simple.

My next line was supposed to be:

"You wanted help."

I said it too fast.

Rinna stopped.

"No."

I looked at page.

"It's four words."

"Yes."

"Useful."

"Not when you use them to escape."

Nella said, "You sound like Harrow is cleverer than her."

"He thinks he is."

"Not there."

I looked at her.

She pointed at page.

"She just said she wanted him home. He doesn't know what to do with that. Pell lets it sit."

Pell.

Better actor.

Good.

"What do you do?" I asked.

Nella frowned.

"When?"

"As Mara. After saying it."

"Nothing."

"Nothing?"

"Yes."

She demonstrated.

"Because I wanted you home."

Then she just looked at me.

No gesture.

No cleverness.

Uncomfortable.

I understood.

Hated it.

Again.

"Then why write me?"

"Because I wanted you home."

Silence.

Longer.

Not theatre-long.

Human-long.

I felt impulse to fill it.

Joke.

Move.

Look away.

Harrow probably felt same.

Good.

"You wanted help."

This time it came smaller.

Nella's answer:

"I did too."

There.

Better.

Rinna did not stop.

We continued.

Harrow tried to make father practical.

Meals.

Doctor.

Money.

Roof.

Mara refused to let him turn return into maintenance contract.

Good scene.

I liked play more now.

At page three, Harrow crossed to door in original blocking.

I could not make that distance smoothly while holding pages and using crutch.

I tucked pages under arm.

Bad.

Lost place.

Rinna stopped.

"Pages on table."

"Then I can't see line at door."

"Don't go door."

"Blocking says door."

"Rehearsal says current body."

That was simple.

Nella said, "Pell goes door because he wants to leave."

"I also want leave."

"Then make me believe it from chair."

Hostile craft.

Good.

I sat.

Harrow's next line was:

"I can send money."

Mara:

"From where?"

Then Harrow lied.

"North office kept me."

Important.

I said lie too smoothly.

Nella laughed.

Out of character.

"That is Greg lie."

"What?"

"You say bad thing like you've already prepared defense."

"I have experience."

"Exactly."

Rinna said, "Harrow hasn't practiced this lie enough."

Interesting.

I tried again.

"I can send money."

"From where?"

"North office kept me."

Too hesitant.

Rinna shook head.

"Now he's twelve."

"Useful spectrum."

"Find middle."

I did.

Three more times.

Not exciting.

Third was worse than second.

Fourth finally had tiny break between North and office.

Like Harrow selected lie while speaking.

Nella's face changed.

She heard it.

"Kept you doing what?"

I answered.

"Accounts."

"Whose?"

"River."

"Which river?"

There.

Character trapped.

I smiled.

Rinna stopped.

"Why are you smiling?"

"Good trap."

"Greg is enjoying writing. Harrow is losing."

Right.

Again.

No smile.

Harder than magic.

Magic at least did not care about expression.

Scene pushed Harrow toward admitting company failed.

He still did not admit money.

Mara knew anyway.

Nella had one line:

"You came home poor and decided shame would be less visible if you carried groceries."

Excellent.

I laughed.

Everyone stopped.

Nella stared.

"That is my line."

"I know."

"Then stop appreciating it."

"Good line."

Rinna said, "Audience can appreciate it. Harrow cannot."

"I disagree."

"Harrow is being disemboweled."

"Metaphorically."

"Act like it."

Again.

This time line hit.

"You came home poor and decided shame would be less visible if you carried groceries."

I felt stupidly seen.

Not because exact.

Because mechanism.

Come back useful enough and maybe nobody asks what failed.

That thought did not belong only Harrow.

Dangerous.

I did not use it as revelation.

I let it make eye contact harder.

Nella saw change.

She kept going.

Good actor.

"Did you think Father would care?"

"I didn't come for him."

First true line.

Nella stopped folding.

"Mara knows that?" I asked.

Rinna said nothing.

Nella said, "She does now."

We ran rest.

Scene ended without reconciliation.

Mara handed Harrow one of father's shirts.

He took it.

Not hug.

Not forgiveness.

Just object.

Harrow said:

"This won't fit me."

Mara:

"I know."

Blackout.

Good.

I sat holding rehearsal shirt.

Rinna said, "Again from 'You came home poor.'"

No praise.

Excellent.

We did it again.

Then again.

By fourth run, my shoulder started complaining from repeated stand-sit with crutch.

Not pain.

Work.

I shifted chair half foot.

Rinna noticed.

"Better?"

"Yes."

Nella said, "You can stay seated after lie."

"Does that change scene?"

"It changes your height."

"Deep."

"It makes Mara stand over you."

I looked at Rinna.

She considered.

"Try."

Specialist authority.

Not mine.

We tried.

Better.

I hated this profession.

Pell's original movement made him walk away when exposed.

My current body made escape cumbersome.

Sitting made Harrow trapped.

Not universally better.

Different.

Rinna said, "Keep for today."

Today.

Good boundary.

At break Renn brought water.

Alive, apparently.

"Recovered?"

"Mostly."

"Your illness inconvenienced me."

"I heard you survived prompt."

"Barely."

He sat nearby.

"Bell seven?"

I stared.

News traveled.

"Nella betrayed me."

Nella from across room said, "You missed it because you watched me."

Renn nodded.

"Understandable."

Traitor.

I drank.

No one asked about money.

No one cared I had paid Vale twelve yesterday.

Theatre possessed its own scale.

A missed bell mattered here.

A line landing mattered.

A shoe hurting mattered.

Pell being sick mattered because scene needed Pell.

Good.

After break, Rinna took pages away.

I objected.

"You know section."

"I know approximate section."

"Good."

"Not same."

"Act."

Cruel.

Without pages, I lost exact word in second exchange.

Nella corrected in character.

I recovered badly.

Rinna did not stop.

Interesting.

Scene survived.

Next run I overcorrected and anticipated cue.

Nella held silence longer just to punish me.

I entered early anyway.

She raised eyebrow.

Character could have done that.

Maybe Nella.

Both.

After run she said, "You keep trying to help me get to my line."

"I support."

"Don't."

"Hostile to teamwork."

"My pause belongs to me."

There.

Good.

That sentence lodged.

My pause belongs to me.

Support instinct could become interference if I optimized other person's timing for them.

Theatre version of management mistake.

Annoying repetition across life.

I did not write it down.

Growth.

Next run I let her pause.

It felt too long.

Then it didn't.

By fifth bell Pell arrived.

Sick enough to look offended by air.

Rinna said, "Go home."

Pell said, "I came to watch."

"Home."

"I can sit."

"You can sit at home."

Nella waved.

"Greg is ruining your part."

Pell looked at me.

"How badly?"

"Professionally."

He smiled weakly.

Then coughed.

Rinna physically pointed toward door.

Pell left.

Good bossing inside her authority.

We did one last run.

This time no stops.

Not because perfect.

Because Rinna wanted whole shape.

I missed one word.

Nella stepped on one line.

A chair leg caught hem.

Renn dropped something backstage.

Scene still worked.

At "You came home poor," I did not think about my own money.

At least not first.

I thought about Harrow.

That was probably acting.

At final shirt exchange, Nella handed it to me.

"This won't fit me."

"I know."

I held shirt.

No blackout because rehearsal.

Silence.

Rinna closed script.

"Fine."

High praise.

"How fine?"

"Enough."

Nella sat and removed painful shoe immediately.

I pointed.

"Victory."

"Blister."

"Less poetic."

"Better."

Rinna gave me one copper.

I looked at it.

"No."

She frowned.

"What?"

"I wasn't on paid call."

"You worked half day."

"You asked read."

"That is work."

There.

Old mistake.

Thinking theatre participation became gift if informal.

No.

Labor.

"Fine."

I took copper.

Cash twenty-one.

Vale eleven.

No debt payment.

Good.

"Does this make me replacement Harrow?"

"No," Rinna said.

"Excellent."

"Pell is better."

"Everyone committed to clarity."

Nella said, "You got less bad."

I bowed from chair.

"Finally."

She threw folded stocking at me.

I missed.

Acting did not improve reflex.

Outside the theatre, evening traffic had thickened around the market turn. I waited for a handcart to clear instead of trying to angle both crutches through the narrowing gap. The driver apologized after he had already blocked everything.

"No injury."

"Still sorry."

"Accepted."

He moved.

I moved.

Nobody wrote it down.

That felt almost luxurious after South Road.

My hands were tired from the rehearsal more than I expected. Not from carrying. From standing, sitting, pages, crutch, shirt, chair, repeated resets. Acting apparently had logistics too.

Of course it did.

On way home I spent one bit on hot bread.

Not because income.

Because bread was hot.

Important distinction.

Lyssa got home later.

She found me trying Harrow's line while fixing my shirt seam with the bad thread she had rejected.

"Why are you talking to yourself?"

"Acting."

"Same question."

I told her I had read Harrow.

She knew play enough.

"The broke brother?"

"Yes."

She looked at me.

"No."

"What?"

"Do not make face."

"I didn't."

"You did."

"Everyone is director."

"Did you do good?"

"Less bad."

"That sounds believable."

I showed one copper.

"Paid rehearsal."

"Good."

No question about Vale.

No accounting.

She picked up my repaired seam.

Pulled once.

Thread held.

She looked annoyed.

"Still bad thread."

"Working bad thread."

"Not same as good."

Hessa had colonized sewing too.

We ate hot bread.

I told her one useful thing from rehearsal.

"Nella said her pause belongs to her."

Lyssa nodded immediately.

"Yes."

"Why everyone knows things before me?"

"Because you talk during pauses."

Cruel.

Accurate.

I considered response.

Did not make one.

Lyssa smiled.

There.

Twenty-one copper.

Eleven Vale.

Bits unspecified.

Magic unchanged.

Work limb unchanged.

No deal.

No organizer contract.

No artifact.

Renn was back.

Pell was sick but still Harrow.

Nella was better actor than me.

Rinna had paid me for work I almost treated like favor.

And for half a day I had not been intermediary, coordinator, debtor, verifier, patient, or experiment.

I had been actor.

Not excellent.

Not secretly brilliant.

Not transformed.

Just good enough to make scene better once I stopped trying to stand outside it and manage what everyone else was doing.

That was plenty.

Tomorrow could become complicated again.

Tonight there was hot bread and bad thread and Lyssa deliberately leaving a pause open after I had just learned not to fill one.

I lasted maybe three seconds.

"What?"

She laughed.

Failure remained available.
